<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'S' set within a square woodcut border featuring dense, swirling acanthus leaves and decorative floral scrollwork.</image-desc>These efforts of mine, humble as they may be, present themselves before **YOUR** gaze. Therefore, although you might perhaps marvel at my audacity in prefixing such illustrious Names to these meager pages; nevertheless, you will not take offense at the piety of **YOUR** admirer, which wished at long last to reveal itself to **YOU** and to others. The more divine nature of **YOUR** soul and **YOUR** wisdom <note>original: "Prudentia"</note>, the appraiser of all things, will look upon the mind of the one offering this; it will not consider the price or the weight of the thing offered. Since the work has no value of its own, I have dared to borrow its worth from **YOU**. The [piety] of their own people has always been pleasing to the highest Princes and men... <note>The sentence ends abruptly due to the page break; the catchword indicates the next word is "piety".</note>

...piety <term>Pietas; a Latin concept encompassing religious devotion, patriotism, and duty to family and state</term>, even if declared by small and slight indicators: just as when a handful of water was offered to a Great Monarch, it seemed a more brilliant index of love and obedience than the enviable gifts of others <note>This refers to a famous anecdote regarding the Persian King Artaxerxes, who graciously accepted a handful of water from a poor peasant when others brought lavish gifts.</note>. Allow it to be a successful interpreter of my devoted affection before **YOU**. Allow it—from one who has no incense in his poor grove and shrine—to be offered to **YOUR** honor with this "salted meal" <term>mola salsa; a traditional Roman sacrificial offering made of grain and salt, used here to represent a humble but sincere academic offering</term>. Allow me, through this document, as if by a formal contract <term>syngrapha; a written bond or obligation</term>, to be publicly bound to **YOU** and to the perpetual cultivation of **YOUR** Name and Virtue. Having fulfilled my vow <note>original: "voti damnatus"; a legal and religious phrase meaning one has received what they prayed for and is now joyfully bound to perform the promised thanks</note>, I shall solicit the Supreme Judge of all things with unwearied prayers, that He may preserve **YOU** for a very long time for the benefit of **YOUR** city—renowned throughout the world and the "little jewel of Silesia" <note>original: "Silesiae ocello"; a term of endearment for the city of Wrocław (Breslau)</note>—and likewise for the Church, the Commonwealth, and the Republic of Letters <term>Res Publica Literaria; the international community of scholars and intellectuals</term>, for whose good **YOU** were born.

# Greetings to the Kind Reader <note>original: "B. L. S." for *Benevolo Lectori Salutem*; a standard Latin epistolary greeting</note>

It was my intention to address you at greater length here, *Friend Reader*. But a certain affliction of health has interrupted my purpose: hence I have been forced to make a brief summary of my words. If you are fair-minded, I shall have no need for an apology; if not, even an apology will achieve nothing. I present to you certain scholarly reflections <term>lucubrations; scholarly work produced by intense study, traditionally by lamplight at night</term> on several Biblical passages, not all of which were developed with the same method or (for why should I deny it?) the same diligence. 

For although for sixteen years now I have embraced a more sacred Philology <term>Philology; the study of language and literature; here specifically the linguistic study of the Bible's original languages</term> with an earnest affection, because I hoped for no negligible assistance toward sacred Theology, to which I have been consecrated from a boy: yet, as the current state of my affairs and fate stands, where my first and last care is almost entirely about earning a living, it is not permitted to always live as I wish. 

But if **GOD** grants a kinder fortune and allows me to struggle out of these miseries, you shall one day expect more solid works, more strictly thought out and in every respect more complete. Meanwhile, I wished to yield to the requests of certain friends and to list those small works <term>opuscula; a term for minor writings or shorter treatises</term> currently in existence which I have published so far, as well as those I have ready for the press, to be published if it should be worth the effort. In this, however, I will first ask you to restore for the better the following things noticed in passing during my re-reading: as also

### II.
## EXERCISE
# ON THE TERAPHIM, OR THE IDOLS OF LABAN, WHICH
->Rachel is recorded to have stolen in Genesis 31.<-

#### § 1.

<margin>Respondent: ERNST EBALD of Hesse, from Neuburg-Lauenburg.</margin>
Just as those do who snatch a sword or a knife from a madman—from whom they themselves would face the most immediate danger to their lives—so that *patriarchal soul, Rachel*, seems to have acted when she stole the materials of idolatry from her idol-worshipping father. By this reasoning, she clearly attempted to restrain him, lest he plunge himself into eternal destruction. Regarding this idol of Laban and the plan of Rachel, it seems not out of place if we bring certain matters into the open and examine the differing opinions of various authors according to the small measure of our own talent, with the modesty that is fitting and right. May the most high JEHOVAH favor our endeavors!

#### §. 2.

Since, however, names are the <term>vehicles</term> <note>original Greek: ὀχήματα (ochēmata)</note> of things, it does not seem foreign to our present treatment if, before we approach the matter itself and what is called the <term>height of the case</term> <note>original Greek: ὑπερβολὴν (hyperbolēn), referring here to the core or most significant part of the argument</note>, we examine the name itself. Accordingly, the idols of Laban are called <term>Teraphim</term> <note>Hebrew: תרפים (Teraphim)</note> more than once in the Sacred Scriptures. Regarding this word, so that we may fulfill all the requirements of a proper treatment, we shall first consider its *Origin*, and then investigate its *signification* <term>with precision</term> <note>original Greek: κατ’ ἀκρίβειαν (kat’ akribeian)</note>. In this, the opinion of those who derive *TERAPHIM* from the root <term>healed</term> <note>original Hebrew: רפא (rapha), meaning to heal or mend</note>, as if they "healed" or "cured" because they drove away disease like *Aesculapius* <note>The Greek god of medicine</note>, does not particularly please us at the outset; from this they also tend to compare the Greek <term>to heal</term> <note>original Greek: θεραπεύειν (therapeuein)</note>. So argues, among others, Gregorius Gregorius Francus in his *Sacred Lexicon, number 357, page 358.* However, the final radical letter <term>Aleph</term> <note>the Hebrew letter א</note> is not usually dropped in derived names according to grammatical analogy.

#### §. 3.

Some among the Hebrews derive the word from the root <term>to be remiss</term> <note>original Hebrew: רפה (raphah), meaning to be slack, weak, or lazy</note>, but they do not provide the same reasoning for the etymology. For some wish these idols to be so named because they called away their worshippers from their accustomed labors, and thus rendered their hands "remiss" or idle.

<summary>This page introduces a scholarly dissertation on the "Teraphim" (household idols) mentioned in the Bible, focusing on Rachel's motives for stealing them and an analysis of the word's linguistic origins.</summary>
<keywords>Teraphim, Laban, Rachel, Genesis 31, idolatry, etymology, Hebrew grammar, Aesculapius, healing, Gregorius Gregorius Francus</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Teraphim," moving from the introduction to specific Rabbinical and academic theories regarding its etymological roots in Hebrew and its potential connection to notions of "weakness" or "shame."</meta>

Thus the Zohar <note>A foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> on Genesis, *column 376*: **Rabbi Jose asked: "What were the Teraphim? They were a type of idol."** <note>original Hebrew: אמר יוסי מאן תרפים אלא עז עז הוו</note> **And a little later: "Why are they called <term>TERAPHIM</term>? Because they cause the hands to sink and become slack, just as it is said: And now** **remit** <note>Hebrew: הרף (hereph), from the root meaning to let go or slacken</note> **thy hand" (2 Samuel 24:16).** <note>original Latin: Dixit R. Joſe: Quid fuerunt Teraphim, niſi ſpecies idoli? ... Quare autem vocantur TERAPHIM? Quia deprimunt & remiſſas faciunt manus, uti dicitur: Et jam remitte manum tuam.</note> Others provide this reason: that the predictions of these idols were "slack" or weak, and rarely came true. Thus Rabbi Bechai says *on Genesis in this place*: **Furthermore, the word <term>TERAPHIM</term> takes its meaning from the word for "the weak"** <note>Hebrew: נרפים (neraphim)</note> **(that is, from the root** **to be slack or remiss** <note>Hebrew: רפה (raphah)</note>**), as if they were called "weak ones"** <note>Hebrew: רפים (raphim)</note> **because their words were a kind of slack or hollow prophecy.** <note>original Hebrew: ומלת תרפים נגזרת מלשון נרפים ויקרא להם רפים לרמוז עליהם כי דבריהם כמו נבואה רפה</note> Dr. David Rungius, in his *Lectures on Genesis, chapter 31, page 924*, reports that some derive <term>TERAPHIM</term> from the root meaning "to cease" or "to be idle" <note>Hebrew: רפה (raphah)</note> because people would cease their work on the Sabbath to devote themselves to the worship of these idols. Johannes Mercerus, in his *Commentary on Genesis, chapter 31, page 533*, mentions others who believe they were so named because they were placed in a fixed location from which they were not easily moved, but rather remained there always. However, since such word forms do not appear in the Hebrew text, and because the reasons added for these etymologies are unsatisfactory for more than one reason, we can hardly lend our support to this derivation.

#### §. 4 

Nor are there lacking among the Hebrews those who derive the word from **shame** or **filth**. <note>Hebrew: תרף (teraph)</note> Thus in the Zohar, in the place cited above, it is said: **"Why are they called <term>TERAPHIM</term>? On account of ignominy, as we are accustomed to say: In the place of filth or shame."** <note>original Hebrew: ובגין דאמאי אקרי תרפים בגין גנאי וחורף כמה דתנינן במקום התורף</note> This same etymology is also approved in the Tanchuma <note>An ancient collection of Jewish homilies</note> on the section **"And he went out"** <note>Hebrew: ויצא (Vayetze), Genesis 28:10–32:3</note> with these words: **"Why are they called <term>TERAPHIM</term>? Because they are a shameful and foul work."** <note>original Latin: Quare dicuntur TERAPHIM? Quia ſunt opus turpe & fœdum.</note> But we suspect, not without reason, that the word for **shame** <note>Hebrew: תורף (toref), or תורפה (torphah), or תורפיתא (torphita)</note> had not yet acquired that meaning for either the Syrians or the Hebrews in the time of Laban, but belongs to a much later age (and perhaps owes its origin to Latin, along with certain other words about which one may consult *The City of David* by **David Cohen de Lara**). And who would believe...

<meta>catchword: dat (from Latin "credat," meaning "believe")</meta>

<summary>This page explores various Rabbinical etymologies for the word "Teraphim," linking it to Hebrew roots for "weakness," "idleness," or "shame," while the author expresses skepticism about these theories based on linguistic history.</summary>
<keywords>Teraphim, Zohar, etymology, Hebrew roots, Rabbi Jose, Rabbi Bechai, David Rungius, Johannes Mercerus, Tanchuma, David Cohen de Lara, idolatry, Genesis 31</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the origin of the word "Teraphim." Having discussed the possibility of Hebrew roots related to weakness or shame, the author now explores the theory that the word is of foreign origin—specifically Syrian or Egyptian—and its potential connection to the "Seraphim" or the Egyptian deity "Serapis."</meta>

...would suggest that idolaters would give a name to their petty deities based on filth or shame?

§. 5. To us, the word <term>TERAPHIM</term> appears to be not Hebrew, but a foreign loanword. Johannes Mercerus <note>A 16th-century French Hebrew scholar</note> acknowledges the same in his *Additions to Pagninus's Thesaurus, page 3185*, but he does not explain what kind of word it might be. Louis de Dieu <note>A Dutch Protestant theologian and orientalist</note> in his *Observations on Genesis 31, page 41*, says among other things that it is Syrian, the same as the name <term>Seraphim</term> <note>original Hebrew: שרפים, a high rank of angels</note>, with the letter *Shin* (ש) changed to *Tav* (ת) according to Syrian custom. His words are these: <note>original Latin: "Fortasse Angelorum fuerunt imagines..."</note> **"Perhaps they were images of angels or dedicated to angels (TERAPHIM), from which an angelic spirit entered them and delivered oracles to those consulting them. From this conjecture, <term>Teraphim</term>** <note>original Hebrew: תרפים</note> **would be the same to me as <term>Seraphim</term>, the very well-known name of the angels, with the *Shin* changed to *Tav* in the Syrian manner."** Master Johannes Gaffarellus, a Frenchman, leans toward the same opinion in his book which he titled *Unheard-of Curiosities concerning the Talismanic Sculpture of the Persians, etc.* (that is: *Unheard-of Curiosities regarding the Talismanic Sculpture of the Persians, the Horoscope of the Patriarchs, and the Reading of the Stars, etc., chapter 3*), whose opinion we shall examine below. But how will de Dieu prove that the Syrians received that name from the Hebrews, or even that the name of the angels, the <term>Seraphim</term>, was already known to them at that time? And why would the sacred writers, writing in Hebrew, not use their own word <term>Seraphim</term> instead?

§. 6. The illustrious scholar Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger, a Swiss philologist and theologian</note> in his *Oriental Treasures, book 1, chapter 5, page 90*, considers the word to be Egyptian and says that the <term>Teraphim</term> were the <term>Serapides</term> of the Egyptians. He suggests this with these words: <note>original Latin: "Vocabulum תרפים cum ipsa fabrica ex Ægypto..."</note> **"The word <term>Teraphim</term>, along with the craftsmanship itself, seems to have passed from Egypt into Judea. They were infants wrapped in swaddling clothes, of which there are many such statues among the Egyptians, which they call Serapides, and they served in the place of amulets** <note>original Greek: φυλακτηρίων (phylaktērion), a protective charm</note>**. And indeed, the Arabic writer Aben-nephius confirms in plain words that the <term>Teraphim</term> are the Egyptian Serapides, in Kircher’s *Oedipus*, Volume II, Part II, Section 4, page 259. Furthermore, the name <term>Serapis</term> is composed of *sor*, meaning a chest or coffin** <note>original Greek: σωר</note>**, and *api*, which in Egyptian denotes a bull** <note>original Greek: ἄπι</note>**, so that it properly signifies 'the chest of the bull,' a deity to whom the Egyptians attributed many miracles. See the same author, Section III, pages 197 and 199."** Later, they were used for various purposes. Therefore, I would rather derive this obscure name from this word—by changing the *Shin* to a *Tav* (a change also admitted by those who believe <term>Teraphim</term> and <term>Seraphim</term> denote the same order of angels)—than from elsewhere, especially since the name was essentially the same in both places.

<summary>The author evaluates theories that the word "Teraphim" is foreign, specifically examining Louis de Dieu's link to the angelic "Seraphim" and Hottinger’s argument that it derives from the Egyptian deity "Serapis."</summary>
<keywords>Teraphim, Seraphim, etymology, Serapis, Louis de Dieu, Johannes Hottinger, Egyptian influence, Syrian language, idols, amulets</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the origins of the word "Teraphim," specifically critiquing the theories of Athanasius Kircher before proposing a new etymology based on Arabic roots.</meta>

...was their function. Kircher collected various figures of them in his *Syntagma IV, page 261*; he has more in *Volume III, Syntagma XVI, page 474* and following. Truly, this most learned man seems to have embraced his own opinion almost without hesitation. For the derivation—by which <term>Teraphim</term> <note>original Hebrew: תרפים</note> is said to descend from <term>Ser</term> and <term>Apis</term> <note>original Greek: σωρ & απ; referring to the Egyptian deity Serapis</note>—is a bit far-fetched. For even if the Syrians change the <term>Shin</term> <note>the 'sh' sound</note> of the Hebrews into <term>Tav</term> <note>the 't' sound</note> in some of their words, we doubt whether they also transform the sibilant <note>hissing</note> letters of other languages in words adopted into their own usage. Nor does the reasoning added to the etymology, which Kircher further produced in his *Coptic Supplement, page 590*, quite fit: what does an "ox-ark" have to do with the figure of a boy wrapped in swaddling clothes? Nor is it certain that the Syrians received the Teraphim from the Egyptians.

§. 7. To add our own contribution, we think the word <term>TERAPHIM</term> is indeed Syriac, but adapted to the Hebrew grammatical form in the Sacred Scripture, and its root can be most conveniently restored from the Arabic language, which is closely related to both Hebrew and Syriac. Since the root <term>Tarafa</term> <note>original Arabic: ترف</note> is still in use in Arabic today, it is not absurd that the same root previously existed in Syriac and even in Hebrew itself. Furthermore, in Arabic, *Tarafa* means: *To overflow with the good things and comforts of life, or to enjoy and rejoice in them*; in the Fourth Conjugation: *To bestow the comforts of life*, as the famous Jacob Golius proves in his *Arabic Lexicon, page 378*, from Al-Firuzabadi's *Kamis* <note>a famous 14th-century Arabic dictionary</note> and Maruphid's *Arabic-Persian Lexicon*. Francis Raphelengius, however, interprets *Tarafa* as: *To make fruitful and affluent*, just as Louis de Dieu also translates the same root in the place cited. Therefore, it does not seem improbable that the "godlings" of Laban the Syrian were called <term>TERAPHIM</term> because they warded off evils (as the "averters of evil" <note>original Greek: αλεξικακοι</note> once did) and conferred all kinds of prosperity upon the household. It was for this same purpose that the Romans worshipped <term>Averruncus</term> <note>a deity who averted calamity</note>, <term>Aesculanus</term> <note>a deity of wealth</note>, <term>Fortuna</term>, and the <term>Lares</term> <note>guardian deities of the home</note>.

§. 8. Furthermore, that word <term>TERAPHIM</term> <note>original Hebrew: תרפים</note> is found used fifteen times in the Sacred Scripture (not only thirteen times, as Rangolius observes on *1 Kings 19:13*), namely: *Genesis 31:19, 34, 35; Judges 17:5; chapter 18:14, 17, 18, 20; 1 Samuel 15:23; chapter 19:13, 16; 2 Kings 23:24; Ezekiel 21:26; Hosea 3:4; Zechariah 10:2*; and it is always in the plural. From this, it is doubtful...

<summary>The author rejects Athanasius Kircher’s Egyptian etymology for "Teraphim" and instead proposes a connection to an Arabic root meaning "prosperity," likening the idols to Roman household protector gods.</summary>
<keywords>Teraphim, Athanasius Kircher, Jacob Golius, Arabic etymology, prosperity, household gods, Lares, Averruncus, biblical philology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Teraphim." The previous page concluded by suggesting an Arabic root, "Tarafa," meaning to live in luxury or comfort, as a possible origin for the term.</meta>

...whether this word was only plural, or whether it truly recognized a certain singular form—as Schindler in his *Lexicon of Five Languages*, Avenarius in his *Lexicon*, and Mercerus in the place cited <note>original: "l.c." or "loco citato"</note> argue—naming the singular as <term>Tereph</term> <note>original Hebrew: תרף</note>, from which the plural <term>Teraphim</term> <note>original Hebrew: תרפים</note> is derived. To us, the word seems to have been of the plural number only, because it is used to refer to a single object in the Book of Samuel and, without a doubt, also in the Book of Judges; nevertheless, we do not object if anyone wishes to abound in their own opinion.

### §. 9.
In order to arrive at the true meaning, it could now be further investigated whether by <term>Teraphim</term> any likeness and image in general is indicated—whether of a human or of any other thing—or whether a certain specific kind of idol is implied. The former view pleases the Jesuit Benedictus Pererius in his commentary on *Genesis 31, Dispute I, page 814, column 2*. He says: "Teraphim signifies images or likenesses, or figures of any kind and of any things, but mostly of men or gods, which are called Idols; I mean images and figures expressed through painting, sculpture, or weaving. I have Saint Jerome, in his 113th Letter to Marcella, as the approver and author of this interpretation." He is followed by the Jesuit Jacob Bonfrère on the same passage, page 260, with these words: "In place of idols, the Hebrew has TERAPHIM; which word signifies any images, figures, statues, and idols, and is rarely used in a positive sense, and it seems in its primary meaning to signify Idols, etc. However, that Teraphim are occasionally also called, and can be called, any statues and images, even if they gave no oracular responses, does not seem doubtful to me and can be proven from 1 Kings 19, verses 13 and 16." <note>The author cites "1 Kings," but in the historical context of the Latin Vulgate, 1 and 2 Samuel were often called 1 and 2 Kings. The story of Michal placing a Teraphim in a bed is found in 1 Samuel 19.</note> Andreas Rivet agrees in his *Commentary on Hosea 3, page 111, column 2*, where he says it seems certain to him that Teraphim signifies in general images made in the likeness of the human body, and depending on the logic of the location and circumstances, they can be taken either as idols and idolatrous likenesses or for any other human representation. The same is the opinion of certain others, whom there is no need to review laboriously. But the latter view <note>that it refers to a specific type of idol</note> seems more likely to us to be consistent with the truth. Indeed, no passage of Scripture can be produced in which you can explain <term>Teraphim</term> as a mere image, except for the passage in Samuel, which we must deal with more fully later.

### §. 10.
Furthermore, Biblical interpreters have translated this word in more than one—

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Teraphim," surveying how various ancient versions and translations have rendered the term across different books of the Bible. The previous page ended by discussing how some scholars believe the word could refer to images in a general sense.</meta>

...translated it in this way. The **Vulgate Translator** <note>The Vulgate is the late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible, primarily the work of St. Jerome.</note> mostly translates it as *Idols*; in the Book of Samuel as a *statue*; and in some places, it retains the term **Teraphim**. Among the Greeks, *Aquila* <note>A 2nd-century AD translator known for his literal Greek rendering of the Hebrew Bible.</note> mostly uses <note>original Greek: μορφώματα</note> *Figures*, and in Hosea, <note>original Greek: φωτισμὸς</note> *Illumination*. The **Septuagint** <note>The "LXX," the most influential ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> in the story of Laban renders it as <note>original Greek: εἴδωλα</note> *idols*; in the story of Michal and David as <note>original Greek: κενοτάφια</note> *cenotaphs* <note>Here referring to a funerary effigy or empty monument; in the story of Michal, it describes the dummy she placed in David's bed to deceive Saul's guards.</note>; in Ezekiel as <note>original Greek: γλυπτὰ</note> *carvings*; in Hosea as <note>original Greek: δήλους</note> *manifestations*; and in Zechariah as <note>original Greek: ἀποφθεγγομένους</note> *those who speak out*. Elsewhere, they tend to retain <note>original Greek: θεραπεῖν</note> *therapein* <note>A Greek transliteration of the Hebrew word Teraphim.</note> (which *Symmachus* and *Theodotion* also do), as in *Judges 17:5, 18:14, 17, 18, 20; 1 Samuel 15:23; and 2 Kings 23:24*.

The **Chaldean** <note>Referring to the Targum, the Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible.</note> in the story of Laban uses <note>original Aramaic: צלמניא</note> *Images*, and so more often; in the story of Michal <note>original Aramaic: דמאין</note> *Simulacra* <note>Likenesses or effigies.</note>; and in Hosea <note>original Aramaic: מחويן</note> *Announcers*. The **Syriac** version in Genesis translates it as <term>Images</term> <note>original Syriac: ܨܠܡ̈ܐ; the author notes this does not necessarily mean "idols" in the pejorative sense, despite the English rendering.</note>; likewise in *1 Samuel 19:13* in the story of Michal as a <term>sculpted likeness</term> <note>original Syriac: ܦܬܟܪܐ ܓܠܝܦܐ</note>; in *2 Kings 23* as <term>idols</term> <note>original Syriac: ܦܬܟܪ̈ܐ</note>; in Hosea as <term>one who places or burns incense</term> <note>original Syriac: ܡܣܩ̈ܝ ܒܣܡ̈ܐ</note>; and in Zechariah as <term>known or familiar ones</term> <note>original Syriac: ܝܕܘ̈ܥܐ; the author notes the English translate this as "diviners".</note>

The **Arabic** in Genesis translates it as an <term>image or likeness</term> <note>original Arabic: تمثال</note>; so also in *1 Samuel 19*. In the story of Michal, and in *2 Kings 23:24*, as an <term>Idol</term> <note>original Arabic: صنم</note>; in Hosea as a <term>teacher</term> <note>original Arabic: مرشد</note> according to the English <note>The author likely refers to the translators of the London Polyglot Bible.</note> (properly: *one who directs in the right way*, for <term>murshid</term> is the Fourth Conjugation participle from <term>rashada</term>, *he stood on the right path*); in Zechariah as <term>speakers</term> <note>original Arabic: المتغوجين</note> according to the English (though *rather talkative or babbling*); and in Ezekiel as <term>carvings</term> <note>original Arabic: المنحوتات</note>. The **Persian** translator Jacob Tavus <note>A 16th-century translator of the Pentateuch.</note> in Genesis translates it as <term>astrolabes</term> <note>original Persian: اسطرلابها</note>. The **Ethiopic** in the same place retained the word <note>original: תرפין</note> *Teraphim*; so also the **Samaritan** version <note>original: ית תרפיה</note>. Whatever the case may be, one and the same meaning can be assigned to the word Teraphim in all passages of Scripture, and there is no need to split it into so many different meanings. And we indeed contend that Teraphim should always be translated as *Idols*, and specifically as *Lares* or *Penates* <note>In Roman religion, the Lares and Penates were deities who protected the household and the domestic hearth.</note>, and never as mere *Images*; we believe that no passage of Holy Scripture contradicts this assertion.

### §. II.
And so much for what we have chosen to bring forward regarding the name. Now we must speak further, both on what kind of things Laban’s idols were, and what Rachel’s intention or plan was in stealing them from her father.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the definition and physical nature of "Teraphim." The previous page concluded by noting how various ancient translations (Vulgate, Septuagint, and Targums) rendered the term as "images," "idols," or "carvings."</meta>

We present, therefore, above all else, the following description of the <term>Teraphim</term> <note>original Greek: τῶν Teraphim</note>: <term>Teraphim</term> were *idolatrous household statues of human form, fashioned by the Syrians from various materials so that they might be the authors of good fortune and the revealers of hidden things.* Here we briefly touch upon their *class*, namely that they were *statues*—not just any kind, but *idolatrous* ones. We derive a more specific distinction from their *efficient cause*, namely the Syrians, by whom (as far as we know) such idols were invented and spread to other peoples; as well as from their *material*; from their *external form*, insofar as <term>Teraphim</term> are considered as statues (which then, through an external designation, become idols through an act of the mind conceiving of divine excellence in the object and an act of the will consenting to submission and veneration); and finally, from their *purpose and use*.

§. 12. First, we shall deal with the *matter and form*, yet treating the *entire construction and its method jointly*. And since wondrous things are read in the Hebrew records, we shall briefly touch upon them here, so that once obstacles are removed, we may thereafter penetrate into the inner sanctuaries of truth more easily and successfully. *Rabbi Eliezer* <note>Eliezer ben Hyrcanus, a prominent Jewish sage (Tanna) of the 1st and 2nd centuries AD.</note>, a most ancient writer, in his "Chapters" <note>The *Pirke De-Rabbi Eliezer*, an aggadic-midrashic work.</note> chapter 36, records the following concerning the <term>Teraphim</term>: <note>original Hebrew: ומה הן התרפים שוחטים אדם בכור ומולקין את ראשו ומולחים אותו במלח ובשמן וכותבין על ציץ זהב שם רוח טומאה ומניחין אותו תחת לשונו ונותנין אותו בקיר ומדליקין נרות לפניו ומשתחוים לו והוא מדבר עמהם</note> i. e. *What then are the <term>Teraphim</term>? They would slaughter a firstborn and wring off* (this is how <term>Buxtorf</term> <note>Johannes Buxtorf, a leading 17th-century Hebraist.</note> translates the word <term>molkin</term> <note>original Hebrew: מולקין</note>; <term>Hottinger</term> and <term>G. H. Vorstius</term> translate it as *split apart*; <term>P. Fagius</term> as *cut off*; <term>Selden</term> <note>John Selden, a 17th-century English jurist and scholar of Oriental antiquities.</note> as *cut with a nail or tore apart with the hands*) *his head and seasoned it with salt and oil, and they wrote upon a golden plate the name of a certain unclean Spirit and placed it under his tongue. Afterward, they placed that head against the wall and lit lamps before it and bowed down before it, and thus that image spoke with them.* These same things are found repeated word for word in the <term>Tanchuma</term> <note>An ancient collection of homiletic midrashim on the Pentateuch.</note> on the portion *Vayetze*. And <term>Jonathan Pseudozielides</term> <note>Referring to the Targum Pseudo-Jonathan, a western targum (Aramaic translation) of the Torah.</note> almost—

This method of composing* <term>TERAPHIM</term> *must be seen as far too cruel and it recoils from all humanity. Carthage is indeed a witness that it was long the custom to appease Saturn with human victims; but it seems in no way likely to me that such savage and foul idolatry prevailed in the family of Laban, since it is clear from Genesis 31:53 that he was not entirely destitute of the knowledge of the true God. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Teraphim, moving from the macabre legends of severed heads mentioned on the previous page to more "scientific" or astronomical interpretations from medieval Jewish scholars like Raschi and Kimchi.</meta>

<margin>2 Kings 23:24</margin> <term>Teraphim: images</term> <margin>Through his sorcery, the one who makes them must focus on one specific hour and a particular year suitable for the task.</margin> That is: *Teraphim were images that spoke through the magic arts. It was necessary for those manufacturing them to observe a certain hour and a certain year suitable for this matter.* And regarding <margin>Ezekiel 21:21</margin>, the same author <note>Referring to Raschi (Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki, 1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French commentator on the Torah and Talmud.</note> says: <margin>The Teraphim is an image that speaks through sorcery; there is a specific prepared hour such that, if one completes the image then, it possesses the power of perpetual speech.</margin> That is: <term>Teraphim</term> *is an image speaking through the magic art, and there is a certain prepared hour in which, if someone should finish it, there is perpetual speech within it.* So also on <margin>Hosea 3:4</margin> <margin>Teraphim are images made by focusing on a specific hour designated for this; they speak of their own accord and announce hidden things.</margin> <term>Teraphim</term> *are images made with a specific intention at an hour suitable for this purpose, and thus they speak of themselves and announce hidden things.* So says he.

§. 15. Some, moreover, ascribe the use of timekeeping instruments <note>original: "horologiorum"</note> to the <term>Teraphim</term>. Rabbi Bechai <note>Bahya ben Asher (1255–1340), a Spanish rabbi and commentator.</note> reports that this was the opinion of Rabbi Moses Nachmanides <note>Also known as Ramban (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar and mystic.</note> <margin>in this place:</margin> <margin>Nachmanides suggested regarding the Teraphim that they were instruments for receiving the hours and they would divine with them to know future things.</margin> That is: *The opinion of Rabbi Nachmanides concerning the <term>Teraphim</term> is that they were instruments made for knowing the hours and divining the future.* And Rabbi David Kimchi <note>Known as Radak (1160–1235), a grammarian and biblical commentator from Provence.</note> <margin>on 1 Samuel 19:13</margin> writes: <margin>And there are some who say that it is a bronze instrument made to know the divisions of the hours, and they would see the future in them by the judgment of the stars.</margin> That is: *Certain people say that the <term>Teraphim</term> was a bronze instrument, made so that the parts of the hours might be known from it, and at the same time the future might be seen through the benefit of the stars.* So also Rabbi Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a polymath and biblical commentator.</note> <margin>on Genesis 31</margin> notes among other things: <margin>And there are some who say that it is a bronze instrument made to know the divisions of the hours.</margin> That is: *There are those who say that the <term>Teraphim</term> was a bronze instrument, made so that they might know the parts of the hours from it.* Jacob Tavvusius <note>Jacob ben Joseph Tavus, who published a Persian translation of the Pentateuch in 1546.</note>, the Persian interpreter, had the same thing in mind when he translated the word as "astrolabes." But Kimchi, in the place cited above, adds that a clock <note>original: "horologium"</note> would be unsuitable, because such a thing would have been placed by Michal in David's bed to pretend it was David <note>Referring to the biblical story where Michal helps David escape by placing an idol in his bed to fool Saul's guards.</note>.

<meta>Catchword: tura. This page continues the exploration of the nature of the Teraphim, moving from the mechanical/astrological theories of the previous page to more gruesome legends found in Jewish tradition, followed by a rationalist critique from Protestant scholars.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

Rabbi Eliezer expressed the Hebrew literally in Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldaicè"</note> in this manner: 
<gloss>And Rachel stole the images: for they would slaughter a firstborn man, whose head they would pull off and salt with salt and spices, and they would write divinations upon a golden plate and place it under his tongue and stand him up against the wall, and he would speak with them.</gloss> <note>This is a translation of the Aramaic text provided: "וגנבת רחל ית צלמניא דהוון נכסין גברא בוכרא..." which describes a macabre legend of a mummified speaking head.</note> That is: *And Rachel stole the images: for they used to slaughter a firstborn man, whose head they would tear off (or "cut off," as the English say) and season with salt and spices; they would write divinations under his tongue and erect him against the wall, and thus they spoke with them.* Compare also Elia Levita <note>A renowned 16th-century German-Jewish grammarian and scholar.</note> the German in his work *Tishbi*. From the Hebrews, Nicolaus of Lyra <note>A 14th-century Franciscan teacher of Jewish descent whose literal commentaries influenced Luther.</note> on *Genesis 31* and *Judges 17*, and Alfonso Tostado of Avila <note>A prolific 15th-century Spanish bishop and commentator.</note> in this place, have transferred this fable into their own commentaries. The former was a Christian from a Jewish background, the latter a "Judaizer" <note>A term often used by later scholars for those who relied heavily on Jewish traditions.</note>; neither was an enemy to such stories.

§. 13. But who does not see that in this opinion—supported as it is by no arguments—there is **nothing sound**? <note>original Greek: "οὐδὲν ὑγιὲς"</note> Hence our Blessed Martin Luther rightly explodes <note>In the sense of "rejects with force."</note> the same in *Volume 11 of the Wittenberg German edition, folio 216.* Nor does the judgment of G. H. Vorstius <note>Willem Hendrik Vorst (1623–1676), a scholar of Rabbinic literature.</note> on the *Chapters of Rabbi Eliezer, page 210*, seem to be disapproved, expressed in the following words: *What Rabbi Eliezer relates here concerning the horrendous preparation of the* <term>TERAPHIM</term> *and, from him, the Tanchuma, the Targum of Jonathan on Genesis 31:19, Elia Levita, and others, contains nothing certain, but is drawn from a heap of traditions. This method of composing the* <term>TERAPHIM</term> *must be seen as too cruel and shrinks from all humanity. Carthage is indeed a witness that it was long the custom to appease Saturn with human victims: but it by no means seems likely to me that such savage and foul idolatry prevailed in the family of Laban, since it is clear from Genesis 31:53 that he was not entirely destitute of the knowledge of the true God. And even if Laban's impiety were perhaps so great, since there is no corroboration for the story from elsewhere, we, led by better reason, leave these* **old wives' tales** <note>original Greek: "γραώדεις μύθους"</note> *to the Jews.*

§. 14. Others think that the <term>TERAPHIM</term> were certain instruments manufactured with a specific skill according to the position of the stars, so that future things could be known from them. Thus most of the Rabbis write that the <term>TERAPHIM</term> were images made by magic art at certain times, which [predicted] the future...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the Teraphim. ...would indicate their nature. Thus, **Rashi on 2 Kings 23:24** <note>Rashi (Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki, 1040–1105) was a preeminent French rabbi and author of comprehensive commentaries on the Talmud and Tanakh.</note> says: <gloss>Teraphim are images that speak by means of his magic; the one who makes them must focus on a specific hour and a specific year suitable for this.</gloss> <note>original: "תרפים צלמים שמדברים על ידי כשפיו העושה אותן צריך לכוין שעה אחת ושנה אחת ראויה לכך"</note> That is: *Teraphim were images speaking by the art of magic. For those fashioning them, it was necessary to observe a certain hour and a certain year appropriate for this matter.* And on **Ezekiel 21:21**, the same author says: <gloss>A Teraphim is an image speaking through magic, and there is a specific hour intended, so that if one makes it then, there is a perpetual speech within it.</gloss> <note>original: "תרפים הוא צלם המדבר במכשפות ויש שעה מכוונת שאם עושהו יש בו דבור לעולם"</note> That is: *A Teraphim is an image speaking through the magic art, and there is a specific hour prepared, such that if someone completes it then, there is a perpetual voice in it.* So also on **Hosea 3:4**: <gloss>Teraphim are images made by the intention of a specific hour made for this, and they speak of their own accord and announce hidden things.</gloss> <note>original: "תרפים הם צלמים הנעשים בכוון שעה אחת העשויה לכך ומדברים מאיליהם ומגידין נעלמות"</note> *Teraphim are images made with the intent of a specific hour suitable for this purpose, and thus they speak from themselves and announce occult matters.* Thus says Rashi.

§. 15. Furthermore, some ascribe the use of clocks or time-keeping devices <note>original: "horologiorum"</note> to the Teraphim. **Rabbi Bechai** <note>Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century Spanish commentator.</note> reports that this was the opinion of **Rabbi Moses Nachmanides** <note>Also known as Ramban, a leading medieval Jewish scholar and mystic.</note> on this passage: <gloss>The observation of Nachmanides regarding the Teraphim is that they were instruments to receive the hours and to divine in order to know the future.</gloss> <note>original: "הערת הרמבן בתרפים כי היו כלים לקבל השעות ויקסמו לדעת העתידות"</note> That is: *The opinion of Rabbi Nachmanides concerning the Teraphim is that they were instruments made for knowing the hours and divining future things.* And **Rabbi David Kimchi** <note>Known as Radak, a 12th-13th century grammarian and biblical commentator.</note> writes on **1 Samuel 19:13**: <gloss>And some say that it is a bronze instrument made to know the divisions of the hours, and they would see the future in them by the judgment of the constellations.</gloss> <note>original: "ויש אומרים שהוא כלי הנחשת העשוי לדעת חלקי השעות ויראו בהם העתידות במשפט המזלות"</note> That is: *Certain people say that Teraphim were a bronze instrument made so that the parts of the hours might be known from it, and that the future might be seen at the same time by the benefit of the stars.* So too **Rabbi Ibn Ezra** <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra, a prolific 12th-century Spanish polymath and commentator.</note> notes on **Genesis 31**, among other things: <gloss>And some say that it is a bronze instrument made to know the divisions of the hours.</gloss> <note>original: "ויש אומרים שהוא כלי נחשת העשוי לדעת חלקי השעות"</note> That is: *There are those who say that the Teraphim was a bronze instrument made so that they might know the parts of the hours from it.* **Jacob Tavus**, the Persian translator, had the same thing in mind when he translated the word as **astrolabes** <note>A historical astronomical instrument used for timekeeping and navigation.</note>. But Kimchi, in the place cited above, adds that a clock <note>original: "horologium"</note> would be unsuitable, since it was placed by Michal in David's bed to deceive people into thinking it was David <note>Referring to the story in 1 Samuel 19, where Michal helps David escape her father Saul by placing a Teraphim in his bed to look like a sleeping body.</note>.

Word for word, the Hebrew of Rabbi Eliezer expressed it in Aramaic <note>original: "Chaldaicè"</note> in this manner:

<gloss>And Rachel stole the images: for they would slaughter a firstborn man, whose head they would tear off and preserve with salt and spices; they would write divinations on a gold plate and place it under his tongue, and they would set it up against the wall, and it would speak with them.</gloss> <note>original Aramaic: "וגנבת רחל ית צלמניא דהוון נכסין גברא בוכרא וחומין רישיה ומלחין ליה במלחא ובוסמנין ויהבין קוסמין בציצא דהבא ויהבון תחות לישניה ומקימין ליה בכותלא וממלל עמהון"</note> 

That is: *And Rachel stole the images: for they slaughtered a firstborn man, whose head they tore off (the English say "amputated") and seasoned with salt and spices; they wrote divinations under his tongue and erected it against the wall, and thus they spoke with them.* Compare also Elias Levita "the German" <note>Elias Levita (1469–1549) was a renowned Renaissance Hebrew philologist and teacher to many Christian humanists.</note> in his work *Tishbi*. From the Hebrews, Nicholas of Lyra (in his commentaries on **Genesis 31** and **Judges 17**) and Alfonso Tostatus of Avila <note>A 15th-century Spanish bishop and prolific commentator known for his immense learning.</note> (at this passage) transferred this fable into their own commentaries. The former was a Jew-turned-Christian, the latter a "Judaizer" <note>A term often used in this period for Christian scholars who relied heavily on Jewish exegetical traditions.</note>; neither was an enemy to fables.

### §. 13.

But in that opinion, supported as it is by no arguments, who does not see that there is <term>nothing sound</term> <note>original Greek: "οὐδὲν ὑγιὲς"</note>? Wherefore our Blessed Luther rightly rejects it in **Volume 11 of the Wittenberg German edition, folio 216.** Nor does the censure of G. H. Vorstius <note>Willem Hendrik Vorst (1592–1652), a scholar known for translating Jewish texts into Latin.</note> in his notes on the *Pirke Eliezer*, **page 210**, seem to be disapproved, conceived in the following words:

> "What Rabbi Eliezer relates here about the horrific construction of the <term>TERAPHIM</term>—and after him the *Tanchuma* (folio 15, 3), *Targum Jonathan* on **Genesis 31:19**, Elias Levita, and others—contains nothing certain, but is drawn from a cesspool of traditions. This method of composing the <term>TERAPHIM</term> must seem too cruel and shrinks from all humanity. Carthage is indeed a witness that it was a custom to appease Saturn with human victims; but that such a savage and foul idolatry prevailed in the family of Laban seems not at all likely to me, since it appears from **Genesis 31:53** that he was not entirely destitute of the knowledge of the true God. And even if Laban's impiety were perhaps that great, since there is no supporting evidence for this fabrication from any other source, we, led by better reason, leave these <term>old wives' tales</term> <note>original Greek: "γραώδεις μύθους," literally "myth-making of old women."</note> to the Jews."

### §. 14.

Others think that the <term>TERAPHIM</term> <note>These household gods or idols are mentioned several times in the Old Testament, often associated with divination.</note> were certain instruments manufactured with a specific skill according to the position of the stars, so that future events could be known from them. Thus most of the Rabbis write that the <term>TERAPHIM</term> were images made at certain times by the magic art, which fu... <meta>catchword: tur (continuing to "futura" / "future")</meta>

While the previous page focused on the gruesome legend of the "severed head," this page examines the views of medieval Jewish commentators like Rashi, Nachmanides, and Kimchi, who suggested the Teraphim were either magical speaking statues or sophisticated astronomical instruments used for divination.</meta>

nature indicated. Thus **Rashi on 2 Kings 23:24** <note>original Hebrew: תרפים צלמים שמדברים על ידי כשפין העושה אותן צריך לכוין שעה אחת ושנה אחת ראויה לכך; original Latin: Teraphim fuerunt imagines, loquentes arte magicâ. Fabricantibus eas necesse fuit observare certam horam certumq; anni rei huic convenientem.</note>: *Teraphim were images that spoke through the art of magic. For those fashioning them, it was necessary to observe a specific hour and a specific year suitable for this purpose.* And on **Ezekiel 21:21**, he says the same: *The **Teraphim** is an image speaking through magic; there is a specific, prepared hour in which, if someone constructs it, it possesses a perpetual voice.* Likewise on **Hosea 3:4**: *The **Teraphim** are images made by focusing on a specific hour suited for this task; thus they speak of their own accord and announce hidden things.* This is his account.

### §. 15.

Some, moreover, attribute the use of timekeeping instruments to the <term>Teraphim</term> <note>original Greek: τοῖς Teraphim</note>. **Rabbi Bechai**, commenting on this passage, reports this to be the opinion of **Rabbi Moses Nachmanides** <note>Also known as Ramban (1194–1270), a leading medieval Spanish Sephardic Jewish scholar and kabbalist.</note>: *The opinion of Nachmanides regarding the **Teraphim** is that they were instruments made to receive the hours and to divine the future.* And **Rabbi David Kimchi** <note>Also known as Radak (1160–1235), a famous grammarian and biblical commentator from Narbonne.</note> writes on **1 Samuel 19:13**: *Some say that it was a bronze instrument, made so that the parts of the hours might be known from it, and the future might be seen through the judgment of the stars.* Likewise, **Abraham ibn Ezra** notes on **Genesis 31**, among other things: *There are those who say that it was a bronze instrument made to recognize the divisions of the hours.* **Jacob Tavvusius**, the Persian translator, had the same idea when he translated the word as "astrolabes" <note>An ancient astronomical instrument used for solving problems relating to time and the position of the stars.</note>. However, Kimchi adds in the same place that a timekeeping instrument would be unsuitable <note>original Latin: ineptum</note> for the story in which Michal placed one in David's bed to pretend it was David, as it would hardly be a convincing substitute.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Teraphim by drawing parallels with magical talismans from Arabic and Samaritan traditions. It transitions from the previous page's analysis of medieval Jewish commentators (Rashi, Nachmanides, and Kimchi) who viewed the Teraphim as astronomical instruments or speaking statues, to a broader philological and historical examination of such "animated" objects in the Near East.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

### §. 16.

Moreover, the images mentioned above—which were constructed according to a specific constellation and the calculation of the horoscopic signs so that they might reveal the future or emit voices—are called *Tilsemas* or *Talismas* <note>original Arabic: طلسمات</note> by the Arabs. Thus, **Antonio Giggei** <note>An Italian orientalist and priest, author of the *Thesaurus Arabici* (1632).</note> in his *Arabic Treasury* on the root <term>طلسم</term> [*t-l-s-m*] says: *Tilsemas* are *images made according to the influence of the stars.* And **Jacob Golius** <note>A celebrated Dutch orientalist and mathematician (1596–1667).</note> in his *Arabic Lexicon*, page 1473, states: *A **Tilsemon** <note>original Arabic: طلسم</note> is a magical image; of the kind that used to be engraved on objects and consecrated under a specific horoscope to achieve a certain efficacy.* The name seems to be a corruption of the Greek <term>τέλεσμα</term> <note>original Greek: télesma; meaning a completed rite or a consecrated object.</note>, although it might also be traced back to the Coptic <term>ⲧⲁⲗⲙⲉϣ</term> [*talmesh*] or the Samaritan <term>צלם</term> [*tsalam*], meaning *to create* or *to make*. Regarding the practice itself, the Magi used to devise certain diagrams for themselves based on the stars, many of which were modeled after the constellations depicted on a celestial globe. If the Sun or another Planet entered these constellations, they would fashion images which they believed, through the influence of the stars, were imbued with the power to ward off evils. (For instance, they believed that a certain scorpion-image, crafted while the celestial Scorpion was in the ascendant, would drive away earthly scorpions.) They even believed these images could emit voices, though in reality, these were produced by assisting spirits <note>literally "assisting forms," implying demonic or spiritual entities.</note> who sought to increase human superstition.

### §. 17.

It will not be a burden for us to bring forward one or two examples of these *Tilsemas* from ancient authors. In the *Samaritan Chronology*, chapter 46, as cited by **Hottinger** <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian.</note>, the Romans are said to have placed a *Tilleman* [Talisman] on the summit of Mount Gerizim. This was a bronze bird that would turn in circles along with the sun itself. *If a Samaritan were to climb the mountain, the bird would cry out: "A Hebrew!"* <note>original Latin: Hebræus.</note> *From this, the Romans, gathering that a Samaritan was on the mountain, would come out and kill him.* 

Hottinger provides another example of a *Tilsema* in the same passage, taken from the *Chorography of the Holy Land* by **Jacob Justus the Jew**: *The Adonis River of Phoenicia—well known and so named from the fable of Adonis—is today called the **River of the Dog**. This is because the inhabitants of that region convince themselves that upon a certain high mountain near that river, there was once a dog cast from metal by magical art. When the ships of pirate enemies crossed the sea, the dog would bark; everyone, warned by its barking, would immediately gather and to*... <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "defen-" suggests the next page continues with "defensionem," meaning "to the defense."</note>

<summary>This page defines talismans (Tilsemas) as magically charged images linked to astrology and provides historical legends of speaking statues, such as a bronze bird on Mount Gerizim and a barking metal dog in Phoenicia.</summary>
<keywords>talismans, Tilsemas, Arabic magic, astrology, Mount Gerizim, River Adonis, Nahr al-Kalb, Antonio Giggei, Jacob Golius, Johann Heinrich Hottinger, speaking statues</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of animated statues and talismans. Having previously established the Arabic and Coptic etymologies for "talismans," the author now moves into historical anecdotes from Western Christendom and classical Greek sources to illustrate the long-standing belief in "speaking" or "prophetic" images, specifically the legend of Pope Sylvester II's brazen head.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

...had taken up arms for defense, and from this, the river acquired its name. To these, we shall add a third example from the Englishman **John Bale**, who, in his *Acts of the Roman Pontiffs, p. 164*, reports the following concerning **Sylvester II** <note>Sylvester II (Gerbert of Aurillac, c. 946–1003) was a prolific scholar and Pope who was posthumously accused of sorcery due to his great learning in mathematics and astronomy.</note>: *Sylvester II concealed magical arts within the papacy and hid that intimacy which he held with the devil. Nevertheless, he kept a brazen head in a certain secret place, from which he always received answers whenever he sought anything from that evil spirit. At last, taking great pleasure in his papal heights, he inquired of his companion, the devil, how long he would possess the pontificate. The devil replied ambiguously: that he would not die before he had celebrated Mass in Jerusalem.*

However, **William of Malmesbury**, a Benedictine monk, relates these matters somewhat differently, writing in his *Deeds of the Kings of the English, book 2, chapter 10*: *Concerning Gerbert (for so Sylvester was called before his Pontificate), rumor spread that he had cast for himself the head of a statue under a specific inspection of the stars—namely, when all the planets were considering the beginnings of their courses (it is doubtful what he means by this)—which would not speak unless questioned, but would pronounce the truth either affirmatively or negatively. For example, when Gerbert said, "Shall I be the Apostolic One <note>A title for the Pope.</note>?" the statue would respond, "Yes." "Shall I die before I sing Mass in Jerusalem?" "No."* 

Truly this Gerbert (called Gilbert by Bale), to add this <term>in passing</term> <note>original Hebrew: אגב אורחא (*agav orcha*), a common phrase in rabbinic literature meaning "by the way."</note>, was miserably deceived by the answer regarding the time of his death. For he thought of the city of Jerusalem; however, the Oracle intended the sacred building so named in Rome. For when the Pontiff, not having grasped the meaning of the oracle, was performing sacred rites on a certain day of the Lenten station in Rome at the Basilica of the Holy Cross—which was called "Jerusalem"—he paid his debt to Satan, and his life *fled with a groan, indignant, to the shades below.* <note>original: "cum gemitu fugit indignata sub umbras." This is a famous line from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, used here to describe a tragic or cursed death.</note>

### §. 18.

Furthermore, **Selden** <note>John Selden (1584–1654), a renowned English polymath and jurist, author of *De Diis Syris*.</note> considers that certain types of talismans were also the Greek <term>stoicheia</term> <note>original Greek: στοιχεῖα; usually meaning "elements," but in an astrological context, it refers to the celestial bodies or consecrated figures used in magic.</note>, about which we read in the so-called *Centiloquy of Ptolemy*: *Those forms which exist in generation and corruption are influenced by the celestial forms. For this reason, those who work with consecrated signs <note>original Greek: στοιχειωματικοὶ (*stoicheiomatikoi*), those who create talismans or astrological "elements."</note> use these things, observing the influences of the stars upon them.* This means: *The faces existing in generation and corruption are subject to the celestial faces...*

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Pope Sylvester II and his prophetic "brazen head," comparing different historical accounts of the Pope's supposed pact with the devil. It then transitions to a scholarly discussion on the Greek "stoicheia" as a form of astrological talisman.</summary>
<keywords>Sylvester II, John Bale, William of Malmesbury, brazen head, Jerusalem, talismans, John Selden, Ptolemy, stoicheia, astrological magic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of talismans and animated statues. While the previous page (page 11) focused on medieval legends like the brazen head of Sylvester II, this page (page 82) delves into the scholarly and linguistic origins of these objects, specifically linking them to the "Teraphim" of the Bible and the magical practices of the Chaldeans and Sabaeans.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

...heavenly things: for this reason, the makers of **elements** <note>original Greek: *stoicheiōn*. In this context, it refers to astral or zodiacal talismans.</note> make use of those who observe the *entry of the stars into them*. To the same category, the same [John] Selden reduces the **consecrated objects of Apollonius** <note>original Greek: *ta tou Apolloniou telesmata*. Apollonius of Tyana was a famous miracle-worker and philosopher of the 1st century AD.</note> mentioned by Justin [Martyr], and the **Iynges** <note>magical spinning wheels or "wheels of fate" used in theurgy to attract divine influences</note>, or the **Hecatean Whirl** or **Hecatean Sphere**, namely gold cylinders or globes called "Hecate's ones," decorated with various characters, containing a sapphire inside, and spun with a leather thong; by rotating these, the Chaldean Magi would invoke demons; as well as the **Magic Rhombus**. 

But we shall not pursue those matters in further detail. More information on **Talismanic images** <note>original: *Tilsemis*, a Latinization of the Arabic/Hebrew term for talismans.</note> will be provided by: **Rabbi Maimonides** in *On Foreign Worship, chapters 3 and 4, and The Guide for the Perplexed, book 3, chapter 29, and elsewhere*; **Leunclavius** in *Turkish Pandects, chapter 130*; **Joseph Scaliger** in *Letters, book 2, letter 119, and on the Barbaric Sphere of Manilius*; **John Selden** the Englishman in *Syntagma I on the Syrian Gods, chapter 2*; **Johann Buxtorf** in the *Talmudic Lexicon, page 879*; **Edward Pococke** in *Notes on Abul-Faraj, page 140*; **Johann Heinrich Hottinger** in *Oriental History, book 1, chapter 8, page 188 of the first edition*; **Hugo Grotius** on *Genesis 31*; **Athanasius Kircher** in *Oedipus [Aegyptiacus], volume 2, part 1, chapter 5, page 383 at the bottom*; **Jacques Gaffarel** in *Curiosities, chapter 3*; and others.

§. 19. Furthermore, the illustrious Hottinger thinks in the cited work (page 196) that Laban’s <term>Teraphim</term> <note>Laban’s household idols, mentioned in the Book of Genesis, which Rachel stole.</note> were also **Talismanic images**; from which it follows that the **Serapides** <note>likely referring to small idols of the god Serapis used for divination</note> must also have been Talismanic images, unless Hottinger wishes to contradict himself. As for us, while we do not entirely deny that Laban’s idols were formed according to a specific constellation in the manner of Talismans, we nevertheless do not think it has been sufficiently proven by the two reasons that most learned man brings forward. 

The first of these is: that the Talismans of the Sabaeans <note>An ancient people of South Arabia often associated with star-worship.</note> (to whom their origin is usually ascribed) were only made at a certain time. He claims the Rabbis testify the same regarding the <term>Teraphim</term>. The second: that while Talismans were mostly **averting gods** <note>original: *Deos averruncos*, deities intended to ward off bad luck or evil influences.</note>, they were sometimes assigned the duty of **oracular responses** <note>original Greek: *chrēmatismōn*</note> or oracles. He argues this same duty belonged to the <term>Teraphim</term>. 

But in response to the first point: even if we were to concede that whatever is made only at certain times is a Talisman—though this is a rather broad definition—it still cannot be proven from the opinion of one or two Rabbis that the <term>Teraphim</term> were only made at a certain time...

<summary>This page discusses the historical and scholarly identification of biblical Teraphim and Hecatean magical objects as "talismans" (Tilsemas). It lists numerous 17th-century authorities who debated whether these idols were constructed under specific astrological conditions to serve as oracles or protective charms.</summary>

...were made: for we can oppose these Rabbis with other "Cadmean little brothers" <note>original: *Cadmæos fraterculos*; a reference to the myth of Cadmus where brothers fought and destroyed one another, implying the Rabbis are contradicting themselves</note> among the Rabbis, who require no such thing in the making of the **Teraphim** <term>Teraphim: ancient household idols or cult objects mentioned in several books of the Hebrew Bible</term>, nor should we immediately believe whatever arises in any Rabbi’s scholar-hood <note>original: *liripipio*; a liripipe or long tail of a hood, used here metaphorically to mean a person's private whims or fancies</note>; for he who does not know how to gather "pearls from the dung of Ennius" <note>original Latin: *ex stercore Enniano margaritas legere*; a famous phrase attributed to Virgil, meaning to find something of value in a rough or lowly source</note> will often find **coals instead of treasures** <note>original Greek: *anthrakas anti thēsaurōn*; a common proverb for something that promises much but delivers nothing</note>. Furthermore, we do not read that Micah’s mother waited for a specific [astrological] hour for the **making** <note>original Greek: *poiēsin*</note> of the **Teraphim** in Judges 17:4; instead, the goldsmith is said to have fashioned the **Teraphim** from material offered to him **by chance** <note>original Greek: *apo tychēs*</note>. To the second point, I respond that it is a poor argument: "The **Tilsemae** <term>Tilsemae: a Latinization of the Arabic word for talismans</term> gave oracles; the **Teraphim** gave oracles. Therefore, the **Teraphim** are **Tilsemae**." In fact, all the oracles of the pagans gave answers, yet they were not necessarily **Tilsemae**. Therefore, we should not easily force upon anyone such claims about the **Teraphim** that we cannot draw from the Holy Scriptures, though we shall not reject them too stubbornly.

§. 20. Reading the traces of the Holy Scriptures with diligence, we shall discern what is revealed there concerning the **Teraphim**. We are most firmly persuaded, therefore, that: I. The **Teraphim** were **Idols**. Away with Genebrardus, who in his *Chronology, Age 6*, wants the **Teraphim** to be understood as "sacred images"; he says the same in his commentary on *Psalm 73*. Similarly, Jacques Gaffarel <note>original: *Joh. Gaffarellus*; a 17th-century French scholar and librarian known for his interests in the Kabbalah and magic</note> in the place cited determined that the **Teraphim** were figures permitted by Moses and piously used by Laban and other Easterners, through which **God** revealed His will in a wondrous way. He claims the Image of Micah was of the same kind as the figures of the Temple, the Seraphim and Cherubim, to which he even adds the [Golden] Calf in the desert and the cows of Jeroboam in Dan and Bethel. This was surely the impious audacity of a most curious man, and well-deserving of the censure of the Sorbonne <note>the prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris</note>. But that the **Teraphim** of Laban were **Idols** is clear from the fact that he explicitly calls them his **gods** in *Genesis 31:30*. Moreover, Laban hints that he is an idolater in verse 53, making a distinction between the **God of Abraham** <note>original Hebrew: *Elohei Avraham*</note> and the **God of Nahor** <note>original Hebrew: *Elohei Nachor*</note> and of their **fathers**, who were "foreigners" as noted in *Joshua 24:2*. Likewise, in *1 Samuel 15:23, 2 Kings 23:25, Ezekiel 21:21, and Zechariah 10:2*, **Teraphim** are necessarily understood to be **Idols**, because they are placed...

...[added] to the side of the **diviner, the rebellious, those who consult spirits and mediums, and other abominations and idols** <note>original Hebrew: *Kosem. Meri. Ovot. Ve-yid’onim. Shikuzim. Gillulim.*</note> and other idolatrous abominations.

§. 21. It remains for us to demonstrate the same regarding the remaining passages in *Judges 17 & 18, 1 Samuel 19, and Hosea 3.* Regarding the story of Micah, Gerhard Johann Vossius <note>A famous 17th-century Dutch scholar</note> in his work *On the Idolatry of the Gentiles, book 1, chapter 3, page 28,* believes that Micah’s **Teraphim** <term>Teraphim: ancient household figurines often used for divination</term> was an image of the true deity. He argues this because Micah’s mother is said to have dedicated the silver—from which the image was fashioned—to the Lord [Jehovah]. Furthermore, he argues that Micah’s intention to worship the **God** of Israel through this object is sufficiently proven by his words: *Now I know that the Lord will do me good, seeing I have a Levite as my priest.* However, it is more likely that Micah mixed the worship of the true **God** with that of demons, especially during that time of **anarchy** <note>original Greek: *anarchias*; referring to the period in the Book of Judges when "there was no king in Israel"</note> when whatever one pleased was permitted. He intended to consult the true **God** through the **ephod** <term>Ephod: a high-priest's ceremonial garment used for seeking divine guidance</term>, but the devil through the **Teraphim**. Indeed, the **Teraphim** are also identified as a **molten image** <note>original Hebrew: *massekah*</note> and a **carved image** <note>original Hebrew: *pesel*</note>, and **God** frequently rebuked the Israelites for such "spiritual adulteries." Consequently, Micah’s religion and worship were not far removed from **Samaritanism** <note>A term used here to describe a syncretic religion that mixes the worship of God with foreign idols, based on 2 Kings 17</note>; regarding this, compare *2 Kings 17:33-34.* Most interpreters, both Christian and Jewish, concede that Micah’s **Teraphim** were idols. The Jews also note in their calendar for the 23rd day of the month of **Shevat** <note>The eleventh month of the Hebrew civil year</note> a fast to commemorate the slaughter of the Benjaminites on account of the concubine, and the Idol of Micah.

§. 22. Furthermore, regarding the **Teraphim** in the story of Michal and David in *1 Samuel 19*, various opinions present themselves. Many deny that it was an idol. Laban was indeed an idolater, which is why he said to her: "Why have you stolen my gods?" But far be it, far be it from us to think there was an idol in the house of David.</gloss> So too does Ramban <note>Nachmanides, a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note> note regarding *Genesis*...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Teraphim—specifically the household image used by Michal to help her husband, King David, escape from King Saul’s assassins in 1 Samuel 19. The author is evaluating various scholarly opinions on whether these objects were forbidden idols or merely harmless household items. The catchword "Ab-" from the previous page completes the first word of this page as "Abnegat" (He denies).</meta>

[He denies] <note>original: *Abnegat*; the first syllable is provided by the catchword on the preceding page</note> that it is likely there were idols in the house of such a holy man; rather, it must be granted that these were certain **Teraphim** <term>Teraphim: ancient household figurines often associated with divination or family heritage</term> which were not idols. This same opinion is held, among others, by Rivet <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a prominent French Huguenot theologian</note>, who in his *Exercises on Genesis, 133* says: *That the image (placed by Michal in David's bed) was not an idol is confirmed by the singular piety of David, who was a man after God's own heart.* He, therefore, along with Ribera <note>Francisco de Ribera (1537–1591), a Spanish Jesuit commentator</note> in his commentary on *Hosea chapter 3, number 21* (on page 111), believes that Michal fashioned a statue resembling a human from various items found here and there, such as those usually thrown before bulls in the circus, or placed in gardens to frighten birds—namely, men's clothing stuffed with straw or hay to simulate the limbs of a human body. From this, she also placed a hairy goat skin at its head, clearly to imitate David’s red hair <note>The Bible describes David as "ruddy," which historical commentators often interpreted as having red hair</note>. We also add a brief note from Rabbi Jacob Abendana <note>A 17th-century Jewish scholar and leader of the Spanish and Portuguese synagogue in London</note> in the *Spicilegium* <term>Spicilegium: a collection of literary "gleanings" or supplementary notes</term> on Rabbi Solomon Ben Melech’s *Miclal Jophi* <note>original Hebrew: *Michlal Yophi*, meaning "The Perfection of Beauty"</note>, recently published. Referring to the passage in the Book of Samuel and citing Abarbanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel (1437–1508), a famous Jewish statesman and philosopher</note>, he relates that wives were formerly accustomed to acquire images of their husbands so that their likeness would always be before their eyes, out of the greatness of their love for them. He suggests Michal's **Teraphim** were this kind of image, made out of her most vehement love for David, and thus there was nothing impious in them; this is why Michal substituted it in David's place, because it resembled him in outward appearance.

§. 23. But we do not see what strongly prevents us from explaining the **Teraphim** here also as idols. They could have been in David’s house without his knowledge and kept by Michal for the sake of sentiment rather than worship. They might be called idols and **Teraphim** by association with their previous nature, because they were originally such things. Our "Great Man" <note>original Latin: *Megalander*; a celebratory title often applied to Martin Luther by his followers</note> Luther agrees with us in the place cited, and we will not be reluctant to quote his exact words: <note>original in Early Modern German</note> *The image that Michal placed in the bed in David’s stead, I hold to be some image or idol that still remained in some place from former superstition and idolatry. Just as among us many images still remain, which are still preserved in several places, in memory of the old [ways]...*

Idolatry / so that it can be seen anywhere and children are allowed to play with it / and not for divine service / such idols in David's time lay hidden here and there in the corners of houses: It was there that Michal in her haste / when nothing else was available / grabbed the image / and laid it in David's place in the bed / for at that time there were without doubt still many signs of superstition / and idolatries / from the land and religion of the Jebusites <note>The original inhabitants of Jerusalem before David's conquest</note> / etc. just as the longing laments in many Psalms testify / in which David with great pain fights and prays against idolatry. <meta>This page continues the argument regarding the nature of the Teraphim. ...and are to be deprived of their idols, nor will they be prostitutes or wives. <note>The author appears to be finishing a thought regarding a biblical prophecy or social condition where idolatry and illicit relationships are stripped away.</note> Compare Rivetus on this passage, page 112; Hakspan in *Biblical Notes, Part 2, page 929*; Hulseus in *Jewish Theology, Part 1, page 25*; and Hoornbeck in *Teschuvat Jehuda, page 130.*

§. 25. Therefore, we have so far proved that the <term>Teraphim: ancient household figurines often associated with divination or family heritage</term> in the sacred scriptures are nowhere described as mere images, but always signify idols. Next, we show (II) that the <term>Teraphim</term> were household idols, or <term>Lares: Roman guardian spirits that watched over the home</term>. Buxtorf correctly states in his *Talmudic Lexicon, page 2660*: *The <term>Teraphim</term> were images in human form, which superstitious Gentiles held as household gods and domestic <term>Lares</term> <note>The author uses Roman terminology—Lares and Penates—to help his 17th-century readers understand the concept of household protective spirits.</note>, seeking answers from them in times of doubt.* Compare also Junius and Tremellius on this passage, and B. Gesner on page 601. This is evident from the phrase in verse 19, where Rachel is said to have stolen <note>original Hebrew: התרפים אשר לאביו</note> the *<term>Teraphim</term> belonging to her father.* And in verse 30, Laban calls them *his gods*. From this, Wolfgang Musculus <note>A 16th-century Reformed theologian</note> concludes in his *Commentary on this passage, page 644*, that Laban had a certain shrine in his home: *Laban, he says, was a pagan and a worshiper of idols. I believe he had a certain domestic shrine where those idols were displayed, so that Rachel had easy access to them.* The same is confirmed by the story of Micah, who is also said in *Judges 17:5* to have had <note>original Hebrew: בית אלהים</note> a house of God (Luther translates this as: *A House of God*), by which a shrine of this kind is most conveniently understood.

§. 26. We assert (III) that the <term>Teraphim</term> possessed a human figure. This is clearly evident from *1 Samuel 19*, where we read that Michal placed the <term>Teraphim</term> in David’s bed to deceive Saul’s messengers, so they would think David himself was present; seeing him as if he were sick, they would be satisfied. This would have been a foolish plan if the <term>Teraphim</term> were <unclear>sun-trackers</unclear> <note>original Greek: σκιαθήרεια, likely meaning sundials or instruments for measuring shadows</note> or clocks, which is the opinion of some among the Rabbinic crowd. However, the human figure of the <term>Teraphim</term> <note>original Greek: τῶν Teraphim</note> is acknowledged by Rabbi Aben Ezra on *Genesis 31*, Rabbi Solomon Jarchi, Rabbi David Kimchi, Rabbi Levi Ben Gerson, and Rabbi Isaiah on *1 Samuel 19*, Rabbi Solomon Ben Melech in *The Perfection of Beauty* on *Genesis 31*, and others. *Aben Ezra says:* <note>original Hebrew: והקרוב אלי שהתרפים צורת בני אדם והיא עשויה לקבל כח העליונים ולא אוכל לפרש והער שהתרפים ששמה מיכל בדת שאול במטה עד שחשבו שומרי הבית שהם דוד</note> i.e., *It seems probable to me that the <term>Teraphim</term> had the form of a human being, made in such a way that it could receive the power of the heavenly beings—though I am not permitted to explain this further. The proof is that the <term>Teraphim</term> which Michal placed in the bed for Saul's men made the guards of the house think it was David.*

...rejection, and they are to be deprived of their idols; they shall be neither harlots nor wives <note>This likely refers to the prophetic passage in Hosea 3:4 regarding Israel’s period of exile without religious or political structures</note>.
