### §. 25. Teraphim as Household Gods

We have thus far proved that the <term>Teraphim</term> mentioned in the Holy Scriptures are never merely bare images, but always signify idols. We now further show II. that <term>Teraphim</term> were household idols, also known as <term>Lares</term> <note>In Roman religion, Lares were protective spirits of the household; the author uses this classical term to explain the function of the Teraphim to a Latin-reading audience</note> or household gods. Buxtorf correctly states in his *Talmudic Lexicon*, page 2660: *The <term>Teraphim</term> were images in human form, which superstitious Gentiles kept as household gods and guardian spirits, seeking answers from them in times of doubt.* Compare also Junius and Tremellius on this passage, and B. Gesner on page 601.

This is evident from the phrasing in verse 19, where Rachel is said to have stolen <gloss>the Teraphim belonging to her father</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: הַתְּרָפִים אֲשֶׁר לְאָבִיהָ</note>. Furthermore, in verse 30, Laban calls them his **gods**. From this, Wolfgang Musculus concludes in his *Commentary*, page 644, that Laban had a certain chapel in his home: *Laban,* he says, *was a pagan and a worshiper of idols. I believe he had a domestic chapel <note>original: sacellum</note> in which these idols were displayed, so that Rachel could easily access them.* This is confirmed by the story of Micah, who is also said in *Judges 17:5* to have had a <gloss>house of God</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: בֵּית אֱלֹהִים; Luther's German: Ein Gottes Hauß</note>, by which a domestic chapel of this kind is most conveniently understood.

### §. 26. The Physical Form of the Teraphim

We assert III. that the <term>Teraphim</term> possessed a human figure. This is clearly evident from *1 Samuel 19*, where we read that Michal placed a <term>Teraphim</term> in David’s bed to deceive Saul’s messengers, so that they would think David himself was present. Seeing him—as they thought—lying ill, they would be satisfied. This would have been a foolish plan if the <term>Teraphim</term> were merely <unclear>sun-dials</unclear> <note>original Greek: σκιαθήρια, instruments for tracking shadows or time</note> or clocks, as is the opinion of some from the Rabbinic circle. 

However, many acknowledge the human figure of the <term>Teraphim</term>, including Rabbi Aben Esra on *Genesis 31*, Rabbi Solomon Jarchi, Rabbi David Kimchi, Rabbi Levi Ben Gerson, and Rabbi Isaiah on *1 Samuel 19*, as well as Rabbi Solomon Ben Melech in *The Perfection of Beauty* on *Genesis 31*, and others. *Aben Esra* says: <gloss>It seems probable to me that the <term>Teraphim</term> had a human form, crafted in such a way that it could receive the power of the celestial beings, though I am not permitted to explain it further. The proof of this matter</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: והקרוב אלי שהתרפים צורת בני אדם והיא עשויה לקבל כח העליונים ולא אוכל לפרש והער שהתרפים ששמה מיכל בכר שאול במטה עד שחשבו שומרי הבית שהם דוד</note> is that the <term>Teraphim</term> which Michal placed in the bed... so that the guards of the house thought they were David. <meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the physical nature and function of the Teraphim as household idols. It specifically moves from their status as "household gods" (Lares) to a discussion of their physical composition and their role in divination.</meta>

...evidence of this is that the <term>Teraphim</term> which Michal, Saul’s daughter, placed in the bed were touched, so that the guards of the house believed them to be David himself <note>Refers to 1 Samuel 19, where Michal tricks Saul's messengers by putting an idol in David's bed to facilitate his escape</note>.

### §. 27. Fourthly, we say the <term>TERAPHIM</term> WERE CRAFTED FROM VARIOUS MATERIALS.

It can be inferred quite clearly from *Judges 17:4* that Micah's <term>Teraphim</term> were made of silver. Many believe that the <term>IDOLS OF LABAN</term> were of the same material; certainly, the Blessed Luther, in *Volume 5 of his German Commentaries on this passage*, calls them <insert>the silver idols</insert> <note>original German: "Die Silberne Götzen"</note>. And Osiander says: *Furthermore, these Idols were perhaps of precious material, gold or silver, etc.* Meanwhile, the statue placed by Michal in David's bed seems hardly to have been silver. We think, therefore, that <term>TERAPHIM</term> always represented a human figure, but did not always represent the same actual height. They were distinct in terms of size and material, so that where the size was greater, the material was more common, and where the size was smaller, the material was more precious; nonetheless, the purpose and use were the same in both cases. For it is clearly evident that the <term>TERAPHIM</term> substituted in David’s place and those of Laban—which Rachel was able to hide among the camel’s straw or saddlebags and sit on comfortably—were not of the same size. G. H. Vorstius conjectures in his *Notes on the Chapters of Rabbi Eliezer, page 220*, that *Michal's Teraphim were a statue stuffed with cloth, wood, straw, or other things, imitating the limbs of the human body.* But that statue could also have been bronze, or wooden, yet hollow, or something else; we will decide nothing since the Scripture is silent, but we hardly believe it was silver or gold.

### §. 28 Finally, V. we know: THROUGH THE <term>TERAPHIM</term> THE DEVIL GAVE RESPONSES TO MEN.

It is certain that idolaters consulted the <term>TERAPHIM</term> regarding uncertain matters. To this point, St. Augustine, in *Question 94 on Genesis*, refers to those words of Laban: <gloss>I have divined</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: נחשתי (*nachashti*)</note> *that God blessed me because of you.* His words are: *As to what he said, however: "Why have you stolen my gods?" hence perhaps is that which he also said he had divined.* Thus also Aben Ezra explains that divination as an interrogation of the <term>TERAPHIM</term>; this is a matter which we leave undecided. For otherwise, the word "divine" <note>original Hebrew: נחש (*nachash*)</note> means to sagaciously perceive or gather. Nevertheless, in *Ezekiel 21:26* <note>In modern translations, this is Ezekiel 21:21</note>, the King of Babylon is expressly said to have questioned the <term>Teraphim</term>. In the same place, there are recounted...

*He will interrogate the Teraphim* (the third type of divination. <meta>This page continues the investigation into the Teraphim, transitioning from their physical description to their function as oracles that "speak" and the theological debate surrounding Rachel's theft of them from her father, Laban.</meta>

**TERAPHIM**, which were consulted, and it is gathered quite clearly both from *Judges 17* and evidently proven from *Zechariah 10:2* that they formed words within themselves and returned answers—sometimes true, sometimes false, and often ambiguous. In the latter passage it is said: <note>Hebrew: "Ha-teraphim dibru aven"</note> *The Teraphim have spoken vanity.* <note>The author quotes Martin Luther’s German translation: "Die Götzen reden eytel Mühe," meaning "The idols speak vain trouble."</note> Mercerus <note>Jean Mercier, a 16th-century French Hebraist</note> rightly says regarding *Genesis 31*: *There is no doubt that through them, as in other idols of the Gentiles, demons gave answers; for otherwise we know that, being destitute of natural organs, they could not have spoken.*

§. 29. And these things concerning the **TERAPHIM** we can assert with certainty from the comparison of Scriptural passages. As for the method of their construction, the formulas for consulting them, the types of responses, the manner of their spread from the Syrians to certain Hebrews, the way they were venerated and worshipped, and any other such matters, we prefer to remain silent and <term>suspend judgment</term> <note>original Greek: "epéchein"</note> rather than say more without a foundation. It could indeed have happened that the Chaldean-Syrian people, who were most devoted to astrological vanities, observed the influence of the stars in their <term>idol-making</term> <note>original Greek: "eidolopoiia"</note>, and consulted the idols with certain ceremonies and peculiar formulas, and the devil responded either in distinct words or sometimes in another way; the idolaters might have had fixed hours and certain laws for worshipping those idols, but who can determine any of these things beyond doubt? We know, meanwhile, that Laban's **TERAPHIM** were household idols, manufactured undoubtedly from silver in a human form, which he used to consult when doubts arose. It could also be said that Laban had only a single idol, like the **TERAPHIM** of Michal <note>Refers to the image Michal used to deceive Saul's messengers in 1 Samuel 19</note>, since the name—though plural in form—refers indifferently to both singular and plural meanings (just as the word <note>Hebrew: "panim"</note> *faces* is expressed with a single ending for the one face of a single man and for the many faces of many men). However, we easily permit anyone to abound in their own sense and judgment on this matter.

§. 30. Now it remains for us, mindful of our promise, to inquire into the purpose of Rachel: why she stole the **TERAPHIM** from her father. On this point, Musculus <note>Wolfgang Musculus, a 16th-century Reformer</note> rightly notes: *Nothing can be answered with certainty regarding the cause of this deed, since Moses does not teach why Rachel did this; it is permitted, however, to discern which conjecture is more probable and more consistent with Scripture.* We find, however, among authors...

<meta>Continuing the investigation into the Teraphim from the previous page, this section transitions from their function as oracles to the famous biblical episode where Rachel steals them from her father, Laban, exploring five different scholarly theories for her motives.</meta>

...there are five opinions regarding Rachel's plan. The **first** is that of those who believe Rachel stole the **TERAPHIM** <note>Hebrew household idols</note> so that her father, by looking into or consulting those idols, would not discover the path by which Jacob had gone. *Thus says Rabbi Eliezer in his "Chapters," chapter 36.* <insert>Therefore Rachel stole them so that they would not tell Laban that Jacob had fled.</insert> <note>Original Hebrew: "lephikak ganavtam Rachel she-lo yaggidu le-Lavan she-barach Ya'akov"</note> *Therefore Rachel stole them, lest they indicate to Laban that Jacob had fled.* Rabbi Aben-Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a prominent medieval Sephardic scholar</note> suggests it is likely that Laban was skilled in the stars, and Rachel feared that her father would inspect the stars and learn where they had fled. Rabbi Albilda in *The Continual Burnt Offering* <note>Original title: "Olat Tamid"</note> regarding this passage, page 99, says: *The reason Rachel stole the Teraphim was so that Laban would not use divination and discover his path.* Tostatus <note>Alonso Tostado, a prolific 15th-century Spanish bishop</note>, who follows Jewish interpretive traditions on this passage, does not disagree. This same opinion is considered probable by Jacques Bonfrère <note>a Jesuit biblical commentator</note> among others on this passage, page 260.

The **second** opinion is that of Flavius Josephus <note>the famous 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian</note>, who thinks Rachel stole her father’s idols so that she could seek refuge in them against her parent's anger. *Thus he says in his Antiquities of the Jews, book 1, chapter 19:* *Rachel carried the images of the gods with her; not because she worshipped those gods—for she had learned to despise them from her husband—but so that if by chance her father caught up with them as they fled, she would have a place to seek refuge to obtain pardon.* <note>The historian suggests the idols were used as leverage or "sanctuary" in a domestic dispute.</note>

The **third** opinion is that of those who believe Rachel, having been accustomed to the worship of idols from her childhood, had not yet fully renounced all devotion to idolatry, and thus she stole them for the sake of worship. *So say St. Chrysostom in his 57th Homily on Genesis; Gennadius; Rupert; Cajetan; and Hieronymus ab Oleastro on this passage; as well as Cornelius à Lapide, whose words on page 245 are these:*

> Others think it more likely that she took the idols not so much as her father's, but as her own—as if she were taking her <term>Penates</term> <note>Roman household gods; used here to describe the Teraphim's role</note> with her, because she was devoted to them and hoped for a successful journey and every good thing from them. For her father Laban and his house—and consequently Rachel as well—worshipped local idols along with the true GOD, as was the custom of her nation. This is evident from Chapter 35, verse 2, where Jacob finally abolished these idols.

Among the Reformed <note>Protestant theologians of the Calvinist tradition</note>, Jean Mercier on this passage, André Rivet in *Exercise 133 on Genesis*, page 517, and others agree. Among our own authors <note>likely referring to the Lutheran tradition</note>, the same view is favored by Dr. Salomon Gesner on this passage, question 3, page 601; Dr. Johann Gerhard in *Genesis*, page 592; and also Dr. Polycarp Lyser, whose words in *On the Life of Jacob* on this passage, page 123, are: *But of these two things, it must be one or the other. Either because she herself was still devoted to idols...*

devoted to them, she stole them so that she might have images before which she could perform her divine worship; or because the gold was pleasing to her, she took them to enrich her own estate with the gold or silver. The **fourth** opinion is that of those who assert that Rachel took the **TERAPHIM** <note>referring here to idols made of gold or silver</note> for herself both as a wage for her husband and as a dowry for herself and her sister. This is considered likely by Pererius <note>Benedictus Pererius, a 16th-century Jesuit commentator</note> and Osiander <note>Lucas Osiander, a Lutheran theologian</note> *on this passage* <note>Latin: "in h. l.," an abbreviation for "in hunc locum," meaning "on this place in the text"</note>. This was the mind of Basil in the *Beginning of the Proverbs*, Nazianzen in his *Second Oration on Easter*, Theodoret in *Question 86 on Genesis*, Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra, a famous medieval Franciscan scholar</note> *on this passage*, as well as our Great Teacher Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius, a leading 17th-century Lutheran scholastic</note> in his *Sixth Disputation against Grotius on Genesis*.

§. 31. If any of these must be accepted at all among these choices, we do not hesitate to cast our vote <note>Latin: "addere calculum," literally "to add a pebble," a classical metaphor for voting</note> for the last opinion, as we see it burdened by none of the difficulties which the earlier ones do not seem entirely to lack. Certainly, as for the **first** opinion, it does not seem commendable. For Rachel, a follower of the true worship, knew that Jacob’s journey was undertaken with God’s consent—indeed, by His command—and also that the GOD of Jacob had taken up their defense against Laban, which is clear from verses 12, 13, and 16; therefore, it is not to be believed that she greatly dreaded Laban’s approach. Furthermore, Rachel easily gathered that such a large company of Jacob's and so many flocks could not be hidden, but would be pointed out by the people of those places through which they passed—as to where or by what way they had gone. And without a doubt, other **TERAPHIM** were still available in that place which Laban could have consulted, and which Rachel could not have stolen.

§. 32. Moreover, the opinion of **Josephus** <note>the 1st-century Jewish historian</note> is absurd. For if Rachel had learned contempt for idols from her husband, what refuge could she hope for from them? What protection from them? From an angry father, how could she hope for pardon because of the idols she had stolen? Josephus would have done something worthwhile if he had explained the reasons for his opinion more clearly.

§. 33. Nor can the **third** opinion comfortably stand. For at that time, Rachel does not seem to have been a worshiper of idols. That she invoked the true God, was heard by Him, and acknowledged His favor, is what **is read**.

And indeed, if they were Rachel's, why does Laban again below in verse 30 call them his gods? Why did he take it so poorly that they were taken from him? Why did he not immediately suspect Rachel had taken them, if she had more right to them than the others? It is certainly absurd that Rachel would have stolen her own Idols for herself!”*

§. 36. Nor does the **fourth** opinion, finally, commend itself to us very much. Rachel had understood long ago from her husband that God had abundantly compensated his labor and had exercised judgment against Laban; hence she had said earlier in *verse 16: All the riches which God has taken from our father are ours and our children's.* And why would she not have snatched anything else rather than those **TERAPHIM**? For in this way [she would] her superstitious parent...

§. 37. We therefore think it most probable that Rachel, having been led by Jacob to the true worship of God and being just about to depart, moved by <unclear>compassion</unclear> <note>The Latin 'copm' is likely an abbreviation for 'compassione' (compassion)</note> and piety, attempted to lead her parent—who was still partially immersed in idolatry—away from the worship of idols. She did this by snatching away the objects of that idolatry, no differently than if she were snatching a sword from a madman or taking fuel away from a fire. We easily dispel the arguments brought by various scholars to the contrary. Aben-Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a 12th-century biblical commentator</note> objects: *If this were the case, she could have hidden them on the road or thrown them into a stream when they crossed the Euphrates; certainly there was no need to keep them for seven days and cast herself into present danger, since she knew the theft would easily be discovered by her father.* But Rachel could have kept the **TERAPHIM** because they were made of precious material, such as gold or silver—as Blessed Luther, Pererius, Bonfrerius, Dr. Gerhard, and others assert—or rather as a testimony of the matter, so that she could show her husband the evidence of her deed. Furthermore, Rachel knew her intention was very good and that she would be defended by God; and so, relying on God's help, she did not think Laban would catch up to her and her husband.

§. 38. A certain most learned man objects: *Evil things are not to be done so that good things may come from them.* <note>A reference to the theological principle that the "end does not justify the means," found in Romans 3:8</note> But we respond that we are only judging Rachel's intention, not excusing the deed entirely. We do not deny that she sinned against the *Second Table* <note>The portion of the Ten Commandments regarding duties toward fellow humans, specifically the prohibition against theft</note>. Actions undertaken with the best intention can sometimes lose favor because of the way they are carried out.

§. 39. Furthermore, Mercerus objects: *As if he could not have fashioned others for himself, unless perhaps he refused out of greed.* Rivetus says the same. But even if he could have made others, that *great and patriarchal soul* (as Nazianzenus <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century Archbishop and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers"</note> calls Rachel) wished to demonstrate by this very act that the **TERAPHIM** were neither gods nor able to protect Laban, since they could not protect themselves from being carried off by theft. Indeed, it is not absurdly thought, says Dr. Gerhard

§. 40. Wolfgang Musculus <note>A significant 16th-century German Reformed theologian</note> also objects: *And she ought to have informed her husband about this matter.* And Rivetus <note>André Rivet, a prominent French Huguenot and professor of theology</note> adds: *Furthermore, if this was her intended goal, why would she hide it from her husband, whom she knew to be a stranger to all idolatry? At the very least, she would have informed him on the road and consulted him on what he judged should be done after they had once been taken away.* But the immediate flight, which allowed for no delay, prevented her from consulting with Jacob on this matter: she knew Jacob was distracted by many worries, and therefore did not want to trouble him, especially since she persuaded herself that her deed would not be disapproved of by him at all; and without doubt, she later explained the whole matter to Jacob after Laban’s departure, when Jacob was inquiring more strictly into the situation. It could also be that she kept them for private use because of their material <note>referring to the gold or silver of the idols</note>, or if she had some other aim, it is certain that her intention was excellent and praiseworthy, even if it was joined with certain human weaknesses. For our purposes at present, the discussion is not about the law, but about the facts and the purpose of Rachel; we leave the rest to the judgment of the Theologians. But if in these things we have said, we have attained the truth, we give most humble thanks and attribute glory to <term>GOD</term>, who is truth itself. But if we have departed anywhere from the path of truth—which we hope we have not—we remember that we are only human and implore the reader’s pardon.

### CONCERNING THE WATERS ABOVE THE HEAVENS.

**I.** By the waters <term>above the firmament</term> <note>original Hebrew: מעל לרקיע (ma'al la-raqi'a)</note> mentioned in *Genesis 1:6*, one should understand neither the good angels (according to Origen <note>An early Christian scholar and theologian from Alexandria</note>) nor the crystalline heaven (according to the mind of Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra, a highly influential Franciscan Bible commentator of the Middle Ages</note>,

<keywords>Rachel, Jacob, Laban, Teraphim, Wolfgang Musculus, André Rivet, Origen, Nicholas of Lyra, Genesis, waters above the heavens, firmament, divine providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a dense scholarly argument concerning the nature of the "waters above the firmament" mentioned in the Book of Genesis. Having previously discussed Rachel's idols, the author now lists various theologians to distinguish between those who view these waters as mere clouds and those who, like the author, believe they are literal waters located beyond the stars.</meta>

...[Ly-]ranus, Tostatus, Cajetanus, and Catharinus <note>These are major medieval and Renaissance biblical commentators</note> [commenting] on this passage) nor atmospheric impressions, rainwater, or clouds (according to Rupert, François Vatable, Benedictus Pererius, Juan de Mariana, Johannes Drusius, Jean Mercier, Franciscus Junius, John Calvin, Augustine Marlorat, Christoph Pezel, David Pareus, Johannes Piscator, Hugo Grotius, [all commenting] on this passage; Matthias Flacius in *Key to the Holy Scriptures*, Part 1, p. 51; Agostino Steuco in *Cosmography*; Girolamo Prado on *Ezekiel*, p. 25; Jean Bolduc on *Job*, chapter 37; Agostino Tornielli in *Annals*, Volume 1, folio 15; Bartholomaeus Keckermann in *System of Physics*, Book 7, chapter 1; Matthieu Béroalde in *Chronicles*, Book 2, p. 136; Guillaume Bucanus in *Theological Institutions*, p. 40; Dionysius Petavius in *Rational Account of Time*, Part 1, Book 1, chapter 1; Friedrich Spanheim in *Miscellaneous Dissertations*, p. 89; Sixtinus Amama in *Biblical Confutation and Anti-Biblical Bible*, p. 176; Conrad Aslacus in *Christian Physics*, p. 198; Heinrich Kipping in *On Creation*, Exercise 13; and others) but rather waters properly so-called, placed above the starry heaven during the first creation, are understood.

II. We prove our thesis: (α) *Whatever was placed in the first creation above the* <term>expanse</term> <note>original Hebrew: *raqia*, a term the author uses to refer specifically to the region of the stars</note>, *was placed above the starry heaven. The super-celestial waters [were placed above the expanse]. Therefore [the waters are above the starry heaven].* The connection of the major premise is proved because the term <term>expanse</term> <note>original: *raqia*</note> throughout the whole of Holy Scripture denotes the starry heaven, namely in *Genesis 1:6, 7, 8, 14, 15, 17, 20; Psalm 19:2; Psalm 150:1; Ezekiel 1:22, 23, 25, 26; chapter 10:1; [and] Daniel 12:3.* They make an exception for one passage, *Genesis 1:20*. But birds fly no more "above" the air than they do "above" the heaven; therefore the <term>expanse</term> in that passage must be explained as: *toward* (as in *Exodus 9:22*; compare Glassius, *Sacred Philology*, p. 773), namely, not referring to the air (since that is where they fly) but to the starry heaven. Furthermore, the <term>continuity of the text</term> <note>original Greek: *synapheia*</note> proves that the expanse is the heaven. In the terrestrial-aqueous mass there was water properly so called (verse 2), which was divided into two parts (verse 5), and once divided, it retained the name of "water" on both sides (verses 7 and 9). Between these parts an expanse was placed (verse 7), which was called <term>Heaven</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Shamayim*</note> (verse 8), and by the joined name <term>Expanse of the Heavens</term> <note>original Hebrew: *Raqia Ha-Shamayim*</note> (verse 14), in which the stars were placed (in the following verses).

(β) *Whatever did not yet exist on the second day, the waters were not placed above it. Clouds and rainwater [did not yet exist on that day]. Therefore [the supracelestial waters are not clouds].* The major premise is evident: a non-existent thing has no properties or passive qualities. The minor premise is proved from *Genesis 2:5-6* <note>The original text cites Genesis 6:5-6, but the context of the "vapor rising from the earth" clearly refers to the creation narrative in Genesis 2</note>, where it is said that on the third day, after the plants were already produced, a vapor finally ascended from the earth and, falling back, irrigated it.

(γ) *Whatever*...

<summary>This page presents a logical and citation-heavy defense of the "waters above the firmament" being literal waters located beyond the stars, rather than mere clouds or atmospheric phenomena. <meta>This page continues the logical defense of the existence of literal waters located above the starry firmament, refuting the idea that these "waters above" are merely atmospheric clouds. The author uses formal logic (syllogisms) and scriptural cross-references to distinguish between the volatile weather of the lower atmosphere and the permanent, divinely ordered waters of the highest heavens.</meta>

...[it] is not above the air; therefore, it is not "super-celestial water," even assuming that by the <term>expanse</term> <note>original Hebrew: *raqia*</note> the air is understood. [Namely] Clouds. And so on. The major premise is evident from the meaning of the name. The minor premise cannot be denied, because no cloud reaches the highest region of the air, let alone transcends it. Furthermore, since water is said to be stored neither *in* the expanse nor *above a part* of the expanse, it is clear that by the expanse, the lower part of the air can in no way be understood. Certainly you would not say that someone submerged in the middle of the sea is "above the sea" relative to someone who is at the bottom.

(δ) *Whatever does not constantly mediate between waters and waters is not the <term>expanse</term>.* The Air [is such]. Therefore... The connection of the major premise is established from [Genesis 1] verse 6, where a constant order and a fixed arrangement of boundaries are set down. The minor premise is proved: At the time of rain—and thus immediately on the third day of creation—lower waters would have been made from upper waters, and from these, by the mediation of the sun, upper waters again, and so on infinitely. Nor was the air then "between" waters and waters; rather, it allowed an easy passage from one to the other—indeed, being full of water on all sides, it was not mediating between them. Thus here there is no distinction of waters, but mere confusion.

(ε) Added to this are *parallel passages:* *Psalm 104:3* (where there is a distinct mention of the waters above the heavens and the clouds) and *Psalm 148:4*, where the super-celestial waters are numbered among <note>original Greek: τὰ ὑπεראνω καὶ ὑποκάτω—"those things above and those things below"</note>; to these waters a constant and perpetual <term>order</term> <note>original Greek: *taxis*</note> is attributed in verse 6, whereas from verse 7 and onward the sublunary things <note>The region beneath the moon, i.e., the Earth's atmosphere</note> are described, and the <term>hymn of praise</term> <note>original Greek: *doxologia*</note> for both is clearly distinguished in verses 5, 7, and 13.

III. *Nor do the following objections stand in the way:*

(α) *That there would thus be three kinds of water.* I respond: There are only two kinds with respect to the intervening <term>expanse</term>. Rainwaters are *under* the expanse. 

(β) *That the text does not use "on"* <note>original Hebrew: *al*</note>, *but "from above"* <note>original Hebrew: *mi-al*</note>. I respond: "From above," by force of opposition, infallibly signifies "above," because "from below" <note>original Hebrew: *mi-tachat*</note> denotes "under." 

(γ) *That Moses describes only visible things.* I respond: If so, perhaps he describes neither the air nor the heaven. Although super-celestial water is not inherently invisible. 

(δ) *That water would be in a "violent" location.* <note>In traditional physics, a "violent" location is a place where an element does not naturally belong, such as water being suspended in the heavens</note> I respond: I deny this, because its natural place is that which was assigned in the first institution of nature. 

(ε) *That there is no proportion between the distinct parts...*

### ON
## ZAPHENAT PAANEACH,
### THE SURNAME OF JOSEPH, BY WHICH HE WAS HONORED BY PHARAOH, AS READ IN GENESIS 41:45.
*A PHILOLOGICAL DISSERTATION.*
### CHIEFLY OPPOSED TO FATHER <note>The "P." stands for "Pater" (Father), referring to Athanasius Kircher, a famous Jesuit scholar and polymath known for his studies on Egypt.</note> ATHANASIUS KIRCHER.

<margin>
Respondent:
JOHANN
Mundschald
of Dresden.
</margin>

#### §. I.
*Joseph* stands as an illustrious example of a most joyful reversal of fortune. For he, with a lofty and unconquered soul, having moved past hatred and contempt for himself, vain pleasures, and the harshest fates, suddenly emerged—with God as his guide—from the lowest condition to the height of honor and happiness, where he saw everything he had previously endured abundantly compensated by the grace of God. He was oppressed by the hatred of his brothers; now the King's favor is offered freely. He was an exile from his fatherland; he becomes Lord of the land which was to sustain both his father and his fatherland. The hand weary from servile tasks is adorned with a golden ring. In place of the cloak which the adulteress <note>Referring to Potiphar’s wife, who attempted to seduce Joseph and kept his cloak as false evidence against him.</note> had torn away, a robe of fine linen is returned. Before bound by shackles, now he is encircled by a golden necklace. He had served captives, now he receives command over free men. From the squalor of the prison, he is transferred to the royal seats of honor. Once despised on all sides, now everyone venerates him with a bended knee. To him who once turned away from the embraces of a lustful mistress, a noble woman is given as a consort of his bed. He who was to his brothers a foolish contriver of dreams, is now honored with a majestic surname by the King. If God thus restores His own in this life after temptations are overcome, what will happen at last in the other?

§. 2. Moreover, it was not unusual in former times among Princes, especially Eastern ones, that if they raised someone to honors, they would give him a new name, so that by this they might bind the person more closely to themselves, and the conferred dignity might become better known to all. <margin>(Compare Glassius, *Sacred Philology, Book 4, Tract 3, Observation 14.*)</margin> Thus Ahasuerus <note>Ahasuerus is the King in the Book of Esther, often identified with Xerxes I.</note> [gave a new name] to *Hadassah*, a girl of nearly the same

The author argues that such name-giving was a standard royal practice used to signal a change in status and loyalty.</summary>

§. 3. Similarly, there is no doubt that Pharaoh wished to provide for Joseph's honor as much as possible with the new name *Zaphenath Paaneah* (Genesis 41:45). Indeed, he omitted nothing that might serve that purpose. **Rabbi Moses Albelda** rightly observes this in *The Continual Burnt Offering* <note>original: עולת תמיד (*Olat Tamid*), a 16th-century philosophical commentary on the Torah</note> on this passage, folio 99, column 1 (Venice edition, Year [of the World] 5361 / 1601 AD; not 5286 / 1526 AD, as "so-and-so" <note>original: ὁ δεῖνα (*ho deina*), a Greek idiom for an unnamed person; the author is correcting a citation error in another work</note> puts it in *B. R. p. 393* through a neglect of the numeral word *lahem*): *Pharaoh wished to show everyone the reason why he had raised him to all this, and therefore he changed his name and called him Zaphenath Paaneah, which means 'Revealer of Secrets,' as the translator <note>Referring to the Targum, the ancient Aramaic translation of the Bible</note> rendered it; for one who possesses this skill is worthy of great eminence.* This is to say: [Pharaoh] being about to declare to everyone the reason why he had exalted Joseph in so many ways, changed his name and called him *Zaphenath Paaneah*, that is, *Revealer of Secrets*, just as the Chaldean <note>The Aramaic Targum Onkelos</note> translated it, suggesting that such a man is worthy of such eminence <note>original: ἐξוֹחῇ (*exoche*)</note>. Compare also Mercerus on this passage, p. 658, and Rivetus, *Exercise 158 on Genesis*, p. 787. Isaac Abravanel (f. 111, col. 1) believes the Hebrew name was changed to avoid envy among the Egyptians—as if, in this way, his foreign origins would escape the notice of his contemporaries. Yet Pharaoh himself later calls Joseph by his original name in verse 55.

§. 4. But that most honorable name of Joseph is today a "cross for the Hebrews" <note>An idiom meaning a source of great difficulty or torment for scholars</note>, as Blessed Martin Luther says in volume 6 of the Latin Wittenberg edition of his *Commentaries on Genesis*, p. 561. Because of this, many scholars, weary of quarrels and trifles, have retreated into a suspension of judgment <note>original: ἐποχὴν (*epochen*), a Greek term for withholding an opinion when evidence is unclear</note>. **Ibn Ezra** offers this dilemma: *If the word is Egyptian, we do not know its meaning; and if it is a translated word, we do not know Joseph's [Egyptian] name.* That is: *If this word is Egyptian, we are ignorant of its explanation (following the proverb: things belonging to strangers are "Greek" and cannot be read); but if it is a translated term [from the Egyptian language into Hebrew], we remain ignorant of the actual Egyptian name given to him—*

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the name "Zaphenath-Paaneah," moving from the initial proposal that the name was given by Pharaoh to a survey of various scholars—Jewish, Catholic, and Protestant—who have struggled to define its meaning or origin.</meta>

<margin>Arguments <note>original Hebrew: *Nimukim*</note>, folio 401, column 1. Elias says...</margin> (then by Pharaoh). Elias Levita agrees with him in his observations on *The Book of Roots* by Rabbi David Kimchi, folio 401, column 1. <margin>"Thus we do not know why Joseph was called so, as Rabbi Ibn Ezra wrote; see there."</margin> That is: *Elias says: "If this is true (namely, that the name Zaphenath-Paaneah is Egyptian), we do not know why Joseph was so named, as Rabbi Ibn Ezra also writes in folio r."* Furthermore, Nicholas of Lyra frankly confessed his ignorance on this passage: *"Whether,"* he says, *"the same name in the Egyptian language means 'savior of the world,' I do not know, except for what our Translation <note>Referring to the Latin Vulgate</note> says, because I am completely ignorant of the Egyptian tongue."* This honesty so pleased the great hero [Martin] Luther that, in such a doubtful matter, he did not hesitate to imitate it. *"I,"* he said, *"simply confess with Lyra that I do not know."* Thus, having weighed all options, Rivet prefers with Mariana to leave those words untouched rather than to judge before others.

§. 5. While the modesty of these men deserves praise, as they suspend or decline their judgment <note>original Greek: *epéchesin*, a technical term for withholding assent in the face of uncertainty</note> in a difficult matter, nevertheless, the presumption of those who measure everyone by themselves is less commendable; they try to block others from a path they themselves have not walked, claiming that all effort spent here is in vain. Thus John Calvin says on this passage (p. 279): *"Because I suspect,"* he says, *"that Egyptian words are being reported by Moses, both in this place and shortly after, I advise readers not to torment themselves in vain."* And [David] Pareus (p. 412): *"But it is a labor in vain regarding those foreign words."* As if, forsooth, one is not allowed to search further for what those two men could not find! We believe that today, in such a great light of learning—which is capable of dispelling even Cimmerian <note>A proverbial term for absolute and legendary darkness</note> or Egyptian darkness—one should not easily despair, even though most people shut their eyes to it.

§. 6. Above all, we must take care not to commit a "shift into another kind of language" <note>original Greek: *metabasin eis allo genos glōttēs*</note>, nor should we deviate from the native origin. For a single word, by reason of its sound, can hold very different meanings in different languages. Thus, for us, *Caplan* <note>The German and Latin term *Kaplan* or *Caplanus* refers to a chaplain or priest</note> is a church official, but for the Turks, it is a tiger. Therefore, unless it is clear which language *Zaphenath-Paaneah* belongs to, all labor will be spent in vain. It is disputed, however, whether it is Hebrew...

§. 5. While the modesty of these men deserves praise—as they maintain or declare their <term>epoche</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπέχεισιν; a philosophical term for the suspension of judgment</note> in a difficult matter—the presumption of those who measure everyone by their own standard is less commendable. These people, seeking to block a path they have not traveled themselves, claim that all effort spent here is in vain. Thus John Calvin says on this passage, page 279: *"Because I suspect that Egyptian words are being reported by Moses, both in this place and shortly after, I advise readers not to torment themselves in vain."* And Pareus says on page 412: *"But one labors in vain concerning those foreign words."* As if, forsooth, one is not permitted to search further for what those two men were unable to find! We believe that today, in such a great light of learning—which is capable of dispelling even Cimmerian <note>A proverbial term for intense or perpetual darkness, named after a mythical people said to live in mist and gloom</note> or Egyptian shadows, even if most people close their eyes to it—we should not easily despair here.

§. 6. Before all else, however, we must be careful that we do not commit a <term>metabasis eis allo genos glōttēs</term> <note>original Greek: μετάβασιν εἰς ἄλלו גέנוס גלֹוֹתּתֵס; a "transition into another kind of language," or a category error in linguistics</note>, nor deviate from the native origin. For a single word, by reason of its sound, can carry very different meanings in different languages. For instance, to us a *Kaplan* is an ecclesiastical person <note>A chaplain or curate in German and related languages</note>, but to the Turks it is a tiger. Unless it is therefore established which language <term>Zaphenath-Paaneah</term> <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> belongs to, all labor will be spent in vain. It is indeed disputed whether it is Hebrew...

<summary>This page explores the scholarly impasse regarding the Egyptian name of Joseph, citing the humility of Luther and Lyra while challenging Calvin’s dismissal of the mystery. The author insists that linguistic precision and modern learning can overcome these ancient obscurities.</summary>
<keywords>Zaphenath-Paaneah, Elias Levita, David Kimchi, Nicholas of Lyra, Martin Luther, John Calvin, David Pareus, Egyptian language, Hebrew etymology, Cimmerian darkness, Kaplan, biblical translation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the name Joseph was given in Egypt. The previous page established the confusion among earlier scholars like Luther and Nicholas of Lyra; here, the author examines specific linguistic arguments from Jewish grammarians who attempted to find Hebrew roots for the name, while also noting the growing consensus that the name is truly Egyptian.</meta>

...[whether] it is a Hebrew name, or an Egyptian one. The Hebrew scholars generally refer <term>Zaphenat</term> <note>original: צפנת</note> to a Hebrew origin; and <term>Kimchi</term> <note>David Kimchi, a 12th-13th century grammarian and biblical commentator</note> in his <term>Book of Roots</term> <note>original: ספר השורשים (Sefer ha-Shorashim), a standard medieval Hebrew lexicon</note>, folio 410, line 1, treats <term>Paneach</term> <note>original: פענח</note> no differently than a Hebrew word:

*Paneach is a descriptive noun of the doubled Palshan form, and its meaning is "revealer of hidden things." They say it is a past-tense verb in the Piel <note>The intensive verb stem in Hebrew</note> form. The correct pronunciation is Paneach with a Chireq vowel under the letter Pe. A poet used every form of this construction, as the poet said: "The one who reveals [Maphaneach] closed-up secrets."* <note>original Hebrew: פענח שם תאר בכפל פלשן ופירושו הצפונות מגלה ואמרו שהוא פועל עבר מן הדגוש והנכון בו פענח בחיק הפא ואמרו הפייטן ממנו כל הבנין כמו שאמר הפייט המפענח צפונות סחומות</note>

That is to say: *Paneach* is an agent noun (denominative) of the form *Palshan* (concerning which, see Rabbi Solomon ben Melech, page 201, line 1) and signifies: *The Revealer of the Hidden*. They also say it is the past tense of the *Piel* and can regularly take a *Chireq* under the first letter, so that it is pronounced *Pineach*. Furthermore, poets inflect it through the entire conjugation, as a certain poet said: *Maphaneach* [The Revealer], *Revealing the hidden and the abstruse.* Thus, Rabbi Bechai <note>Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century Spanish commentator</note> also notes in this place that in a certain prayer it is read as: *Hamaphaneach hanelamim*, or "The Revealer of latent things." However, Kimchi adds in the same place with some hesitation:

*And some say that Zaphenat Paneach is an Egyptian word.* <note>original Hebrew: ויש אומרים כי צפנת פענח לשון מצרי הוא</note>

Nor was <term>Münster</term> <note>Sebastian Münster, a 16th-century German cartographer and Hebraist</note> any more certain, whose words in this place are: *Zophnat Paeneach is interpreted by some, following Onkelos <note>The author of the famous Aramaic translation (Targum) of the Torah</note>, as "revealer of secrets" or "Interpreter." Others think they are Egyptian words, but so far there has been no one who could translate them from the Egyptian language into Latin, neither Jew nor Christian.* These are his words.

### §. 7. 

But it is admitted by most that the name is Egyptian, which is why the <term>Vulgate</term> <note>The standard 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> translator confidently added on his own: *in the Egyptian language*, although this is found explicitly neither in the Hebrew, nor the Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note>, nor the Greek manuscripts, as <term>Pererius</term> <note>Benedictus Pererius, a 16th-century Jesuit commentator</note> admits along with others in his *Disquisition on Genesis 41, Disp. 4, n. 21.* He could have dispensed with that addition, since it does not exist in the text. Otherwise, it is easily apparent that the name imposed on Joseph by an Egyptian king, in Egypt, for the sake of the Egyptians, is not Hebrew. Hence, Rabbi Solomon ben Melech rightly says on folio 14, column 3:

*And some say that Zaphenat Paneach is an Egyptian word, just as Nebuchadnezzar gave Daniel and his companions names in the Aramaic language; and regarding those commentators who explained that it is in the Holy Tongue [Hebrew], it is a wonder how...* <note>original Hebrew: ויש אומרים כי צפנת פענח לשון מצרי הוא כמו שקרא נבוכדנצר לדניאל וחביריו שם לשון ארמי והמפרשים שפירשו שהוא לשון קדש תמה הוא איך</note>

<summary>This page explores the linguistic debate over Joseph's Egyptian name, Zaphenath-Paaneah, contrasting Hebrew grammatical explanations from scholars like Kimchi with the emerging consensus that the name is truly Egyptian.</summary>
<keywords>Zaphenath-Paaneah, David Kimchi, Solomon ben Melech, Sebastian Münster, Hebrew grammar, Egyptian language, Biblical philology, Joseph, Vulgate</keywords>

### Signature E

...Pharaoh called the name in the Holy Tongue <note>original Hebrew: קרא שם בלשון הקדש פרעה</note>. That is to say: *Others say that Zaphnat-Paneach is an Egyptian word, just as Nebuchadnezzar gave names from the Chaldean language (which was native to him) to Daniel and his companions. Furthermore, as for those interpreters who believe this name is Hebrew, it is surprising that Pharaoh (an Egyptian) would call anyone by a Hebrew name.*

§. 8. Nor is there reason for anyone to object that the Egyptian name was translated by Moses <note>The author is refuting the idea that Moses took an Egyptian name and "translated" its meaning into Hebrew for his readers</note>, especially since it was not Joseph’s proper name, but an honorary title—which is why he is never called "Zaphnat-Paneach" afterward, but is always called Joseph, even by Pharaoh himself in verse 55. For the phrase "Zaphnat-Paneach" <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> is not the name of some ordinary office in Pharaoh’s court, nor is it merely a <term>complimentary title</term> <note>original Greek: εὔφημον (euphemon)</note>, but a new and proper name for Joseph, by which the King wished him to be distinguished from all others. The constant use of the phrase "he called the name" <note>original Hebrew: קרא שם (kara shem)</note> easily proves this; followed by a specific name, it always denotes the imposition of a proper name or a name uniquely assigned to a person or place for a specific reason—something one can observe nearly a hundred times in the active voice of the verb "to call" in the Holy Scriptures. Therefore, if "Zaphnat-Paneach" is a name uniquely assigned to Joseph by Pharaoh, it seems it was by no means changed, nor should it have been changed, by Moses. 

Like other sacred historians, Moses retained foreign proper names. For instance, in Genesis 31, verse 47, the Aramaic name for the "witness heap" given by Laban the Aramean occurs: *Yegar-Sahadutha* <note>original Aramaic: יגר שהדותא</note>. Likewise, the name Hagar in Genesis 16, verse 1 (from which come the Hagrites in Psalm 83:7, whom the scholar *Aben Esra* <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a 12th-century Spanish Jewish polymath</note> believes descended from Hagar’s second marriage) is Arabic-Egyptian; for in Arabic, <term>Hagar</term> <note>original: חגר</note> means *rock, stone, or cliff*, and in Egyptian, *Gjajrim* (as noted by Scaliger in *Selections*, page 364). In other sacred writers, more than one Persian name is found. *Cyrus* <note>original: כורש (Koresh)</note> comes from the Persian <term>Khor</term> <note>original Persian: خور</note> or <term>Khorshid</term> <note>original Persian: خورشید</note>, meaning *Sun*. For Plutarch, following the historian Ctesias <note>A Greek physician and historian who lived at the Persian court</note>, testifies that <term>"the Persians call the sun 'Kyros'."</term> <note>original Greek: κῦρον τοὺς πέρסας καλεῖν τὸν ἥλιον</term> (Compare Waser in his notes on *Gesner’s Mithridates*, page 125; Hottinger, *Oriental Treasures*, page 77; and Levinus Warner, *Persian Proverbs*, 89, page 41). Likewise, *Darius* comes from the Persian <term>Darush</term> <note>original Persian: دروش</note>, which means <term>"a doer" or "worker"</term> <note>original Greek: ἑρξίης (erxies)</note>, from which...

<summary>The author argues that Joseph's Egyptian name was a unique proper name rather than a translated title, citing Moses's habit of preserving foreign names like those from Aramaic, Arabic, and Persian throughout the Bible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological argument from page 13. The author is moving from the general practice of naming in the ancient Near East toward a specific Coptic/Egyptian analysis of Joseph's name, Zaphnat-Paneach.</meta>

...whence Darius, according to Herodotus in Book 6 (*Erato*), is explained as "the doer" <note>original Greek: ἐρξείης (erxeiēs)</note>. Ahasuerus <note>original Hebrew: אחשורוש (Achashverosh)</note> is from the Persian <term>ahadash</term> <note>original Persian: احدش</note> meaning <term>vahmten</term> <note>original Persian: قهمتن</note> or "Majesty and honor," as the Persians explain; and the suffix <term>var</term> or <term>varu</term> <note>original Persian: ور or وار</note> means "endowed with," thus he is "endowed with Majesty, powerful." Artaxerxes <note>original Hebrew: ארתחששתא (Artachshashta)</note> is from the Persian <term>ar</term> <note>original Persian: ار</note> meaning "bringing" (the present participle of <term>avardan</term> <note>original Persian: اوردن</note>), <term>vahmten</term> <note>original Persian: قهمتن</note> meaning "perfection," <term>Shah</term> <note>original Persian: شاه</note> meaning "King," and <term>stah</term> <note>original Persian: ستاه</note> meaning "worthy of praise." Otherwise, Artaxerxes (or, as Scaliger advises in Book 6 of *On the Emendation of Time*, more correctly *Artaxeres*) is explained by Herodotus in the place cited as "great warrior" <note>original Greek: μέγας ἀρήϊος (megas arēios)</note>. Indeed, the very name of Pharaoh is <term>by its naming</term> <note>original Greek: ὀνομαστικῶς (onomastikos)</note> not Hebrew, but an Egyptian name common to all Kings of Egypt, signifying "King." It has no other function than the Persian <term>Shah</term> <note>original Persian: شاه</note> or the Turkish <term>Sultan</term> <note>original Arabic/Turkish: سلطان</note>. In Coptic it is <term>ouro</term> <note>original Coptic: ουρο</note>, with the article it is <term>Pi-ouro</term> <note>original Coptic: Πιουρο</note>, and with the possessive prefix <term>Pa-ouro</term> <note>original Coptic: Παουρο</note>, meaning "King" or "my King." Hence Josephus in Book 8 of *Antiquities of the Jews*, Chapter 2, writes: "Pharaoh among the Egyptians signifies king" <note>original Greek: ὁ Φαραὼν κατ' Αἰγυπτίους βασιλέα σημαίνει</note> (Compare Drusius, *Miscellanies*, Century 2, Chapter 92, page 63, and Athanasius Kircher, *Coptic Supplement*, Chapter 6, page 589). Furthermore, it should not offend anyone that after that <term>naming</term> <note>original Greek: ὀνομασίαν (onomasian)</note>, Joseph is still called by his original name by Pharaoh himself; for new names do not always abolish old ones, but are often used alongside them. Thus Daniel, after the changing of his name (Daniel 1, verse 7), is called interchangeably Belteshazzar <note>original Hebrew: בלטשאצר; meaning "Treasurer of Bel," for he took that name from the god Bel according to Daniel 4:5</note> and Daniel. The name of Jacob was changed by his Almighty Adversary to Israel in Genesis 32, verse 28, yet he is often called Jacob thereafter, even by God in Genesis 46, verse 2, and indeed by himself in Genesis 49, verses 2, 7, and 24. And properly speaking, Joseph was distinguished by the name Zaphnat-Paneach as an <term>honorary title</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπονομάσεως (eponomasēōs)</note>; it was a surname, not one that removed the name he had used up to that point.

§. 9. Since, therefore, we have established that the name is Egyptian, it follows of its own accord that its true meaning must be sought only from the Egyptian language. Here, however, many people despair once more, as if the ancient Egyptian language had vanished and remained neither in the speech of men nor in the monuments of literature. *The ancient language of the Egyptians*...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Egyptian language, specifically addressing whether modern Coptic is a direct descendant of the ancient language of the Pharaohs. It follows the previous page's introduction of the term "Pharaoh" and its Coptic equivalent "Pi-ouro."</meta>

...tongue," says Bartholomew Mayer in *Sacred Philology*, Part I, page 123, "just as it has long since ceased to exist, so too it is completely unknown."

§. 10. However, Father Athanasius Kircher <note>A famous 17th-century Jesuit polymath and pioneer in the study of Coptic</note> would hardly concede this point. He strives to restore the Egyptian language from modern Coptic, which, in terms of its vocabulary, is almost half-Greek. From this, the illustrious man had argued in his *Coptic Forerunner* <note>original: *Prodromus Coptus*</note>, chapter 7, page 171, that the Greek language had emerged from it, and that there was no more difference between the two than between Italian and Latin. However, after being advised by learned men and convinced by the evidence of the facts, he himself revoked this claim in his *Coptic Supplement*, chapter 1, page 507. Furthermore, he does not present modern Coptic as pure Pharaonic Egyptian, but says it arose from and was corrupted by it. To this point belong his own words in *Coptic Forerunner*, page 150: *Since, therefore, no language is more similar to ancient Egyptian than Coptic, it certainly remains beyond doubt that it is the true and genuine daughter of ancient Egyptian. It is not pure, to be sure—such as it flourished from the beginning, namely during the height of the Egyptian empire in the time of the Patriarchs—but was corrupted over the succeeding centuries by the various mixing of peoples and languages and the frequent changes in the Egyptian state.* He briefly reviews the decline and death of the Egyptian language, as well as the manner in which its remnants <note>original Greek: λείψανα (leipsana)</note> and ruins <note>original Greek: ἐρείπια (ereipia)</note> passed to the Copts, based on their accounts, in his *Coptic Supplement*, page 509.

§. 11. Johann Stephan Rittangel <note>A Jewish convert to Christianity and professor of Oriental languages known for his polemical style</note>, a Christian from a Jewish-Christian background, attacked Kircher rather harshly. In his *New Year's Collections* published in German, he included a certain exhortation in which he wields the censor's rod against "Hebraizing Christians"—scholars such as Scaliger, Cappell, Myslenta, Vechner, G. H. Vorstius, L'Empereur, Slevogt, Schickard, and above all, Athanasius Kircher. Perhaps he was not always off the mark, yet he acted with a certain lack of self-control. In the meantime, he could have kept his own judgment about the Coptic language to himself. He thinks that the literature and the Coptic language itself is in no way Pharaonic, but belongs to the Greek Christians living in Egypt, which is why the dialect [is filled with] words...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Athanasius Kircher’s theories on the origins of the Coptic language. The previous page ended by discussing how Kircher revised his view that Coptic was identical to Greek, instead arguing it was a "daughter" of the ancient Egyptian tongue, albeit a corrupted one.</meta>

...mixed with the Egyptians. Regarding the script, Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar famous for his attempts to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> continuously shouts with a stentorian voice that all Orientals write from right to left <note>original: "sinistrorsum," literally 'towards the left'</note>: 

"Where has it ever been heard in the whole world, that outside of Europe and Christendom in the entire Orient or Asia any people or nation could have been found who would have guided a script from the left to the right? Go through all the historians and centuries, from the time when Egypt and the entire Orient were inhabited by humans until this present day, and see if it does not indeed and in truth behave as such?" etc. <note>original German: "wo ist iemahls in der gantzen Welt erhöret worden/ das ausserhalb Europa und der Christenheit in gantzen Oriente oder Asia einiges Volck oder Nation solte gefunden worden seyn / die eine Schrifft von der lincken zur rechten geführet hätte / gehet alle Historicos und secula durch / von der Zeit an / da Aegypten und gantz Orient von Menschen bewohnet worden ist / bis auf diesen heutigen Tag / und sehet ob es sich nicht also in der That und Warheit verhalte?"</note>

But indeed, whatever the case may be regarding the modern Coptic script, the same language can remain under foreign letters. The *Chaldean* <note>Aramaic</note> portions of the sacred Bible, although they consist of Hebrew characters, are nevertheless not Hebrew. The *Russians* use elements that are for the most part Greek, yet their language is not Greek. And what is this "outspokenness" of Rittangelius <note>Johann Stephan Rittangel, a 17th-century professor of Oriental languages</note>, claiming that all nations outside Europe write their letters from right to left? Certainly the *Armenians*, *Abyssinians* <note>Ethiopians</note>, and the *Indian Brahmins* write toward the right in the European manner, while the *Chinese* write downward. Are all these not sufficiently "outside Europe"? It seems that while he is being most careful, this "careful man" has been caught in his own trap.

§. 12. Furthermore, that the modern Coptic language is not "corrupt Greek," but rather corrupt Egyptian—that is, originated from the Pharaonic tongue, though successively debased and mixed with many Greek words (a fellowship which other languages have also admitted, as *David Coen de Lara* <note>A Sephardic rabbi and philologist</note> proved regarding *later Chaldean* in his work *The City of David* <note>original Hebrew: עיר דוד (*Ir David*)</note>)—besides the name itself and so many testimonies of the Copts, these reasons persuade us:

1. The grammatical analogy of the Coptic language differs from the Greek *completely* <note>original Greek: δὶς διὰ πασῶν (*dis dia pason*), a musical metaphor meaning 'by a double octave' or 'entirely'</note>, so much so that there is a greater agreement between the structure of Polish and Greek than between Greek and Coptic.

2. Although the Coptic language has adopted very many Greek words, it has nevertheless retained its own *native* <note>original Greek: αὐθιγενεῖς (*authigeneis*)</note> words alongside them, as can be seen even from the numerals. (*Scaliger, The Great Emendation of Time, pages 66, 67, 68; Kircher, Coptic Primordial, Part 6, chapter 2.*)

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Coptic language's relationship to ancient Egyptian. Following the previous page's critique of Athanasius Kircher's views on script direction, the author now addresses how Coptic might help decipher specific Egyptian names preserved in the Bible, specifically the name given to Joseph by Pharaoh.</meta>

...and it could be proven with countless other examples <note>original Latin: "sexcentis exemplis," literally "six hundred examples," a common idiom for an innumerable amount</note> without much labor. It is further clear that these words are from the old Egyptian language, because Egyptian words encountered in both sacred and secular authors find a convenient explanation from that source, as Kircher shows by examples in his *Supplement*, chapter 7, and throughout his *Oedipus* <note>Athanasius Kircher's *Oedipus Aegyptiacus* (1652–54), a massive, though often imaginative, work on Egyptian antiquities and hieroglyphs</note>.

§. 13. Since it is therefore certain that the Coptic language is descended from the ancient Egyptian, who would deny that it may be usefully called to our aid in digging out the true meaning of <term>Zaphenat-Paaneah <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note></term>? Before we do that, however, something certain must first be decided regarding the <term>orthography <note>original Greek: ὀρθογραφίᾳ; the correct spelling</note></term> of the name. In the Authentic Text <note>referring to the Hebrew Bible</note>, it is written as <term>Zaphenat-Paaneah <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח or פַּעְנֵחַ</note></term>, but in the version of the Seventy Elders <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the early Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> it appears as <term>Psonthomphanech <note>original Greek: Ψονθομφανήχ</note></term>. Certainly, **Zaphenat-Paaneah** and **Psonthomphanech** are not a little different. It is asked, therefore, which of these is <term>genuinely <note>original Greek: γνησίως</note></term> Egyptian? Athanasius Kircher, in his *Prodromus Coptus*, page 130, contends that **Psonthomphanech** is the <term>correctly spelled <note>original Greek: ὀρθόγραφον</note></term> Egyptian form, while **Zaphenat-Paaneah** was corrupted by the Hebrews. He bears dissenters very poorly; hence, he says of the scholar from Gubbio <note>Agostino Steuco (1497–1548), also known as Eugubinus, a librarian at the Vatican and a noted scholar of the Bible</note> on page 131: *“I cannot wonder enough what came into the mind of Eugubinus, that he dared to assert that Tsophnat panech is the true Egyptian, and Psonthomphanik is the corrupt one.”* Yet Eugubinus's own words on this opinion are somewhat hesitant in his *Notes on Genesis 41*: *“These words are corrupted in the Septuagint version; for in their place they put Psonthomphanech, unless perhaps they changed them intentionally because they had understood from the Egyptians that the words were rather corrupted among the Hebrews—though this does not seem likely to me.”* Henning Henning <note>Henning Henning (1641–1689), a German scholar of Oriental languages</note> agreed with Kircher after others in his *On Muhammad Praying*, page 192: *“The Syrian version expresses the words Psonthomphanech corruptly along with the Hebrew text, etc.”* Is that so? Corruptly along with the Hebrew text? Remember, however, that these things should be leveled more sparingly against Moses <note>The author is defending the Hebrew text, traditionally authored by Moses, against the charge of linguistic corruption</note>.

§. 14. But no reason appears, even a plausible-looking one, to convict **Zaphenat-Paaneah** of corruption. Certainly, Pharaoh, the King of Egypt, undoubtedly pronounced Joseph’s name correctly. Furthermore, Moses—born and educated in Egypt—[mastered] all the Egyptian language and wisdom...

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name Pharaoh gave to Joseph: Zaphenat-Paaneah. The author is currently disputing the theories of Athanasius Kircher, who argued that the Hebrew spelling in the Bible is a "corrupted" version of an original Egyptian name due to the linguistic limitations of the Hebrew people.</meta>

...Was Moses, who was least of all ignorant of the learning of the Egyptians <note>original Latin: "Aegyptiorum minimè rudis"; a reference to Acts 7:22: "And Moses was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians."</note>, the one who would have reported that name corruptly? For as to the fact that the Sacred Writers sometimes express foreign words in a corrupted form—for example, in Daniel 3, *pesanterin* <note>original Hebrew: פסנתרין</note> for the Greek *psaltērion* <note>original Greek: ψαλτήριον</note>, and *sumponyah* <note>original Hebrew: סומפוניה</note> for the Greek *symphōnia* <note>original Greek: συμφωνία</note>, and so on—this is because those words were commonly pronounced no differently by the people whose history they were weaving or in whose language they were writing. Therefore, historical truth demanded that they record the words exactly as they were uttered by those people. 

But here the reasoning is quite different, where an Egyptian word was pronounced by an Egyptian King. Kircher uses the excuse of the "difficulty of sound" <note>original Greek: δυσφωνίαν (dysphōnian); refers to a word being hard to pronounce in a different language</note> of the word *psonthon* <note>original Greek: ψονθον; the first part of the name as it appears in the Greek Septuagint</note> among the Hebrews. Thus, in his *Prodromus* <note>Kircher's *Prodromus Coptus sive Aegyptiacus* (1636)</note>, page 130, he says: *Pfontomphanech was corrupted by the Hebrews due to the difficulty with which they pronounced Egyptian words.* And on page 131: *Therefore, that "psonthon" was turned into "Tsophnat" to avoid the difficulty of pronunciation will be easily apparent to anyone comparing those names.* 

But first: Moses did not produce these words as they were spoken by the Hebrews—of whom it is not even certain they had ever spoken them before him—but rather he received them from the Holy Spirit exactly as they had been uttered by Pharaoh. Secondly, I do not yet grasp what "difficulty of sound" the word *psonthon* would pose for the Hebrews. None can be imagined at all, except perhaps concerning the letter *psi* <note>the Greek letter Ψ, which combines 'p' and 's' sounds</note>, which the Hebrews do not have. But in truth, not even the Egyptians had that letter in their own words, as we shall say below. 

Nevertheless, if the Hebrews were able to pronounce the *ps* sound in *pesanterin*, what, I ask, prevents them from doing so in *psonthon* or **Zaphenat**? But let it be so. Suppose they expressed the *psi* with the letter *tsade* <note>the Hebrew letter צ, which makes a 'ts' sound</note>. They would therefore have pronounced the whole word as *Zaphenat* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note>, but certainly not as *Zaphenach* <note>original Hebrew: צפנח</note>. Where then did that final letter *het* <note>the Hebrew letter ח, a gutteral 'h'</note> come from? 

Kircher responds that it was adjusted for the sake of Hebrew analogy. *Since they understood this word,* he says, *to mean "revealer of secrets," it is likely that they transmuted "Pfonton" into "Tsaphan" (for "tsaphan"* <note>original Hebrew: צפן</note> *is the Hebrew word for "to hide" or "that which is hidden"), so that they might make the reading more similar to a Hebrew phrase.* 

But first, the similarity is no greater between *psonthon* and *Zaphenach* than it is between *Phanech* and *Paaneah*. Why then would Moses not have also transmuted the other word for the sake of his fellow Hebrews? Perhaps because a hybrid word pleased him more? But it is certain that Moses neither translated, nor transmuted, nor altered foreign names...

<summary>The author critiques Athanasius Kircher’s claim that the Hebrew Bible "corrupted" Joseph’s Egyptian name due to pronunciation difficulties, arguing instead that Moses recorded the name exactly as Pharaoh spoke it.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Athanasius Kircher, Zaphenat-Paaneah, Joseph, Egyptian language, Hebrew philology, phonology, Septuagint, biblical transcription</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Athanasius Kircher's linguistic theories regarding the name Zaphenat-Paaneah. The previous page established that the biblical author (Moses) would have recorded the name accurately as it was spoken, rather than "corrupting" it due to a lack of phonetic ability, as Kircher claimed.</meta>

...it is more certain, such that we now wonder more how Father <note>Kircher was a Jesuit priest, hence "P." for *Pater* (Father).</note> Kircher was able to accuse the word *Zaphenat* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note> used by Moses of being a corruption.

### §. 15.
Lest he be without a witness, he calls upon **Maimonides** <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), also known as Rambam, one of the most influential Torah scholars and physicians of the Middle Ages.</note> to his side. To these things, he says, *Rabbi Moses the Egyptian, commonly called Rambam, an outstanding interpreter of the greatest difficulties, who also, on account of the exceptional solidity of his learning, earned this eulogy from later Hebrews in his epitaph—which can be seen to this day at Lake Gennesaret in Palestine—"From Moses to Moses, there has arisen none like Moses"* <note>original Hebrew: ממשה עד משה לא קם כמשה; this famous phrase compares Maimonides to the biblical Moses.</note>. *This Moses, I say, an Egyptian by nation, asserts in his commentary on this passage in express words that the word "Zophnatphanecheffe" is Egyptian, by which they signify a "revealer of secrets," and that it was corrupted by the Hebrews, and that he himself received this from those skilled in the Egyptian language.*

Is Moses Maimonides an "Egyptian by nation" to you, O Most Illustrious Man? Certainly he was no more Egyptian by nation than any of us; for he was born in Córdoba, in Spain. He is called "the Egyptian," however, because he lived in Egypt for a long time. (Compare regarding his person *Buxtorf’s Preface to the translation of the Guide for the Perplexed* <note>The *More Nevochim*, Maimonides's philosophical masterpiece.</note>). But let that pass. Where indeed did Maimonides—whether he be Egyptian or Spanish—write that *Zaphenat-Paaneah* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> was corrupted by the Hebrews? "In the commentary on this passage," namely Genesis 41:45. It would have been worthwhile if Kircher had brought this passage from Maimonides into the light, or at least said where on earth Maimonides’s *Commentary on Genesis* exists. But that will perhaps happen at the Greek Calends <note>original Latin: "ad Kalendas Græcas"; an idiom meaning "never," as the Greek calendar had no Calends.</note>. We fear that Kircher may have confused *Ramban*, that is, Nachmanides <note>Moses ben Nahman (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar whose commentary on the Pentateuch is famous.</note>, with *Rambam*, or Maimonides. For a commentary by the former on the Pentateuch does exist, yet the commentary cited by Kircher is not found there. Finally, if Maimonides were an Egyptian, why would he need to learn Egyptian matters from the Egyptians?

### §. 16.
Since, therefore, *Zaphenat* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note> is the genuine form, it follows by an easy consequence that *psothon* <note>original Greek: ψόγϑον</note> or *psontom* <note>original Greek: ψοντόμ; these are the variations of the name found in Greek translations.</note> is the corruption. Nevertheless, Kircher attempts to prove its authenticity <note>original Greek: γνησιότητα (gnēsiotēta)</note> both from the translation of the Septuagint <note>The LXX, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, who were supposedly so instructed by Egyptian priests, and from the Coptic Version preserved in the Vatican Library. But regarding the version, which...

<meta>This page continues the argument against Athanasius Kircher, focusing on the reliability of the Septuagint (the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament) as a source for reconstructing "original" Egyptian names.</meta>

Regarding the translation that is today attributed to the **Seventy Interpreters** <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally attributed to seventy-two Jewish scholars in Alexandria.</note>, the case is still before the judge as to whether it is truly theirs. Distinguished men have long since proven that the version we have today contains either nothing or merely the **remnants** <note>original Greek: λείψανα (*leipsana*)</note> of the ancient one. Compare Sante Pagnino, *Introduction to the Holy Scriptures, ch. 9*; Andreas Masius, *Preface to the Greek edition of the Book of Joshua*; Drusius, *Observations, book 6, ch. 9*; Wilhelm Schickard, *Bechinath Happerushim, p. 39 and following*; Glassius, *Sacred Philology, p. 41, 42*; the Magnificent Doctor Calovius, *Sacred Criticism, p. 835 and following*; the Reverend Doctor Müller, *Prologue on Judaism, no. 12, p. 85*; Hottinger, *Philosophical Treasury, book 1, ch. 3, section 3, question 12, p. 344*; Rivetus, *Introduction to the Holy Scriptures, p. 140*; the Illustrious Salmasius, *On Hellenists, p. 243*; Alting, *Historical Theology, locus 2, p. 108 and following*; Christopher Cartwright, *on Genesis, p. 287*; Buxtorf, *On the Antiquity of Points, p. 122*; James Ussher and Johannes Wower, *Treatise on the Septuagint Version*. The Excellent Doctor Wasmuth, *Vindication of the Hebrew, Part 1, p. 118*.

I will not be overly rigorous, nor will I assert with Ussher that whatever survives today under the title of the "Seventy Interpreters" was never even seen by those men; I will be content to conclude with Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Cardinal and influential Jesuit theologian.</note>, *On the Word of God, book 2, ch. 6*, that the version of the Seventy still exists, but is so **corrupt and spoiled** that it seems to be something else entirely. Thus, it is no longer safe to correct Hebrew or Latin texts based on the Greek manuscripts. From what basis, then, will Kircher prove that the name *Psonthomphanech* <note>original Greek: Ψοντομφανήχ; this is the Septuagint's spelling of the name given to Joseph by Pharaoh.</note> originated from the Seventy?

Even if it were so, those much-celebrated Seventy Elders very often distorted proper names. In this very history of Joseph, instead of *Potiphar* <note>original Hebrew: פוטיפר</note> and *Potipherah* <note>original Hebrew: פוטי פרע</note>, they write *Pentaphres* <note>original Greek: πενταφρὴς, πεντεφρῆ</note>. Likewise, elsewhere they write *Ioilan* <note>original Greek: Ἰωϊλὰν</note> for *Havilah* <note>original Hebrew: חֲוִילָה</note>, or in another place *Euilat* <note>original Greek: εὐειλὰτ</note> for the same word; they write *Ambakum* <note>original Greek: Ἀμβακὺμ</note> for *Habakkuk* <note>original Hebrew: חֲבַקּוּק</note>, and so on. What wonder is it, then, if they transformed *Zaphenat* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note> into *Psonth*, or *Psomton*, or *Psonthon*, or *Psothon*? Who would not rather place their faith in the Holy Spirit <note>Referring to the Hebrew text as divinely inspired.</note> than in those seventy **errable little men** <note>original Latin: *homuncionibus labilibus*</note> who were all too familiar with making such slips? Hottinger provides a whole hodgepodge of **misinterpretations** <note>original Greek: παρερμηνειῶν (*parermēneiōn*)</note> arising from the confusion of letters in his *Treasury, book 1, ch. 3, section 3, question 15*.

Nor is there any reason for anyone to think that because they were instructed by Egyptian priests, or lived in Egypt, they would easily provide the correct orthography of an Egyptian word. For even if we do not question the historical reliability of the Seventy, nor diminish their authority, we might say that they wrote with a faulty
