<meta>This page continues the argument against Athanasius Kircher, focusing on the reliability of the Septuagint and the Coptic language as sources for reconstructing ancient Egyptian names. The author argues that by the time of the Greek translation, the original Egyptian language had already changed significantly.</meta>

[the writing of the version] proceeded not from the *authors*, but from the *copyists*. We know, however, that in the time of *Ptolemy Philadelphus* <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus (309–246 BCE), the King of Egypt who famously sponsored the translation of the Hebrew Bible into Greek (the Septuagint).</note> the common use of the *Pharaonic* language <note>The author refers here to the "original" Ancient Egyptian language of the Pharaohs.</note> had already ceased. Kircher, in his *Coptic Supplement, page 509*, reports—following the opinion of the Coptites—that this language had already begun to decline when *Cambyses* <note>Cambyses II, the Persian King who conquered Egypt in 525 BCE, roughly 250 years before the Septuagint was created.</note> invaded Egypt. This is not to mention that those Seventy Teachers <note>The legendary 72 Jewish scholars who translated the Septuagint.</note> came to Ptolemy with the intention of teaching, and they would have considered it a point of shame to laboriously seek out the opinions or advice of the Coptic people for the explanation of Holy Scripture.

§. 17. But what will now become of the *Coptic* version? Its author was a *Copt*—who would dare suspect that he was ignorant of the true pronunciation of an Egyptian word? He, indeed, has <term>Psonthon Phanech</term> <note>original: ψονθον φανηχ. This is the Coptic rendering of Joseph's Egyptian name, Zaphenath-paneah.</note>. Kircher, perhaps for a better <term>comparison</term> <note>original Greek: σύγκρισιν (*synkrisis*)</note>, took several passages of that version from the Vatican Library, but did so unfortunately. For, first: he reviewed those words, <term>he placed him upon the second chariot</term> <note>original Coptic in Greek script: αϥταλοϥ εϫεν † βερεσωου τεϥεμαh (*aftalof ejen t-beresoou tefemah*)</note>, twice due to an <term>oversight</term> <note>original Greek: ἀβλεψίαν (*ablepsian*), meaning a failure to see or a mental lapse.</note> and thereby caused his readers great distress <note>original Latin: *crucem fixit*, literally "fixed a cross," meaning he created a torturous problem for them.</note>; though few know or care about such things. Second: although he promises a translation of the Coptic text, he subsequently provides the Vulgate version <note>The Vulgate is the late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible which became the Catholic Church's official version.</note>, from which the Coptic sometimes differs. For instance, these words: <term>and a herald cried out before him</term> <note>original Coptic in Greek script: οϥωϣ πικυρξ εϥωϣ εβολ hαψωϥ</note>, and the following: <term>And he set him over the whole land of Egypt</term> <note>original Coptic in Greek script: οϥοh αϥϣαϥ εϫεν πκαhι τηϥ νχηµι</note>, ought to have been translated as such. Kircher, however, thrust forward the Vulgate translation: *With a herald crying out, so that all should bow the knee before him* (on this point the Coptic Interpreter is silent) *and should know that he was set over the whole land of Egypt*. Third: regarding those words: *Pharaoh said to Joseph, "Because God has revealed to you all that you have spoken, could I find anyone wiser and more like you?"*—which he places in his Latin version—there is not a trace nor a sign <note>original Latin: *nec vola comparet nec vestigium*, an idiom meaning "not even a palm-print nor a footprint."</note> of them in the Coptic texts he himself presented. These matters did not seem right to conceal as the opportunity arose. But back to the point.

<meta>catchword: §. 19. De. This page continues the scholarly debate over the linguistic origins of Joseph’s Egyptian name. After dismissing Coptic as a primary source for ancient Pharaonic Egyptian in the previous page, the author here argues that the Coptic Bible is a late translation based on the already-flawed Greek Septuagint. He then turns his attention to the Samaritan Pentateuch, defending its accuracy against claims of textual corruption.</meta>

The *Coptic* version of the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note> belongs to a more recent age. The Vatican copy was written in the *Year of the Martyrs 1115* <note>The "Era of the Martyrs" or Diocletian Era began in 284 CE. Therefore, 1115 translates to 1399 CE.</note> and is thus not yet three hundred years old. However, it is not sufficiently clear when this version was first established. It must, nonetheless, have been produced by Coptic Christians long after the birth of Christ. For according to the Coptic *Martyrology* cited by Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar and polymath who claimed to have deciphered hieroglyphs.</note> in his *Prodromus*, page 186, the Holy Scripture began to be translated into the Coptic language about three hundred years after the birth of the Lord. Furthermore, that version kept in Rome, whenever it may have originated, seems to be modeled after the translation of the "Seventy" <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> which was already corrupted. A significant suspicion of this arises from the present passage: where the Hebrew text has <term>abrek</term> <note>Hebrew: אברך. A title of honor shouted before Joseph’s chariot in Genesis 41:43.</note> and all other versions have attempted to translate the word, the Coptic, along with the Seventy, left it uninterpreted <note>original Greek: ἀνερμήνευτον (*anermēneuton*).</note>. Thus, error gave birth to error. Nor can anyone object that the Coptics could have corrected the faulty writing of the Seventy from their own language—we do not deny the possibility, provided it were certain they were learned in Hebrew and possessed the skill to return to the original text for an easy correction. But without such knowledge, correcting a word so greatly corrupted was impossible.

§. 18. Before we move on from here, something must be mentioned regarding the *Samaritan* codex. The famous Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and expert in Oriental languages.</note> notes in his *Exercises Against Morin*, page 49, that the name <term>Zaphenath-paneah</term> <note>Hebrew: צפנת פענח. The Egyptian name given to Joseph by Pharaoh.</note> is corrupted there into <term>Zaphenathi-paneah</term> <note>Hebrew: צפנתי פענה. A slight variation in the spelling of the name.</note>. It also appears this way in the English edition <note>Likely the London Polyglot Bible of 1657.</note> and the reading is provided as: *Saphneti phane*. However, this is not the consistent reading of all Samaritan codices, but seems attributable to certain scribes. Witness to this is John Viccars in his *Decapla on Psalm 105*, page 301: "*The Samaritan Pentateuch manuscript, which I owe to the most distinguished Sir Thomas Cotton, and five other manuscripts which I saw in Paris, as well as the one in the Vatican at Rome, read* <term>Zaphenath-paneah</term> <note>Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> *just as it is in the Hebrew.*"

<meta>continuity with previous page: This page shifts the focus from the Coptic and Samaritan versions of the Pentateuch to the Armenian version. §. 19. Finally, the meaning of the highly corrupted word *Fesuut* in the Armenian version has been sought in vain until now. The words of that version, expressed here in Roman letters, are: *Evi gbuociazz Paraun banun Housepu Fsuut* <note>original: "fanun" in Kircher’s text should be restored to "banun." The phrase translates to: "And Pharaoh called Joseph's name Fsuut."</note>. Regarding this word, Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), the Jesuit scholar mentioned on the previous page.</note> says in his *Prodromus*, page 130: *“What this word properly signifies, none of the Armenians with whom I have frequent familiarity here in Rome could explain to me.”* And Viccars <note>John Viccars (1604–1660), an English linguist and author of a work on the various versions of the Psalms.</note> notes in the same place: *“In the Armenian Manuscript at the Vatican, the word is 'Fesuut'; neither when he [the scribe] wrote it, nor in my conversation with the aforementioned Father Athanasius, do the Armenians know how to interpret it.”*

However, both the Armenians and Kircher seem to have "searched for a knot in a bulrush" <note>A Latin idiom (*nodum in scirpo quaerere*) meaning to look for a difficulty where there is none, or to overcomplicate a simple matter.</note>. They consulted experts in vain about a word that is not actually Armenian. It appears that *Fesuut* is simply a corrupted form of *Psonton* <note>A shortened version of *Psonthomphanech*, the Greek Septuagint's rendering of Joseph's Egyptian name.</note>. Indeed, all evidence suggests that the Armenian version was translated from the Greek. Since *Psonton* meant nothing in either Greek or Armenian, the Armenian translators retained the term but also shortened it. This practice of shortening names is not unusual for them. For example, for *Athanasius* they say *Athanas*; for *Darius* they say *Dareh*; for *Areopagus* they say *Ariubach*; for *February* they say *Februar*; and for *Elizabeth* they say *Elisa*, and so on.

Thus, in place of *Psonton*, the Armenian translator likely wrote *Psunt* or *Psont*. Perhaps he added a *badiù* <note>A horizontal line used in Armenian manuscripts to indicate an abbreviation or a contraction.</note> or abbreviation mark, which was later ignored by copyists. Furthermore, anyone who carefully compares the shapes of the Armenian letters—whether in the *Nodr* (cursive), *pulur* (round), or *chelba* (majuscule) scripts—using Clemens Galanus’s *Armenian Grammar* (published in Rome, 1645), will see how easily the Armenian letter *Nu* (n) could be corrupted into *Uieu* (u). As for words like *Fesuti* <note>Found in Rivola’s Dictionary, page 376.</note> or *Fasaduti* <note>Found on page 404.</note>, while they might sound similar to *Fsuut*, their meanings are entirely unrelated to this context.

§. 20. Thus, the correct spelling is established. Now we turn to the meaning. The *Vulgate* <note>The late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible which became the Catholic Church's official version.</note> translation, which renders the name as *Savior*—

<meta>continuity with previous page: The author continues the investigation into the translation of Joseph’s Egyptian name, specifically focusing on the interpretation of "Savior of the World" and how it transitioned from Greek and Latin sources into broader theological scholarship.</meta>

*...savior of the world* <note>original: "vatore m mundi," the conclusion of "Salvatorem mundi" from the previous page</note>, is defended by the Roman Catholic scholars. In the *Greek Oxford Manuscript of the Octateuch* <note>The first eight books of the Old Testament.</note>, after the name *Psonthomphanech* <note>The Greek Septuagint's transcription of Joseph's Egyptian name, Zaphenath-Paneah.</note>, a similar addition is found: *the savior of the world* <note>original: "ὁ θεὶ σωτὴρ κόσμο"</note>, as reported by Patrick Young <note>Patritius Junius (1584–1652), a Scottish scholar and royal librarian.</note> in his *Annotations on the Alexandrian Manuscript of the Septuagint*. Furthermore, Blessed Jerome handed down that interpretation in his *Hebrew Questions* on this passage. "Although," he says, "this name in Hebrew sounds like 'discoverer of hidden things,' yet because it was given by an Egyptian, it must take its meaning from that language. Therefore, in the Egyptian tongue, *Zaphanat Phaanecha*—or as the Seventy <note>The "Seventy" refers to the Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> chose to render it, *Psonthomphanech*—is interpreted as **Savior of the world**, because he delivered the orb of the earth from the impending destruction of the famine."

§. 21. Yet, until now, no one has been able to show the grammatical reasoning for this interpretation from the Egyptian language itself. For the most part, the Roman Catholic scholars follow the Latin Vulgate with their eyes closed, and are content to justify the translation based on that assumption. Those who go further have no evidence to offer beyond the authority of Jerome; they argue it is unlikely he invented the meaning, but rather received it from the Egyptians themselves with whom he lived. Thus, Alfonso Tostado <note>A prolific 15th-century Spanish theologian.</note>, commenting on this passage, responds to the question *“Why do these names signify 'Savior of the world' in Egyptian?”* by stating that Jerome recorded this because he knew the Egyptian language: "for he lived in Egypt and traveled through all the hermitages of the monks throughout the great deserts of Egypt, as he himself relates in the *Life of Blessed John of Thebes*, etc." Agostino Steuco, Cornelius à Lapide, and Jean Bonfrère proceed in no different a manner.

§. 22. But this translation is displeasing to many. Benedict Rungius, in his *Lectures on Genesis* (page 1158), calls it "too arrogant." Salomon Glassius, in his *Sacred Philology*, calls it "foreign" <note>original: "alienam," meaning it does not fit the context or language.</note>. It is likewise refuted by Sisto Amama, André Rivet, Christopher Cartwright, and others, especially

...firstly, because they see no grammatical reasoning for it. "Let someone tell me," says Sixtinus <note>Sixtinus Amama (1593–1629), a Dutch Reformed theologian and Hebraist.</note>, "which word—the first or the second—signifies 'world' or 'Savior'? Furthermore, Rivetus <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot director of the Leiden University.</note> suspects that Blessed Jerome was deceived by some bold person; for if that meaning had been very well known in Egypt, the Seventy <note>The translators of the Septuagint, the ancient Greek version of the Old Testament.</note> would not have left the name untranslated <note>original: "ἀνερμήνευτον," meaning "uninterpreted" or "left in the original tongue."</note>, nor would Philo, who lived in Egypt, have explained it differently. It is clear that he was deceived, even without those reasons.

§. 23. However, we cannot pass over in silence here the conjecture <note>original: "στοχασμὸν," a guess or scholarly speculation.</note> of the Distinguished and Famous Mr. Giles Gutbier <note>Ægidius Gutbier (1617–1667), a German scholar of Oriental languages.</note>, formerly our guide <note>original: "ὁδηγοῦ"</note> <gloss>in this manner</gloss> in these letters, whose memory we recall with a most grateful mind. He, intending to assert Jerome’s faithfulness and reputation, <margin>Zaphenat Paneah <note>The Hebrew transcription of Joseph's name: צפנת פענח.</note></margin> derived it from the Coptic words *Psenphazmonē* <note>original Greek script: "Ψενφαζμωνη"</note>, which he explained as: *Relaxation of life*, by which he thought Jerome’s version approached the closest sense. But after weighing everything thoroughly, we are unable to hold to our Teacher’s opinion. For, 1. they differ not a little in sound: *Zaphenat* and *Psenphat*, as well as *Paneah* and *monē*. 2. Abu Ishac Ebno-laassel <note>Abu Isḥāq ibn al-ʿAssāl, a 13th-century Coptic scholar.</note> in the *Selected Scale* (that is, a Coptic-Arabic Lexicon), explains *Psenphat* <note>original Greek script: "Ψενφατ"</note> as shaking or trampling <note>Hebrew: רפש, meaning "mud" or "to trample/foul water."</note>, and Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), the famous Jesuit polymath who studied Coptic.</note> moreover translates it as *winnowing fan*, confusing the words on page 445. Whether *Psenphat* truly denotes "relaxation" has not been established thus far.

§. 24. Most recently, the Reverend and Distinguished Mr. Andreas Müller <note>Andreas Müller (1630–1694), a German Orientalist and provost in Berlin.</note>, *Provost of Berlin and Most Skillful in Oriental Languages*, our esteemed friend, intending to bring help to the same Jerome, noted in his remarks on certain excerpts of the Turkish codex of Aziz al-Nasafi <note>Aziz ibn Mohammed al-Nasafi, a 13th-century Persian Sufi philosopher.</note>, a Tatar, that the first word was taken from the Coptic *psōnt* <note>original Greek script: "πσωντ"</note>, which means *creature*—and by the custom of other languages,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Joseph’s Egyptian name, Zaphenat-Phaanecha. It focuses on the traditional interpretation "Savior of the World," its origins in the writings of St. Jerome, and its defense by Catholic scholars versus the criticism of Protestant philologists. The catchword "primis" at the bottom of the page indicates the start of the next section.</meta>

...[Sa]vior of the world, is defended by the Papists <note>A term used here for Roman Catholic scholars who defended the Latin Vulgate's translation.</note>. In a Greek **manuscript of the Octateuch at Oxford**, after the name *Psonthomphanech* <note>The Greek transcription of Joseph's name in the Septuagint.</note>, the phrase "the god, savior of the world" is similarly added, as reported by Patrick Young <note>Patritius Junius (1584–1652), a Scottish scholar and Royal Librarian to King Charles I.</note> in his *Annotations on the Alexandrian Manuscript of the Septuagint*. Moreover, **Blessed Jerome** handed down that interpretation in his *Hebrew Questions on Genesis*: "Although," he says, "this name in Hebrew sounds like *discoverer of hidden things*; nevertheless, because it is given by an Egyptian, it must follow the logic of that language. Therefore, in the Egyptian tongue, *Zaphenat Phaanecha*—or as the Seventy <note>The translators of the Septuagint.</note> chose to translate it, *Psonthomphanech*—is interpreted as *Savior of the world*: because he delivered the whole earth from the imminent destruction of famine."

§. 21. Yet until now, there has been no one who could show the grammatical reasoning for that exposition from the Egyptian language. The Papists for the most part follow the **Vulgate** <note>The late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible that became the Catholic Church's official version.</note> with closed eyes and are content to have given a reason for the fact based on that hypothesis. Those who go further have nothing to claim beyond the authority of Jerome; they argue it is unlikely he fabricated it, but rather received it from the Egyptians themselves, with whom he had interaction. Thus **Alfonso Tostado** <note>Alphonsus Tostatus (c. 1400–1455), a Spanish bishop known for his massive biblical commentaries.</note> in his commentary on this passage, responding to the question of *why these names signify 'Savior of the world' in Egyptian?*, replies that Jerome set this down because he knew the Egyptian language: "for he lived in Egypt and traveled through all the hermetic dwellings of the monks across the great solitudes of Egypt, as he himself relates in the *Life of Blessed Paul the Hermit* <note>The text says "Blessed John of Thebes," but Tostado refers to Jerome’s accounts of Egyptian monasticism.</note>, etc." Nor do **Agostino Steuco** <note>Augustinus Eugubinus (1497–1548), an Italian humanist and librarian of the Vatican.</note>, **Cornelius à Lapide** <note>A highly influential Jesuit commentator (1567–1637).</note>, or **Jacques Bonfrère** <note>Jacobus Bonfrerius (1573–1642), a Flemish Jesuit scholar.</note> proceed otherwise.

§. 22. But this translation displeases many. **David Rungius** <note>A Lutheran theologian (1554–1604).</note> calls it "excessively arrogant." **Salomon Glassius** <note>Author of *Philologia Sacra* (1593–1656), a key work on biblical linguistics.</note> calls it "foreign" <note>Meaning it does not fit the linguistic evidence.</note>. **Sixtinus Amama**, **André Rivet**, **Christopher Cartwright** <note>An English scholar known for comparing Rabbinic texts with the Bible.</note>, and others refute the same, especially...

<summary>This page analyzes the claim that Joseph's Egyptian name means "Savior of the World," tracing the idea to St. Jerome and noting that while Catholic commentators defend it based on Jerome's authority, many linguists and Protestant scholars reject it for lacking grammatical evidence.</summary>

...at first because they see no grammatical reason. *Let someone tell me*, says Sixtinus <note>Sixtinus Amama (1593–1629), a Dutch Protestant Hebraist known for criticizing the Latin Vulgate</note>, *which word, the former or the latter, signifies "world" or "Savior"; let someone show any trace of these meanings from the Egyptian language. Let them bring even one conjecture that adds strength to this version, and we will gladly admit it, as it would better serve the mystery of Christ, of whom antiquity handed Joseph down as a type in many ways.* Furthermore, Rivetus <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian</note> suspects that Blessed Jerome was deceived by some bold person; for if that meaning had been a very well-known interpretation in Egypt, it is clear that neither the Seventy <note>The translators of the Septuagint</note> would have left the name untranslated, nor would Philo, who lived in Egypt, have explained it differently—even without those other reasons.

§. 23. We cannot, however, pass over in silence here the conjecture <note>original Greek: *στοχασμὸν* (stochasmon)</note> of the Illustrious and Celebrated Aegidius Gutbier <margin>37 <note>The Hebrew numeral ל"ז (37), likely a reference to a specific section or page.</note></margin>, formerly our guide <note>original Greek: *ὁδηγοῦ* (odegou)</note> in these letters <note>A common way of referring to the study of literature or Oriental languages.</note>, whose memory we recall with a most grateful mind. He, intending to assert the faithfulness and reputation of Jerome, derived <margin>Zaphenat-Phaanecha <note>The name written in Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note></margin> from the Coptic words *Psenphazmone* <note>original: Ψενφαζμωνη</note>, which he explained as: *Relaxation of life*, by which sense he thought Jerome’s translation came closest. But having weighed everything thoroughly, we cannot hold to our Teacher’s opinion. For 1. the sounds of *Zaphenat* and *Psenphat* differ considerably, as do *Phaanecha* and *mone*. 2. Abu Ishac Ebno-laassel in the *Selected Ladder* <note>original: *Scala electa*, referring to the *Sullam al-Mukhtār*, a famous Coptic-Arabic dictionary.</note>, that is, a Coptic-Arabic Lexicon, explains *Psenphat* as *Pashach*, that is, *shaking* or *kicking*; and Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who published early Coptic lexicons.</note> moreover translates it as: *winnowing fan*, confusing *Pashach* and *Pashaph* on page 445. Whether *Psenphat* actually denotes "relaxation" is not yet established.

§. 24. Most recently, to bring help to the same Jerome, the Reverend and Illustrious Master Andreas Müller, Provost of Berlin, most skilled in Oriental languages and our venerable friend, noted in his remarks on certain excerpts from the Turkish codex of Aziz Nesephæus, a Tatar <note>Aziz ad-Din bin Mohammed al-Nasafi, a 13th-century Persian Sufi scholar whose works were studied by early modern Orientalists.</note>, that the first word was taken from the Coptic *mount* <note>original: μουντ</note>, which signifies *creature*, and according to the custom of other languages,

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of Joseph’s Egyptian name, specifically addressing the interpretations of the word "Zaphenat." It transitions from critiquing modern etymologies to surveying traditional Jewish and Classical interpretations, specifically focusing on the meaning "Revealer of Secrets."</meta>

...of [the race of men], let it denote the human race: hence *Psontomphanech* <note>A Greek transcription of the Egyptian name</note> is the *Savior of men*. Compare numbers 21 and 40. But although we do not yet see what the Most Distinguished Man <note>Likely Andreas Müller, mentioned on the previous page</note> determines regarding the second word, we hardly think the first can be derived from *mashot* <note>original Hebrew: משות (meaning "to draw out")</note>, which is too far removed from *Zaphenat* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note>, the authenticity <note>original Greek: γνησιότητα (gnēsiotēta)</note> of which we have proven.

§. 25. The most widely accepted opinion is that *Zaphenat-Paneah* <note>original Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> means *Revealer of Secrets* or *Interpreter*. Onkelos <note>The author of the standard Aramaic translation of the Torah</note> expressed it thus: <note>original Aramaic: וקרא פרעה שום יוסף גברא דמטירן גלין ליה</note> "And Pharaoh called the name of Joseph: The man to whom hidden things are revealed." Pseudo-Uzielides <note>The author of the Targum Pseudo-Jonathan</note> agrees: <note>original Aramaic: וקרא פרעה שמה: דיוסף גברא דמטירן מפרסם</note> "And Pharaoh called the name of Joseph: The man who makes hidden things public." All the Jews agree as one. Philo Judaeus, in his *Book on Joseph*, says: <note>original Greek: μετωνομάζει δ' αὐτὸν ἀπὸ τῆς ὀνειροκριτικῆς ἐγχωρίῳ γλώττῃ προσαγορεύσας κρυπτῶν εὑρετὴς ἢ ὀνειροκρίτης</note> "And he changed his name based on his dream-interpretation, addressing him in the local tongue as a discoverer of secrets or a dream-interpreter." And Josephus, in the second book of *Antiquities*, chapter 3: <note>original Greek: Καὶ προσηγόρευσεν αὐτὸν ψονθονφάνηχον ἀπιδὼν αὐτῷ πרὸς τὸ ἄδοξον συνέσεως, σημαίνει γὰρ τὸ ὄνομα κρυπτῶν εὑρετήν</note> "And he called him *Psonthonphanechos*, looking toward his extraordinary understanding; for the name signifies a discoverer of secrets."

In the *Bereshit Rabbah* <note>An ancient Jewish commentary on Genesis</note>, folio 90, it is taught that *Zaphenat-Paneah* is the same as "Illuminating the Hidden" <note>original Hebrew: צפונור מופיע (Tzepunor Mophia)</note>, or as Rabbi Hezekiah says: "Illuminating the hidden through knowledge" <note>original Hebrew: צפונור מופיע בתורה (Tzepunor Mophia ba-Torah)</note>. Supporting this are Rashi, Rabbi Bahya, Ibn Ezra, Ralbag <note>Levi ben Gershon</note>, Rabbi M. Albilda, Abravanel, Rabbi Melech, and others on this passage; Rabbi David Kimchi in his *Book of Roots*; Mordecai Nathan in his *Concordance*; to which may be added as a crowning touch the charming author of the *Ze'enah U-Re'enah* <note>A popular Yiddish/Hebrew work for laypeople</note>, folio 53, column 1.

The Kabbalist Jacob ben Asher <note>also known as the Baal Ha-Turim</note> calls upon the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>The Jewish mystical tradition</note> for support and says that by <term>Gematria</term> <note>A system where words with the same numerical value are linked</note>, "Zaphenat-Paneah" <note>צפנת פענח</note> has the same value as "revealing hidden things" <note>מגלה נסתרים (Megalleh Nistarim)</note>—both resulting in 828, provided that the letter *Yod* is omitted from the word *Nistarim* [hidden things]. Furthermore, he explains it through <term>Notaricon</term> <note>A method of interpreting letters as acronyms for full words</note> as follows:

For **Zaphenat** (צפנת):
**Z** [צ] for **Zaddik** [צדיק - the righteous one],
**P** [פ] for **Pashat** [פשט - he subdued],
**N** [נ] for **Nephesh** [נפש - the soul],
**T** [ת] for **Ta'avah** [תאוה - desire] (meaning he subdued the desire of the soul, or the concupiscent faculty).

For **Paneah** (פענח):
**P** [פ] for **Potiphar**,
**A** [ע] for **Anah** [ענה - he afflicted],
**N** [נ] for **Naphsho** [נפשו - his soul],
**H** [ח] for **Hinam** [חנם - without cause].

And he explains **Zaphenat** (צפנת) in another way as well:
**Z** [צ] for **Tzopheh** [צופה - watcher/seer],
**P** [פ] for **Poreh** [פורה - redeemer or fruitful],
**N** [נ] for **Navi** [נביא - prophet]...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Zaphenat-Paneah," moving from the Jewish rabbinical traditions discussed on the previous page to a broad survey of ancient Eastern versions (Syriac, Arabic, Coptic, etc.). It documents how various traditions interpreted the name as "Revealer of Secrets."</meta>

*Prophet*, <term>Sustainer<note>original Hebrew: תומך (*Tomekh*). The scholar Buxtorf translates this as "He who holds the scepter"</note></term>, <term>Interpreter<note>original Hebrew: פותר (*Poter*)</note></term>, *gentle* <note>original Latin: mansuetus; Hebrew: ענו (*Anav*). In the *Bereshit Rabbah*, an ancient collection of Jewish lore, the word used is ערום (*Arum*), meaning "shrewd" or "cunning"</note>, *Prudent* <note>original Hebrew: נבון (*Navon*)</note>, *Foreseer* <note>original Hebrew: חוזה (*Chozeh*), specifically of future events</note>. Compare Buxtorf’s *Biblical Lexicon* and Helvicus Hakspan’s *On the Jewish Cabala in Miscellanea*, page 291.

§. 26. Furthermore, other Eastern versions agree. The **Chaldean-Samaritan** translates it as: <note>original Aramaic/Samaritan: טמירתי גלה (*Temirti gela*)</note> that is, according to Edmund Castell: *He revealed my secret* <note>The author asks: Why not translate it as "Gale," meaning "Revealing"?</note>. The **Syriac version of the Maronites** <note>A Lebanese Christian community using the Syriac language</note> says: <note>original Syriac: וקרא פרעון שמה דיוסף צפנת פענח דכסיתא גלין לה</note> *And Pharaoh called the name of Joseph Zaphenat Paneah, because secrets were revealed to him.* Hence, in the **Greek Scholia** <note>Ancient marginal notes on the Bible</note> it is written: *The Savior holds [the name], he who knows hidden things,* as cited by Flaminio Nobili in his *Notes on the Various Readings of the Septuagint for this passage.* And the **Syriac Onomasticon** <note>A dictionary of proper names</note> explains it as: <note>original Syriac: משכחנא דכסיתא</note> *Finder of hidden things.* Furthermore, the **Mauritanian-Arabic codex** of Erpenius has, in place of *Zaphenat Paneah*: <note>original Arabic: כאשף אלאשראר</note> *Revealer of hidden things.* The **Arabic Pentateuch of Harith Ibn-Sina** <note>A medieval translation</note> reads: <note>original Arabic script</note> *And Pharaoh called Joseph's name Zaphenat Paneah, that is, the one revealing secrets to himself.* The **Arabic version of Saadia** <note>Saadia Gaon, a famous 10th-century scholar</note>, along with the Paris edition of Michel de Jay, the English Polyglot, and various manuscripts, has: <note>original Arabic: מוצח אלחפיאת</note> *The Manifester of things hidden.* The **Arabic-Samaritan version**, however, expressed an almost opposite meaning, as cited by the famous Edmund Castell in his *Samaritan Observations* on this passage: <note>original Arabic: כנז אלעלם</note> *He stored away knowledge.* The **Persian version** of Jacob Tavvus retained the Hebrew word, as is his custom with <term>difficult-to-translate terms<note>original Greek: δυσερμηνεύτοις (*dysermēneutois*)</note></term>. Compare Thomas Greaves in Volume 6 of the *English Polyglot*, page 48, and Brian Walton’s *Prolegomena* 16, number 7. Likewise, the **Coptic version** retained the original name, but in a distorted form: *ouoha Pharao tref Phran nios Phont-hon Phanech.* That is, *And Pharaoh called the name of Joseph Pfont-hon-phanik.* The **Armenian version**, as we have seen, substituted the highly corrupt word *Psuut*. Regarding the **Ethiopic version**, it is not sufficiently certain, though it is otherwise established that it was translated from the Greek of the Seventy <note>The Septuagint</note>.

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Joseph’s Egyptian name, "Zaphenat-Paneah." Having surveyed Eastern translations, the author now turns to the consensus among Christian Church Fathers and later European scholars, while also noting a parallel in Islamic tradition. The discussion shifts toward the linguistic tension between interpreting the name via Hebrew roots versus its purported Egyptian meaning.</meta>

§. 27. This same opinion is pleasing to most Christian interpreters. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 349–407 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his preaching</note> explained it as **a knower of hidden things** <note>original Greek: τῶν κρυπτῶν γνώστην</note>. Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–458 AD), a bishop and theologian</note> called him **an interpreter of secrets** <note>original Greek: τῶν ἀποῤῥήτων ἑρμήνευτήν</note>. The same is held by Diodorus in the *Chain of the Fathers* <note>A "Catena" or chain is a form of biblical commentary comprised of stringing together extracts from earlier theologians</note> and Procopius of Gaza, among other ancient writers. A great number of more recent scholars support this: Jerome of Oleastro on this passage, Arias Montanus, François Vatable, Willem Estius, Piscator, Wolfgang Musculus (page 775), Christoph Pelargus (page 618), Hugo Grotius, the Weimar scholars <note>original Latin: Vinarienses, referring to the authors of the "Ernestine" or Weimar Bible</note>, the Dutch <note>original Latin: Belgæ</note>, and others. Indeed, the matter was even known to the Muslims. For in the *Excerpts* from the Turkish author *Azizi* (page 72), it is said: *Because he was a foreknower and interpreter of secrets, that is yours* <note>original Turkish/Arabic script: כהאולר עאלם אשראר אולור שין</note>; here it seems Joseph is hinted at, for mention is made in the same place of Jacob, Moses, and others.

§. 28. None of these authors, however, published the reasoning for their interpretations based on the Egyptian language. Most seem to have looked toward a **Hebraism** <note>An interpretation based on Hebrew linguistic rules</note>, from which the word <term>Zaphenat<note>Hebrew: צפנת</note></term> can be given some explanation. From there, they concluded by conjecture the meaning of the second word, <term>Paneah<note>Hebrew: פענח</note></term> (*which has no equivalent in the rest of the Bible*, as Rashi <note>Shlomo Yitzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note> remarks). Let us hear Jerome of Oleastro: *It seems to me that if the words are Hebrew, there is no doubt that the first, "Zaphenat," signifies "hidden," from the root "saphan," which is to hide or conceal. Regarding the meaning of the second word, there is more doubt, because it is found only in this place. Yet the Rabbis and all others think it signifies "Revealer." Since there is no other conjecture to hunt down its proper meaning, the common opinion must be held.* Indeed, many believe that a different focus—either on the Hebrew or the Egyptian idiom—uniquely produced these two interpretations mentioned so far. As Pererius says in the place cited: *The reason for this variation is that those two words can be taken either as Egyptian words—and thus they mean **Savior of the world**—or as Hebrew words—and thus they denote the **Revealer and discloser of hidden things**.* Compare also Münster on this passage, B. Gesner in *Disputation 31 on Genesis* (page 618), Gvalperius in his *Collection of Versions* (page 785), and Bonfrère in the place cited, whose conjectures on these occasions—

<summary>This page documents the scholarly consensus among Christian, Jewish, and even Islamic commentators that Joseph’s name signifies a "Revealer of Secrets." It highlights a major linguistic debate: whether the name should be translated using Hebrew roots or its original Egyptian context, which suggests the meaning "Savior of the World."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Zaphenat-Paneah." The author moves from traditional Hebrew-based etymologies to a critique of early Coptic studies, specifically targeting the influential but flawed work of Athanasius Kircher.</meta>

...interpretation given by St. Jerome. Furthermore, it seems too meager an explanation that Gregory Francus (*Lexicon Sanctum, number 51, page 57*) derives <term>Paneah</term> <note>Hebrew: פענח</note> from the roots <term>yapha</term> <note>Hebrew: יפע; to shine or be bright</note> and <term>nachah</term> <note>Hebrew: נחה; to lead</note>, and from this interprets <term>Zaphenat-Paneah</term> <note>Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> as **He who shines forth by teaching obscure things.** It is merely a flight of fancy, however, that Guichard (in his *Harmonical Etymology*) and likewise Avenarius (*Hebrew Dictionary, page 603*) attempt to derive the names of the gods **Faunus** and **Pan** from the word <term>Paneah</term> <note>original Hebrew: פענח</note>.

§. 29. However, it was shown above that <term>Zaphenat</term> <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note> and <term>Paneah</term> <note>original Hebrew: פענח</note> are actually Egyptian words. Therefore, Athanasius Kircher <note>A 17th-century Jesuit polymath known as the "Master of a Hundred Arts," who made early, though often inaccurate, attempts to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> finally attempted to show the sources of this exposition not in Hebraisms, but in Egyptian remnants. He claimed in his *Prodromus Coptus* (chapter 5, page 126) that in the Egyptian language, *psonton* signifies **Future things** and *paneah* or *phanech* signifies an **augur** or **prophet**. Very famous men lent him a willing ear, including Samuel Bochart (*Phaleg, book 1, chapter 15, page 67*), Hottinger (*Exercitationes Anti-Morinianae, page 47*), Cartwright (*on Genesis, page 351*), Edmund Castell (*Samaritan Animadversions on this passage*), John Viccars (*On the Captivity in Psalm 105, page 301*), and others; nor do we deny that we ourselves were once deceived by his authority.

§. 30. But truly, although Kircher has hitherto ruled alone in this literature—as the saying goes, "ruling in an empty hall"—let us now be permitted to doubt his reliability. Lest we seem to do this without cause, we will produce several specimens of Coptic patterns that he failed to observe, taken solely from his own Latin translation of the *Scala Electa* (the Copto-Arabic Lexicon). We do this not with the intent of nitpicking, but so that it may become manifest that a simple **"He himself said it"** <note>original Greek: αὐτὸς ἔφα; a reference to the Pythagorean tradition of accepting a master's word without proof</note> is not sufficient here. The infallible sign of the first person among the Copts is the prefix **an-**, while the second person is marked by **ak-** (compare Kircher's own *Egyptian First-Fruits, pages 308 and 311*). Therefore, Kircher incorrectly translates *amere* as **he testified**, instead of **I testified** (Arabic: <term>shahadtu</term> <note>original Arabic: שהדת</note>, page 295); *aksere* as **you conquered** instead of **I conquered** (Arabic: <term>alaltu</term> <note>original Arabic: עלבת</note>, compare page 394 and 392); *akhmo* as **you were hungry** instead of **I was** (ibid.); *anageh* as **I guarded** instead of **you guarded** (page 480); and *anôô* as **it is** instead of **you are** (page 397). Furthermore, **met** is a Coptic particle used to form abstract nouns. For example, he badly...

<summary>The author critiques various etymologies of Joseph's name, shifting from Hebrew puns to a detailed debunking of Athanasius Kircher’s Coptic linguistic claims, citing grammatical errors in Kircher's own translations.</summary>
<keywords>Zaphenat-Paneah, etymology, Athanasius Kircher, Coptic language, linguistics, translation errors, Hebrew roots, Egyptian language</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological critique of Athanasius Kircher’s Coptic scholarship, specifically debunking his attempts to provide Coptic etymologies for the name "Zaphenat-Paneah" by highlighting his linguistic errors and lack of evidence in authoritative lexicons.</meta>

Kircher renders <term>metoule</term> <note>original Greek script: μετουλε</note> as **lame**, instead of **lameness** or **limping**. In Arabic, the word is <term>'araj</term> <note>original Hebrew script: עראג</note>, not <term>'arah</term> <note>original Hebrew script: עראח</note> (*page 303*). Thus, the root <term>ga'al</term> <note>original Hebrew script: גאל</note> forms nouns of agency <note>the "name of the actor"</note>. Therefore, Kircher incorrectly translates <term>niphes hoteb</term> <note>original Coptic/Greek script: νιφεϚ hωτεϐ</note> as **Slaughter**, instead of **Homicide** <note>or Murderer</note>. The Arabic word <term>al-qatala</term> <note>original Hebrew script: אלקטלה</note> is a "broken plural" derived from <term>qatil</term> <note>original Hebrew script: קאתל</note>, meaning "slayer" (*page 292*). The character [†] is a marker for the feminine singular. Consequently, Kircher mistakenly translates <term>touse</term> <note>original Coptic script: τουϚη</note> as **serious men**, based on the Arabic <term>al-tikal</term> <note>original Hebrew script: אלתכאל</note> or <term>ittikalon</term> (*page 311*). And by what right is <term>pibeni</term> <note>original Coptic script: πιϐενι</note> considered a **feast day**, when it always denotes **iron**? (*page 335*; compare *Coptic Nomenclator, page 203*). <term>Mbeei</term> <note>original Coptic script: μϐεει</note> does not mean **new**, as Kircher claims, but rather **boundary** <note>original Hebrew: תחור; takhur</note>; from this, <term>osmbeei</term> <note>original Coptic script: οϚμεϐεει</note> signifies **termination** or **boundary-setting** (*page 324*). The phrase <term>Nestatyl os</term> <note>original Coptic script: ΝεϚατυλ οϚ</note> denotes **his column**—Arabic <term>umuduhu</term> <note>original Hebrew script: עמדוהו</note> from the singular <term>umud</term> <note>original Hebrew script: עמוד</note>—and not **he baptized him**, as if the Arab were intended to say *Ammadabu*; Kircher might even have known this from Greek influence (*page 416*). Likewise, <term>homos</term> <note>original Coptic script: hομως; Greek: ὅμως</note> corresponds to the Arabic <term>wakad</term> <note>original Hebrew script: וקד</note>, meaning **certainly**, and not **he burned**, as if from *wakada* (*page 435*); elsewhere <term>omos</term> is paired with <term>waq</term> <note>original Hebrew script: וק</note> (*page 421*). <term>Eshonh</term> <note>original Coptic script: ΕϚωυh</note> denotes **living beings** <note>Arabic: al-hayah; original Hebrew script: אלחיאה</note>, not a **beard**, according to Kircher (*page 476*). Collecting more examples of this kind would require very little effort.

§. 31. However, it is not enough merely to have cast suspicion upon Kircher's reliability; other obstacles also prevent us from so easily following in his footsteps. For, first: it is established that the reading <term>psonton</term> <note>ψοντον</note> is a corruption, whereas <term>Zaphenat</term> <note>צפנת</note> is the genuine <note>original Greek: γνησιον</note> form; therefore, one should seek the origins of the latter, not the former. Second: the letter <term>Psi</term> <note>ψ</note> is found in no word that is authentically <note>original Greek: γνησίως</note> Coptic. Consequently, <term>psonton</term> cannot even be a Coptic word. Third: neither in the *Coptic-Arabic Nomenclator* of **Sheikh Ibn Kabar** <note>a 14th-century Coptic scholar and encyclopedist</note>, nor in the *Lexicon* of **Ahmad Abu Ishaq ibn al-Assal** <note>a prominent 13th-century Coptic Christian writer and lexicographer</note>, is the word <term>psonton</term> explained as **future things**; indeed, it does not even appear there. Perhaps <term>Zaphenat</term> <note>original Hebrew: צפנת</note> could be more easily derived from the root <term>saphan</term> <note>original Hebrew: ספן</note>, meaning **to be** or **to come to pass**. Kircher, therefore, ought to have sought confirmation for his assertion elsewhere. He tries in vain to prove it from the Coptic version or that of the **Seventy** <note>the Septuagint</note>. For the Seventy, since they did not know the meaning of the foreign word, left it untouched rather than translating it. The **Coptic translator**, for his part, followed the Seventy; he did not explain <term>Psonthen Phanech</term> <note>ψονϑον Φανηκ</note> through equivalent Coptic words, even though he could have done so.

<summary>This page presents a detailed philological debunking of Athanasius Kircher's Coptic-Arabic translations, arguing that his interpretations of "Zaphenat-Paneah" are based on linguistic errors and non-existent Coptic words.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, Coptic, Arabic, Hebrew, etymology, philology, Sheikh Ibn Kabar, Ibn al-Assal, Septuagint, Zaphenat-Paneah</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Zaphenat-Paneah" given to Joseph by Pharaoh. The author is critiquing various sources, including the Alexandrian Chronicle and the scholarship of Athanasius Kircher, while proposing a new Coptic-based etymology for the name.</meta>

...had been. Thus, the words of the *Alexandrian Chronicle* <note>original: *Chronici Alexandrini*, a 7th-century Byzantine universal chronicle</note> are cited in vain for this matter: "The Egyptians called Moses *Psonthonphanche* <note>original Greek: ψον-θον-Φαγχῆ</note> after he brought the plagues, which is interpreted as, *he to whom the future was revealed*." For it is likely that its author flourished in the seventh century after Christ, as he ends his work with the deeds of Heraclius <note>Byzantine Emperor from 610 to 641 AD</note>. He possessed nothing certain regarding the name except what he might have known from Philo or Josephus, judging the spelling from the Septuagint <note>the Greek Old Testament</note>; indeed, he even seems to have confused Moses with Joseph.

§. 32. What, then, shall finally become of <term>Zaphenat-Paneah</term>? <note>Hebrew: צפנת פענח</note> We would prefer to learn from those more learned than to offer a definitive judgment. However, because one is permitted to speak freely in a scholarly republic, after weighing the matter thoroughly, we conclude that *Zaphenat* <note>Hebrew: צפנת</note> is the Coptic **Pson-nat**—that is, *desired things, sought but not found*—and *Paneah* <note>Hebrew: פענח</note> is **paukha**, meaning *Teacher* or *Indicator*. Thus, Joseph was called the **Indicator of desired things, which everyone anxiously sought but no one was able to find**. In this way, the various translators retain their due honor. In this way, the Hebrew reading is upheld. It is clear from verse 39 <note>Genesis 41:39, where Pharaoh acknowledges Joseph's unique wisdom</note> that Pharaoh intended exactly this, from which the true cause for both this naming and all the honors shown to Joseph must be sought.

§. 33. Nothing remains, therefore, but to prove our exposition from the Coptic language. We believe that *Zaphenat* <note>Hebrew: צפנת</note> must be derived from the Coptic word **Pson-nat** or **Sop-nat**. (The letter *Shei* <note>original: ϣ, the Coptic "sh" sound</note> is somewhat unusual in Coptic words, but the Hebrew letter *Tsade* [צ] and *Shei* are similar elements.) Consequently, in the *Scala Electa* <note>A prominent Coptic-Arabic dictionary used by scholars like Kircher</note>, **Sop-nat** is explained by the Arabic translator as: <term>mafqudun</term> <note>Hebrew script: מפקודון; an Arabic term for "missing" or "lost"</note>, which means: *Sought in vain and not found, desired, they are not visited*, as Kircher translates it. When the secondary syllable *ce* is removed, **Pson-nat** remains. Furthermore, Coptic speakers frequently add a **t** <note>original: τ</note> to the end of plural words, especially if they end in a vowel. For example: **tacho** (to perish), plural **tentakhiout** (we perish); **tobbo** (to purify), plural **setobbēout** (they purify); **skhai** (to write), plural **seskhηout** (they write), and so on. Thus, a **t** or **ti** may sometimes be added to the end for other reasons as well. It is sufficient that the root letters are present. Therefore, the Coptic language admits **Pson-nat**, from which comes *Zaphenat*, or, on account of...

<meta>catchword: propter (on account of)</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the Alexandrian Chronicle's confusion of Moses and Joseph and proposes a new Coptic etymology for Joseph's Egyptian name, Zaphenat-Paneah, meaning "Indicator of sought-after things." This etymology is supported by linguistic comparisons between Hebrew and Coptic roots found in the Scala Electa.</summary>
<keywords>Zaphenat-Paneah, Joseph, Moses, Alexandrian Chronicle, Coptic language, Hebrew etymology, Athanasius Kircher, Scala Electa, philology, Pharaoh</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological argument from page 51 regarding the Coptic etymology of Joseph's Egyptian name. The author is now specifically addressing the first part of the name, "Zaphenat," and arguing for a linguistic connection between Coptic and Hebrew.</meta>

...on account of the **Meteg** <note>a Hebrew diacritic mark used to indicate vowel length or stress</note> in the text *Zaphnat*. Whether this, with the passage of time, degenerated into *Psonat*, and finally into *Psonton*, let others decide.

§. 34. Furthermore, this exposition of the word *Psonphan* <note>Greek: ψονπαν</note> receives no small strength from the Hebrew language and its root *tsapan* <note>Hebrew: צפן</note>, meaning **to hide** and **to lie concealed**. For although the Coptic language was by no means a dialect of Hebrew—which Sixtinus Amama <note>a 17th-century Dutch scholar of Hebrew</note> considers more than certain in his *Antiquitates Biblicae, p. 274*—it is nevertheless no less false that "barely two or three purely Egyptian words exist which in any way agree with Hebrew." This was the assertion of the celebrated Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger, a Swiss philologist</note> in *Exercitationes Anti-Morinianae, p. 48*, where he subjoins those three, namely: *sea*, *iom* <note>Hebrew: ים, yam</note>; *stag*, *ereiobl* <note>Hebrew: איל, ayal</note>; and *water*, *muōsh* <note>Hebrew: מים, mayim</note>. 

But on this occasion, in addition to those three, we shall list thirty Egyptian words observed by us that agree with Hebrew in both sound and sense: 

1. *emna* — **faith** <note>Hebrew: אמונה, emunah</note>. 
2. *ali* — **to lift up** <note>Hebrew: העלה, ha'alah</note>. 
3. *shōshen* — **lily** <note>Hebrew: שושן, shoshan</note>. 
4. *hemhom* — **shook or crushed** <note>Hebrew: המם, hamam</note>. 
5. *erman* — **pomegranate** <note>Hebrew: רמון, rimmon</note>. 
6. *khale* — **weak or infirm** <note>Hebrew: חלה, chalah</note>. 
7. *ser* — **to conquer** <note>Hebrew: שרר, sharar</note>. 
8. *las* — **tongue** <note>Hebrew: לשון, lashon</note>. 
9. *tebs* — **ring** <note>Hebrew: טבעת, tabba'at</note>. 
10. *gol* — **wave** <note>Hebrew: גל, gal</note>. 
11. *khet* — **end** <note>Hebrew: קץ, qetz</note>. 
12. *anok* — **I** <note>Hebrew: אנכי, anoki</note>. 
13. *shōl* — **prey** <note>Hebrew: שלל, shalal</note>. 
14. *pikorben* — **offering** <note>Hebrew: קרבן, qorban</note>. 
15. *pkomor* — **Gomor** <note>Hebrew: גמר; a dry measure</note>. 
16. *thel* — **mound** <note>Hebrew: תל, tel</note>. 
17. *othoi* — **Woe** <note>Hebrew: אני, oi</note>. 
18. *olaloliuat* — **gleanings** <note>Hebrew: עוללות, olelot</note>. 
19. *oshkira* — **new wine** <note>Hebrew: שכר, shekar</note>. 
20. *sarh-theos* — **whirlwind** <note>Hebrew: סער, sa'ar</note>. 
21. *Phnet-moōt* — **he who is dead** <note>Hebrew: מת, met</note>. 
22. *thom* — **shut or closed** <note>Hebrew: אטם, atam</note>. 
23. *hem* — **heat** <note>Hebrew: חם, cham</note>. 
24. *mashi* — **to depart** <note>Hebrew: מוש, mush</note>. 
25. *gamol* — **camel** <note>Hebrew: גמל, gamal</note>. 
26. *iaro* — **river** <note>Hebrew: יאר, ye'or</note>. 
27. *laboi* — **lion** <note>Hebrew: לביא, labi</note>. 
28. *ebrekh* — **lightning** <note>Hebrew: ברק, baraq</note>. 
29. *maklibi* — **staff** <note>Hebrew: מקל, maqqel</note>. 
30. *lashmē* — **morsel** <note>Hebrew: לחם, lechem, meaning bread</note>; *obi* — **food** <note>Hebrew: פרי, peri, meaning fruit</note> etc. 

And so, it is not surprising that *Psonphan* <note>the Greek-Coptic form</note> and *tsapan* <note>the Hebrew form</note> agree in their meaning.

§. 35. We find manifest traces of the other word, **Paneah** <note>Hebrew: פנעח</note>, in the *Scala Electa* <note>a famous Coptic-Arabic dictionary</note> p. 283, where *Psonphanicha* <note>Greek: πζ πονπανιχα</note> is explained as *al-muntazir* <note>Arabic: אלמנטר</note>, that is: **One who indicates, teaching, a judge, or a doctor** <note>meaning a learned teacher</note>, from the fourth conjugation of the root *natar* <note>Arabic: נטר</note>, **he observed, saw, or considered**; from which comes *antar*, which is **to show** <note>Hebrew: הראו</note> or **to teach** <note>Hebrew: הורה</note>.

<summary>The author argues that the name Zaphenat-Paneah is linked to the Hebrew root for "hiding" and provides a list of thirty Coptic words that share roots with Hebrew to prove linguistic commonality. He also identifies "Paneah" in a Coptic-Arabic dictionary as meaning a teacher or one who reveals knowledge.</summary>

G 3

And *mantor* <note>original: מנטר</note> is *marau* <note>original: מראו</note> or *moro* <note>original: מורו</note>, so that *Psomthat Panecha* <note>original: Ψομθατ πανηχα</note> or *Zaphenat Paneah* <note>original: צפנת פענח</note> becomes **Teacher of the perplexed** <note>original: מורה המבוכים</note> or **Teacher of secrets** <note>original: הצפונות</note>. Furthermore, in *P-son Panecha* <note>original: π-σον πανηχα</note> the first syllable *P-* can be an article and *son* added from elsewhere, or certainly *Pson Panecha* seems to be a corruption of *Zaphenat Paneah*. For those skilled in the Coptic language know that *Panecha* <note>original: πανηχα</note> possesses its own peculiar root. At the very least, no one will deny that the Egyptian word *Zaphenat Paneah* gains great light from the Coptic *Panecha*.

§. 36. If we have thus far indicated the origin of the name *Zaphenat Paneah*, which has been so anxiously sought after, we shall by no means arrogantly claim that "**I have found it**" <note>original: ευρηκα, the famous Greek exclamation "Eureka"</note> for ourselves; to God be the glory. If we have not, we shall not cease to seek it. In the meantime, we rejoice that our opinion has not altogether displeased several very learned men to whose judgments we appealed in the first edition. Let the decision also rest with other sensible men. They will be able to judge, the unlearned to learn, and the superficial to do neither. Let the words of Augustine from *Book 1 on the Trinity, chapters 2 and 3*, seal this writing:

> *I will not be loath to seek, if I am anywhere in doubt; nor will I be ashamed to learn, if I am anywhere in error. Whoever reads these things, where he is equally certain, let him go on with me; where he equally hesitates, let him search with me; where he acknowledges his own error, let him return to me; where he acknowledges mine, let him call me back.*

->The End.<-

### COROLLARIES
### REGARDING CERTAIN OTHER WORDS, WHETHER
->*TRULY OR ACCORDING TO REPUTATION FOREIGN TO HOLY SCRIPTURE.*<-

I. *Kiyyun* <note>original: כיון</note> in **Amos 5:26** is Arabic-Persian: *Caiwân* <note>original: כיואן</note>, that is, *Zachal* <note>original: זחל</note> or **Saturn**, whose Egyptian synonym *Remphan* <note>original: ρεμφαν</note> (or *Rephan* <note>original: ρηφαν</note>, <term>The God of the Hidden One</term> <note>original Coptic: φ† ντεπιχεרνος; the author identifies this as a Coptic gloss for the planet Saturn</note>) appears in **Acts 7:43**. Compare.

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of "exotic" or foreign words found in the Hebrew Bible, transitioning from the name of Joseph to other significant names and terms like Gad, Abrech, and Moses.</meta>

54

Selden, *On the Syrian Gods, Synthesis 2, chapter 14.* <note>John Selden (1584–1654) was an English jurist and scholar of Oriental languages.</note> Louis de Dieu, *On Acts 7.* Edward Pococke, *Notes on Abul-Faraj, page 390.* Athanasius Kircher, *Volume 1 of the Egyptian Oedipus, Synthesis 4, chapter 22, page 387.* <note>Kircher was a famous Jesuit polymath known for his attempts to decipher hieroglyphs.</note> Hottinger, *Oriental Smegma, page 78.* <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger was a Swiss philologist; "Smegma" here refers to a "cleansing" or "scrubbing" of oriental philology.</note>

II. **Gad** <note>original Hebrew: גד</note> in *Isaiah 65:11* is the Star of Jupiter, so called from "good fortune," which among the Arabs is *al-jadd* <note>original Arabic: אלג'ד</note>. However, with this word— <margin>**Gad** <note>original Hebrew: גד</note> instead of **with a troop** <note>original Hebrew: בגר</note></margin> —comes a crowd in *Genesis 30:11*, which has nothing in common with it except the sound, contrary to what Fuller thought in *Miscellanea, book 5, chapter 17.* For at that time Leah did not favor superstition, much less Arabic superstition. Compare *Genesis 29, verses 32 and 33*, and Jacob himself alludes to the notion of a **troop** <note>original: "turmæ"</note> in *Genesis 49:19*.

III. Regarding the word **Abrech** <note>original Hebrew: אברך; a title cried out before Joseph's chariot</note> in *Genesis 41:43*, there is a marvelous ferment of debate among interpreters. See two dissertations on this by Dr. J. Ernst Gerhard, and Fesselius, *Sacred Adversaria, book 9, page 376 and following.* To us, it seems to be **Egyptian** <note>original: "Ægyptia"</note> (from *afex* or *afqex*, meaning **he inclined himself**), yet it is reconcilable with Hebrew, like certain other words.

IV. **Moses** <note>original Hebrew: משה</note> is not an Egyptian name derived from *mōs* <note>original Greek: μωος</note>, meaning **water**, and *usēs* <note>original Greek: υσης</note>, meaning **saved**, as those deceived by the authority of Philo and Josephus think—namely Athanasius Kircher in *Prodromus Coptus, chapter 5, page 135 and following*, and *Oedipus, volume 1, page 122*; Angelo Rocca of Camerino, *Vatican Library, page 306*; Marcantonio Marsilio, *On the Lustral Font, page 59*; Hottinger, *Oriental Smegma, page 398*, and *Anti-Morinus Exercises, page 48*, and others. Rather, it is **Hebrew**, derived from the verb **to draw out** <note>original Hebrew: משה, "mashah"</note>, and it seems to have been given not by Pharaoh’s daughter, but by his **mother**. For: 
1. The Holy Spirit expressly indicates the Hebrew origin of the word in *Exodus 2:10*. Indeed, it is not probable that Pharaoh’s daughter spoke Hebrew. 
2. The immediately preceding feminine verbs—namely **and she took**, **and she brought him**, **and she nursed him** <note>original Hebrew: ותקח / ותביאהו / ותניקהו</note>—refer to the mother of Moses; why then should **and she said** and **and she called** <note>original Hebrew: ותאמר & ותקרא</note> refer to Pharaoh’s daughter? 
3. If the word **I drew him out** <note>original Hebrew: משיתהו, "meshithihu"</note> were in the first person, it would require a *yod* <note>the Hebrew letter 'y'</note> before the suffix, without which it is necessarily in the second person <note>The author argues the grammar implies "You [the mother] drew him out" or is spoken by the mother herself</note>. 
4. The phrase **and she called** <note>original: "ותקרא"</note> should be understood here in the pluperfect sense <note>i.e., "she had called"</note>; for who would believe the name was only then bestowed by Pharaoh's daughter upon an adult? The matter spea- <note>original Latin catchword: "loqvi-", likely "loquitur ipsa" meaning "the matter speaks for itself"</note>

<summary>This page analyzes the etymologies of the biblical names Gad, Abrech, and Moses, arguing against Egyptian origins in favor of Hebrew roots, specifically challenging the popular Greco-Egyptian derivation of Moses' name.</summary>
<keywords>Gad, Abrech, Moses, etymology, Hebrew language, Egyptian language, Athanasius Kircher, philology, Genesis, Exodus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philological analysis of Hebrew biblical terms that may have Egyptian, Arabic, or Persian origins. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing the name of Moses and the title "Abrech."</meta>

55

...refers to what had been imposed for some time by the mother. The sense, therefore, is: *“And the mother had called the boy's name Moses, from which she said: ‘Behold, Lady, the boy entrusted to me, whom I have called Drawn Out <note>original: "Extractum"</note> because of a peculiar event, because you indeed drew him out of the waters.’”* Compare, following the commentary of Nachmanides, Isaac Abravanel <note>Isaac Abravanel (1437–1508) was a celebrated Jewish statesman, philosopher, and Bible commentator.</note> *on this passage.*

V. **Sha’atnez** <note>Hebrew: שעטנז; a biblical prohibition against wearing "mixed" fabric of wool and linen</note> (*Leviticus 19:19*). That it is Egyptian, or at least its beginning suggests so, for <term>out</term> <note>original Coptic: ουτ</note> is “spun wool” to the Coptic people, especially “cotton” <note>original Latin: "Gossipinum"</note> (Arabic: *al-ghazal* <note>original: אלעזל</note>). See Scaliger’s *Major Work, page 122.* Whether the other component part could be from <term>sha’ash</term> or <term>sha’asha’</term> <note>Hebrew: שעש or שעשע</note> meaning “silk” (Scaliger’s *Major Work, page 121 and Selections, page 427*), let this be the judgment of the learned.

VI. **Manna** <note>Hebrew: מן</note> (*Exodus 16:31*). Hizkuni, Drusius, Meyer, Grotius, and others have said it is the Egyptian pronoun for “What?” and Castell asserts the same for the Copts in his *Observations on the Samaritan Pentateuch* on this passage, which we do not believe. Nor do we like what others seek from Syriac influence. Without prejudice, we say it is Hebrew, fashioned from *mah* [what] and *nu* or *an*. Thus we see similar formations in words like *mahem*, *malkam*, *moreh*, etc. The sense requires this.

VII. **Rahab** <note>Hebrew: רהב</note> (*Psalm 87:4 and Isaiah 30:7*). This seems to be the same as the Egyptian *Rib* or *Riph*, by which name the **Delta**, or the triangular part of Egypt enclosed by the mouths of the Nile, is called because of its pear-like shape. See Bochart, *Sacred Geography, part 1, page 66.*

VIII. **Sohar** <note>Hebrew: סהר; used for the prison where Joseph was kept</note> (*Genesis 39:20*). Bochart, following Ibn Ezra, wants this to be Egyptian because of the added explanation in the text. But most derive the word, not incorrectly, from “roundness” and interpret it as a tower, although Hottinger (*Oriental Smegma, page 178*) prefers it to denote a “guard” or “watch” from the Arabic *shahara* [to keep watch].

IX. **Chartummim** <note>Hebrew: חרטמים; typically translated as magicians or diviners</note> (*Genesis 41:8 and in Daniel, etc.*). Meyer makes this word Egyptian without foundation. Hottinger makes it Persian from *khardan* [to know]. But it seems to be Hebrew, from <term>cheret</term> <note>Hebrew: חרט; an engraving tool or stylus</note>, because these **Magi** <note>Originally a Persian priestly class, here used for Egyptian wise men</note> performed most of their tasks with written characters.

X. The names of the Egyptian magicians <term>Jannes and Jambres</term> <note>original Greek: ἰαννῆς & ἰαμβρῆς</note> (*2 Timothy 3:8*).

<summary>This page analyzes the etymological origins of several biblical Hebrew terms (Sha’atnez, Manna, Rahab, Sohar, and Chartummim), debating whether they are truly Egyptian loanwords or derive from Hebrew roots. The author relies on 17th-century philologists like Bochart and Scaliger to navigate these linguistic connections.</summary>

<keywords>Sha'atnez, Manna, Rahab, Sohar, Chartummim, Jannes and Jambres, Egyptian etymology, Hebrew philology, Coptic language, Bochart, Scaliger</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the origins of Biblical Hebrew words, moving from Egyptian (Coptic) influences into Persian and Arabic derivations.</meta>

56

Regarding 1 Timothy 3:8, Buxtorf <note>Johann Buxtorf (1564–1629) was a celebrated Hebraist and author of the *Lexicon Chaldaicum, Talmudicum et Rabbinicum*</note> derives the term in vain from *John* and *Ambrose* <note>Referring to the Church Fathers John Chrysostom and Ambrose of Milan</note> with others in his *Talmudic Lexicon, column 946*. One might compare the Coptic *Gjave* <note>original: "Gjave"</note> meaning delicate, pleasant, or delightful (Scaliger’s *Selections, page 300* <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was a Dutch scholar of historical chronology</note>), or *Gjavi* <note>original: "Gjavi"</note> meaning clamor (*page 347*), and *maune* <note>original: "μαυνη"</note> meaning breast or lap (where the *p* is the article, *page 405*). Or, if the reading *maubes* <note>original: "μαυβης"</note> is more pleasing, it may come from *mene* <note>original: "μενε"</note> meaning "to love" (*page 298*), or *mbe* <note>original: "μβε"</note> if it refers to new wine (*page 414*).

XI. There are many words in the sacred text that must be explained through Persian influence <note>original: "Persismo"</note>. For example: **Achashteranim** <note>Hebrew: אחשתרנים</note> (*Esther 8:10*). These are "great mules," derived from **achash** <note>Hebrew: אחש</note> meaning "great" and **shatar** <note>Hebrew: שתר</note> or **ashtar** <note>Hebrew: אשתר</note> meaning "mule" (*Rosarium Musulmanum 305; Tawa’us on Genesis 6:24*). It does not come from **shatur** <note>Hebrew: שתור</note> meaning "camel," because the phrase **sons of mares** <note>Hebrew: בני רמכים</note> follows it. See Bochart <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667) was a French Protestant scholar and author of *Hierozoicon*, a study of biblical animals</note> in *Hierozoicon, Part 1, book 2, chapter 20, page 236*.

**Pur** <note>Hebrew: פור</note> (*Esther 3:7, etc.*), that is, **goral** <note>Hebrew: גורל</note> meaning "fate" or "a portion happening by chance," matches the word **pareh** <note>original: "פארה"</note> meaning "part," although "fate" is more commonly called **par'ah** <note>original: "פרעה"</note> (see both in *History of Christ, page 479*).

**Nishtevan** <note>Hebrew: נשתון</note> (*Ezra 4:7, 18*), meaning a "decree" or "official letter," from **nushten** <note>Persian: נושתן</note> or **nevishten** <note>Persian: נוישתן</note> meaning "to write," from which comes **nushteh** <note>Persian: נושתה</note> meaning "scripture" (*Greaves’ Elements, page 18; De Dieu’s Rudiments, page 2; History of Christ 415; Rosarium Musulmanum 214*).

**Achashdarpanim** <note>Hebrew: אחשדרפנים; usually translated as "satraps"</note> (*Esther 3:9, Daniel 3:2, Ezra 8:36*). This comes from **achash** <note>Persian: אחש</note> meaning "great" and **darban** <note>Persian: דרבאן</note> meaning "doorkeeper," as if you were to say "Prefects of the Royal Gate." In Turkish, this is **Kapigi bassa** <note>Modern Turkish: Kapıcıbaşı, the "Chief Gatekeeper" or a high palace official</note>.

**Databraya** <note>Aramaic: דתבריא</note> (*Daniel 3*), as if it were **dad-var-ha** from **dadan** <note>Persian: דאדן</note> meaning "to give" and the little word **var**, meaning "Dispensers" <note>Judges or law-officers</note>. Elsewhere, it is not unlike the Turkish **Difternar** <note>Modern Turkish: Defterdar, the head of the financial treasury</note>, the Prefect of the Treasury, from **daftar** <note>meaning "ledger" or "register"</note>, from which **datbar** is formed by a slight rearrangement of letters.

**Gizbar** <note>Hebrew: גזבר</note> (*Ezra 1:8*), meaning "Treasurer," from the Persian **Kensj** <note>Persian: גנז</note> meaning "treasure" (*Rosarium Musulmanum 450; History of Christ 63*) and **var**, from which also comes the name **Caspar**.

XII. **Pardes** <note>Hebrew: פרדס; meaning an orchard, park, or "paradise"</note> (*Song of Songs 4:13, Nehemiah 2:5, Ecclesiastes 2:8*). This is indeed in use among the Persians, and it could have passed from them directly to the Hebrews. However, during the time of Solomon, it could have come from the Arabs; yet it is not Persian in origin (to whom a "garden" in general is called **bagh** <note>Persian: באג</note>, from which **bagh-ban** is "Gardener"; specifically **bushtan** <note>Persian: בושתאן</note> is a "Rose garden," and **miutan** <note>Persian: מיותאן</note> an "Orchard" or "fruit-garden," etc.). Rather, it is Arabic, from **fardasa** <note>Arabic: פרדש</note>, meaning "to spread on the ground" or "to fix in place," from which comes any garden that is planted or fortified with driven stakes.

<summary>This page provides a philological analysis of Hebrew biblical terms with Persian, Arabic, and Coptic origins, specifically focusing on administrative titles like "satrap" and "treasurer," and the term "pardes" (paradise).</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Persianisms, Satraps, Achashteranim, Purim, Nishtevan, Gizbar, Pardes, Coptic language, Turkish administration</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Biblical terms, specifically focusing on problematic or debated Hebrew phrases in the books of Genesis and Numbers. The previous page discussed Coptic and Persian influences; this page moves into specific exegetical debates regarding the "breeze of the day," "mandrakes," and the "Book of the Wars of the Lord."</meta>

57

### Other Supplements <note>original: "Επισάγματα" (Episagmata) — Greek for additions or supplementary burdens.</note>

->I.<-

The phrase **at the breeze of the day** <note>original: "לרוח היום" (L'ruach hayom)</note> in *Genesis 3:8* signifies neither a wind blowing divine voices into the ears of our First Parents <note>original: "Protoplastorum" — a term for Adam and Eve</note> (see the works of Junius and Piscator <note>Francis Junius (1545–1602) and Johannes Piscator (1546–1625) were influential Reformed theologians</note>), nor the sound of a wind stirred up as a sign of the divine presence (see Mercer and others <note>Jean Mercier was a 16th-century French Hebraist</note>), nor a place suited for catching a breeze (see Fuller’s *Miscellanea*, book 3, chapter 5), nor the direction from which God arrived (according to Nicholas of Lyra <note>A 14th-century Franciscan whose literal commentaries were highly influential</note>), nor the morning time (see Calvin and Rivet <note>André Rivet (1572–1651) was a French Huguenot theologian</note>). Rather, it refers to the time nearest the evening, when the day is cooled by the blowing West Wind <note>original: "Zephyro"</note>, as is evident from the related phrase in *Song of Solomon 2:17* and the opposite description in *Genesis 18:1* <note>Genesis 18:1 refers to the "heat of the day," the opposite of this cooling breeze</note>.

->II.<-

The **Dudaim** <note>Hebrew: דודאים</note> mentioned in *Genesis 30:14* were not mandrakes <note>A root historically associated with fertility and superstition</note> (despite the opinions of the Septuagint <note>The Greek Old Testament</note>, the Vulgate, and the Syriac and Arabic versions; as well as Cornelius à Lapide, Bonfrère, and other Catholic commentators; and Fuller, Lemnius in his *Herbal of the Bible*, Stuckius in *Ancient Banquets*, and Ursinus). Nor were they melons, or lilies, or jasmine, or violets, or the "Musa" of Matthioli <note>A type of plantain or banana described by the botanist Pietro Andrea Mattioli</note>, or the "Pala" of Clusius <note>A plant described by the pioneering botanist Carolus Clusius</note>, and so on. They were, in fact, any pleasant and lovely flowers in general, a gathered bunch of which Reuben seems to have brought home. This is clear because: 1. of the general etymology of the word; 2. its use in *Song of Solomon 7:13*; 3. its plural number; and 4. the fact that it is unlikely that the boy Reuben would have noticed anything beyond the beauty and variety of their color and scent.

->IV.<-

Regarding the citations from the **Book of the Wars of the Lord** <note>A lost non-canonical book mentioned in the Hebrew Bible</note> in *Numbers 21:14-15*, there is an amazing heat <note>i.e., intense debate</note> among the interpreters. The true sense seems to be this: *He took Vabeb in Supha* (understanding "someone" as the subject who occupied or attacked it) *and the torrents at Arnon* (Compare *Deuteronomy 2:24, Joshua 12:1, and Judges 11:13*) *and the bed* (or the flow) *of those torrents of Arnon is that which extends* (or turns, diverges, or inclines) *as far as the seat* (or dwelling) *of Ar (compare Deuteronomy 2:19) and rests on the border...*

<summary>This page provides philological corrections to common interpretations of Biblical Hebrew phrases, arguing that "the breeze of the day" refers to evening and "Dudaim" refers to generic flowers rather than mandrakes. It also attempts to clarify a difficult geographical passage in Numbers 21 cited from the lost Book of the Wars of the Lord.</summary>
<keywords>Genesis, Numbers, Dudaim, mandrakes, breeze of the day, Arnon, Book of the Wars of the Lord, biblical botany, philology, Reuben</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a series of philological and exegetical notes on Hebrew terms in the Old Testament. It concludes a discussion on the geography of Moab from the previous page and then addresses specific debates regarding Rahab's profession, Samson's labor in prison, and the description of Goliath as a "champion."</meta>

58

...from Moab. Therefore, it is a shortened testimony of Moses (though sufficient for his present purpose) drawn from a certain well-known Chronicle, declaring the location of the Arnon and the borders of the Moabites.

### IV.

The name **zonah** <note>Hebrew: זונה — usually translated as "prostitute" or "harlot"</note> used regarding **Rahab** in *Joshua 2:1*, denotes not an innkeeper <note>The author is refuting an interpretation intended to "cleanse" Rahab's reputation by suggesting she only ran a hostel</note>, but a prostitute. This is 1. because of its consistent usage in Holy Scripture, and 2. its explanation in the New Testament by the name **porne** <note>Greek: πορνὴ — meaning a woman for hire or prostitute</note> in *Matthew 1:5*, *Hebrews 11:31*, and *James 2:25*. (Compare the commentaries of Serarius, Masius, and Cornelius à Lapide <note>Prominent Jesuit and Catholic scholars of the 16th and 17th centuries</note> on this passage).

### V.

The **grinding** <note>original: "Molitura"</note> performed by **Samson** in *Judges 16:21* does not refer to sexual relations with the wives of the **Philistines** (as some suggest the word *tachan* <note>Hebrew: טחן — to grind; sometimes used as a euphemism for the sexual act</note> is used in *Job 31:10*), but by synecdoche <note>A figure of speech where a part represents the whole, or an action represents a condition</note> it denotes his servile condition and forced labor in prison; for this reason, he was also blinded. (Compare Cornelius à Lapide and others; Johann Conrad Dieterich’s *Biblical Antiquities*, Part 2, p. 48; Sixtinus Amama’s *Antibabilonia Biblica*; and Hakhspan’s *Biblical Notes*, Part 1, p. 630).

### VI.

**Goliath** is called a **man of the between-space** <note>Hebrew: איש הבינים (*ish habbenayim*), traditionally translated as "champion"</note> in *1 Samuel 17:4*, not because he was a <unclear>bastard</unclear> <note>Hebrew: ממזר (*mamzer*)</note> as is stated in the *Sotah*, folio 42 <note>A tractate of the Talmud</note>, or a "spurious son" conceived between two fathers (according to the Vulgate, Cornelius à Lapide, and others), nor because he was stationed between two camps (as most **Hebrews** suggest), nor because of his giant mass, but as if he were a mediator and a duelist <note>original: "duellio"</note> who had come to settle the fight through single combat <note>original: "monomachia"</note>.

For if the term merely implied he was stationed between two camps, why is he specifically said to have "gone out from the ranks of the Philistines"? (Compare verse 23, as well as verses 8, 9, and 10).

# DISSERTATION On SHILOH, SON OF JUDAH,

<sig>H 2</sig>

<meta>catchword: Do-</meta>

### Genesis 49:10.

->§ 1.<-

<margin>
**Respondent:**
**Abraham**
**Hinckel-**
**mann, of**
**Döbeln in**
**Meissen**
</margin>
The blindness <note>Greek: *aorasia* — a state of being unable to see, often used in a spiritual sense</note> of the people of Sodom was stupendous; it so demented them that they did not perceive what was placed right before their eyes in broad daylight. The matter would be less wonderful if they had been physically blinded or deprived of their eyes; however, Augustine <note>In *The City of God*, book 22, chapter 19</note> judged that, in such a state, *they would have sought a guide rather than the door.* Yet, while otherwise possessed of sight, they wandered about for a long time like drunken men, and though exhausted by looking, they did not perceive what ought to have been felt with their very hands. Furthermore, milk is nowhere more similar to milk than these "Judaeasters" <note>original: *Judæastri* — a derogatory suffix implying "false" or "base" Jews</note> are to those of Sodom; the most elegant prophet **Isaiah** did not hesitate to call their ancestors the "Rulers of Sodom" <note>Hebrew: *Ketzinay Sedom*</note> at the very beginning of his book. For these men also "seeing they see, but do not understand" <note>Isaiah 6:9; Hebrew: *Ra’u ra’u v’lo yed’u*</note>; in this, they are worse than the Sodomites themselves because they close their eyes, and thus of their own accord and willingly they turn their day into night and their splendor into darkness. Of them, one might say without injustice *that they are dim in a faint light, purblind in a moderate light, blind in a greater light, and insane in the greatest light.*

§ 2. A proof of this can be found in that most illustrious Old Testament oracle concerning the coming of **Shiloh** in Genesis 49:10, delivered by the divinely inspired patriarch **Jacob** as he neared death. This prophecy shines among others "as much more splendidly as the Evening Star <note>original: *Hesperus*</note> shines than the other stars, and as much as the golden Moon <note>original: *Phoebe*</note> shines more than the evening." While this strikes the eyes of all who behold it, the Jews alone suffer from a darkening of vision <note>original: *scotomate* — a medical term for a blind spot in the visual field</note>; indeed, they deny seeing what they see most clearly. Because they cannot bear the light, they conceive various phantasms in their minds, with which they wretchedly deceive their own foolish common people. Meanwhile, you may often see them fighting with their adversaries...

<summary>This page introduces a scholarly defense of the Messianic prophecy in Genesis 49:10, comparing Jewish rejection of the "Shiloh" prophecy to the spiritual blindness of the people of Sodom. The author characterizes the passage as one of the most radiant oracles of the Old Testament.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Judah, Genesis 49:10, blindness, Sodom, Isaiah, Jacob, Messianic prophecy, Abraham Hinckelmann</keywords>
