By divine appointment: <note>original: *Theia epithesei*</note> namely, according to that saying: *The Cadmean brothers engage in battle among themselves.* <note>The "Cadmean brothers" refers to Eteocles and Polynices, sons of Oedipus, who killed each other; it is used here as a metaphor for self-destructive internal conflict among interpreters.</note>

->§ 3.<-

Furthermore, if my mind were set on either reviewing or examining all the delusions with which his degenerate descendants corrupt the decree of the Divine Elder <note>Jacob, who delivers the prophecy in Genesis 49</note>, we would be able to compose not only a proper discourse but massive commentaries. For here the harvest of errors is so abundant that that **Homeric** line comes to mind: *What shall I recount first, what next, and what last?* <note>Greek: *ti proton, ti d' epeita, ti d' hystation katalexo*</note> Others have already taken up this task before us, among whom we name the most prominent: Raymundus Martinus in *The Dagger of Faith*, part 2, chapter 4; Petrus Galatinus in *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth*, book 4, chapters 4 through 8; **Blessed** Luther in volume 8 of the Jena German edition, ***On the Jews and Their Lies***; Paulus Fagius in his *Commentary on the Paraphrase of Onkelos* at this passage; Dr. Sal. Gesner on this passage, page 961 and following; Dr. Jacob Martini in *On the Three Elohim*, book 3, chapters 5 through 10; Dr. W. Franzius in *School of Sacrifices*, Disputations 11 and 12; Dr. Helvicus in the *Giessen Disputations*, volume 4, Disputation 12, from page 298 to 314; Dr. Daniel Cramer in *Prophetic Schools*, Class 2, Prophecy 1 regarding Shiloh; Dr. Michael Walther in *Biblical Exercises on the Prophecy of Jacob*, chapter 6, from page 97 and following; Dr. Johannes Müller in *Judaism*, part 1, chapter 8, page 182 and following; Michael Havemann in ***The Way-Light***, part 1, chapters 4, 5, and 6, and page 275; Andreas Rivetus in *Exercises on Genesis*, 178 and 179; Johannes Hoornbeek in *The Answer of Judah*, page 131 and following; and Jacob Alting in *On Shiloh*, books 1, 4, and 5.

->§ 4.<-

After so many Rosciuses <note>Quintus Roscius Gallus was the most famous actor of ancient Rome; the author uses the name to refer to the great scholars who have already "performed" on this subject</note>, although it may not seem necessary to step onto the stage, nor is it our primary purpose to refute the Jews professionally, it is nevertheless pleasing to illuminate for a little while the machinations of the "Apellas" <note>A name used by the Roman poet Horace to mockingly refer to Jews; the author uses it here to mean Jewish polemicists</note> against heavenly truth, so that by this means we may know that *truth is one, but falsehood is many-branched.* <note>Greek: *to alethes hen, to de pseudos polyschides einai*</note> This certainly was not the intention of Menasse ben Israel, who, when he had recited eleven opinions on this passage in his *Conciliator* <note>A famous work by the rabbi Menasse ben Israel (1604–1657) which sought to reconcile apparent contradictions in the Hebrew Bible</note>—specifically in question 65, which is the last one on Genesis—finally

<summary>The author lists numerous Christian theologians who have defended the Messianic interpretation of Genesis 49:10 against Jewish "delusions" and announces his intent to briefly examine these Jewish arguments to show that truth is singular while error is multifaceted.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob's prophecy, Shiloh, anti-Jewish polemic, Menasse ben Israel, Raymundus Martinus, Martin Luther, Biblical commentary, truth vs. falsehood</keywords>

H 3

prophecy, he subjoins: however it may be, whether you embrace this interpretation or any of the others, the difficulty is removed. But we shall "count the cooked entrails." <note>original Latin: *cocta numerabimus exta*. This is a classical idiom suggesting that the time for preparation or speculation is over and it is time to examine the final results or the core facts.</note>

->§ 5.<-

However, there are two words above all others in this prophecy that the Jews find troublesome: **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: *shevet* (שבט), usually translated as "scepter" or "staff of office"</note> and **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: *shiloh* (שילה), a title interpreted as referring to the Messiah</note>. And first, in the *Genesis Rabbah* <note>a collection of ancient Jewish homilies and interpretations on the book of Genesis</note>, they explain **Shevet** as a *staff*, and metaphorically as a *support* or *help*. They link this to *Machir*, the son of Ammiel, who is said to have aided David while he was fleeing from his son Absalom. Nevertheless, Menasseh <note>referring to Manasseh ben Israel, a prominent 17th-century Dutch rabbi and scholar</note> applies it to all Jews, to whom Jacob predicts help will not be lacking from somewhere until the Messiah comes to punish the nations. But the first interpretation is far too foolish, since here nothing can be rightly referred to Machir individually; hence Menasseh had to smooth it over. Yet even so, the lie shines through. For, to pass over other matters, from what nation, I ask, did help come to the Jews against Titus Vespasian and the Romans? If help certainly ceased at that time, then the Messiah must have already come.

->§ 6.<-

But in the same place, Menasseh "shakes another oak." <note>a Latin idiom meaning to try a different approach or argument</note> For he says that **Shevet** can be explained as the *judge’s rod*, and thus as the <term>Sanhedrin</term> <note>the supreme legal and religious council of ancient Israel</note>, which it is established was always of David's lineage. Let us grant that. But the Sanhedrin has ceased. Therefore, the Messiah has come. Meanwhile, let Menasseh feel the judge’s rod himself, because in this distortion <note>original Greek: *strebilōtēti*</note> of his, he has completely ignored or concealed these words: **until Shiloh comes** <note>original Hebrew: *ad ki yavo shiloh* (עד כי yבא שילה)</note>.

->§ 7.<-

But **Shevet**, if one believes the Jews <note>original Latin: *verpis*, a derogatory term for the circumcised</note>, can also be taken as the *rod of punishment*, as in Psalm 2:9, and **Mechokek** <note>original Hebrew: *mechokek* (מחוקק), usually "lawgiver" or "ruler's staff"</note> as the *punisher*; they suggest this to imply that afflictions will not leave the Jews until the coming of the Messiah. That fabrication appealed to Rabbi Lipmann in the *Nizzachon* <note>The *Sefer Nizzachon* (Book of Victory), a famous medieval Jewish polemical text</note>, number 46, and to Rabbi Joel ben Sueb. But Dr. Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer, a 17th-century Lutheran theologian</note> rightly says in *The Christian Way* <note>original Latin title: *Hodosophia Christiana*</note>, page 831: "When our circumcised Jews seek a refuge in this place from the rod of punishment, if [they embrace] that..."

<summary>This page analyzes Jewish interpretations of Genesis 49:10, specifically the Hebrew words "Shevet" and "Shiloh." The author argues against the views of Menasseh ben Israel and others, claiming that if the "scepter" or "staff" represents Jewish autonomy or the Sanhedrin, its disappearance proves the Messiah has already come.</summary>

<keywords>Genesis 49:10, Shiloh, Shevet, Mechokek, Menasseh ben Israel, Sanhedrin, Messiah, Jewish-Christian polemics, Genesis Rabbah, Dannhauer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the interpretation of the word "Shevet" (scepter) in Jacob's blessing of Judah. It follows the previous page’s refutation of Menasseh ben Israel's claim that "Shevet" refers only to a "rod" of support or punishment rather than royal authority.</meta>

*...they understand it in the way that the following interpretation deserves; they should be heard, but all others are liars.* Against him stand his own well-equipped <note>original: *ceucaphracti*, likely a variation of *cataphracti*, referring to heavily armed soldiers, here used metaphorically for scholars well-armed with arguments</note> kinsmen, both the ancients: the three Targumists <note>translators of the Hebrew Bible into Aramaic</note>, the authors of the Talmud <note>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism</note> in *Sanhedrin chapter 1, folio 8 and Horayot chapter 3, folio 11*; and also the more recent ones: Raschi <note>Rashi, the preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note>, Aben Esra, Kimchi, Rabbi Bechai, Rabbi Abraham the Spaniard. Also the author of Haskuni, Tanchuma, Rabbi Bechai, Rabbi Moses Nachmanides, Rabbi Isaac Arama and others: Menasseh could not have been ignorant that these men take **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט, meaning scepter or staff of office</note> to mean royal dominion and authority, and therefore he ought not to have polished up that fiction about a "rod." But Jacob himself, as if with a **clear voice** <note>original Greek: λευκάδι φωνῇ</note>, protests that he portends for <term>Judah</term> <note>the fourth son of Jacob and the ancestor of the tribe from which the Davidic kings and the Messiah descend</note> not rods and whips, but every sort of distinguished and excellent thing, for which he is to be carried to the heavens with praises by the rest. Nor was the "rod" unique to Judah, but was common to all the tribes—indeed, it belonged to others before him. But [royal power] also often slackened, or rather was removed entirely, while the tribe of Judah flourished with others according to their desire, especially after the beginning of the Davidic kingdom. Finally, **Shevet** is used somewhere in the Holy Scriptures to express power, but certainly nowhere to express misery.

§. 8. Furthermore, Rabbi Moses Albelda in *Olat Tamid on this passage, folio 105, column 3*, thinks that a **coercive rod** <note>original Hebrew: שבט הנוגש (*shevet ha-nogesh*), literally the "rod of the taskmaster"</note> is hinted at, namely, one by which it is necessary for the Jews to compel the other nations and prescribe laws for them; but he claims that this will cease in the time of the Messiah, and the nations will obey of their own accord, moved solely by the excellence of the Messiah. But where are the nations to whom the Jews have dictated laws and rights within the last nearly fifteen hundred years? Whom have they restrained with punishments? They are found nowhere, **neither among men on land, nor in hollow ships**, as it is written in Pindar <note>referring to a quote from the Greek poet Pindar suggesting total absence</note>. But if that **lawgiving** <note>original Greek: νομοθετικὴ</note> and nation-taming force has ceased, it follows that the Messiah has already come.

§. 9. Don Isaac Abravanel <note>a Portuguese Jewish statesman, philosopher, and Bible commentator</note> in his *Commentary on the Pentateuch, folio 121*, takes **Shevet** to mean **an appointment of high rank, leadership, and dominion over the rest of his brothers** <note>original Hebrew: מנוי מעלה והנהגה ושררה על יתר אחיו</note> through whatever prerogative and **eminence** <note>original Greek: ἐξοχῇ</note> of Judah and his descendants p-

<summary>The author refutes Jewish interpretations that define the "scepter" (Shevet) as a mere rod of punishment or support, citing numerous Jewish authorities who instead associate it with royal authority. He argues that if the Jews' legislative and coercive power over other nations has vanished, it proves the Messiah has already arrived.</summary>

<keywords>Shevet, scepter, Judah, Genesis 49:10, Menasseh ben Israel, Rashi, Nachmanides, Abravanel, Messiah, Jewish interpretation, royal authority</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic and theological debate regarding Genesis 49:10 ("The scepter shall not depart from Judah"). The previous page ended with the catchword "prae-," which completes the first word of this page, "praecæteris" (above the others).</meta>

63

...above the others; [he asserts] that it has been constantly preserved since that time, and that the leaders of the French and Spanish synagogues from the tribe of Judah maintain its honor even in exile itself. Certainly, Hugo of St. Victor <note>a 12th-century Saxon philosopher and theologian</note> and John Calvin explained the word **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט, meaning scepter or staff of office</note> in the same sense in this place <note>original Latin: h. l. (*hoc loco*)</note>, though they differ in other matters. There is no doubt that **pre-eminence** <note>original Greek: ἐξοχὴν</note> is indicated here, but not just any kind—rather, a political one accompanied by jurisdiction, of which the Jewish school leaders certainly do not possess even a shadow. And what **pre-eminence** <note>original Greek: ἐξοχὴ</note> could they have over their fellow tribesmen? For those [leaders], nothing is certain concerning the ten tribes led away by Shalmaneser <note>the Assyrian king who conquered the northern Kingdom of Israel in the 8th century BCE</note>; indeed, since all genealogical records were burnt long ago, they themselves cannot explain to which tribe they belong. **Abravanel** <note>Isaac Abravanel, a prominent 15th-century Jewish statesman and commentator</note> indeed boasts of a Davidic lineage, but most vainly. It would be a matter of destiny for such **Thrasos** <note>a reference to "Thraso," a character of a boastful, self-deluded soldier in Roman comedy</note> if they were forced to show that they are not the illegitimate offspring of other nations, let alone the **descendants** <note>original Greek: ἀπογόνους</note> of David. Compare Alting, *On Shiloh*, book 1, chapters 12 and 13.

§. 10. **Menasseh** <note>Menasseh ben Israel, a leading 17th-century Portuguese rabbi in Amsterdam</note> in the place cited, page 92, leans heavily toward the idea that **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט</note> denotes a "tribe," as has also pleased some Christian scholars. Furthermore, according to Menasseh, the sense is that although the ten tribes are led away, the tribe of Judah will never be removed from human knowledge but will have its own teachers until the Messiah. But **Shevet** cannot be explained as "tribe" without creating a **tautology** <note>original Greek: ταυτολογίαν; a redundant repetition of the same idea</note>, if not an **absurdity** <note>original Greek: ἀτοπίαν</note>. And it is false that today the tribe of Judah can be distinguished from the Benjaminites and Levites, who experienced the same fate. With the tribes certainly confused, and the family records broken and scattered, none of the **Apellas** <note>a derogatory Roman nickname for Jews, implying they were gullible or "cut off" (circumcised)</note> has been able to show for sixteen centuries now which tribe he is from.

§ 11. Someone in the presence of Dr. Müller once explained **Shevet** as "law," based on Psalm 23:4, as reported in *Judaism*, page 229. But even if we do not mention that "law" is nowhere in the **Holy Scriptures** <note>original Latin: SSa (*Sacra Scriptura*)</note> called **Shevet** in an absolute sense, what, I ask, would the tribe of Judah have received here more than the others,

<vocab>Synagoga, tribus Juda, Salmanassar, Abravanel, Menasseh, Messias, genealogy, Altingius, Müllerus</vocab>

<summary>This page refutes the arguments of Jewish scholars like Abravanel and Menasseh ben Israel who claim that the 'scepter' (Shevet) of Judah refers to tribal identity or religious leadership rather than royal power. The author argues that since genealogical records are lost, no modern Jew can prove their tribal descent, rendering these interpretations invalid.</summary>

<keywords>Shevet, Tribe of Judah, Genealogy, Shalmaneser, Abravanel, Menasseh ben Israel, Messianic Prophecy, Scepter, Genesis 49:10</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of various interpretations regarding the timing and nature of the "scepter" of Judah mentioned in Genesis 49:10. The author is refuting Jewish and earlier scholarly views that equate "Shiloh" with figures like Moses or David.</meta>

64

...especially over the *Levites*, who teach the law? And why was it necessary to add this: "until *Shiloh* comes," unless perhaps that obscure little man <note>original Latin: *homuncio*, a derogatory term for a person of little importance</note> intended that after his arrival, the law would be taken away from Judah?

**§ 12.** But let these remarks regarding the **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט, meaning scepter or rod of office</note> suffice. Let us see now what defenses a bad cause might seek in the name **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note>. Some claim that it refers to *Moses*, both because of the passage in *Deuteronomy 33:5* (which is irrelevant <note>original Greek: ἀλλοτριον</note> here), and because of the deceptive numerical equivalence <note>original Greek: ὁμοσψηφίαν, referring to the practice of Gematria where words with the same numerical value are linked</note> of the letters in **Shiloh** and **Moses**. Thus argue the *Baal HaTurim* <note>Jacob ben Asher, a 13th-century rabbinic authority</note> on this passage, *Rabbi Bahya* <note>Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century Spanish commentator</note> in his commentary on the Pentateuch, leaf 59, and from him the author of the *Tzenah Urenah* <note>a popular Yiddish prose work for women, first published in the late 16th century</note>, a marvelous craftsman in sewing together fables, leaf 63, page 2. And in the *Tikkunei Zohar* <note>a mystical appendix to the Zohar, the primary text of Jewish Kabbalah</note>, Tikkun 21, leaf 52, page 2 (as Rittangel <note>Johann Stephan Rittangel, a 17th-century professor and convert from Judaism who translated Hebrew texts</note> reports in his work on the *Sefer Yetzirah* <note>The "Book of Formation," one of the oldest Jewish mystical texts</note>, pages 132–133), Moses is greeted as *Shiloh* from this oracle. Finally, to *Menasseh* <note>Menasseh ben Israel, the 17th-century Amsterdam rabbi and diplomat</note> it seems very likely that the rule taken from Judah was transferred to Moses, who was from the tribe of Levi. [He suggests] rule was transferred to Moses—rule which had not even existed, while the entire people groaned under the harshest yoke of slavery. And the **pre-eminence** <note>original Greek: ἐξοχήν</note> which the tribe of Judah held before Moses, it retained after him. Such trifles are tiresome.

**§ 13.** Others prefer that **Shiloh** be *David*, in this sense: *The **great scepter** <note>original Hebrew: שבט גדול</note>, the scepter of majesty, shall not depart from Judah until David comes.* Thus argue *Ibn Ezra* <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a 12th-century Spanish scholar</note> and *Rabbi Solomon ben Melech* in the *Mikhlal Yofhi* <note>a 16th-century linguistic commentary on the Hebrew Bible</note> at this passage, leaf 15, page 2. But they place the end of the prophecy right where its beginning is, as *Mercer* <note>Jean Mercier, a prominent 16th-century French Hebraist</note> rightly observes on this passage, page 731. For David was the first king from the tribe of Judah. Before him, King Saul and the Judges—with one or two exceptions—belonged to other tribes. To be sure, there was a **pre-eminence** <note>original Greek: Εξοχὴ</note> of the tribe of Judah before then, because it preceded in rank and led the column in ceremonies and in the movement of the camps, and because it stood in the front line of battle, etc.; just as the **pre-eminence** <note>original Greek: ἐξοχὴ</note> of Joseph was not insignificant—namely, the right of a double portion (*1 Chronicles 5:1–2*); yet neither of these things constituted that **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט, meaning scepter</note> which is promised here to Judah.

<summary>This page analyzes and rejects the interpretations that identify the biblical "Shiloh" as Moses or David, arguing that these associations fail to account for the actual history of the tribe of Judah’s political power.</summary>
<keywords>Genesis 49:10, Shiloh, Judah, Moses, David, Scepter, Gematria, Rabbinic commentary, Menasseh ben Israel, Ibn Ezra</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s refutation of various Jewish interpretations of Genesis 49:10, specifically focusing on the meaning of the word "Shiloh." The previous page dismissed the idea that Shiloh refers to Moses or David.</meta>

65

I

**§. 14.** On the other hand, Rabbi Lipmann <note>referring to Yom-Tov Lipmann Mühlhausen, a 14th-century controversialist</note> in the place cited understands **Shiloh** to be the city well-known from the sacred scriptures. He suggests this implies a prerogative remains with Judah "until they come to **Shiloh**" <note>original Hebrew: עד שיבאו לשילה</note>, namely, to anoint Saul the Benjaminite. But Lipmann is talking nonsense. Indeed, the **Shevet** <note>original Hebrew: שבט, meaning scepter or tribal staff</note>, which the tribe of Judah did not possess beforehand, could not be lost once Saul was anointed. Furthermore, where do the Holy Scriptures ever say that Saul was anointed in Shiloh?

**§. 15.** **Rabbi Joseph** proceeded differently, according to the testimony of Pagninus in his *Thesaurus of the Holy Tongue* <note>Santes Pagnino, an influential 16th-century Hebraist</note>. He distorted the words **yasur** <note>original Hebrew: יסור, meaning "shall depart"</note> and **yavo** <note>original Hebrew: יבא, meaning "shall come"</note> into a different meaning, in this manner: The kingdom shall not be gathered to (or approach) Judah, until the Tabernacle of **Shiloh** shall **set** <note>original Hebrew: יבא, playing on the word's use for the setting of the sun</note>, that is, be destroyed; once it was destroyed, David was surely anointed. This distortion <note>original Greek: στρεβλότητος, meaning "twisting" or "perversity"</note> is mentioned by Rabbi Bahya, Abraham ibn Ezra, the author of the *Tzenah Urenah* <note>a popular Yiddish commentary</note> on this passage, and Menasseh ben Israel in the place cited. But **yasur** never signifies "to approach or be gathered to something," especially when constructed with the preposition **mi** <note>the Hebrew letter מ indicating "from"</note>, as Johannes Hoornbeek <note>a 17th-century Dutch theologian</note> rightly noted on page 141. And although **bo** <note>the root of yavo</note> may signify "to set" when referring to the sun, it never means "to be destroyed" when referring to a town. Furthermore, how does the phrase "and to him shall be the gathering of the peoples" <note>original Hebrew: ולו יקהת עמים</note> fit the city of Shiloh? Everywhere these interpretations are merely "loose broomsticks" <note>original Latin: *scopæ dissolutæ*, an idiom for disconnected or worthless arguments</note>. Compare D. Walther in his *Third Controversy*.

**§. 16.** However, Rabbi Hezekiah, the author of the **Hizkuni** <note>a 13th-century French rabbi and commentator</note>, does not want **Shiloh** to be the city itself, but rather a man originating from there—specifically **Ahijah** <note>original Latin: *Abiam*, likely referring to Ahijah the Shilonite</note>. He is the one who, having torn Jeroboam’s garment into twelve parts, returned ten to him. Wolfgang Franz <note>a Lutheran theologian</note> reports in his *Scholastic Sacrifice, Disputation 11, thesis 52, 92*, that a certain "Apelles" <note>a reference to a generic skeptic or opponent</note> objected to this same false interpretation. But what is this unbridled license? One moment **Shiloh** is the town of **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שלה, noting the variant spelling without the letter Jod</note> whenever it pleases them; the next, it becomes a man originating from the town; and finally, specifically **Ahijah**. Yet even if we forced the text to mean that this person is **Shiloh**, by what right is the phrase "to him" <note>the word "v'lo" in the Hebrew text</note> dragged to refer to Jeroboam? Clearly, everything is permitted to the Jews. "There is nothing inside the olive, and nothing hard on the outside of the nut" <note>original Latin: *Nil intra est oleam, nil extra est in nuce duri*, a quote from Horace meaning that logic has been completely inverted</note>.

<meta>catchword: §. 17.</meta>

<summary>This page critiques Jewish interpretations of "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10, refuting theories that link it to the city of Shiloh, the anointing of Saul, or the prophet Ahijah. The author argues these interpretations ignore the grammatical structure of the Hebrew text and historical facts.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Judah, Scepter, Rabbi Lipmann, Rabbi Joseph, Hizkuni, Ahijah, Jeroboam, Saul, biblical interpretation, polemic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of various Jewish and historical interpretations of the word "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10. Having previously addressed associations with Moses and David, the author now turns to theories involving Jeroboam, Nebuchadnezzar, and Solomon.</meta>

66

**§. 17.** Others, following **Rabbi Joseph** (cited by Havemann <note>Michael Havemann, a 17th-century German theologian and Hebraist</note> on page 124), prefer to understand **Shiloh** (שילה) as referring to **Jeroboam** himself, who was anointed at Shiloh. In this view, "Shiloh" is first taken as the name of the town, and then by extension for the man who was anointed in that town. This is quite an arbitrary decree. Meanwhile, the sacred page of Scripture records that Jeroboam was anointed not at **Shiloh**, but at **Shechem**.

**§. 18.** Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra, an influential 14th-century Franciscan commentator known for his attention to the literal Hebrew sense</note> reports in this place that others explain **Shiloh** (שילה) as referring to **Nebuchadnezzar**; however, he does not actually approve of this view. Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon, a renowned classical scholar</note> wrongly attributed this opinion to Lyranus in his *Commentary on Baronius* (Exercise 1, no. 2, p. 7), writing: *"Lyranus refers it to Nebuchadnezzar, which is truly absurd; but it seems the less-than-ideal Latin version misled this most learned man."* Matthias Martinius incautiously drew the same conclusion from Casaubon in his *Philosophical Lexicon* under the entry "Silo." In truth, it was a careless reading of Lyranus's own words that misled Casaubon. 

Furthermore, this opinion concerning Nebuchadnezzar was once favored by **Julian the Apostate** <note>The Roman Emperor Julian (r. 361–363), who rejected Christianity and attempted to restore Roman paganism</note>, as Cyril of Alexandria testifies in Book 8 of his writings. However, since neither the term **Shiloh** (שילה) nor the term **lawgiver** (מחקק <note>original Hebrew: *mechoqeq*</note>) can be applied to Nebuchadnezzar, and since Jacob predicts prosperity here for Judah, this interpretation is less pleasing even to the Jews today.

**§. 19.** Another fabrication remains from **Rabbi Nissim**, which Abravanel <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a prominent 15th-century Jewish philosopher and statesman</note> cites: that **Shiloh** (שילה) should be translated here as **"Error"**. The passage would then be read in this manner: *"until the Error comes,"* referring to the sin of Solomon, at which point the kingdom would be divided. But there is a great harvest of errors here. For **Shiloh** can by no right be translated as "error," much less specifically the "sin of Solomon"; nor—to pass over other points—could the "obedience of the peoples" mentioned in the verse ever fit such an error.

**§. 20.** We have seen what the Jews, driven by a spirit of confusion <note>original Latin: *spiritu vertiginis*, literally a "spirit of dizziness," often used in theological polemics to describe a state of being lost in error</note>, have attempted with the words **Shevet** (שבט <note>the scepter or tribal staff</note>) and **Shiloh** (שילה). Yet their audacity does not stop there. They also attack the small connecting words **ad ki** (עד כי <note>usually translated as "until"</note>) and create a massive gap between the two, so that the meaning becomes: *"The **Shevet** shall not depart from Judah **forever** (עד), **because** (כי) Shiloh will come."* This is the interpretation found in the *Bereshit Rabbah* <note>an ancient collection of Jewish homilies and explanations on the book of Genesis</note> at folio 99, and also in the works of Rabbi Bahya <note>Rabbi Bahya ben Asher, a 13th-century commentator</note> on this passage.

<summary>The author refutes several alternative interpretations of "Shiloh," dismissing links to Jeroboam, Nebuchadnezzar, or the "error" of Solomon, and criticizes Jewish scholars for reinterpreting the Hebrew grammar to avoid Christian messianic conclusions.</summary>

<sig>I 2</sig>

<margin>Book 1 and from there</margin>...an inept actor, the author of the *Ze'enah u-Re'enah* <note>The *Ze’enah u-Re’enah*, often called the "Women’s Bible," is a popular Yiddish prose commentary on the Torah by Jacob ben Isaac Ashkenazi, first published around 1616</note>, and others. These people must have been blind, because they did not see the **Athnach** <note>original: "Atnach"; a Hebrew cantillation mark that indicates the strongest pause in the middle of a verse, similar to a semicolon</note> pause before the little word **until** <note>original: "עד" (*ad*)</note>—which otherwise creates the greatest **interval** <note>original: "διάστημα" (*diastēma*)</note> within an entire period, except in the books of **Emet** <note>The "Books of Truth," an acronym for the poetic books of Job, Proverbs, and Psalms, which use a different system of musical accents</note>—or they were at least stupid for neglecting it. Indeed, the word **until** (unless a particle either actually precedes it or is repeated **in common** <note>original: "ἀπὸ κοινοῦ" (*apo koinou*), a rhetorical device where a word governs two clauses</note>) nowhere signifies "forever," much less when followed by the letter **Kaph** <note>original: "כ"</note>. (Compare *J. Hoornbeek* p. 133; *Havemann* p. 120; *Alting* p. 28; *Hackspan's Biblical Notes* p. 263; and the excellent *Dr. Wasmuth* in his *Vindication of the Hebrew*, Part 2, chapter 1, location 8; and others.) A strange itch for innovation led **Wilhelm Lange** <note>A 17th-century Danish mathematician and theologian</note> to adopt that Jewish gloss as his own—unprecedented among Christians—in his *Second Book on the Years of Christ*, chapter 10, page 425. For this, he was noted with a just censure by his most learned countryman, **Johannes Wandalin**, in his *Discourse on the Day of the Passion and the Three Days of Christ's Death*, page 140 and following. Furthermore, the **allies** <note>original: "σύμμαχοι" (*symmachoi*)</note> of the "Apella" <note>A classicized name for a Jew, derived from the Roman poet Horace's satires; used here to mock Lange for following Jewish interpretations</note> Lange hope for support in vain, whether from the **Yitiv** <note>original: "Itif"; a minor Hebrew musical accent</note> accent—which, compared to the **Athnach**, is considered as almost nothing—or from the paraphrase of **Onkelos** <note>The author of the official Aramaic translation of the Torah</note>, where the phrase "forever" <note>original: "עד עלמא" (*ad alma*)</note> is inserted. For, not to mention that this is absent from the **Royal versions** <note>Refers to the great Polyglot Bibles, such as the Antwerp or Paris Polyglots</note> (as **Le Clerc** has well noted), they ought to have noticed that Onkelos, by translating it as "until he comes" <note>original: "עד דייתי" (*ad deyeytey*)</note>, recognized the word **until** as a particle, and did not explain the word **until** by the words "forever," as **Matthias Martinius** correctly observes in the place cited.

**§. 21.** The rest of the Jewish **trifles** <note>original: "φλυαρίαι" (*phlyariai*)</note>, which they babble when improvidently reduced to a stalemate, do not deserve examination, much less do they withstand it. They soon object, along with **Rabbi Nachmanides** <note>Also known as Ramban (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note>, that kings or a kingdom are not promised until the arrival of the Messiah, but that whenever they should be present, they would be from the tribe of Judah by force of the prophecy; this assertion not only fights against the outcome of history, but is **diametrically** <note>original: "καδδιάμετρον" (*kaddiametron*)</note> contrary to the words of the holy old man [Jacob] himself. Soon they admit that the time-limit fixed for the arrival of the Messiah has passed, but that he is delayed by their sins: as if God would wish to lie because of the sins of the Jews, or as if His predictions were uncon-

<meta>catchword: ditio. (Completing the word "unconditional")</meta>

<summary>The author uses Hebrew grammar and cantillation marks (accents) to prove that the prophecy in Genesis 49:10 sets a specific time limit for the Messiah's coming. He refutes both Jewish interpretations and Christian scholars like Wilhelm Lange who argue that the text implies an eternal kingdom or a delay due to human sin.</summary>

[tra]ditioned <note>original: "ditionatas"; likely part of "traditionatas" (traditional) or "pactitionatas" (agreed upon)</note> things to change (compare Blessed Luther’s **On the Jews and Their Lies**, in the place cited, page 17). Soon they lie splendidly about certain Utopian places where the Jews might still hold power today—namely, beyond the Sabbatical River <note>The Sambation, a mythical river in Jewish legend that flows with stones and sand six days a week but stops on the Sabbath, supposedly hiding the Ten Lost Tribes</note>, on one bank of which Pygmies play, while on the other, swans about to die *warble sweet songs with failing tongues* <note>The author is quoting a classical poetic trope about the "swan song" to mock the absurdity of these legends</note>. Regarding this, he who desires to know more should consult Fagius *on Deuteronomy 31*; Galatinus *on the Secrets of Catholic Truth, book 11, chapter 9*; Fuller *Miscellany, book 1, chapter 9*; Buxtorf *Talmudic Lexicon, column 1417*; Cunaeus *on the Hebrew Republic, book 2, chapter 24*; Selden *on the Law of Nature and Nations, book 3, chapter 13*; L’Empereur *on the Itinerary of Benjamin, page 206*; Th. Spizelius *on the American Israelites, page 123*; and others.

§. 22. But I have chosen to touch upon these things lightly so far, so that it might be clear how easily **truth, the all-conqueror** <note>original: "πανδαμάτωρ ἀλήθεια" (*pandamatōr alētheia*)</note>, triumphs over everything. Now, a very wide field would open up if I wished to wander into the commentaries of Christians. For although they do not disagree about the truth of the fulfillment and coming **of Shiloh** <note>original: "τοῦ Schilo"</note>, shown already sixteen centuries ago, they nonetheless hold different views concerning the circumstances of the event and certain parts of the prophecy.

§. 23. To begin with, most understand **scepter** <note>original: "שבט" (*shebet*)</note> as the **supreme power of the state** <note>original: "τὸ κύριον τῆς πολιτείας" (*to kyrion tēs politeias*)</note> or **princely dignity** <note>original: "ἀρχικὸν ἀξίωμα" (*archikon axiōma*)</note>. However, there are some who prefer to explain **scepter** <note>original: "שבט"</note> as a "tribe" or a "tribal form" [of government], among whom are Jerome of Oleastro (who references *Psalm 78, verses 67–68* here), Tremellius in this location, Johann Tarnovius in *Biblical Exercises, book 2, page 545*, and from our own contemporaries, Jacob Alting in *On Shiloh, book 3*. Furthermore, Dionysius Petavius joins the scepter and the tribe together in *The Account of Time, part 2, book 3*; Diodati and the Genevans also tend toward this in their notes.

§. 24. Furthermore, a wonderful variety is found everywhere in the explanation **of the lawgiver** <note>original: "τοῦ מחקק" (*mechokek*)</note>. The *Vulgate version* <note>The standard 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> has: *Leader*, and from there Dietemberger <note>Johannes Dietemberger, a 16th-century Catholic translator who opposed Luther</note> absurdly renders it: "**still some Duke**" (as if each were to remain as an individual). Thus formerly the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> translated it as **governor** <note>original: "ἡγούμενος" (*hēgoumenos*)</note>.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Genesis 49:10, specifically focusing on the identity and role of the "lawgiver" (mechoqeq) mentioned in the prophecy, following the previous discussion on the "scepter" and the mythical legends surrounding the Jewish exile.</meta>

69

I 3

<meta>catchword: until he comes [original: ven-]</meta>

According to Pagnino, Arias Montanus, Junius, and others, the word *mechoqeq* <note>original: מחקק (*mechoqeq*); a Hebrew term usually translated as lawgiver or ruler’s staff</note> means **Legislator**. Thus the French Geneva Bible has *The Legislator* <note>original: *Le Legislateur*</note>. The English has *A law-giver*. The new Dutch has *The Lawgiver* <note>original: *De Wetgever*</note>. Piscator has *a Law-giver* <note>original: *ein Gesetz-geber*</note>; which Aquila might also have had in mind when he translated it as *the one who observes the law with precision* <note>original: ἀκριβαζόμενος (*akribazomenos*)</note>. For others, it means **Scribe**, as in Vatablus’s notes. They were preceded by Onkelos <note>A famous 2nd-century convert to Judaism known for his Aramaic translation of the Torah</note>, who explains it as *scribe* <note>original: ספרא (*safra*)</note>, and Aben Ezra and Kimchi, who explain it as *scribe* <note>original: סופר (*sofer*)</note>. Tavvufius translates it into Persian as <unclear>scribe</unclear> <note>original: دبیر (*dabir*)</note>. Jerome explains it as **Teacher**. So Blessed Luther has: *a Master* <note>original: *ein Meister*</note>. The new Danish has: *Master-Teacher* <note>original: *Loeremestere*</note>. Altingius (on page 153) understands it specifically as an **ecclesiastical** figure, in which sense the Chaldean Paraphrasts Jonathan and the Jerusalem Targum rendered it **Teachers of the Law**; while the Syriac translator used <unclear>Teacher</unclear> <note>original: ܡܠܦܢܐ (*malpana*)</note>, meaning **Expositor**. Rashi and Abravanel <note>Prominent medieval Jewish commentators</note> define it by the term **leader** <note>original: מנהיג (*manhig*)</note> in a general sense. Castalio translates it as **Rector** [Ruler]; Phaletranus as **High Priest** (page 10); others in other ways.

§. 25. Next, most authors take the letter *v* <note>The Hebrew letter *Vav*, acting as a conjunction</note> prefixed to the word *lawgiver* **copulatively** [as "and"], so that the sense is: *Neither* the **scepter** <note>original: שבט (*shebet*)</note> *nor* the **lawgiver** shall depart; in this case, the negative particle must be understood as applying to both terms <note>original: ἀπὸ κοινοῦ (*apo koinou*); a grammatical term where a single word applies to two or more parts of a sentence</note>, as was done by the Jerusalem Targum. Others take it **disjunctively** [as "or"], a view favored by Blessed Walther (page 152) and Hackspan (page 254), so that the sense is: The **scepter** shall not depart, *or at least* (even if the scepter departs, nevertheless) the **lawgiver** or **Sanhedrin** <note>The supreme council and court of ancient Israel</note> shall not; which is also the goal of those who imply the little word *together*, meaning: The **scepter** and **lawgiver** shall not depart *together*—that is, perhaps one or the other might depart, but not both at once, until Shiloh <note>A title for the Messiah in this prophecy</note> comes. Others, such as Georg Eric Phaletranus (*Exercises on the Prophecy of Jacob*, chapter 1, page 11), take it **explanatorily**, so the sense is: The **scepter and lawgiver** shall not depart—that is, the **Priestly scepter** or **royal-priestly rule** <note>original: ἀνακτοτελεταρχία (*anaktoteletarchia*)</note>. Regarding the following phrase, **from between his feet** <note>original: מבין רגליו (*mi-bein raglav*)</note>, it is too well known to need explaining that some refer it to **procreation** and others to **sitting in school** <note>Sitting at the feet of a teacher to learn the law</note>.

§. 26. Furthermore, many writers up to this point take **Judah** <note>original: יהודה (*Yehuda*)</note> to refer to the tribe of Judah as distinct from the others, and specifically in terms of its nature and greatness from the blessing of Jacob until the arrival of the Messiah...

It also analyzes the grammatical relationship between the "scepter" and the "lawgiver" to determine the timeline of the Messiah's arrival.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 69 regarding the interpretation of Jacob’s prophecy in Genesis 49:10, specifically analyzing how various scholars reconcile the rule of the Maccabees and Herod the Great with the promise that the "scepter shall not depart from Judah."</meta>

...come. Some of these scholars, lest the rule of the Maccabees <note>The Hasmonean dynasty of priests who ruled Judea in the 2nd and 1st centuries BCE</note> conflict with the prophecy, do not hesitate to claim they were from the tribe of Judah by origin and fatherhood <note>original: ἀρχῆθεν καὶ πατρόθεν (*archēthen kai patrothen*)</note> (as do Genebrard in *Chronicles*, book 2, and Cesare Baronius in his *Appendix to the Ecclesiastical Annals*, folio 8). Others believe that they could be grafted into the Davidic lineage at least through the maternal line (such as Nicholas of Lyra, Denis the Carthusian, Alfonso Tostado, and many after them). Their opinion has been thoroughly dissected by Pererius in *On the Blessing of the Twelve Patriarchs*, Isaac Casaubon in his notes on the *Appendix to Baronius*, Richard Montagu in *Analecta*, Exercise 2, folio 8, and Phaletranus in *The Oracle of Jacob*, chapters 2 to 5. The latter ultimately explains Judah <note>original: יהודה (*Yehudah*)</note> as the Jewish kingdom gathered from the tribes of Judah, Benjamin, and Levi after the separation of the ten tribes, in chapter 6. Similarly, Petrus Cunaeus in *The Hebrew Republic*, book 1, chapters 9 and 10, Abraham Scultetus in *Evangelical Exercises*, book 1, chapter 3, and their followers strictly interpret it as the tribe of Judah separated from the others in the time of Rehoboam. These scholars point out that although the state was changed under the Hasmoneans or in other ways, the Republic nevertheless always remained in the hands of the people of Judah. Finally, some have understood the prophecy to refer to all twelve Israelite tribes, led by Eusebius in *The Demonstration of the Gospel*, book 3, chapter 2, and followed by Pererius (number 92) and Casaubon (in the *Appendix to Baronius*, Exercise 1, number 2, page 11).

§. 27. Now, regarding the point of fulfillment, or the time when the scepter <note>original: שבט (*shevet*)</note> and lawgiver <note>original: מחקק (*mechoqeq*)</note> were removed and Shiloh <note>original: שילה (*Shiloh*); traditionally interpreted by Christians as the Messiah</note> was manifested, not everyone is of the same mind here either. Pererius, Baronius, Estius, Sa, Tirinus, Menochius, Bulenger, Phaletranus, and a great number of others fix this point at the presence <note>original: ἐνδημίᾳ (*endēmia*)</note> of Herod the Great, since he was a foreigner <note>Herod was of Idumaean descent, and many theologians argued his reign marked the end of native Jewish rule, thus fulfilling the prophecy</note> (though this is challenged by Joseph Scaliger in his notes on Eusebius, number 1983; Casaubon in his notes on Baronius, Exercise 1, numbers 3 and 4, starting at page 24; Drusius on Sulpicius, book 2, page 250; Torniellus in the year of the world 4018, number 7, etc.). They do not think it stands in the way that Herod preceded the birth of the Messiah by several years, since prophecies are not fulfilled at the exact moment <note>original: τῷ νῦν (*tō nyn*)</note>, but rather in a certain proximity; or they say that Herod's reign was illegitimate up to that point. Others push the endpoint back to the final destruction of the Temple, as Cunaeus does in the cited work, chapter 11; or they establish three stages for the destruction of the Jewish polity...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Genesis 49:10 ("The scepter shall not depart from Judah..."), specifically listing historical moments scholars identify as the "departure" of Jewish autonomy and the arrival of the Messiah. ...fallen: 1. Under Pompey <note>The Roman general who captured Jerusalem in 63 BCE</note>. 2. Those, however, who take the terms <term>lawgiver</term> <note>original: מחקק (*m'choqeq*)</note> and <term>scepter</term> <note>original: שבט (*shevet*)</note> separately, concede that the scepter was taken away before the arrival of the Messiah—indeed, even before the Hasmoneans—but that a <term>legitimate</term> <note>original: γνήσιον (*gnēsion*)</note> Sanhedrin <note>The supreme council and court of justice in ancient Israel</note>, whose President at least was always from the tribe of Judah, lasted until the very year of the Savior’s birth. Nor are there lacking those who place the arrival of <term>Shiloh</term> <note>original: שילה (*Shiloh*); a messianic title from the prophecy</note> beyond his birth. For *Montagu* distinguishes between the <term>humble and the glorious</term> coming of the Messiah. He says this coming is either *general* or *particular*, such as occurred, for example, when he came as a judge to inflict <term>utter destruction</term> <note>original: πανωλεθρίαν (*panōlethrian*)</note> upon the Jewish Republic. And *Piscator* and others understand his arrival to be that which he accomplished when his kingdom was solemnly inaugurated on the day of Pentecost.

§. 28. We would now add our own <term>judgment</term> <note>original: Ἐπίκρισιν (*Epikrisin*)</note>, were we not well aware of the customs of our age, and if it did not seem somewhat foreign to the modesty with which we venerate the shadows of learned men. Yet, lest we seem to have said nothing, or to intentionally keep others in suspense—as the saying goes, <term>"to be carried off midway between earth and heaven"</term> <note>original: μεσηגὺς γαίης τε καὶ οὐρανοῦ ἀφερέσθαι (*mesēgys gaiēs te kai ouranou apheresthai*); a Greek idiom for being left in a state of uncertainty or limbo</note>—we shall not hide that, for the sake of extracting the genuine sense of the Oracle and showing the "universal harmony" of the prophecy and its fulfillment, the following points seem noteworthy to us:

I. By <term>Judah</term> <note>original: יהודה (*Yehudah*)</note> is understood the tribe of Judah as distinct from the others.

II. The <term>scepter</term> <note>original: שבט (*shevet*)</note> does not specifically denote a <term>royal dignity</term> <note>original: ἀξίωμα βασιλικὸν (*axiōma basilikon*)</note> or <term>leadership</term> <note>original: ἡγεμονικὸν (*hēgemonikon*)</note> or any other specific rank, but rather <term>the sovereign power</term> <note>original: τὸ κύριον (*to kyrion*)</note> in general, under whatever form the Republic might take.

III. The <term>scepter</term> belonged to <term>Judah</term>, even if they chose a King or Leader from a tribe not their own, provided they recognized him as their own and as the governor of their Republic.

IV. The <term>lawgiver</term> <note>original: מחקק (*m'choqeq*)</note> denotes the one exercising <term>the judicial office</term> <note>original: τὸ δικάζον (*to dikazon*)</note>, that is, the dictator of Law within the Sanhedrin.

V. The phrase <term>from between his feet</term> <note>original: מבין רגליו (*mibbeyn raglav*)</note> does not describe the physical place of those who teach, but rather <term>lineage or birth</term> <note>original: γονείαν (*goneian*)</note>, and it restricts the verb <term>depart</term> <note>original: יסור (*yasur*)</note> to the "lawgiver" and not to the "scepter": therefore, at no time were there not some from the Sanhedrin...

...at least, that he ought to have been from the tribe of Judah. VI. By the word <term>depart</term> <note>original: סור (*sur*)</note> is implied not just any disturbance or weakening, but *utter destruction* <note>original: πανωλεθρίαν (*panōlethrian*)</note>, that is, the total abolition of the Jewish State, such as did not happen before the time of the Savior's birth. VII. IX. After the arrival of Shiloh, it was not necessary that [the departure] happen immediately; rather, the words "until he comes" <note>original: עד כי יבא (*ad ki yavo*)</note> allow for an interval of time before the removal of the scepter and lawgiver followed. X. Furthermore, the removal of the scepter and lawgiver was a *criterion* <note>original: κριτήριον (*kritērion*)</note> and infallible proof that Shiloh had come. All these points, however, we wish to remain always subject to the judgment of learned men, especially Orthodox theologians.

§. 29. But having been content to touch upon these matters with our fingertips, as it were—as it was not our intention to dwell on them—we now prepare ourselves for that which we were determined to do above all else. Namely, since we have noticed that in the exposition of this Jacobite oracle <note>A reference to Jacob’s blessing of his sons in Genesis 49</note>, interpreters get most stuck on the principal word <term>Shiloh</term> <note>original: שילה</note>, we thought we should test our own strengths here, and thus we have undertaken the present writing. If this seems a trivial matter to some, as if it were a merely grammatical argument, we allow them to enjoy their own inclinations; although ignorance seems all the more shameful and unworthy of a serious man the more trivial that thing is which ought to be known but is not. *The voice was once that of an ox, not a man: The better you are as a grammarian, the worse you will be as a philosopher and theologian.* <note>This Latin proverb reflects a historical tension where deep study of language (grammar) was sometimes mocked by theologians who felt it distracted from spiritual truths.</note>

§. 30. Furthermore, in order for the judgment regarding the meaning of the word <term>Shiloh</term> <note>original: שילה</note> to be more sound, its spelling must be established above all else. Regarding this, the manuscripts of all ages, which agree in this spelling: שילה, do not allow us to doubt. <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, transitioning from a general discussion of its meaning to a technical examination of its spelling in Hebrew manuscripts and ancient versions.</meta>

K

<note>original: *Clav. SSe P. 1. c. 1143.*</note> I have seen many manuscript copies where the letter *Jod* <note>the smallest letter in the Hebrew alphabet, often transliterated as 'i' or 'j'</note> was absent, which would better agree with the etymology of "happiness." Indeed, it would more closely align with the Jewish commentary regarding the city of Shiloh.

§. 31. In the meantime, our manuscripts are supported by our faithful scribes, the **Masoretes** <note>a group of Jewish scholar-scribes who, between the 6th and 10th centuries, standardized the pronunciation and punctuation of the Hebrew Bible</note>, whose judgment on this passage is as follows: *Shiloh* <note>original: שילה</note> *written with these specific letters does not appear elsewhere; rather, in eight places it is written as* שלו <note>original: *Schilo*</note> (which are found in Judges 21:19, 1 Samuel 1:24, 3:21, 14:3, Jeremiah 7:14, 26:9, 41:5, and Psalm 68:60) *and in all other instances it is written as* שלה <note>original: *Sheloh*</note>. Thus, the **interlinear Masorah** <note>short technical notes written between the columns or lines of the biblical text</note> also denies that the word spelled specifically as *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note> appears again. And in the book of the **Zohar** <note>the foundational work of Jewish mystical thought, the Kabbalah</note>, according to the testimony of Buxtorf <note>original: *Anticr. p. 714*</note>, it is expressly written that elsewhere it is read as *Shilo* <note>שילו</note> with a *Vav*, or elsewhere as *Sheloh* <note>שלה</note> without a *Jod*; but here it is written as *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note> with both a *Jod* and a *He* at the end, to indicate the divine name **Yah** <note>original: יה; a shortened form of the name of God, often found in 'Hallelujah'</note>. Nor did the **Talmudists** <note>the ancient rabbinic authorities of the Talmud</note> write it any other way than *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note> in the tractate *Sanhedrin* folio 98, page 2, not to mention other sources.

§. 32. Nor do we dwell on the fact that the **Samaritan Pentateuch** <note>a version of the first five books of the Bible preserved by the Samaritan community, which often differs from the standard Hebrew text</note> omits the letter *Jod* from the word *Shiloh*, a choice also imitated by the Samaritan translation, which reads: *Until the Shiloh comes* <note>original: עד הלא ייתי שלה</note>. For the authority of that Samaritan Pentateuch has been so undermined by the famous Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger, a 17th-century Swiss philologist</note> in his exercises against Morin, that one would have to be very reckless to pledge their faith to it today.

§. 33. But the **Catholic scholars** <note>original: *Pontificii*, literally 'the Pontificals'</note> level a more serious accusation against the text. They quite boldly assert that it was originally written as *Schiloach* <note>original: שלח</note> and that it was maliciously corrupted into *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note> by the Jews, who were cornered by the clarity of the matter. As Salmeron <note>Alfonso Salmeron, a founding Jesuit scholar</note> judges, the word is either of no meaning or of unknown meaning. **"I believe,"** says Steuchus Eugubinus <note>Agostino Steuco, a 16th-century Italian humanist and bishop</note>, **"that the Jews corrupted this passage."** Gordon Huntley <note>James Gordon of Huntly, a Scottish Jesuit controversialist</note> also describes the method and the goal: **"Formerly,"** he says, **"in the Hebrew it was** *Schiloach* <note>original: שלח</note>, **meaning 'The Sent One' (that is, the one who is to be sent, past...** <meta>catchword: teritum</meta>

...past for the future in the manner of the Prophets) *but in truth the Jews corrupted the name of Christ, Shiloh, in more than one way. First, they added the letter Jod; then they changed the letter Chet into He; and because they saw this was against the rules of Grammar, they again claimed that the letter He (which they themselves added) was placed there instead of a Vav. They do all these things so that from the name of the Messiah, Shiloh, they might make the name of the city, Shiloh, so they could drag this prophecy to apply to either King David, or Saul, or Jeroboam, or Nebuchadnezzar.* These are his words. Finally, so that no part of the story is missing, Gabriel à Costa <note>Uriel da Costa (1585–1640), a philosopher who famously questioned traditional religious dogmas</note> declares that this was done only after the time of Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, the 4th-century translator who produced the Latin Vulgate</note> in his *Commentary on Genesis 49, Treatise 9, section 180, page 50.*

§. 34. Hugo Grotius <note>a highly influential Dutch jurist and theologian</note> brushed against the ears of the Papists <note>a historical term for Roman Catholics</note> in his *Annotations on this passage, page 53. To me,* he says, *the version of Lana <note>referring to a specific scholarly interpretation or manuscript</note> proves itself most clearly, suggesting that* Shiloh <note>Hebrew: שילה</note> *stands for* Shiloah <note>Hebrew: שלוח, meaning "sent" or "one who is sent"</note>. *For what is easier than for letters so similar in both sound and shape to be swapped due to a varying dialect or an errant scribe?* Nor, surprisingly, was Flacius <note>Matthias Flacius Illyricus, a Lutheran reformer known for his work on biblical interpretation</note> far from this opinion. He says in the cited passage: *Furthermore, if I did not embrace the first explanation, I would prefer to embrace the exposition of Jerome and say that in his time it was written as* Shiloah <note>original: שילוח</note>; *but afterward, because the Jews were being pressured by Christians with this and similar words and had no answer, they corrupted it out of malice and stubbornness, making a scribal change.* Just as they corrupted the word *kaari* <note>Hebrew: כארי, meaning "like a lion"</note> in *Psalm 22* <note>A famous point of debate: Christian versions read "they pierced my hands," while the Hebrew Masoretic text reads "like a lion my hands"</note> and many others. Thus far Flacius, who, while he does not prove why *Shiloah* would be a corruption, simply asserts that *kaari* was corrupted from *karu* <note>Hebrew: כרו, meaning "they pierced"</note>.

§. 35. Furthermore, the translator of the **Vulgate** <note>the standard Latin translation of the Bible</note> provided the Catholics an opportunity for error by translating: *until he comes who is to be sent* SHILOH. Dietemberger <note>Johann Dietenberger, a 16th-century Catholic theologian who published a German translation of the Bible</note> follows him with closed eyes, rendering it: *Until he who is to be sent shall come* (so also in the edition of Ferdinand Hesse, *The Scourge of the Jews, page 28*). Clearly, since the Hebrew root *shalach* <note>שלה</note> means "to send," they thought that "the one to be sent" should be called *Shiluach* <note>שלוח</note> rather than *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note>. However, expressing "the one to be sent" as *Shiloh* is beyond controversy...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10, specifically addressing the arguments made by Catholic scholars who favor the Latin Vulgate's reading ("the one who is to be sent") over the Hebrew text preserved by Jewish scribes.</meta>

...they placed it, because the Vulgate had said so. This is not denied by Cornelius à Lapide <note>a highly influential Jesuit biblical commentator (1567–1637)</note>, whose words on this passage on page 307 are as follows: *It is most probable that instead of Shilo with a "he" [ה], one should read Shiloach with a "chet" [ח], that is, an envoy, one who is sent, or one to be sent, from the root shalach <note>Hebrew: שלח, meaning "to send"</note>; for thus our translator [Jerome] renders it, and this was formerly a common name for the Messiah.* So also Jacobus Bonfrerius <note>a Jesuit scholar known for his work on biblical geography and commentary</note>: *Jerome’s version,* he says, *makes it probable that this passage was corrupted by them (the Jews), for he undoubtedly read shiluach <note>Hebrew: שלוח, meaning "sent"</note> in the Hebrew, etc.* To these may be added A Costa <note>referring to a scholar at the University of Coimbra in Portugal</note>, that primary interpreter of sacred literature in the Academy of Coimbra, whose confession is this: *However it may be, we follow the Vulgate edition.* This, therefore, is an agreed-upon principle <note>original Greek: ὁμολογούμενον (homologoumenon)</note> among the Papists <note>a historical term for Roman Catholics, reflecting the Protestant perspective of the author</note>: wherever the Vulgate translation differs from the original text, the latter is deemed corrupted, and the former must be followed. Rightly did Dr. Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer (1603–1666), a Lutheran theologian and teacher of Spener</note> remark in *The Way of Christianity*, page 831: *Evidently the sun neglected its own course because the clock went wrong!*

§ 36. On our side stand all the manuscripts. Let the Papists show even one where *shiluach* <note>Hebrew: שלוח</note> appears, and then let them claim victory. We are certain that none existed, and none will exist, unless they themselves choose to corrupt one. Before Jerome, there existed the authors of the *Zohar* <note>a foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>, the *Talmud* <note>the central text of Rabbinic Judaism</note>, and the *Masorah* <note>the tradition of the professional scribes who preserved the Hebrew text</note>, who uniquely confirm our reading; and yet, if we believe the Papists, the corruption only occurred after Jerome's time. Furthermore, their own versions abundantly prove that the reading *shiluach* <note>Hebrew: שלוח</note> was present neither in the era of the "Seventy" <note>original Greek: τῶν ὁ' (the Septuagint, the 3rd-century BCE Greek translation of the Old Testament)</note> nor in the time of the Targumists <note>the translators who produced the Aramaic paraphrases of the Bible</note>.

§ 37. Furthermore, although it matters little to us what the Vulgate translator intended—since we are not his slaves—we still do not believe that he read *shiluach* <note>Hebrew: שלוח</note> or—as Cappel <note>Louis Cappel (1585–1658), a French Protestant scholar who pioneered textual criticism</note> conjectures in *Critica Sacra*, book 5, chapter 9—*shalach* <note>Hebrew: שלח</note>. For he expressed our very word *Shiloh* <note>Hebrew: שילה</note> with the Latin letters **SILOH** (not *Siloah*, nor *Siloë*, nor *Siluah*, or otherwise); had he intended to translate it entirely, there would have been no need to retain the name. Therefore, he inserted that phrase, "the one who is to be sent," parenthetically <note>original Greek: ἐπευθετικῶς (epeuthetikos)</note> or for the sake of greater clarity...

<meta>The previous page discussed the Catholic preference for the Latin Vulgate's reading of Genesis 49:10 (interpreting "Shiloh" as "the one who is to be sent") and the accusation that Jewish scribes corrupted the Hebrew text. This page continues the rebuttal, arguing that the Hebrew reading is original and that the Jews themselves acknowledge it refers to the Messiah.</meta>

...failed. He <note>Referring to Jerome, the 4th-century translator of the Vulgate</note> could also have thought that the roots *shalach* <note>Hebrew: שלח, meaning "to send"</note> and *shalah* <note>Hebrew: שלה, meaning "to be quiet" or "prosperous"</note>, since they differ by only a single letter and one produced by a similar vocal organ <note>the throat letters "chet" and "he"</note>, were of the same meaning or at least not very different. But whether he intended this or something else, we know he was a man, and it is not foreign to the human condition to err.

§. 38. But to what end, finally, would the Jews have changed that word *Shiloh* <note>Hebrew: שלה</note>, especially since they could have more easily found a certain "refuge" <note>original Greek: κρησφύγετον (krēsphugeton), meaning a place of escape or a loophole</note> in it—as admitted by Porchetus <note>Porchetus Salvaticus, a 14th-century monk who wrote against Jewish interpretations</note> in *Victory against the Hebrews*, Part 1, Chapter 2, and Galatinus <note>Pietro Galatino (1460–1540), an Italian scholar of Hebrew</note> in *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth*, Book 4, Chapter 4? Indeed, it is so far from the truth that they greatly deny *Shiloh* is the Messiah, that a great number of ancient and modern Jews assert it; even the Targumists <note>translators of the Hebrew Bible into Aramaic</note> express the word *Shiloh* by the very name of the *Messiah*. *All this is ridiculous*, says Sixtinus Amama <note>a Dutch professor of Hebrew (1593–1629)</note> in *Censura Vulgaris Versionis*, page 75, *which the zealots dream up here. For since the Hebrews before Jerome and after him accepted this passage as referring to the Messiah, who would believe that this passage was corrupted by them? And specifically for the purpose, as those people want, to prevent it from being explained as the Messiah?* Furthermore, how weakly Gordonus <note>likely James Gordon (1541–1620), a Scottish Jesuit scholar</note> proves that *Shiloh* is the name of the Messiah from the *waters of Shiloah* in Isaiah 8:6 and the *pool of Siloam* in John 9:7! He treats both too indulgently—not to say foolishly, to quote Horace. Even so, was that word and no other to be used here? That is a "very great demand" <note>original Greek: μέγα λίαν αἴτημα (mega lian aitēma), a logical fallacy of assuming what needs to be proved</note>. But compare Dr. Glassius <note>Salomon Glassius (1593–1656), a Lutheran theologian</note> in *Sacred Philology*, Book 1, Tract 1, page 50; Dr. Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686)</note> in *Annotations against Grotius* on this passage; B. D. Waltherus in *Controversy 20*; Daniel Chamierus <note>a French Huguenot theologian</note> in *The Catholic Panoply*, Volume 1, Book 12, Chapter 12; Sixtinus Amama in *Censura* and *Biblical Antibarbarus*, page 281; Hottingerus <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist</note> in *Philological Treasury*, page 171; and Buxtorfius <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664)</note> in *Anticritica*, page 769.

§. 39. Since, therefore, it is sufficiently manifest from these points that the reading represented by our manuscripts is the genuine one, we proceed directly to draw out the true sense of the word. However, we shall not be as concerned with the thing signified by the name as we are with its formal and grammatical meaning. For that the Messiah is indicated by the word *Shiloh* in the prophecy is, among...

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Jewish people historically understood Genesis 49:10 as a prophecy of the Messiah. The previous page ended with a quote from Sixtinus Amama regarding the interpretation of the word "Shiloh."</meta>

Among Christians, it is placed beyond controversy. Nor do the Jews oppose it, if you except a few of the lower sort of "Arcadian cattle" <note>original: "Arcadica pecora" — a classical idiom for stupid or dull people</note>, *whom hot blood or ignorance of things vexes.*

§. 40. Certainly the *Targumists* <note>Ancient Aramaic translators and commentators who paraphrased the Hebrew Bible</note>, as we have said, speak as if with one voice. For the translation of *Onkelos* <note>The most famous Aramaic translator of the Torah, 1st–2nd century AD</note> is this: *A ruler shall not pass away from the house of Judah, nor a scribe from his children's children (forever, or ever—which is, however, absent from the Royal editions) until the MESSIAH comes, to whom belongs the kingdom, and whom the peoples shall obey.* <note>original Aramaic: לא יעדי עביד שולטן מדבית יהודה וספרא מבני בנוהי (עד עלמא) עד דייתי משיחא דדיליה היא מלכותא וליה ישתמעון עממיא</note> Onkelos could not have rendered it more suitably to our taste (provided he fully grasped the force of the word *mechokkek* <note>Hebrew: מחקק — traditionally "lawgiver" or "scepter"</note>). Moreover, that this was the opinion not only of his contemporaries <note>original Greek: συγχρόνων (synchronōn)</note> but also of his predecessors, is apparent from what Rabbi Joseph Albo says in *Sepher Ikkarim* (Book of Principles), Book 4, Chapter 42: that Onkelos received the Oral Law <note>original Hebrew: שבעל פה (She-be'al peh)</note> from *Shemaya* and *Abtalion*, and specifically learned from them that *Shiloh* in *Genesis 49:10* was to be explained as referring to the Messiah. Rabbi Jeshua Levita, in *Halichot Olam* (Paths of the World), Part 1, Chapter 2, testifies that those men lived in the sixth age of the Mishnic scholars; regarding them, one can also consult Rabbi Abraham Zacuto in *Juchasin* (The Book of Lineage), folio 18.

§. 41. To this *Jonathan* <note>Referring to Targum Jonathan, another Aramaic paraphrase</note> harmonizes—not indeed that "falsely-named" one <note>original Greek: ψευδώνυμος (pseudōnymos)</note>, but this honest and sincere one, *pure from the top of his head to the soles of his feet* <note>A Latin idiom for someone completely honest or thorough</note>, speaking thus <note>original Greek: ὧδε φράזων (hōde phrazōn)</note>: *Kings and rulers shall not cease from the house of Judah and scribes teaching the law from his seed, until the time in which the King Messiah comes, the youngest of his sons, and because of him the peoples shall melt away in fear.* <note>original Aramaic: לא פסקן מלכין ושליטין מדבית יהודה וספרין מאלפי אוריתא מזרעיה עד זמן די ייתי מלכא משיחא זעיר בנוי ובדיליה יתימסון עממיא</note> Added to this is the *Jerusalem Targumist*, an obscure author indeed, but one whose interpretation is *sweeter to me than the thyme of Hybla* <note>A poetic reference to the famous honey-producing region in Sicily, meaning the interpretation is very pleasing</note>: *Kings shall not cease from the house of Jud...* <note>original Aramaic: לא פסקן מלכין מדבית יה</note>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration that historical Jewish translations and commentaries—specifically the Targums, Persian versions, and the Talmud—unanimously identify "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10 as the Messiah. It follows the previous page’s discussion of Onkelos.</meta>

...Judah, nor shall they be without a teacher of the law from among his descendants until the time that the King Messiah comes, for the kingdom is his, and all the kingdoms of the earth shall be subject to him. <note>original Aramaic: יהודה אף לא סברין מלפי אוריתא... This is a variation of the Jerusalem Targum.</note> That is to say: *Kings shall not cease from the House of Judah, nor skilled teachers of the law from his children’s children, until the time when the KING MESSIAH comes, to whom the kingdom belongs, and to whose service all the kingdoms of the earth shall submit themselves.*

§. 42. Following in the footsteps of the *Targumists* <note>Ancient Aramaic translators</note>—as we might note as an <term>eyewitness</term> <note>original Greek: αὐτοψιαδικῶς (autopsiadikōs) — meaning through direct observation of the texts</note>—*Jacob Tawusus* <note>A 16th-century scholar who translated the Pentateuch into Judeo-Persian</note> took his stand in his *Persian* paraphrase of this passage, which follows: **The sovereignty shall not pass from Judah, nor the scribe from the midst of his children, until his Messiah comes, and the nations shall be gathered to him.** <note>original Persian: נה כרדן סלטנת אז יהודה... translated via the Latin: Non recedet imperium à Juda...</note>

§. 43. But we must not pass over the *Talmudists* here, whose testimony we seek from the *Tractate Sanhedrin, chapter 11, folio 98, column 2*, where the following is recorded concerning the names of the *Messiah*:

Rab said: The world was created for no one but David. But Samuel said: For Moses. And Rabbi Jochanan said: For the Messiah. What is his name? Those of the school <note>The Hebrew "De-be" refers to the students or the academy of a specific Rabbi</note> of Rabbi Shila said: His name is *Shiloh*, as it is said: *Until Shiloh comes* (Genesis 49:10). Those of the school of Rabbi Yannai said: His name is *Jinnon* <note>A name meaning "to flourish" or "continue"</note>, as it is said: *May his name endure forever; as long as the sun, may his name flourish (Jinnon)* (Psalm 72:17). Those of the school of Rabbi Hanina said: His name is *Chanina* <note>A name meaning "Grace" or "Favor"</note>, as it is said: *For I will show you no favor (Chanina)* (Jeremiah 16:13). And some say his name is *Menachem the son of Hezekiah* <note>A name meaning "Comforter"</note>, as it is said: *Because the Comforter (Menachem) who should restore my soul is far from me* (Lamentations 1:16). And the Rabbis say: *The Leper of the house of Rabbi* is his name, as it is said: *Surely he has borne our griefs* (Isaiah 53:4). <note>The original Hebrew/Aramaic text is a series of wordplays where students derived the Messiah's name from their own teacher's name using scripture.</note>

This is to say: *Rab said: the world was not established except for the sake of David. But Samuel said: For the sake of Moses. And Rabbi Jochanan said: For the sake of the Messiah. What then is his name? Those of the house (the School, that is, the disciples and followers) of Rabbi Shila said: Shiloh is his name, according to that verse: until Shiloh comes (Genesis 49:10). From the School of Rabbi Yannai they said: His name is Jinnon, according to that verse: May his name be forever...*

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the Talmudic Tractate Sanhedrin (folio 98b) regarding the various names attributed to the Messiah by different rabbinic schools, specifically focusing on the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10.</meta>

...and his name shall endure as long as the sun: <term>Jinnon</term> <note>original Hebrew: ינון (Yinnon), a name implying "increase" or "perpetuity"</note> is his name, according to Psalm 72:17. Those from the School of Rabbi Chanina said: **Chanina** is his name, according to the saying: *For I will show you no* **favor** <note>original Hebrew: חנינה (Chanina), a pun on the Rabbi's name</note> [Jeremiah 16:13]. Others said: **Menachem** <note>Meaning "Comforter"</note> son of Hezekiah is his name, according to that verse: *Because the* **comforter** *that should relieve my soul is far from me* [Lamentations 1:16]. Our Masters said: **The Leper of the House of Rabbi** is his name, as it is said: *Surely he has borne our griefs and carried our sorrows; yet we esteemed him stricken, smitten by God, and afflicted* [Isaiah 53:4]. It should be noted here in passing how these Teachers all alluded to their own names in these interpretations. For the school of Schila wanted the Messiah to be called **Schilo** (Shiloh) after their own name; the school of Jannai wanted **Jinnon** after theirs; and the school of Chanina wanted **Chanina** after theirs. This was also observed by the <term>glossator</term> <note>A marginal commentator</note> who said: *Each one interpreted the verse according to his own name.* These accounts are also found in the *Echa Rabbati* <note>A great midrashic commentary on the Book of Lamentations</note> regarding Lamentations 1:16.

§. 44. Furthermore, in the *Midrash on Proverbs, folio 71, column 3*, **Shiloh** is likewise listed among the eight names of the Messiah. It will be worthwhile to provide the full passage here, as it is highly relevant to our current task:

> Where is it shown that Israel is called a vineyard? In that place where it says: *For the vineyard of the Lord of Hosts is the house of Israel, and the men of Judah his pleasant plant* [Isaiah 5:7]. Just as with a planting, at the time you plant it, its place is clearly marked; so too God planted the kingdom in the tribe of Judah as if in a garden or vineyard, until the King Messiah sprouts forth, according to the verse: *The scepter shall not depart from Judah, nor a lawgiver from between his feet, until Shiloh comes; and unto him shall the gathering of the people be* [Genesis 49:10]. Rabbi Huna said: The Messiah is called by eight names, and these are they: **Jinnon** [Psalm 72:17], **The Lord Our Righteousness** [Jeremiah 23:6], **Tzemach** <note>The Branch</note>, **Menachem** <note>The Comforter</note>, **David**, **Shiloh**, and **Elijah**.

That is to say: *Where is Israel called a vineyard? In that place: For the vineyard of the Lord of Hosts is the house of Israel and the man of Judah is his pleasant plantation. How does a plantation behave? At the time when you fix the plant, its place is noted. In this same way, God planted the kingdom in the tribe of Judah like a garden or vineyard, until the King Messiah buds forth, according to that verse: The scepter shall not depart, etc. Genesis 49:10. Rabbi Huna says, the Messiah has eight names, which are these (counting "Messiah" itself as the first): 2. Jinnon, Psalm 72:17. 3. The Lord our Righteousness...*

<meta>The previous page concluded a list of messianic names (like Comforter and David) and cited various Jewish and Christian authorities who interpret the "Shiloh" prophecy in Genesis 49:10 as referring to the Messiah. <margin>Jesus, peace be upon him <note>original Arabic: عيسي عليه السلام (*‘Isa ‘alayhi as-salam*)</note></margin> **And this is a clear argument** in favor of the prophecy of **Jesus**, the blessed. We praise this confession even when it comes from an enemy and otherwise fierce adversary.

§. 47. Since the person signified <note>the Messiah</note> is thus established, it might now be asked whether **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note> is merely a proper name, and thus hardly requires a careful investigation of its etymology? However, although the labor spent on digging out the true meaning would not be in vain even in that case, we nevertheless—along with the illustrious **Jacob Alting** <note>Dutch philologist and theologian (1618–1679)</note> in Book 3, Chapter 17—deny that it is a strictly proper name (that is, one merely assigned by arbitrary choice, or by which the Messiah was commonly called **for the sake of distinction** <note>original Greek: διακρίσεως ἕνεκα</note>). Instead, we declare it to be an appellative and natural name <note>one that describes the nature of the person</note>. This can be clearly demonstrated by the fact that the word **Shiloh** has a grammatical affix <note>a suffix</note>, which proper names among the Hebrews completely reject, as can be read in **Ephodeus** <note>Profiat Duran, a 14th-century Jewish scholar and grammarian</note> in *The Work of Grammar*, Chapter 9, and **Moses ben Habib** in *The Healing of the Tongue* <note>original: *Marpe Laschon*</note>, page 3.

§. 48. Regarding those translators who have retained the word **Shiloh** in their versions, they are by no means to be thought of as considering it a mere proper name, but rather as a natural epithet of the Messiah that is **difficult to interpret** <note>original Greek: δυσερμηνεύτῳ</note>. Thus **Pagninus** <note>Santes Pagnino, 16th-century Hebraist</note> has: *Until Siloh comes.* **Arias** <note>Benito Arias Montano</note>: *Until Siloh shall come.* The **Geneva French** version: *Until Scilo comes.* The **English**: *Until Shiloh come.* The **New Dutch**: *Until Silo comes.* The **New Danish**: *Until Siloh comes.* The **Spanish**, **Italian**, and **Bohemian** versions do the same. The **Hungarian** version places the name **Messiah** in place of "Silo." Meanwhile, the authors of these versions have for the most part sufficiently revealed their thoughts in their notes, as we shall see below.

§. 49. Therefore, those of the circumcision <note>original Latin: *verpis*, a derogatory term for Jewish scholars in this context</note> who interpret **Shiloh** as the proper name of a city are making a great effort over great trifles. For besides the fact that the text hardly allows for it, as was shown above, the spelling also contradicts it. Indeed, the name of that town <note>the city of Shiloh</note> is written either as **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שִׁלֹו</note> or **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שִׁלֹה</note>, never [with the letter *yod*]... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword *omni-*, likely for *omnino* or "entirely."</note>
