<meta>This page continues the discussion on the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, transitioning from a list of authorities to a grammatical and philological analysis of whether the word is a proper name or a descriptive title for the Messiah.</meta>

Jesus, peace be upon him <note>original Arabic: عيسي عليه السلام</note> *And this is a clear argument for the prophecy of Jesus, the blessed. We praise this confession even when it comes from an enemy and an otherwise sharp adversary.*

§. 47. Since the identity of the person indicated is established, one might now ask whether **Shiloh** <note>Hebrew: שילה</note> is merely a proper name, making a curious investigation into its etymology unnecessary? However, even if it were a proper name, the effort spent digging out its true meaning would not be in vain. Nevertheless, along with the famous Jacob Alting <note>Jacob Alting (1618–1679), a Dutch linguist and theologian</note> (Book 3, chapter 17), we deny that it is a strictly proper name—that is, a name that is merely assigned or chosen by whim—by which the Messiah is commonly called **by way of distinction** <note>original Greek: ἐξαιρέσεως ἕνεκα</note>. Instead, we declare it to be an appellative <note>a descriptive name or title</note> and a natural one. This can be clearly demonstrated if it is said that *Shiloh* possesses a suffix, which proper names in Hebrew generally reject, as can be read in Ephodeus <note>Isaac ben Moses Ha-Levi, a 14th-century Jewish grammarian</note> (*Grammatical Works*, chapter 9) and Moses ben Chavif in *Healing of the Tongue*, page 3.

§. 48. As for those interpreters who retained the word *Shiloh* in their versions, they should by no means be thought to have considered it a strictly proper name. Rather, they saw it as a natural epithet of the Messiah that is **difficult to interpret** <note>original Greek: δυσερμηνεύτῳ</note>. Thus, Pagninus <note>Santes Pagnino, an influential 16th-century Hebraist</note> translates: *Until Shiloh comes.* Arias <note>Benito Arias Montano, editor of the Antwerp Polyglot</note>: *until Shiloh should come.* The French Geneva Bible: *Until Shiloh comes.* The English: *Until Shiloh come.* The new Dutch: **Until Silo comes.** The new Danish: **Until Shiloh comes.** The *Spanish, Italian, and Bohemian* versions do the same. The Hungarian version places the name *Messiah* in place of 'Shiloh.' Meanwhile, the authors of these versions have usually sufficiently revealed their thoughts in their notes, as we shall see below.

§. 49. Therefore, those among the circumcision <note>original Latin: *verpis*; a polemical term for Jewish scholars</note> labor greatly over trifles when they interpret **Shiloh** <note>Hebrew: שילה</note> as the proper name of a city. For besides the fact that the text does not allow for this, as shown above, the spelling also contradicts it. Indeed, the name of that town is written as either **Shilo** <note>Hebrew: שִׁלוֹ</note> or **Shiloh** <note>Hebrew: שִׁלֹה</note>, but never in this manner: omni. <note>the catchword "omni" suggests the next page continues the linguistic argument</note>

L
<meta>catchword: omni.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10 is a descriptive title for the Messiah rather than a simple proper name or a reference to the city of Shiloh, citing Hebrew grammatical rules and various European Bible translations.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Messianic prophecy, Jacob Alting, Hebrew grammar, etymology, biblical translation, Genesis 49:10</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. Having previously argued that it is a descriptive title rather than a simple proper name, the author now prepares to categorize and critique the various scholarly interpretations of its meaning, responding to claims that the word is either meaningless or possesses multiple contradictory definitions.</meta>

...entirely **Shiloh** <note>Hebrew: שילה</note>, so that the Holy Spirit himself intended this name of the Messiah to be distinguished from the city of the same name by a certain unique character.

§. 50. Thus, having finally struggled through various difficulties, the question remains—the central hinge on which the matter turns: how should the word **Shiloh** be formally explained? Here at last, you will see as many opinions as there are men. From this, it has perhaps happened that many dread the word **Shiloh** like some kind of **bugbear** <note>original Greek: μορμολύκειον, a terrifying phantom or object of fear used to scare children</note>. Certainly, that primary interpreter of Holy Scripture from Coimbra, **Gabriel à Costa** <note>A reference to the Jesuit commentators of the University of Coimbra</note>, complains that *so many and such varied things are put forward concerning this word by Hebrews, Latins, and Greeks alike, that they confuse almost every mind.* And **Salmeron** <note>Alfonso Salmeron (1515–1585), a Spanish Jesuit theologian and scholar</note> said that the word **Shiloh** **means nothing** in Hebrew, and the famous **Marinus** <note>Marcus Marinus, a 16th-century Hebraist and author of the dictionary "Arca Noe"</note> was of the opinion that its origin was not established. Many others, however, leave the whole matter undecided and give the reader the choice to pick whichever opinion they prefer.

§. 51. On the other hand, **Menasseh ben Israel** <note>A famous Portuguese rabbi and founder of the first Hebrew printing press in Amsterdam</note> asserted—not without some absurdity—that the word **Shiloh** possesses multiple meanings (*The Conciliator*, page 89). But where are these various meanings to be found, since the word written in this specific way is used only once in Holy Scripture? If anyone thinks that several literal senses <note>original: sensus literalis</note> of a single word can be "stabled" <note>the author uses the verb "stabulari" to suggest the crowding of many meanings into one place</note> in that one instance, we pray they find a sounder mind. Menasseh ought to have known that the differing opinions of authors do not immediately create just as many actual meanings for a word.

§. 52. In what follows, we shall examine the various opinions of authors, as many as we have encountered, reviewing them in order as is fitting. This is so that if any mind has been confused by them, it may be settled again. At the same time, **God willing**, we will show that the word **Shiloh** does indeed mean something in Hebrew <note>original: Ebraicè</note>, and it is only to Salmeron and his like that it has either no meaning or an unknown one.

<summary>The author addresses the scholarly confusion surrounding the word "Shiloh," refuting claims that it is meaningless or holds multiple simultaneous meanings, and prepares to evaluate existing interpretations.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Messias, Gabriel à Costa, Salmeron, Marinus, Menasseh ben Israel, Hebrew etymology, literal sense, philology</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. Having previously discussed various scholarly viewpoints, the author now shifts to a critique of mystical and numerological interpretations—specifically those from the Kabbalistic tradition—which he intends to set aside in favor of a strictly grammatical approach.</meta>

§. 53. From the very threshold, however, we reject those interpretations that rely on any reasoning other than grammatical—specifically, the *Cabbalistic* <note>Kabbalistic: relating to Jewish mystical traditions of scriptural interpretation, often involving the hidden meanings of letters and numbers</note> ones. Such is the interpretation found in the *Baal Turim* <note>A famous Torah commentary by Jacob ben Asher (c. 1269–1343)</note>: **"Shiloh shall come"** <note>Hebrew: *Yavo Shiloh* (**יבא שילה**)</note> and **"Messiah"** <note>Hebrew: *Mashiach* (**משיח**)</note>. That is, by **Gematria** <note>A system of calculation where Hebrew letters are assigned numerical values</note>, the words **"Shiloh shall come"** and the name **"Messiah"** yield the same numerical total: 358. From this, the author concludes that "Shiloh" is indeed the Messiah. This same Cabbalistic argument is cited in the *Chaskuni* <note>A 13th-century commentary by Hezekiah ben Manoah</note> and the *Venice Pentateuch* with the triple Targum <note>Aramaic translations of the Hebrew Bible</note> in the margin.

§. 54. Through another variety of Cabbala, which they call *Notaricon* <note>A method of forming new words using the initial or final letters of phrases, similar to an acrostic</note>, the name **JESU** <note>Hebrew: *Yeshu* (**ישו**)</note> is extracted from the initial letters of the three words **"Shiloh shall come, and to him..."** <note>Hebrew: *Yavo Shiloh Ve-lo* (**יבא שילה ולו**)</note>. This is noted by Galatinus <note>Pietro Galatino (c. 1460–1540), a Franciscan friar and Hebraist</note> in his work *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth* (Book 3, Chapter 20), citing the *Gali Razia* of *Rabbenu Hakkados* <note>Judah the Prince, the 2nd-century redactor of the Mishnah</note>. Truly, whether that book was ever seen by Galatinus or any other man **lies on the knees of five judges** <note>original Greek: ἐν πέντε κριτῶν γόνασι κεῖται; a classical idiom meaning the matter is still being decided or remains highly uncertain</note>. However, that Cabbalistic reading would have been more plausible if it represented the full name of our Savior, **Jesus** <note>Hebrew: *Yeshua* (**ישוע**)</note>, rather than the shortened form—which the Jews use to his disgrace, as Elias Levita <note>A 15th-century Hebrew grammarian</note> admits in his work *Tisbi*.

§. 55. Perhaps among the Jews such Cabbalistic arguments carry some weight, as they are based on a principle somewhat accepted by them. For this reason, **Ludovicus Carretus** <note>A 16th-century Jewish physician from Florence who converted to Christianity</note>, a physician and Jewish convert to Christianity, testified in his book *On the Visions of God* that he was converted to Jesus because he saw His name—*Jesu* <note>Hebrew: **ישו**</note>—secretly inscribed, as it were, in so many sacred structures and dimensions, and woven in a Cabbalistic manner into so many sayings concerning the Messiah. Meanwhile, among Christians—however much such things might piously delight if they are indeed true—they possess no strength as a logical argument.

§. 56. Especially since that **Gematria** is so constructed that it can elicit truth from falsehood just as easily as falsehood from truth. Indeed, the same *Baal Turim*, after he had "carved out" the name **Messiah** from the phrase **"Shiloh shall come,"** adds—as if immediately forgetting his own trail: **"Shiloh" in Gematria equals "Moses"** <note>Hebrew: *Moshe* (**משה**)</note>, **of whom it is written: "And he shall be the head of the people..."** <note>Hebrew: **שילה בגימט' משה רכתיב ביה ויהא ראשי עם שאו היתר יה**; The author is pointing out that the same numerical logic can be used to link Shiloh to Moses rather than the Messiah, thereby undermining the specificity of the argument</note>.

<summary>This page critiques the use of Kabbalistic methods, such as Gematria and Notaricon, to interpret the word "Shiloh." The author argues that while these mystical associations might be pious or persuasive to some, they lack the logical and grammatical rigor necessary for sound biblical scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>Cabbala, Kabbalah, Gematria, Notaricon, Baal Turim, Shiloh, Messiah, Ludovicus Carretus, Moses, numerology, Jewish-Christian polemics</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of mystical interpretations of Genesis 49:10. While the previous page addressed the numerical connection between "Shiloh" and "Messiah," this page examines a different Kabbalistic calculation linking the word to "Moses," before transitioning into an analysis of early Greek and Latin translations.</meta>

<margin>That is: "Judah to His sanctuary," as it is written: "The princes of Judah and their company" <note>original Hebrew: יהודה לקדשו דכתיב שרי יהודה רגמתם</note>. **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note>, by **Gematria** <note>The practice of assigning numerical values to letters to find hidden meanings</note>, is **Moses** <note>original Hebrew: משה</note> (both yielding the number 345). Concerning him [Moses], it stands written: "And he came with the heads of the people" (Deuteronomy 33:21). For at that time (namely, when the tribe of Gad came with the heads of the people), Judah was in the process of consecrating him, as it is written: "The princes of Judah and their company" (Psalm 68:27-28). Truly, this interpretation is "more bristly than butcher’s-broom, and viler than discarded seaweed!" <note>A quote from Virgil’s *Eclogues* (7.41-42) used here to describe a worthless or nonsensical explanation.</note> Rabbi Bechai reinforces this same idea [at folio 59. 1], and following him, the author of the *Tze'enah u-Re'enah* <note>A popular Yiddish-language collection of Jewish homilies and commentary for laypeople</note> [at folio 63. 2] says: The word **Shiloh** has a numerical value as great as **Moses**, meaning, so to speak, the word **Shiloh** points to **Moses**. From this, it is clear how little reliability one should expect from such **Kabbalah** <note>Jewish mystical tradition</note>.</margin>

§. 57. Therefore, having dismissed the Kabbalists, we meet with others, and above all the **Septuagint Interpreters** <note>The "Seventy," referring to the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note>, who translated the words **"until Shiloh comes"** <note>original Hebrew: עד כי יבא שילה</note> as: *until the things stored up for him come* <note>original Greek: ἕως ἂν ἔλθῃ τὰ ἀποκείμενα αὐטῷ</note>, or, according to others, *he to whom it is stored up* <note>original Greek: ᾧ ἀπόκειται</note>—namely, the **scepter** <note>original Hebrew: שבט (Shevet)</note>. The former reading was used by **Justin Martyr** in his *Dialogue with Trypho* (though he also recognizes the other), **Origen** in *Philocalia, chapter 1*, and **Eusebius of Caesarea** in *The Proof of the Gospel, books 3 and 8* (though he also has the other reading in books 2 and 7). It was also used by **Athanasius** in *On the Incarnation* (who himself has the other reading in *On the Passion and Cross of the Lord*), **Cyril** in the *Glaphyra*, and others among the Greeks.

The latter reading [*he to whom it is stored up*] was used by Saint **Ignatius**, **Irenaeus**, **Gennadius**, **Chrysostom**, **Cyril of Jerusalem** in *Catechesis 12*, **Theodoret** in *Question 110* (in whose manuscripts the version "he for whom it is stored up" also appears), and among the Latin writers, **Tertullian** in *On the Trinity*, **Ambrose**, **Jerome**, **Pope Leo**, and others. Regarding this variation, one should consult the notes of Flaminius Nobilius on the various readings of the Septuagint, and Patrick Young’s annotations on the Alexandrian manuscript of the Septuagint. Furthermore, Eusebius, in *The Proof of the Gospel, book 8*, is a witness that **Theodotion** <note>A 2nd-century Hellenistic Jewish scholar who translated the Hebrew Bible into Greek</note> retained the entire version of the Septuagint in verse 10 and followed the reading "the things stored up for him," while **Symmachus** <note>An author of another 2nd-century Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, though otherwise different in his version, followed the other reading: "he to whom it is stored up."

<summary>The author dismisses Kabbalistic numerology linking Shiloh to Moses as nonsensical and shifts to analyzing early Greek translations (the Septuagint) and their citations by Church Fathers. He focuses on two primary Greek interpretations: "the things stored up for him" and "he for whom it is stored up."</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Moses, Gematria, Kabbalah, Septuagint, Church Fathers, Justin Martyr, Origen, Eusebius, Theodotion, Symmachus, Genesis 49:10</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, moving from Kabbalistic numerical theories to the historical translations provided by the Septuagint and early Christian scholars.</meta>

§. 58. Truly, regarding that phrase **for whom it is reserved** <note>original Greek: ᾧ ἀπόκειται</note>, **Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the Renaissance</note> in his notes *on Eusebius* explains it as **in whom matters are hidden and concealed**. For **Justin Martyr** <note>An early Christian apologist (c. 100–165 AD)</note> had said in his *First Apology* that "Shiloh" contains **a significance of unknown things** <note>original Greek: ἀγνώστων σημασίαν</note>. Some draw another reading toward the same meaning: **the things laid up for him** <note>original Greek: τὰ ἀποκείμενα αὐτῷ</note>, as if it meant **to him for whom the things are laid up**. However, the first explanation seems simpler.

§. 59. We must rather look at what led the **Septuagint** <note>The earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, often abbreviated as LXX</note> to this version. Should we perhaps say they read something other than **Shiloh** <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note> in their manuscript? **Cappellus** <note>Louis Cappel (1585–1658), a pioneer of biblical textual criticism</note> asserts in *Critica Sacra, Book 5, Chapter 2* that they certainly read it as **shel-lo** <note>original Hebrew: שֶׁלּוֹ</note>, meaning **which is his**. But truly, while we would not deny they had that meaning of **shel-lo** in mind, we deny they read anything other than **Shiloh**. They simply thought the word was formed irregularly from **shel-lo**. This is also the view of **Rashi** <note>Rabbi Shlomo Itzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval Jewish commentator</note>, who reads it as "Shiloh" yet nonetheless explains it as **that the kingdom is his** <note>original Hebrew: שהמלוכה שלו</note>.

§. 60. Therefore, the illustrious **Andrew Rivetus** <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian</note> judges correctly in his *Exercise 178* that the Septuagint took **Shiloh** as meaning **which is his**, namely **the one for whom [these things] are reserved**. Nor is it necessary to assume they took the letter **Shin** <note>ש</note> to stand for **she** <note>שֶׁ, a relative pronoun prefix</note>, as **Wolfgang Musculus** <note>A 16th-century Protestant reformer and Hebraist</note> suggests on page 847. He says: *It appears they understood the word "Shiloh" thus: "Shin" for "who," "yod" for "is," and "lo" for "to him"; as if to say, "He who is promised to him and stored away for his proper time."* However, the dropping of the letter **vav** is too harsh, such a triple composition is unusual in common nouns, and the verb "to be" can easily be understood. Why then the **Shin**?

§. 61. We believe even less what **Leo Castrius** says in his *Apology for the Septuagint, Book 3*, that they read it as **shai lo** <note>original Hebrew: שַׁי לוֹ</note>, meaning **a gift promised to him**. For if they had intended that, why would they ignore what the text is said to contain (namely, the word **gift**) and instead express in their translation something the text does not have, but which is perhaps left to be understood or supplied?

§. 62. Furthermore, what we have said regarding the Septuagint must also be held regarding the **Chaldean Paraphrasts** <note>The Targums, which are ancient Aramaic translations/interpretations of the Hebrew Bible</note>, specifically **Onkelos** and the **Jerusalem Targum**, both of whom translate it as: **Whose is the kingdom**. Evidently, they also regarded **Shiloh** as standing for **shel-lo** (as **Rabbi Bechai**, **Rashi**, **Ibn Ezra**, **Solomon ben Melech**, and others have long noted concerning Onkelos) and understood or repeated the word **scepter** <note>original Hebrew: שבט</note> from the preceding clause.

<summary>This page analyzes the linguistic origins of the translation of "Shiloh," arguing that the Septuagint and Aramaic Targums viewed the word as a grammatical variation of "that which belongs to him," referring to the Messiah's right to the kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Septuagint, Onkelos, Rashi, Scaliger, Andrew Rivetus, Hebrew grammar, Messianic prophecy, Targum, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, moving from the Septuagint's interpretation to how other ancient versions—including the Arabic, Syriac, and Ethiopic—understand the word as "he to whom it belongs."</meta>

§. 63. In a similar way, it is said in the **Genesis Rabbah** <note>original: *Bereschit rabba*, an ancient collection of Jewish explanations on the book of Genesis</note>:
**Until Shiloh comes: until he comes to whom the kingdom belongs. And this matter is like a man who says to another: "Take this matter now until I grant you more later."** <note>original Hebrew: עד כי יבא שילה עד שיבא מי שהמלוכה שלו. וענין זה כאדם שאומר לאחר קח ענין זה עתה עד שאחלוך יותר</note>
That is: **Until Shiloh comes: until he comes whose the kingdom is. Furthermore, this matter is like a man who says to another: "Take this thing now, until I give you the rest."** Let us not add more now from the **Midrash Tanchuma** <note>another collection of early Jewish sermons</note> or others.

§. 64. Regarding **Saadia** <note>Saadia Gaon (882–942), a famous Jewish philosopher and translator</note> and the **Syriac interpreter**, nothing is more certain. For Saadia translates it as: **Until he comes who is his** <note>original Arabic: إِلَي أَنْ يَجِيءَ الَّذِي هُوَ لَهُ</note>, that is: **Until he comes whose he himself is** (**shel-lo** <note>original Hebrew: שֶׁלּוֹ, a combination meaning "which is his"</note>), understanding the object to be: **the scepter** <note>original Arabic: *al-qadib*</note> or **the decree** <note>original Arabic: *ar-rasm*</note>. Likewise, the Syriac: **Until he comes whose it is** <note>original Syriac: ܥܕܰܡܳܐ ܕ݁ܢܺܐܬ݂ܶܐ ܡܰܢ ܕ݁ܕ݂ܺܝܠܶܗ ܗ݈ܺܝ</note>, meaning **Until he comes, whose it is** (**shel-lo**), namely **the scepter** <note>original Syriac: *shabta*</note>. Nor is there any doubt that the **Ethiopic version** followed in the footsteps of the **Septuagint** <note>the early Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> and thus aimed at the same goal. Its words are these: **Until he finds what awaits him**, which the most noble **Job Ludolf** <note>1624–1704, a scholar who founded Ethiopic studies in Europe</note> in his *Ethiopic Lexicon, page 439*, translates as: **Until he finds what expects him, or what is prepared for him.** And why not translate it thus: **Until that should happen which is destined for him or remains for him** <note>referring to Ludolf’s Lexicon p. 114</note>, meaning **until that which is laid up for him should come** <note>original Greek: ἕως ἂν ἔλθῃ τὰ ἀποκείμενα αὐτῷ</note>.

§. 65. The illustrious **Angelo Canini** <note>1521–1557, a renowned Italian linguist</note>, in his *On the Obscure Passages of the New Testament, page 34*, did not hesitate to defend the version of the word "Shiloh" explained so far. He says: **To me, the reading "shilo," which the Septuagint and the Chaldean Paraphrast <note>The Targum, or Aramaic translation of the Bible</note> followed, is more pleasing.** And a little later: **That reading "shilo" fits Christ best, to whom all power is given in heaven and on earth.** The Arabic name for God, **Li**, points to the same meaning as this "shilo." However, the rules of grammatical analogy do not allow the form **Shileh** <note>original: שילה</note> to stand in for **Shilo** <note>original: שילו</note>. For that letter <term>Shin</term> <note>the 'sh' sound</note> ordinarily requires a <term>Segol</term> <note>a vowel mark representing an 'e' sound</note> followed by a <term>Dagesh</term> <note>a dot inside a letter indicating it should be emphasized or doubled</note>; nor, unless you use a <term>Patach</term> <note>an 'a' vowel sound</note>, does it allow any other vowel point before a letter capable of receiving a dagesh. Furthermore, it assumes an intolerable omission of words: **Until he comes to whom...** that is, to whom all things are stored up, or to whom everything belongs. But if you repeat the word **scepter** <note>original Hebrew: *shevet*, שבט</note> as a common element, it would mean: **whose is that scepter which departed from Judah.**

<summary>This page analyzes the word "Shiloh" through various ancient versions (Arabic, Syriac, Ethiopic, and Greek), arguing that it refers to the person "to whom the kingdom belongs." The author also examines the grammatical difficulties of deriving this meaning from the Hebrew letters, noting the technical rules of vowel points and letter emphasis.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Genesis Rabbah, Saadia Gaon, Job Ludolf, Angelo Canini, Ethiopic Bible, Syriac Bible, Hebrew grammar, Segol, Dagesh, biblical prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. The previous page discussed interpretations from the Septuagint and the Arabic and Syriac versions, specifically the idea of "he to whom it belongs." This page concludes the critique of certain Arabic etymologies before moving on to Jewish interpretations involving the concept of "gifts."</meta>

...is implied, I do not know how that would be very consistent with the spiritual Kingdom of Christ. But the Arabic name **Allah** <term>الله</term> is dragged in, as if by the neck <note>a Latin idiom for a forced or strained argument</note>, to explain the word **Shiloh** <term>שלה</term>. For it is certainly not fashioned from **al** <term>ال</term> and **lah** <term>له</term>, as Caninius <note>Angelo Canini (1521–1557), an Italian grammarian and linguist</note> thinks, but descends from the root **alaha** <term>أله</term>, meaning "he worshipped" or "revered"—a fact not unknown to those even slightly acquainted with Arabic.

§. 66. Now we move forward and turn our attention to those who derive **Shiloh** <term>שילה</term> from the noun **shai** <term>שי</term>, meaning "a gift" or "tribute," and the pronoun **lah** <term>לה</term> used in place of **lo** <term>לו</term>, meaning "to him." Rashi <note>the famous medieval French rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki</note> testifies that it is explained this way in the **Midrash Aggadah** <note>a collection of Jewish homiletic teachings</note>, citing the passage in **Psalm 76:11** [Hebrew v. 12]: **"Let them bring gifts to the Awesome One"** <note>original Hebrew: יובילו שי למורא</note>. Another passage in support of this view is brought forward from the **Genesis Rabbah** <note>original: *Bereschit Kezara*, a collection of ancient rabbinic interpretations of Genesis</note>, section 79, as cited by Raymond <note>Raymond Martini (c. 1220–1285), a Dominican friar and author of *Pugio Fidei*</note>, page 251: **"Until Shiloh comes: for the nations of the world are destined to bring a gift to the Messiah, the son of David, as it is said in Isaiah 18:7: 'At that time a gift will be brought to the Lord of Hosts.'"** <note>original Hebrew: עד כי יבא שילה שעתידין אומות העולם להביא דורון למשיח בן דוד שנאמר בעת ההיא יובל שי ליהוה צבאות</note>. Furthermore, Amandus Polanus <note>1561–1610, a German Reformed theologian</note> is not far from this opinion in his *Syntagma*, book 2, chapter 37.

§. 67. But this etymology is also unsatisfactory for more than one reason. For it quite boldly demands that **shai** <term>שי</term> (which nowhere else signifies a "gift" in this exact form) be used, and that a noun be combined with the personal pronoun **to him** <term>לו</term> without any precedent. Such a construction can only be explained through harsh omissions <note>the grammatical term "ellipses"</note>. Specifically, reading **Shiloh** <term>שילה</term> as "a gift to him"—that is, the one to whom gifts are brought—or: "until a gift comes to him," namely, to the scepter of Judah that was promised.

§. 68. Furthermore, if there are any who unravel the name **Shiloh** <term>שילה</term> into **She-Yah** <term>שליה</term>, meaning "He who is of GOD," namely the Son—as Johann Gerhard <note>1582–1637, a leading Lutheran scholastic theologian</note> reports on page 862—they must be quite bold in inventing etymologies. For indeed, it is easy to move any letters you wish whenever you like: to change silent letters into active ones, to treat the vowels and all grammatical rules as nothing, and finally to supply whatever meanings please you. If we do that, everything becomes possible—though I used to deny that such things could be done.

<summary>This page critiques various etymological derivations of the word "Shiloh," specifically rejecting a forced link to the Arabic "Allah" and analyzing the Jewish tradition that "Shiloh" means "a gift to him" (referring to the Messiah). The author expresses skepticism toward linguistically creative interpretations that ignore standard grammatical rules.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Messiah, etymology, Arabic, Hebrew grammar, Midrash Aggadah, Genesis Rabbah, Rashi, Johann Gerhard, Amandus Polanus</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Shiloh" (Genesis 49:10). The previous page ended by discussing a rabbinic interpretation from the Midrash Aggadah regarding "gifts" being brought to the Messiah. This page shifts to examine how various ancient versions—specifically the Arabic, Syriac, and Ethiopic—interpret the word as "he to whom it belongs," while also critiquing the grammatical feasibility of this reading.</meta>

§. 63. In a similar manner, it is said in the **Genesis Rabbah** <term>Bereschit rabba; an ancient collection of Jewish explanations on Genesis</term>:
**Until Shiloh comes: until he comes to whom the kingdom belongs. And this matter is like a man who says to another: "Take this thing now, until I give you more."** <note>original Hebrew: עד כי יבא שילה עד שיבא מי שהמלוכה שלו. וענין זה כאדם שאומר לאחר קח ענין זה עתה עד שאתנלך יותר</note>
That is: *Until Shiloh comes: until he comes whose is the kingdom. Furthermore, this business is similar to a man who says to another: "Accept this thing now, until I give you the rest."* We need not even add more from the **Midrash Tanchuma** <note>another collection of homiletic teachings on the Torah</note> or other sources.

§. 64. Regarding **Rabbi Saadia** <note>Saadia Gaon (882–942), a famous scholar and translator of the Bible into Arabic</note> and the Syriac translator, nothing is more certain. For the former translates it: **Until he comes to whom it belongs** <note>original Arabic: إِلَى أَنْ يَجِيءَ الَّذِي هُوَ لَهُ</note>, where one must understand "it" to refer to the **scepter** <note>original Arabic: القَضِيبُ</note> or the **decree** <note>original Arabic: الرَّسْمُ</note>. Likewise, the Syriac: **Until he comes, whose it is** <note>original Syriac: ܥܕܰܡܳܐ ܕ݁ܢܺܐܬ݂ܶܐ ܐܰܝܢܳܐ ܕ݁ܕ݂ܺܝܠܶܗ ܗ݈ܺܝ</note>, namely, the **kingdom** <note>original Syriac: ܡܰܠܟ݁ܽܘܬ݂ܳܐ</note>. Nor is there any doubt that the **Ethiopic** translator followed the tracks of the Septuagint <note>the early Greek translation</note> and aimed at the same goal. His words are these: *Iska: ama: Iyrakyb: zazynub: lotu:* which the most noble **Job Ludolf** <note>1624–1704, a pioneer of Ethiopic studies in Europe</note> in his *Ethiopic Lexicon*, page 439, translates as: **Until he finds what expects him, or what is prepared for him.** Or why not translate it thus: **Until that which is destined for him, or remains for him, comes to pass** (see his Lexicon, page 114), following the Greek phrase: **until the things laid up for him come** <note>original Greek: ἕως ἂν ἔλθῃ τὰ ἀποκείμεna αὐτῷ</note>.

§. 65. The illustrious **Angelus Caninius** <note>Angelo Canini (1521–1557), an Italian grammarian</note> did not hesitate to defend the version of the word **Shiloh** explained thus far in his *Notes on Obscure Places of the New Testament*, page 34. He says, *"The reading 'she-lo' [whose it is] which the Septuagint and the Chaldean Paraphrast followed, pleases me more."* And a little later: *"That 'she-lo' fits Christ perfectly, to whom all power in heaven and on earth is given."* Indeed, he even claims the Arabic name for God, **Allah** <note>original Arabic: ألله</note>, has the same meaning as that **she-lo** <term>שלו</term>. However, grammatical analogy does not allow **Shiloh** <term>שילה</term> to stand for **she-lo** <term>שלו</term>. For that letter **Shin** <term>ש</term> ordinarily requires a **Segol** <note>a Hebrew vowel mark</note> followed by a **Dagesh** <note>a dot indicating a double consonant</note>, and it does not permit any other vowel point before a doubleable letter, unless you except the **Patach** vowel. Furthermore, an intolerable omission <term>ellipsis</term> is being assumed: **Until he comes to whom...** that is, to whom all things are stored up, or to whom all things belong. But if you repeat the **Shin** from the general context, the meaning of **Shiloh** would be: **He to whom that belongs, which was about to depart from Judah...** <note>The text breaks off as it moves to the next page</note>

<summary>This page analyzes the interpretation of "Shiloh" as meaning "he to whom it belongs," citing support from the Genesis Rabbah, Saadia Gaon, and the Syriac and Ethiopic versions. The author then begins a grammatical critique of this theory, arguing that the Hebrew spelling does not support the contraction "she-lo."</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Genesis 49:10, Genesis Rabbah, Saadia Gaon, Job Ludolf, Ethiopic Bible, Syriac Bible, Septuagint, Angelus Caninius, Hebrew grammar, Messianic prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. The previous page discussed interpretations of the word as "he to whom it belongs" (the Messiah), referencing the Septuagint and ancient Near Eastern translations. This page critiques the forced use of Arabic roots and examines the theory that the name means "a gift to him."</meta>

...[of these secrets] is implied, I do not know how that would be very consistent with the spiritual kingdom of Christ. As for the Arabic word **prayer** <term>original Arabic: صلوة; salat</term>, it is dragged in as if by the neck <note>a Latin idiom for a forced or unnatural argument</note> to explain the word **Shiloh** <term>original Hebrew: שלה</term>. For it is certainly not compounded from the elements **sal** <note>original: صل</note> and **wa** <note>original: וة</note>, as Caninius <note>Angelo Canini (1521–1557), a famous Italian grammarian</note> supposes, but descends from the root **salá** <term>original Arabic: صلى; meaning he worshipped or revered</term>, a fact that those even lightly dipped in Arabic studies are not ignorant of.

§. 66. We now step forward and move toward those who derive **Shiloh** <term>שילה</term> from the noun **shai** <term>שי; meaning a gift or tribute</term> and the pronoun **lah** <term>לה</term> used in place of **lo** <term>לו; meaning "to him"</term>. Rashi <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval Jewish commentator</note> testifies that this is explained in the **Midrash Aggadah** <note>a collection of Jewish homiletic teachings</note>, and this is because of the passage in **Psalm 76:11**: **They shall bring gifts to the one who is to be feared.** <note>original Hebrew: יובילו שי למורא</note> Another passage is brought forward in support of this opinion from the **Bereschit Kezara** <note>likely a reference to a section of the Midrash Rabbah on Genesis</note> section 79, as cited by Raymundus <note>Raymundus Martini (c. 1220–1285), author of 'Pugio Fidei'</note> in part 2, chapter 4, number 4, page 251: **Until Shiloh comes; for the nations of the world are destined to bring a gift to the Messiah, son of David, as it is said: "In that time, a gift shall be brought to the Lord of Hosts."** <note>The author provides a translation of the Hebrew commentary which references Isaiah 18:7</note> Furthermore, Amandus Polanus <note>a 16th-century Reformed theologian</note> is not far from this opinion in his **Syntagma** <note>Theological Framework</note>, book 2, chapter 37.

§. 67. But this etymology <term>the study of the origin of words</term> is also displeasing for more than one reason. For it rather boldly demands that **shai** <note>שי</note>—which nowhere else signifies "gift" in this specific grammatical form—be used, and that the noun be joined with the personal pronoun **lah** <note>לה</note> without any precedent. Such a thing can only be declared through harsh ellipses <note>grammatical shortcuts where words are omitted and must be assumed</note>. Namely, they suggest **Shiloh** <note>שילה</note> stands for **a gift to him** <note>שי לה</note>—that is, the one to whom gifts are brought—or: **until the gift comes to him**, referring to the scepter promised to Judah.

§. 68. Furthermore, if there are those who reweave the name **Shiloh** <note>שילה</note> into **sheliyah** <note>שְׁלִיָה</note>, meaning **he who is of God** (that is, his son), as the Blessed Gerhard <note>Johann Gerhard (1582–1637), a leading Lutheran theologian</note> reports on page 862 of this work, they must be quite bold in manufacturing their word-origins. For if it is permitted to relocate letters wherever one pleases, to change silent letters into vowels, to hold vowels and all rules of linguistic analogy as nothing, and finally to supply whatever suits one's fancy, then anything could mean everything—and I deny that such a thing is possible.

<summary>This page critiques various forced etymologies for the name "Shiloh," specifically rejecting a connection to Arabic "prayer" and a Hebrew theory interpreting the name as "a gift to him." The author argues that these interpretations rely on flawed grammar and linguistic "gymnastics" that disregard the rules of the language.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Messiah, etymology, Midrash Aggadah, Rashi, Arabic linguistics, Hebrew grammar, gift-offering, Johann Gerhard, Isaiah 18:7, Psalm 76:11</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. Having previously dismissed derivations from Arabic roots and "shai" (gift), the author now examines and critiques theories linking the name to "Shalom" (peace) or to a "transgression" involving King Solomon.</meta>

§. 69. Nor is the conjecture of Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf (1561–1610), a Reformed theologian</note> much more successful, who thinks in the place cited that **Shiloh** <term>Hebrew: שילה</term> can be derived from **Shalom** <term>Hebrew: שלום; meaning peace</term>, because he [the Messiah] ought to bring peace to the whole world. However, that letter **M** <term>Hebrew: ם; the final letter of the root SH-L-M</term> can in no case disappear in derived words; for this reason, Salomon Glassius <note>Salomo Glassius (1593–1656), a Lutheran theologian known for his work on biblical philology</note> rightly criticized Polanus in his **Names of the Prophetic Messiah** <note>original: *Onomatol. Messiæ Prophet.*, page 220</note>.

§. 70. But **Rabbi Nissim** <note>Likely Nissim of Gerona (c. 1310–1375), a prominent Talmudist and philosopher</note>, though perhaps more stubbornly adhering to the rules of grammar, seems to have exhausted every possible explanation—if indeed he intended what **Abravanel** <note>Isaac Abravanel (1437–1508), a Jewish statesman and commentator</note> attributes to him: namely, that **Shiloh** signifies **"His Error."** The words of Abravanel regarding this matter are as follows:

<note>original Hebrew: וכפי הדרך הזה נראה לי יותר נכון לפרש שילה מגזרת וידבר אתו בשלו. ויכהו שם על השל שהוא לשון פשיעה ועון. יאמר שלא יסור שבט מיהודה וגו' עד שיבא חטאו שהוא פשע שלמה שאחר זה יסור ממנו שבט המלכות שהיה לו על כל ישראל ע"כ לו יקהת עמים, ר"ל שישארו עמו שני השבטים והוא יהודה ובנימין אשר כנה בשם עמים</note>

*According to this line of reasoning (explaining Rabbi Nissim’s opinion that the prophecy concludes with Solomon, due to whose lapse the tribes were split), it seems more correct to me that Shiloh should be explained by the analogy of those passages in 2 Samuel 3:27 and 6:7, where the words **shal** and **shelo** <term>original Hebrew: של & שלו</term> are understood in the sense of transgression and sin. Thus, he would be saying: "The scepter shall not depart from Judah, etc., until his sin comes," that is, the transgression of Solomon; for afterward, the scepter of the kingdom which he held over all the Israelites would depart from him. Nevertheless, to him shall be the **obedience of peoples** <term>original Hebrew: יקהת עמים; Yiqhat ammim</term>, meaning that two tribes would remain in his power, namely Judah and Benjamin, which he [Jacob] hints at by the name of "peoples."* These are the words of Abravanel.

§. 71. But Abravanel has wasted his effort. For it has not yet been proven that **Shiloh** signifies the same thing as **shal** or **shelo**. Even if this were granted, by what right is this suffix referred to Solomon, of whom no mention had preceded, and no occasion for thinking of him had arisen? Moreover, unless it is referred to Solomon—as it certainly cannot be—how does the **obedience of the peoples** fit with that "error," as we said above? Therefore, it is no wonder that Abravanel himself could hardly digest such a half-baked <note>original: *crudum*; literally "raw" or "undigested"</note> fiction.

<summary>This page critiques two failed etymological theories for the name "Shiloh": one linking it to "peace" (Shalom) and another, attributed to Rabbi Nissim and Abravanel, linking it to the "error" or sin of Solomon that divided the Kingdom of Israel.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Amandus Polanus, Salomon Glassius, Abravanel, Rabbi Nissim, Solomon, etymology, Hebrew grammar, Messianic prophecy, Genesis 49:10</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10. Having previously critiqued the derivation from "Shalom" (peace) due to grammatical issues with the letter 'M', the author now explores a more popular derivation from the root "shalah," which also relates to peace and tranquility.</meta>

§. 72. More weight should be granted to those who derive the name **Shiloh** <term>Hebrew: שילה</term> from the root **shalah** <margin>root: שלה</margin>, which means *to be quiet* or *to be tranquil*. In this sense, the name expresses the **Tranquil One**, the **peaceful, prosperous, and happy one**. It can also be understood in an active sense as the **Tranquillizer**, the **Peacemaker**, the **Savior** (many believe the title "Savior" originated from this root), or the **Preserver**. This opinion has been embraced by a vast number of authors, including theologians and linguists, both Catholic and Reformed, as well as those of our own tradition <note>The author refers to the Lutheran tradition</note>. Among these, we find:
*   **Jerome ab Oleastro** <note>A 16th-century Portuguese Dominican commentator</note> (whose view **Bonfrerius** favors); 
*   **François Vatable** and **Johann Ferus** in their commentaries on this passage; 
*   **Sebastian Castellio**; 
*   **Johannes Mercerus** <note>page 731</note>; 
*   **Wolfgang Musculus** <note>page 847</note>; 
*   **Sixtinus Amama** <note>in his *Antibiblica*, page 281</note>; 
*   **Andreas Rivetus** <note>page 872</note>; 
*   **Johann Heinrich Hottinger** <note>in his *Treasury of Philology*, page 161</note>; 
*   **Abraham Scultetus**; 
*   **Johannes Hoornbeek** <note>page 128</note>; 
*   **Henry Ainsworth** <note>in his notes on the English version</note>; 
*   **Jacob Alting** <note>in his work on Shiloh, page 174</note>; 
*   **Philip Melanchthon** <note>the famous collaborator of Luther</note>; 
*   **Johannes Brenz** <note>Volume 1, page 340</note>; 
*   **Johann Forster** <note>in his Hebrew Dictionary, page 855</note>; 
*   **Matthias Flacius Illyricus** <note>in his *Key to Sacred Scripture*, part 1, column 1143</note>; 
*   **Paul Fagius** <note>in his notes on the Targum</note>; 
*   **Valentin Schindler** <note>in his *Pentaglott Lexicon*, column 1865</note>; 
*   **Johann Avenarius** <note>Hebrew Dictionary, page 780</note>; 
*   **Erasmus Marbach**; 
*   **Johann Gerhard** <note>in his commentary on Genesis, page 862</note>; 
*   **Salomo Glassius** <note>in his *Prophetic Names of the Messiah*, page 220</note>; 
*   **Michael Walther**; 
*   the **Weimar scholars** <note>original: *Vinarienses*, referring to the authors of the Weimar Bible</note>; 
*   **Gualtperius**; 
*   **Johann Konrad Dannhauer**; 
*   **Theodor Hackspan**; 
*   **August Varenius**; 
*   **Johann Dilherr**; 
*   and **Georg Erasmus Phaletranus**, among others.

§. 73. It is also clear enough from an added interlinear note that the **Blessed Luther** had this same idea in mind when he translated the word as **"the Hero"** <gloss>German: *der Held*</gloss>. However, in the "golden commentary" which he published on Genesis—truly as one **inspired by God** <note>original Greek: θεοφορύμενος, "theophoroumenos"</note>—he follows a completely different path, as we shall see later.

§. 74. Furthermore, we do not at all begrudge this opinion to those who find pious delight in it. Indeed, it is very much in keeping with the **analogy of faith** <term>A principle of interpretation suggesting that unclear passages should be understood in light of the core, established doctrines of the faith</term>. After all, our Savior is truly our peacemaker. In Him, all things succeeded prosperously, contrary to the wisdom and power of this world, and against sin, death, the Devil, and all the gates of hell—over all of which He openly triumphed by His own power.

The author notes that while Luther initially favored this view, he later proposed a different interpretation in his commentary on Genesis.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from the prophecy in Genesis 49:10. The author is evaluating various etymologies, moving from the critique of the derivation from "Shalom" (peace) to examining a more controversial interpretation involving "error" or "sin."</meta>

§. 69. Nor is the conjecture of **Polanus** <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf, a Reformed theologian</note> much more successful, who <margin>in the place cited</margin> thinks that **Shiloh** can be derived from **Shalom** <note>original: שלום, meaning "peace"</note>, because he [the Messiah] ought to bring peace to the whole world. Indeed, that **Yod** <term>The Hebrew letter 'י', which often stays present in related words</term> of the root **shalam** <note>original: שלם</note> cannot, in any case, vanish in its derivatives; for this reason, **Salomo Glassius** <note>A Lutheran theologian known for his work on biblical philology</note> rightly criticized Polanus in his *Names of the Prophetic Messiah*, page 220.

§. 70. But **Rabbi Nissim**, being perhaps more strictly observant of grammar, seems to have exhausted all reasoning—if he indeed intended what **Abravanel** <note>Isaac Abarbanel, a famous 15th-century Jewish statesman and commentator</note> attributes to him: namely, that **Shiloh** signifies **"His Error."** The words of Abravanel on this matter are as follows: <note>original Hebrew: וכפי הדרך הזה נראה לי יותר נכון לפרש שילה מגזרת וידבר אתו בשלו. ויכהו שם על השל שהוא לשון פשיעה ועון. יאמר שלא יסור שבט מיהודה וגו' עד שיבא חטאו שהוא פשע שלמה שאחר זה יסור ממנו שבט המלכות שהיה לו על כל ישראל ע"כן לו יקהת עמים, ר"ל שישארו עמו שני השבטים והוא יהודה ובנימין אשר כנה בשם עמים:</note> that is: *“According to this way of explaining (namely, the opinion of Rabbi Nissim, who thinks the prophecy ends with Solomon, because of whose lapse the tribes were torn apart), it seems more correct to me that **Shiloh** be explained by analogy with those passages in 2 Samuel 3:27 and chapter 6:7, where **shelo** <note>שלו</note> and **shal** <note>של</note> are understood as terms for transgression and sin. Thus, Jacob would only be saying: ‘The scepter shall not depart from Judah, etc., until his sin comes’—that is, the transgression of Solomon—after which the scepter of the kingdom which he held over all Israel shall depart from him. Nevertheless, there shall still be for him the **obedience of peoples**, meaning that there will be left in his power two tribes, namely Judah and Benjamin, which he hints at by the name ‘peoples.’”* These are the words of Abravanel.

§. 71. But Abravanel has wasted his effort. For it has not yet been proven that **Shiloh** signifies the same thing as **shal** or **shelo**. Even if it were granted, by what right would the suffix [the “his” in “his error”] be referred to Solomon? No mention of him had preceded this, and there was no occasion for thinking of him. Furthermore, unless it is referred to Solomon (which certainly cannot be done), how does the “obedience of the peoples” fit with that “error,” as we said above? Therefore, it is no wonder that Abravanel himself could hardly digest such a raw invention.

§. 72. More weight should be given to those who derive the name **Shiloh** from **shalah** <note>original: שָׁלָה</note>, meaning *he was quiet* or *he was tranquil*, so that it expresses the **Tranquil One, the peaceful, the prosperous, the happy one**, or effectively the **Tranquillizer, the peacemaker, the Savior** (many believe the title "Savior" originated from this) the **Preserver**, and so on. This opinion has been embraced by by far the most authors, both theologians and philologists, both Roman Catholic and Reformed, as well as our own <note>the author's fellow Lutherans</note>. Among these, the following come to our aid: **Jerome of Oleastro** (to whom **Bonfrère** is more favorable); **François Vatable** and **Johann Ferus** *on this passage*; **Sebastian Castellio** *on this passage*; **Mercerus** *page 731*; **Wolfgang Musculus** *page 847*; **Sixtinus Amama** in his *Antibiblica Biblica, page 281*; **Rivet** *on this passage, page 872*; **Hottinger** in his *Treasury of Philology, page 161*; **Scultetus** in his *Evangelical Exercises, in the place cited*; **Johannes Hoornbeek** *in the place cited, page 128*; **Ainsworth** in his *Notes to the English Version*; **Alting** in his *On Shiloh, page 174*; **Melanchthon** <note>Philipp Melanchthon, a key figure in the Protestant Reformation</note> *on this passage*; **Johannes Brenz** in *Volume 1 of his Works, Commentary on this passage, page 340*; **Forster** in his *Hebrew Dictionary, page 855*; **Flacius** in his *Key to the Holy Scriptures, part 1, column 1143*; **Fagius** in his *Notes on the Targum on this passage*; **Schindler** in his *Pentaglot Lexicon, column 1865*; **Johann Avenarius** in his *Hebrew Dictionary, page 780*; **Erasmus Marbach** *on this passage, page 430*; **Gerhard** in his *Commentary on Genesis, page 862*; **Glassius** in his *Names of the Prophetic Messiah, page 220*; **Walther** in his *Biblical Exercise on the Prophecy of Jacob, page 15*; the **Weimar Editors** <note>original Latin: Vinarienses, referring to the scholars of the Weimar Bible</note> *on this passage*; **Gualtperius** in his *Collation of Versions on Genesis, page 832*; **Dannhauer** in his *The Road to Christ, page 832*; **Hackspan** in his *Biblical Notes, Part 1, page 260*; **August Varenius** in his *Annual Disputation on the Scepter not being taken from Judah before Shiloh, conclusion 12*; **Dilherr** in his *Questions on the Birth of Christ*, and with him **G. Er. Phaletranus** in his *Treatise on the Oracle of Jacob, chapter 7*, and others.

§. 73. And that the Blessed **Luther** looked back at the same point when he translated it as **"the Hero"** <note>original German: der Held</note> is clear enough from an added interlinear note; although in the golden Commentary which he published on Genesis, truly **divinely inspired** <note>original Greek: θεοφορούμενος, transliterated theophoroumenos</note>, he insists on a completely different path, as we shall see below.

§. 74. Furthermore, we in no way begrudge that opinion to those who piously delight in it. For it is very much in conformity with the **analogy of faith** <note>the principle that any interpretation of Scripture must agree with the whole of Christian doctrine</note>, since in truth our Savior is our peacemaker, for whom all things succeeded prosperously against the wisdom and power of the world, against sin, death, the Devil, and even all the gates of hell, over which he triumphed openly through himself.

<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the etymology of "Shiloh," specifically examining whether it stems from the Hebrew root for peace or rest.</meta>

90
M
<meta>catchword: which</meta>

Nothing is truer, nothing is more certain, nothing is more desired for Christians to hear.

§. 75. Meanwhile, we would hardly assert that the root **shalah** <note>original: שלח; the author is likely referring to the root שָׁלָה (shalah), meaning to be at rest</note>, and the names derived from it, **shalev** <note>שלו, meaning "at ease"</note> and **shalvah** <note>שלוה, meaning "tranquility"</note> (which they compare to **Shiloh** <note>שילה</note>) formally imply such a thing. Rather, they express a gentle rest which one enjoys for oneself, secure from others, rather than successful triumphs against enemies. To say it as it is, the words **shalev**, **shalvah**, and **shalah** speak of the absence of all enemies and the security arising from that, not an attack by those present and the triumphs that follow; this will be clear to the eye from other passages: *Jeremiah 22:21; 49:31; Ezekiel 16:49; Zechariah 7:7; Psalm 30:7; Psalm 122:7; Proverbs 1:32; 17:1; Job 3:25; 16:12; 21:23; 1 Chronicles 4:40.*

§. 76. Furthermore, **Grammatical Analogy** <term>Analogia Grammatica: the principle that words within a language follow predictable patterns of spelling and structure</term> is less favorable to these most learned men. For the entire Hebrew language recognizes no absolute noun ending in the letter **He** <note>ה</note> preceded by the vowel **Cholem** <note>the 'o' sound</note>. Therefore, those who compare **Shiloh** with nouns such as **kidor** <note>Job 15:24</note>, **tzinok** <note>Jeremiah 29:26</note>, **kitor** <note>Genesis 19:28</note>, **kimosh** <note>Hosea 9:16</note>, and any others, gain nothing at all, because the final **He** is not present in those words. It cannot be denied, however, that not everything that applies to words ending in a movable letter can be immediately applied to those ending in a "quiescent" or silent **He**, unless the evidence of examples settles the dispute.

§. 77. **Hakspanius** <note>Theodor Hackspan (1607–1659), a noted Lutheran theologian and Orientalist</note> indeed seemed to have untied this knot when, on page 260, he brought forward the name **Shlomoh** <note>Solomon - שלמה</note>. But even so, he did not settle the matter. For first, there is a huge difference between common nouns—the kind we have been discussing—and proper names, which cannot be so rigorously forced under the rules of grammarians, much less establish the standard for common nouns. Secondly, **Shlomoh**, when viewed in its origin, possesses a suffix; David wished to call his son **HIS PEACE** <note>original: PACEM SUAM</note> *2 Samuel 12:24*. Just as it is not unusual elsewhere for proper names to have suffixes in their origin (even if they lose them as proper names once the name is established), for example: **Ben-Ammi** <note>Genesis 19:36, "son of my people"</note>, **Ben-Oni** <note>Genesis 35:18, "son of my sorrow"</note>, **Hephzibah** <note>2 Kings 21:1, "my delight is in her"</note>, **Yakhdo** <note>1 Chronicles 5:14</note>, and the place names **Enam**, **Ono**, etc.

§. 78. **Ludovicus de Dieu** <note>Louis de Dieu (1590–1642), a leading Dutch Protestant scholar of Middle Eastern languages</note> put forward an entirely singular opinion concerning **Shiloh**, which he alone has embraced without followers. His words are <margin>on this passage, page 53.</margin> *Since everyone is forced to admit that this form is unusual in the Hebrew language...*

<summary>The author critiques the theory that "Shiloh" derives from the Hebrew root for tranquility (shalah), arguing that the word's grammatical structure (ending in a "He" with a "Cholem") is nearly unique and does not follow standard Hebrew patterns for common nouns.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Hebrew grammar, etymology, tranquility, Hackspanius, Ludovicus de Dieu, proper names vs common nouns</keywords>

<meta>The text continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, transitioning from the Hebrew root for "peace" to potential Arabic and Hebrew roots meaning "seed" or "offspring."</meta>

91
M 2
<meta>catchword: which</meta>

As for the word that remains for us to consider, we readily seek assistance from the Arabic language, where the verb *sayl* <term>سيل</term> meaning "to flow" and the noun *sayl* <term>سيل</term> meaning "a flow," or "a discharge of blood" <note>original: سيل الدم (sayl al-dam)</note>, are common. With the suffix, it becomes "his flow" or "his discharge" <note>original: سيله (sayluhu)</note>. For the Arabs are accustomed to using the letter **He** <note>ה</note> for the Hebrew suffix **He**, which Hebrew also imitates in many places. I translate it, therefore: *until his discharge comes—that is, his seed, which shall have flowed from him.* Thus says that author <note>referring to Louis de Dieu, a 17th-century Dutch Orientalist</note>.

§. 79. Indeed, we are not unwilling to admit that meaning of the Arabic word. *Sala* <term>سال</term> is "to flow," and *sayl* <term>سيل</term> is "a flow, specifically a torrent or canal." Thus, Dominicus Germanus of Silesia <note>a Franciscan scholar and author of an Arabic-Latin dictionary</note> in his *Fabric of the Arabic Language* (p. 813) explains *sala* <term>سال</term> as "to flow, to strain," and on page 474, he defines *sayl* <term>سيل</term> as a "flow common to all fluid things," and on page 488, "a little ditch, see aqueduct," etc.; compare the same on pages 33, 329, 1030. See also Raphelengius's *Arabic Lexicon* (p. 198 and 229), Golius's *Arabic Lexicon* (column 1244), Fabricius on the *Poem of Abu'l-Ala* (p. 139), and Pococke on *al-Tughrai* (p. 118). Nevertheless, this seems to be sought from too far away: the "discharge" of Judah is his seed; his seed is his son; the son is the Messiah. Therefore, the "discharge" of Judah is the Messiah. De Dieu would have said something of value if he had proved that the Arabs restricted the word *sayl* <term>سيل</term> to mean a son or at least "seed" in any context.

§. 80. It remains to survey the opinion of those who derive *Shiloh* <note>שילה</note> from *shil* <note>שיל</note>, meaning "a tender infant" or "embryo," and with the suffix, explain it as "his son," resulting in this meaning: *until his little son comes—that is, Judah's son, specifically the Messiah par excellence* <note>original: κατ' εξοχην (kat' exochen)</note>. And this, at last, whether we call it our Guiding Star or our North Star <note>original: "Helice or Cynosura," navigational metaphors for the Great Bear and Little Bear constellations</note>, we feel shining upon us amidst so many waves of conflicting opinions.

§. 81. Certainly, we do not think anyone would be so deranged or unfair as to not admit that this sense is most convenient, provided it can be shown to arise from the principles of the language. This, therefore, is the last thing that remains to be done. At the outset, it does not seem necessary for us to be so anxious about a verbal root, since the Hebrew language does not reject primitive nouns of this kind. We could, however, imagine an unused verb *shul* <note>שול</note>, from which *shil* <note>שיל</note> is formed, just as *shir* <note>song</note> comes from *shur*. But since the verb *shul* is found nowhere, and the noun *shil* no longer occurs in the Holy Scriptures, the matter itself suggests that we must judge the meaning of both from related roots and words.

<summary>The author critiques Louis de Dieu's Arabic-based etymology of "Shiloh" as "discharge/seed," finding it too indirect. Instead, he proposes that "Shiloh" derives from a term for a "tender infant" or "son," identifying it as the most promising interpretation of the Messianic prophecy.</summary>
<keywords>Shiloh, Messianic prophecy, etymology, Arabic language, Hebrew roots, Louis de Dieu, embryo, Judah, philology</keywords>

<meta>The text continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, moving from Arabic comparisons to Hebrew grammatical rules regarding the permutation of letters and the specific meaning of "offspring" or "afterbirth."</meta>

92
<meta>catchword: בן (son)</meta>

By this means, the celebrated **Johann Forster** <note>Johann Forster (1496–1556) was a German Lutheran theologian and Hebraist who authored a famous Hebrew Lexicon</note> successfully overcame many, and indeed most, difficulties in his *Lexicon*.

§. 82. Truly, the "Priscianus of the Hebrews," **Rabbi David Kimchi** <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), also known as Radak, was a foundational medieval Hebrew grammarian and exhgete. Priscian was a famous Latin grammarian, used here as a title of honor for Kimchi's expertise</note>, taught this same principle long ago in his *Grammar* (book 31, column 2) with these words: <gloss>This is the way of the Hebrews: to use a verb for a single matter whose letters resemble the letters of another verb, even if they are reversed in the order of their letters; both will be used to express the same meaning.</gloss> <note>original Hebrew: כן דרך העברים לאמ' בענין אחד הפעל שידמו אותיותיו לאותיות פעל אחר אף על פי שהם הפוכים בסדר אותיותיהם יאמרו שניהם בענין אחד</note> That is: *This is the custom of the Hebrews, that they apply a verb to one thing whose letters agree with the letters of another verb, both of which express the same sense, even if the order of the letters is changed.* He is speaking, moreover, about the permutation of classes in imperfect verbs <term>imperfect verbs</term> <note>In Hebrew grammar, these are "weak" verbs where certain letters like 'waw' or 'yod' can disappear or change</note>, and he strives to prove with many examples that the meaning is not changed by this.

§. 83. Therefore, an appeal from the roots **shul** <note>שול</note> and **shil** <note>שיל</note> to **shalach** <note>שלח - to send</note> and its derivatives is entirely valid, since it is well established that those two classes for the most part sustain the same or a similar meaning. For example: *sarah* and *shur* / *buvah* and *buz* / *chamah* and *hum* / *dakah* and *duch* / *damah* and *dum* / *shatah* and *shut* / *rahah* and *rur* / *kafah* and *kuf*, etc. Furthermore, those who correctly weigh the affinity of sound and what we noted above regarding the words *shalu*, *shali*, *shaluch*, etc., will easily grant that no derivative from *shalach* refers more to our word **Shiloh** than **shilyah** <note>שליה - typically meaning afterbirth or offspring</note>.

§. 84. Indeed, most scholars explain **shilyah** in *Deuteronomy 28, verse 57* <note>The OCR reads 27:27, but the biblical reference for 'shilyah' is Deut 28:57</note> as the *chorion*, the *afterbirth*, or the *placenta* <term>secundina</term>. It is used in this sense among the Talmudic writers in *Berachot* (folio 6, 1), *Niddah* (folio 26, 1), and elsewhere. However, others prefer that Moses intended a recently born fetus, named after the afterbirth; the <term>connection</term> <note>original Greek: συνάφεια (synapheia)</note> from verses 53 to 57 strongly favors this view. To this we add that in the *Mishnah Chullin* (chapter 4), *shilyah* is understood in this way.

§. 85. From what has been said, it remains that **shil**, when compared to **shilyah** according to grammatical laws, denotes a *fetus or a recently born infant, emerging, as it were, from the afterbirth*. To this is added the masculine suffix of the third person, and thus arises **shilo** <note>שילו</note>, and from there—by changing the *Vau* into a *He* (which is not so unusual, as it occurs twice in the following verse, namely in *sutoh* and *iroh*)—comes **Shiloh** <note>שילה</note>. For we do not listen to those who make *Shiloh* an absolute noun or adjective. Thus, *Shiloh* is explained as: *His recently born little son—namely, Judah's.*

§. 86. We are also not slightly confirmed in this opinion by the fact that from *shalal*, a likewise related root, comes *a mature embryo or fetus*...

<summary>This page explores the etymology of "Shiloh" by linking it to the Hebrew word for "afterbirth" or "offspring" (shilyah). Using rules of grammatical permutation cited from David Kimchi, the author argues that Shiloh refers specifically to a "newborn son" of the line of Judah.</summary>
<keywords>David Kimchi, Johann Forster, Hebrew grammar, Shiloh, etymology, afterbirth, fetus, Messiah, Deuteronomy 28:57, Talmud, Mishnah</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>The text continues the philological investigation into the name "Shiloh" from Genesis 49:10, exploring how linguistic variations in Hebrew and Arabic roots support the translation of the name as "his son" or "offspring."</meta>

93

A **son of nine months** <note>original Hebrew: בן תשעה חדשים</note>, *a nine-month-old*, and thus one near to birth, is called in the Talmudic writings a <term>sheliyl</term> <note>original Hebrew: שליל; an embryo or fetus that is fully formed</note>. See the *Tractate Cholin in the Mishnah, chapter 7, at the beginning; Zevachim, folio 35.1; and elsewhere.* Now, it is clear from many examples that verbs with "weak" letters <note>original Hebrew: נחי״ו; referring to verbs containing letters like Waw or Yod that often drop out or change</note> and **doubled verbs** <note>original Hebrew: כפולים; verbs where the second and third root letters are the same</note>, as well as the names derived from them, can exchange their forms while preserving their meaning. For example: *goz* and *gazah* [to cut], *mut* and *mot* [to shake], *ramam* and *rum* [to be high], *garar* and *gur* [to drag or sojourn], *baqaq* and *buq* [to empty], *qatat* and *qut* [to loathe], *tsarar* and *tsur* [to bind], *makak* and *muk* [to be low], *rakak* and *ruk* [to be tender], and so forth. Why then is it strange if **Shiloh** <note>שילה</note> approaches the meaning of **sheliyl** <note>שליל</note>?

§. 87. Here, the Rabbis are also assisted by an **Arabism**, from which even pure Hebrew today (which was succeeded by the dialect of the Mishnah) receives no small measure of support. For in Arabic, <term>Sals</term> is *a male offspring, specifically one recently born, as if extracted from the mother*, from the root <term>Sala</term>, meaning *he led out or extracted*, as Golius reports from Al-Jawhari in column 1194. We wished to point this out so that no one would suspect that the word **Shiloh** was merely an invention of the Rabbis.

§. 88. A **recapitulation** <note>original Greek: Ἀνακεφαλαίωσιν</note> of these statements is provided by the words of **Rabbi David Kimchi** in his *Book of Roots, chapter 505, on the root sh-y-l:* "Until **Shiloh** comes, that is, *his son*." Shortly after: "And this is from the meaning of the word **ubeshilyatah** <note>original Hebrew: ובשליתה; 'and toward her afterbirth'</note> in *Deuteronomy 28:57*. Although the root of this word is **shalah** <note>שלה</note>, nothing prevents there being two roots with the same meaning. Furthermore, our Rabbis here use a word (under a different form) arising from two roots, one with a weak letter and one doubled. For they say (*Mishnah Cholin, chapter 4*): 'If anyone slaughters a beast and finds **hashilyah** <note>the afterbirth</note>, a person of refined soul <note>one not easily nauseated</note> may eat it' (meaning, without a doubt, not the placenta itself, but the fetus within the placenta). And elsewhere they say (*Mishnah Cholin, chapter 7*): 'And it is customary to observe (the sciatic nerve) in a **sheliyl**' (in an embryo). Therefore, if **Shiloh** likewise carries that meaning (of an embryo or offspring), there will be a triple root with the same meaning: **shul**, **shalah**, and **shalal** <note>שול שלה שלל</note>, just as with the roots for 'high'—*rum*, *ramah*, and *ramam*—as we wrote in our *Grammar*."

§. 89. Furthermore, the interpretation of the word **Shiloh** handed down thus far is consistent with the "analogy of faith." He who is called here the **tender son of Judah** is elsewhere called the *seed of Abraham* in *Genesis 22:18*; the **Branch** <note>original Hebrew: צמח; Tzemach</note> in *Zechariah 3:8 and 6:12*; the **sprout of David** in *Jeremiah 23:5*; the **rod** *from the stem of Jesse and a branch from his roots* in *Isaiah 11:1*; and *a child born to us, a son given to us* in *Isaiah 9:6*. This is, of course, on account of his humble beginnings...

...beginnings in his assumed human nature. Nor is it without mystery that the holy Old Man <note>Jacob, who is delivering the prophecy in Genesis 49</note>, though he could have used another title for the son, chose one that specifically referred to the mother. There is no doubt that he intended to point toward **that seed of the woman** mentioned in *Genesis 3:15* and the **birth from a virgin** revealed after his own time in *Isaiah 7:14*. Blessed Luther <note>Martin Luther</note> says that *this grammatical explanation pleases him, and he rests in it with the greatest confidence* in his commentary on this passage (*page 657*).

§. 90. As for the other mysteries that others draw from this passage, we leave it to them to prove their claims. For instance, Alphonso Tostado <note>A Spanish bishop and prolific commentator</note> notes in his commentary on *Genesis (page 743)* that the meaning flows from the Hebrew: *until the son comes—namely, he who is the Son by excellence, the Son of God the Father, from whom all sonship in heaven and on earth derives.* Rabbi Bechai, following the *Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism or Kabbalah</note>, says that the addition of the letter *He* <note>The Hebrew letter ה</note> in the word **Shiloh** <note>original: שילה</note> *hints at the final He of the Tetragrammaton* <note>The sacred four-letter name of God, YHWH</note>. Dr. Johann Tarnovius mentions in his *Biblical Exercises (book 2, page 545)* that the letter *He* *is silent at the end so that we may learn to rest* <note>A play on the grammatical term "quiescent" (resting) for the letter He and the Latin word "quiescere" (to rest)</note> *in such a stupendous miracle and a birth outside the natural order of things.* Porchetus, Galatinus, Paul Weidner in his *Principal Points of the Christian Faith (Treatise 3, page 409)*, Hammer, and others believe the grammatical suffix is feminine and refers not to Judah, but to the Virgin Mary. Dr. Johann Tarnovius (*page 547*) says the suffix is feminine according to its consonant but masculine according to its vowel, so that the Messiah is hinted at as being, as God, <term>without mother</term> <note>original Greek: ἀμήτωρ (amētōr)</note>, but as man, <term>without father</term> <note>original Greek: ἀπάτωρ (apatōr)</note>. These and other such ideas are indeed piously conceived, but they acknowledge no real foundation in the actual text.

§. 91. Meanwhile, the Jewish commentators agree with us on the main point of the matter, such as the Targum of Jonathan <note>An Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note>, which emphatically expressed **Shiloh** as *the youngest of his sons* (though we assert, against Buxtorf’s *Chaldean Grammar (page 397)*, that he likely had our roots *Shiloh* and *Shelyah* <note>referring to offspring or the afterbirth</note> in mind, rather than defining it as the "smallest of sons" after whom birth ceased). Others who agree include Rabbi Bechai, Aben Ezra, Rabbi Levi, Arama, Solomon ben Melech, and others on this passage; and Rabbi David Kimchi. Among the Catholics, we find Raymundus Martinus in his *Dagger of Faith (Part 2, chapter 4)*, Joseph Voisin of Bordeaux in his *Observations (page 269)*, Porchetus in his *Victory Against the Impious Hebrews (Part 1, chapter 2)*, and Petrus Galatinus...

...in the place cited, Paul Weidner, a Jew-turned-Christian, and others. From the Reformed tradition: Calvin in this place, page 233; Tremellius, Junius, and Piscator in this place; Paraeus in this place, Volume 1 of his *Works*, page 453; Spanheim in *Evangelical Doubts*, Part 1, doubt 22, page 132; Buxtorf in his *Hebrew Lexicon*, page 811; Gregorius Gregorii of Frankfurt in *Holy Lexicon*, number 410; to whom must be added the Dutch <note>original: "Belgae"</note> on this passage. Finally, from our own <note>the Lutheran tradition</note>: the Blessed Luther in the Wittenberg Latin Edition, Volume 6, in his commentary on this place, folio 657, and in the Jena German Edition, Volume 8, *On the Lineage of Christ*, folio 144 and following; Gesner in this place, page 960; David Chytraeus in this place, page 515; Lyser in *Joseph*, on this place, page 508; Tarnovius in the place cited; Johann Quistorp in *Biblical Annals*, page 70; Müller in *Judaism*, page 631 and following; Havemann in *Way-Lamp*, page 111; the Danish Scholiast <note>a scholar who writes marginal notes</note> on this place (meaning "his lineage" <note>original Danish: "hans byrd"</note>); and many others, whose words there is no time to cite now.

§. 92. Let us see, however, what greatly holds back those who think differently from our opinion. First, they desire examples from Holy Scripture where *Shiloh* <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note> signifies "son." (Compare Blessed Rungius in his *Lectures on Genesis*, page 1411). But that seems to be an excess of rigor. For since the name *Shiloh* (or with the grammatical suffix, *Shilto*) occurs only this once in the whole of Holy Scripture in this specific form, there can be no similar examples. Nevertheless, the path to other means of interpretation <note>original Greek: ἑρμηνείας</note> is not thereby closed. Otherwise, the opinion of those who interpret it as "The Peacemaker" or "The Savior" would be pressed by no less difficulty, since Holy Scripture provides no examples where *Shiloh* has that specific meaning.

<margin>Page 35.</margin> §. 93. Nor are we greatly moved by what Caninius says, who claims those who "dream" that *Shiloh* is "son" rely solely on conjecture. For if conjectures are a permitted and accepted means of investigating meaning, what remains to us in the explanation of Holy Scripture besides conjectures? All learned men admit that in the absence of a root and similar examples, such a means is the comparison <note>original Greek: σύγκρισιν</note> of roots and related words, either in the same language or in kindred ones. However, Caninius would have done better if he had not dreamed up his own "dream" regarding the name *Shiloh*.

§. 94. Others complain of circuitous explanations. Sixtinus Amama says on page 287: "That common interpretation, that **SHILOH** means 'his son,' cannot be explained except through many circuitous steps. First, *Shiloh* [שילה] would be for *Shilo* [שילו], which is not without example. Then *Shilo* would be for *Shil lo* [שיל לו], and this again for *She-yalad lo* [שילד לו], just as *benah* is for *benanah*. Third, it would be a metonymy <note>a figure of speech where a concept is referred to by the name of something closely associated with it</note>." Thus, the Blessed Dannhauer, in *The Way-Finder*, page 832, calls that same interpretation "involved in circuitous steps" and "more daring than tropes and anomalies," and Wolfgang Musculus on page 847 thinks it does not fit well with the simplicity of the Mosaic style of writing. However, there is no need for those circuitous steps which Amama invents. We do not say that *Shiloh* is for *Shilo*, nor that for *She-yalad lo*, nor do we even establish a metonymy. Rather, we hold *Shiloh* to be a name distinct from *shilyah* <note>original Hebrew: שליה, meaning afterbirth or the membrane surrounding a fetus</note>, yet of a related meaning, as is common in similar cases. And although *shilyah* sometimes denotes the "afterbirth" <note>original Latin: "secundina"</note> among the Rabbis, in Holy Scripture nonetheless...
