<meta>The previous page concluded by arguing that the lack of other scriptural examples for the word "Shiloh" should not prevent us from interpreting it as "son," especially since the word is a unique grammatical form in the Bible. This page continues the refutation of scholars who find the "son" or "offspring" interpretation problematic.</meta>

...which we think should be explained by interpreting *Shiloh* <note>original Hebrew: שילה</note> as "offspring."

§. 95. Furthermore, Sixtinus Amama <note>a Dutch Hebraist, 1593–1629</note> (on page 282) fears that "afterbirth" <note>original Latin: "secundæ," referring to the placenta, which some scholars linked etymologically to the word Shiloh</note> is less appropriate for a man; so too does Tarnovius, who is otherwise of the same opinion as us <note>original Greek: ὁμόψηφος</note> (page 548). But we do not attribute the afterbirth to Judah himself, but rather the newborn infant coming forth from the afterbirth—unless, perhaps, a son cannot be attributed to his father.

§. 96. Others are troubled by the fact that afterbirth is common to all people, and thus it would grant the Messiah no special status. So argues Phaletranus (page 88). On the contrary, Christ had to be like us in all things, except for sin. Yet it must not be denied that the Holy Spirit had His own reasons why He preferred to use *Shiloh* here rather than simply "his son" <note>original Hebrew: בנו (*beno*)</note>; these reasons one may weigh in the fear of the Lord.

§. 97. As for the absurdity that Altingius <note>Jacob Alting, 1618–1679, a Dutch philologist</note> objects to (*Book 3, Chapter 8*), as if our opinion results in this meaning: *that there would be sons from Judah until his son comes*—who does not see that this arises from his own fundamental error <note>original Greek: πρώτῳ ψεύδει</note>? It is as if the "scepter" <note>original Hebrew: שבט (*shebet*)</note> refers merely to a tribal reckoning or a succession of sons in the posterity of Judah. From this principle also flows that uncertainty which he fears: that they would have had to expect the end of the tribe in every single generation.

§. 98. The same Altingius thinks that in this way the Messiah could not have been distinguished from the other sons of Judah, especially the illustrious ones like David, Solomon, or Zerubbabel. The same thing, of course, was to be feared when He was called "the seed of the woman," "the seed of Abraham," "the branch of David," or "the child or son" in an absolute sense, etc. Whatever the case may be, it could have been clear enough to Altingius from the circumstances of the text that neither David, nor Solomon, nor Zerubbabel, nor anyone else was intended in this place.

§. 99. Let the wise judge how redundant it would be to call the Messiah the "son of Judah," even if it is placed in a prophetic sense according to Altingius: *There will be a continuous succession of sons in the posterity of Judah until his son—namely the Messiah—comes in a preeminent sense* <note>original Greek: κατ᾽ ἐξοχὴν</note>. Meanwhile, Altingius has not yet sufficiently proven that the word "scepter" implies what he claims it does, nor could he free his own hypothesis from redundancy in *Book 3, Chapter 3*. Let the learned judge.

§. 100. And these are the things which it seemed good to bring forward at this time regarding *Shiloh*. We have followed the truth, not the authority of any man, which truth we hope we have reached. However, we will yield to those who point out more correct views with reason. Man is a creature prone to slipping into error <note>original Greek: Εὐόλισθον πρὸς ἁμαρτίαν πρᾶγμα</note>. Perhaps I have fallen; he who went before me fell; he who follows us will fall—this is our shared lot.

->*If I have said anything well, Christ, the glory is Yours.*<-

<note>The original Hebrew תושלבע is an acronym for *Tam Venishlam Shevach La'el Borei Olam*, a traditional Jewish and academic formula for the end of a manuscript.</note>

<summary>In this concluding section, the author defends the interpretation of "Shiloh" as "his son" or "offspring" against objections from scholars like Alting and Amama, arguing that the term emphasizes Christ's true humanity. The text ends with a humble acknowledgment of human fallibility and a prayer of completion.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the interpretation of "Shiloh" in Genesis 49:10, arguing that it refers to the Messiah as the "offspring" of Judah. This page begins a new, separate inquiry (Shediasmation) concerning the story of Jephthah's daughter in the Book of Judges.</meta>

### ESSAY
### ON THE SACRIFICE OF JEPHTHAH'S DAUGHTER
### *Judges 11:31 ff.*

->Question: *Did Jephthah sacrifice his daughter?*<-

The negative view <note>the view that he did not kill her, but dedicated her to perpetual virginity</note> is pleasing to Kimchi, Solomon ben Melech, Ralbag <note>Levi ben Gershon</note>, Lyra, Vatablus, Estius, Pellicanus, Grotius, Junius, L. de Dieu, Drusius, the Dutch translators on this passage, Amama in *Antibarbarus Biblicus* page 387, and among our own <note>Lutheran</note> authors, Brentius and Chytraeus on this passage, Franzius in *Oration 71*, Mayer in *Sacred Philology* Part 1, page 313, Hackspan in his notes on this passage, and Fesselius in *Adversaria* Book 5, Chapter 8. The affirmative view <note>the view that he did actually offer her as a burnt sacrifice</note> is more approved by us, along with the Ancients and Tostatus, Cajetan, Carthusianus, Salianus, Torniellus, Serarius, Bonfrerius, Menochius, Tirinus, etc., as well as the Blessed Luther, Dannhauer in his *Disputation on the Vow of Jephthah*, Dr. Calovius in *Antigraphes*, and others. For: I. What Jephthah vowed, that he did (*verse 39* <note>The text says v. 41, but the biblical account of the fulfillment is verse 39</note>). He vowed a burnt offering (*verse 31* <note>The text says v. 33</note>). For an example where the repeated Hebrew letter *Vau* <note>The conjunction "and/or"</note> in a vow does not act as a disjunctive <note>meaning "or"</note>, see *1 Samuel 1:11*. II. A vow narrated so carefully by the Holy Spirit must have been something notable. What great matter would it be to sacrifice a common ox, sheep, or goat? Especially since such animals might not even have been in Jephthah's own house. Furthermore, the complete phrase "to come out to meet" <note>original Hebrew: יצא לקראת (*yatsa likrat*)</note> is only attributed to human beings in the Holy Scriptures, and in the context of this passage, it means "to congratulate someone on their return." It is clear, therefore, that "coming out to meet" presupposes a *person*. III. Why would he be so devastated and distressed upon seeing his daughter if she was merely to be consecrated to God? How could that be such a great evil? But was she forbidden to marry because she was consecrated? On what basis is this claimed? Samuel was consecrated to God and yet was a father (*1 Samuel 8:1*). But some argue that consecrated women were forced to live as celibates. Grotius tries to prove this from *2 Samuel 20:3*, where, however, the text does not refer to a *vow of virginity for maidens*, but rather the *condemnation of defiled concubines to lifelong imprisonment*. What is the connection? Furthermore, what would it benefit Jephthah if his daughter did not marry? According to the Hebrews, a woman has no "house of the mother" <note>meaning she could not carry on the paternal lineage or legal inheritance in her own right</note>. Nor is it an obstacle that in *verse 31* the letter *Vau* might be taken disjunctively <note>meaning "or" instead of "and"</note>. *Response:* It is taken that way sometimes, but not necessarily here. Moreover, what kind of distinction would that be: "*It shall be the Lord's, OR it shall be sacrificed?*" As if, forsooth, the things sacrificed were not also the Lord's! See *Exodus*...

<summary>This page weighs the arguments for and against the actual human sacrifice of Jephthah's daughter, listing major historical theologians and analyzing the Hebrew grammar of the vow.</summary>
<keywords>Jephthah, Jephthah's daughter, human sacrifice, biblical vows, Judges 11, Grotius, Luther, Hebrew grammar, celibacy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Affirmative" argument—the position that Jephthah did indeed sacrifice his daughter—by systematically refuting the common objections raised by those who believe she was merely dedicated to lifelong celibacy.</meta>

*Exodus 13:2, chapter 34, verse 18; Deuteronomy 15:19; Luke 2:23, etc. Neither [is it true] 2.* That Jephthah made his vow while divinely inspired <note>original Greek: "theophoroumenon," meaning one moved or carried by God</note>. *See verse 29 and Hebrews 11:32.* Therefore, it was not a human sacrifice <note>original Greek: "anthropothysian"</note>, which is so hateful to God. *Response:* He is not said to be "divinely inspired" regarding that vow, but rather in the conquering of cities (*verse 29*). Thus, the faith of Jephthah is not praised in *Hebrews 11:32* because he kept what he had vowed, but because, relying on God, he vanquished his enemies (*verse 34*). Furthermore, others are included in that catalog <note>The "Hall of Faith" in Hebrews 11</note> whose falls into sin were notorious, yet they were forgiven through faith in Christ. Anselm, Serarius, Tirinus, and others attribute Jephthah’s vow to divine instinct. They bring up the examples of Samson, Abraham, and others. Finally, they seek a "type" or symbolic foreshadowing of Christ in the woman. There is no need for such evasions. Cappellus, in his *Dissertation on Jephthah’s Vow*, judges it to have been a *cherem* <note>original Hebrew: "חרם," a thing devoted to God, often involving total destruction</note> (*Leviticus 27*), and therefore to be kept by law; as if a *cherem* must necessarily be carried out as a human burnt offering. *Neither 3.* Did God approve the vow by the outcome (*verses 29, 30, 31, 32*). *Response: Denied.* Rather, the recklessness of the vow was shown to him afterward. God looked upon his faith beforehand, not the Vow. *Neither 4.* Did she weep for her virginity rather than her life (*verse 37*). *Response:* She wept, not because she was to remain a virgin [for life], but because she was to die a virgin before her time. *Neither 5.* Is it true that in the execution of the vow it does not say that he slaughtered her (*verse 39*). *Response:* Nor does it say that he consecrated her. *Neither 6.* Is this [a valid objection] when it is added: *And she knew no man.* *Response:* The meaning is: *She had not known [a man].* This is stated to emphasize the tragedy of the deed. It is far too mournful that an untouched girl be snatched by her father for a burnt offering. *Neither 7.* Did the virgins meet every year *to speak with her*—for to what purpose would they speak with a dead woman? (*verse 40*). *Response:* Not *with* her, but *about* her <note>The Latin "cum" can mean "with," but the author argues the Hebrew preposition should be understood as "concerning"</note>. For the matter was to be remembered throughout all ages. *Neither 8.* Would the Priests not have allowed the deed? *Response:* They might have, even against their will. Nor was it necessary for the act to be carried out in the Tabernacle. *Neither 9.* That Jephthah took it badly that she would die childless <note>original Greek: "ateknos"</note>. *Response:* How could she be "childless" if the daughter were still alive? For if she lived, she would not be prohibited from marrying, nor would Jephthah be prohibited from fathering children <note>original Greek: "paidopoiein"</note>.

->THAT IS ALL.<-

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on Jephthah's vow in the book of Judges. This page begins a new academic dissertation focusing on a famous textual problem in Psalm 22, shifting from a theological debate to a "critico-philological" analysis of the Hebrew text.</meta>

# THE CRITICS' CROSS,
### OR, CONCERNING THE INTEGRITY OF THE WORD *KAARI* IN PSALM 22:17.
### *A CRITICO-PHILOLOGICAL DISSERTATION.*

-> §. 1. <-

<margin>Respondent: Heinrich Engelbrecht of Hamburg.</margin>
It is a remarkable testimony of divine providence that the *Jewish* people, who are otherwise most hostile to Christians, were nevertheless required to provide them with *the oracles of God* <note>original Greek: "ta logia tou Theou"</note> to the destruction of their own most wretched cause. Hence, the Blessed Augustine called the Jews *the chest-bearers of the Christians, carrying the Law and the Prophets as a testimony to the Church's claims* <note>From Augustine's work *Against Faustus the Manichaean*, book 12, chapter 13. The "chest-bearer" or *scriniarius* was an official in charge of records or a portable book-chest.</note>. And in his commentary on *Psalm 40*, he says, *The Jews serve us; they are, as it were, our book-carriers; they carry the volumes while we study.* Finally, on *Psalm 56: They have become our librarians, just as servants are accustomed to carry books behind their masters, so that the former may grow weary by carrying, while the latter may profit by reading.*

§. 2. This duty the Jews performed so diligently that they would have suffered death a hundred times rather than allow even a tiny letter <note>original Latin: "literula," literally a "little letter" or "tittle."</note> to perish through their own fault. For this reason, they kept a count not only of every phrase in the holy books, but of the words and even the letters themselves, as is shown by the remains of the *Masorah* <note>The *Masorah* is a collection of ancient marginal notes on the Hebrew Bible intended to preserve the text's exact spelling and punctuation.</note>, which they—not without reason—call the "Fence and Rampart of the Law."

§. 3. Therefore, those critics seem to be most unjust who burden the Jews with the accusation that they entered into a conspiracy to corrupt the text of the Old Testament here and there. However, neither Christ nor the Apostles ever wrote such an indictment <note>original Latin: "dicam," a formal legal charge or writ.</note> against them, nor has anyone to this day been able to convict them of the crime of forgery. This has been demonstrated at length, following others, by Simeon de Muis in his *Assertion of the Hebrew Truth*, André Rivet in his *Introduction to the Holy Scriptures*, chapter 8; Daniel Chamier in his *Universal Soldiering*, volume 1, book 12, chapter 11; the Blessed Salomon Glassius in his *Sacred Philology*, book 1, treatise 1, page 11 and following; Theodor Hackspan in his *Treatise on the Use of Rabbinic Writings*, chapter 2, section 1, page 293 and following; Sixtinus Amama in his *Biblical Antibarbarus*, volume 1, general error 3, chapters 1, 2, 3, page 7; and Johann Heinrich Hottinger in his *Philological Treasury*, book 1, chapter 2, question 4.

<summary>This page introduces a scholarly defense of the Hebrew Old Testament's integrity, arguing that the Jewish people preserved the scriptures with extreme care despite their theological differences with Christianity. The author cites several early modern scholars to refute claims that the Hebrew text was intentionally corrupted by Jewish scribes.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 22:17, Kaari, Masorah, textual criticism, Jewish-Christian relations, Hebrew Bible integrity, Augustine, biblical philology</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page concludes the theological investigation into Jephthah’s vow from the Book of Judges before the manuscript transitions to a new dissertation on Psalm 22. The author is debating whether Jephthah actually sacrificed his daughter as a burnt offering or merely dedicated her to a life of perpetual virginity.</meta>

*Exodus 13:2, chapter 34:18; Deuteronomy 15:19; Luke 2:23, etc. Nehemiah 2.* That Jephthah made his vow while being **inspired by God** <note>original Greek: "theophorumenon," literally meaning "carried by God."</note>. *verse 29. Hebrews 11:32.* Therefore, it was not **human sacrifice** <note>original Greek: "anthropothusian"</note>, which is so hateful to God.

**Response:** He is not said to be "inspired by God" in the making of that vow, but rather in the "conquest of cities" (*verse 29*). Likewise, the faith of Jephthah is not praised in *Hebrews 11:32* because he kept what he had vowed, but because he defeated his enemies by relying on God (*verse 34*). Furthermore, there are others in that catalog of faith who had notorious lapses, yet were pardoned through faith in Christ. Anselm, Serarius, Tirinus, and others attribute Jephthah’s vow to divine instinct. They bring up the examples of Samson, Abraham, and others. Finally, they seek a "type" or symbolic representation of Christ in the woman <note>Jephthah's daughter</note>. There is no need for such convoluted arguments. Cappellus, in his *Seventh Treatise on the Vow*, judges that it was **devoted** <note>original Hebrew: "cherem," referring to something consecrated to God, often for destruction as per Leviticus 27</note>, and thus must be kept by law; as if a "devoted thing" had to be made into a human burnt offering.

**Objection 3:** That God approved the vow by the outcome. *verses 29, 30, 31, 32.*
**Response: Denied.** Rather, the rashness of the vow was shown to him afterward. God looked upon his faith beforehand, not his vow.

**Objection 4:** That she mourned not her life, but her virginity. *verse 37.*
**Response:** She wept, not because she was to remain a virgin, but because she was to die a virgin before her time.

**Objection 5:** That in the execution of the vow, it does not say that he slaughtered her. *verse 39.*
**Response:** Nor does it say that he consecrated her.

**Objection 6:** Yet it is added: *And she knew not a man.*
**Response:** The sense is: *She had not known [a man].* This is for the sake of emphasizing the tragedy: it was too mournful for a pure girl to be snatched away by her father for a burnt offering.

**Objection 7:** That the young women met every year **to speak with her**—but why with her if she were dead? *verse 40.*
**Response:** Not *with* her, but *concerning* her. For the matter was to be remembered by all generations.

**Objection 8:** That the Priests would not have allowed the deed.
**Response:** Perhaps even against their will. Nor was it necessary for the act to be carried out in the Tabernacle.

**Objection 9:** That Jephthah took it poorly that she would die **childless** <note>original Greek: "ateknos"</note>.
**Response:** How would she be childless if the daughter lived? In that case, she would not have been prohibited from marrying, nor would Jephthah have been prohibited from **procreating children** <note>original Greek: "paidopoiein"</note>.

->THE END.<-

<meta>This page continues the dissertation on the textual integrity of the Hebrew Old Testament, specifically focusing on the most controversial variant in the entire Bible: Psalm 22:17. The author acknowledges that this specific verse is the strongest argument used by those who claim the Jewish scribes intentionally corrupted the text to obscure Christian prophecies.</meta>

§. 4. One passage above all others from the entire Old Testament Scripture is cast by our opponents <note>original Greek: οἱ ἐξ ἐναντίας (hoi ex enantias), referring to those who argue the Hebrew text is corrupted</note> like a "Medusa's head" <note>An argument intended to paralyze or terrify the opposition</note> in this conflict. It is found in **Psalm 22, verse 17**, where they assert that the word *ka'aru* <note>Hebrew: כארו, translated as "they pierced" or "they dug"</note> was erased and *ka'ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי, meaning "like a lion"</note> was fraudulently inserted in its place. On this point, many who otherwise defend the cause of the Jews against unfair critics find themselves at a standstill <note>original Latin: "aqua haeret," literally "the water stops," an idiom for being stuck or stumped</note>, as if defeated by the sheer evidence of the matter, and they report the Jews as if caught red-handed <note>original Greek: ἐπ’ αὐτοφώρῳ (ep’ autophorō)</note> in an act of sacrilege. From our own side, the Blessed Aegidius Hunnius, in his *Examination of Bellarmine*, book 2, "On the Word of God," chapter 2, page 204, says: *“That the Jews erased 'ka'aru' [they pierced] in their manuscripts and substituted 'ka'ari' [like a lion] is no secret, but is a matter of public record; they can be convicted of this crime of forgery from their very own copies.”* Among the Reformed, Daniel Chamier, having called this corruption so manifest that there is hardly a Christian who has not recognized it, confesses that the Jews' position here is quite difficult (*Panstratia*, vol. 1, book 12, chapter 12, sections 26–27). To these we may add, from the Catholic side, Simeon de Muis, otherwise a rigid defender of the "Hebrew Truth" <note>A term for the integrity of the Hebrew Masoretic text</note>, who says on page 14: *“I would indeed dare to maintain that, except for this passage in Psalm 22—for other things noted by others are trivial and of almost no consequence—where we, with Saint Jerome, read 'They pierced my hands and my feet,' while the Jews read 'Like a lion, my hands and my feet,' nothing can be found in the entire Hebrew manuscripts that does not possess an excellent, coherent, pious meaning entirely consistent with the Christian faith.”*

§. 5. Thus, this passage has occupied the most serious men and the most excellent minds, so much so that the finest judge of Biblical difficulties, the Blessed Michael Walther, in his *Biblical Harmony*, page 497, did not hesitate to call it the <term>cross of the theologians</term> <note>A phrase used for a problem so difficult it "tortures" the mind of the scholar</note>, which we might with equal right call the <term>scandal and cross of the critics</term>.

§. 6. Nevertheless, after six hundred others, let us also step onto the stage; and let this little writing of ours be added to the total, just as the *wandering moon is surrounded by stars* and *smaller streams fall into the ocean.* <note>The author uses these poetic metaphors to modestly suggest that his small contribution is being added to a vast body of existing work.</note> We shall be a burden to no one, nor shall we be devoted to any side beyond what is fair.

<summary>This page addresses the famous controversy of Psalm 22:17, where the Hebrew text reads "like a lion" but Christian tradition reads "they pierced." The author cites major scholars across denominations who admit this looks like a rare instance of Jewish textual corruption.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 22:17, ka'aru, ka'ari, textual corruption, Aegidius Hunnius, Daniel Chamier, Simeon de Muis, Michael Walther, Hebrew Truth</keywords>

## N 3

If some points have slipped out that were not strictly reasoned or fully considered, we yield to fair-minded judges. Having erred, we can be corrected, which a good mind interprets as being set right. May God make all things pure and fruitful. <note>original Greek: ἅπαν ἄπυρον πύειμον (hapan apyron pyeimon). This is a pious wish that the work be refined without the "fire" of harsh controversy and prove productive for the reader.</note>

§. 7. We find, however, that the authors are divided into two camps. Some defend the **authenticity** <note>original Greek: γνησιότητα (gnēsiotēta)</note> of the most common written reading, the **Kethiv** <term>Kethiv: From the Hebrew "what is written"; the actual letters appearing in the body of the Hebrew manuscript, which in this case read "like a lion" (ka'ari).</term>; others strive to demonstrate its **corruption** <note>original Greek: νοθείαν (notheian)</note> and judge the **Qere** <term>Qere: From the Hebrew "what is read"; the traditional or marginal reading suggested by Jewish scribes to be spoken aloud, which in this case suggests "they pierced" (ka'aru).</term> to be the genuine one, demanding its restoration. The crop of supporters for this latter opinion <note>That the "they pierced" reading is the original one</note> is so abundant that it is difficult to even count them.

Among the **Papists** <note>original Latin: *Pontificiis*, referring to Roman Catholic scholars</note>, it is defended by Paul of Burgos, Pagninus, Bredembachius *on this passage*, Porchetus in *Victory against the Jews*, Jerome of Santa Fe in *Book 1 against the Jews, chapter 8*, Galatinus in *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth, book 8, chapter 17, page 46*, Ferdinand de Escalante in *Shield of the Council, page 656*, Leo à Castro in his *Apology, book 3*, Huntley in *Controversy 1 on the Word of God, chapter 10*, Alfonso Salmerón in *Volume 1, Prologue 4, section 4, folio 32*, Gretser in *Defense of Bellarmine, book 3, chapter 453*, Diego Payva de Andrada in *Defense of the Council of Trent, book 4*, Bellarmine in *On the Word of God, book 2, chapter 2, and on this passage in Volume 5 of his Works, page 118*, Baronius in *Ecclesiastical Annals for the Year of Christ 34*, François Vatable *on this passage*, Jean Lorin *on this passage, page 380*, Gilbert Genebrard *on this passage, page 62*, Johann Isaac in *Book 2 of the Defense of the Hebrew Truth against Lindanus, page 101*, Adam Conzenius in *Politics, book 5, chapter 24*, Siméon de Muis *on this passage, page 112*, Jean D'Espeires in *Treatise 1 on the Hebrew Text, Disputation 2, doubt 2*, Philippe Guadagnolo in *Treatise 1, chapter 6 of the Apology for Christians against Ahmed the Persian*, and others.

Among the **Reformed** <note>Protestant scholars</note>, it is defended by Calvin *on this passage, page 99*, Drusius in *Grammar of the Holy Tongue, page 55, and Questions via Letter, 65*, Scaliger in his *Letters, page 472*, Isaac Casaubon in his *Exercises against the Annals of Baronius, 16, number 77, page 444*, Junius *on this passage and in his Observations on Bellarmine*, Fuller in *Miscellanies, book 3, chapter 12, page 347*, William Whitaker *against Bellarmine*, Amandus Polanus in *Syntagma of Theology, book 1, chapter 37, page 78*, Mercer in his *Notes on Pagninus’s Lexicon, page 166*, André Rivet in *The Prophetic Psalms, pages 159, 160*, Chamier in *Panstratia* (as cited), Sixtus Amama in *The Grammatical Crown, page 115, and Biblical Antibarbarus, page 459*, Buxtorf the Elder in *Manuscript Diatribes on this passage, found in Gisbert Voetius’s Select Disputations, Part 1, page 35*, Johann Heinrich Hottinger in *Philological Treasury* (as cited), page 193, and Edward Pococke in *Notes on the 'Port of Moses' by Rabbi Moses Maimonides, page 58*.

<meta>This page continues the specific investigation into Psalm 22:17, focusing on the scholarly consensus—or lack thereof—regarding the potential corruption of the Hebrew text. §. 4. One passage above all from the entire Old Testament scripture is thrown forward by our adversaries <note>original Greek: οἱ ἐξ ἐναντίας (hoi ex enantias), meaning those on the opposite side of the debate</note> like a Medusa’s head <note>A metaphor for an argument so shocking or undeniable that it petrifies the opponent.</note> in this conflict. It is found in ***Psalm 22, verse 17*** <note>In most English Bibles, this is Psalm 22:16.</note>, where they assert that the word <term>ka’aru (כארו): "they pierced" or "they dug"</term> has been erased and the word <term>ka’ari (כארי): "like a lion"</term> has been fraudulently shoved in its place. Here, many who otherwise defend the Jewish cause against unfair critics are left at a total loss, as if they had been conquered by the evidence of the crime and caught the Jews in the very act <note>original Greek: ἐπ' αὐτοφώρῳ (ep' autophōrō)</note> of sacrilege. From our own side, the Blessed **Aegidius Hunnius**, in his *Examination of Bellarmine, book 2, "On the Word of God," chapter 2, page 204*, says: *“That the Jews in their own copies erased 'ka'aru' (they pierced) and substituted 'ka'ari' (like a lion) is not hidden, but is a public fact; they can be convicted of this crime of forgery from their own manuscripts.”* From the Reformed tradition, **Daniel Chamier**, having called this corruption so manifest that there is hardly a Christian who does not recognize it, confesses that the Jewish position here is quite difficult to maintain (*Panstratia Catholica, Volume 1, book 12, chapter 12, sections 26 and 27*). To these we may add **Simeon de Muis**, a Roman Catholic who is otherwise a rigid defender of the "Hebrew Truth" <note>A scholarly movement (Hebraica Veritas) that argued the Hebrew text was more reliable than the Latin Vulgate.</note>, who says on page 14: *“I would indeed dare to guarantee that—except for this passage in Psalm 22 (for other things noted by others are trivial and of almost no importance)—where we, with St. Jerome, read 'They pierced my hands and my feet,' but the Jews read 'Like a lion my hands and my feet,' nothing can be found in the entire Hebrew codices that does not possess an excellent, coherent, pious meaning entirely consistent with the Christian faith.”*

§. 5. This passage has therefore exercised the most serious men and the highest, most excellent minds, so much so that the Blessed **Michael Walther**, that most excellent judge of Biblical difficulties in his *Biblical Harmony, page 497*, did not hesitate to call it the *“cross of theologians,”* which we could with equal right call the ***scandal and cross of critics.***

§. 6. Nevertheless, let it be permitted even for us to step onto the stage after six hundred others, and let this little writing of ours be added to the count, just as *the wandering moon is encircled by the stars, and smaller streams fall into the ocean.* We shall be a burden to no one, nor shall we be overly devoted to anyone beyond what is fair.

<meta>catchword: John.</meta>

If some things have slipped out that were not strictly reasoned or considered from every angle, we submit to the judgment of fair-minded readers. When we have slipped, we are capable of being corrected, which a well-disposed mind interprets as being set upright. May God grant that <note>original Greek: ἄπαν ἄπυρον πύειμον (apan apyron pyeimon), literally "everything unburnt and productive," a prayer that the work be refined like gold in fire and bear fruit.</note> everything be refined and fruitful.

§. 7. We find, however, that authors are divided into two camps. Some defend the <note>original Greek: γνησιότητα (gnēsiotēta)</note> authenticity of the most common reading, the <term>Kethiv (כְּתִיב): the "written" text as it appears in the body of the Hebrew Bible</term>. Others strive to demonstrate the <note>original Greek: νοθείαν (notheian)</note> forgery of that same reading; they judge the <term>Qere (קְרֵי): the "read" version or marginal correction</term> to be the genuine one and demand it be restored. The crop of supporters for this latter opinion is so abundant that it is difficult to even count them. Among the **Catholics** <note>original: "Pontificiis"</note>, this view is defended by Paul of Burgos, Santes Pagninus, and Bredembach *on this passage*; Porchetus in *Victory against the Jews*; Jerome of Santa Fe in *book 1 against the Jews, chapter 8*; Galatinus in *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth, book 8, chapter 17, page 46*; Ferdinand de Escalante in *Shield of the Council, page 656*; Leo of Castro in his *Apology, book 3*; James Gordon Huntly in *Controversy 1, On the Word of God, chapter 10*; Alfonso Salmeron in *Volume 1, Prologue 4, section 4, folio 32*; Gretser in *Defense of Bellarmine, chapter 2, book 3, column 453*; Diego Payva de Andrada in *Defense of the Council of Trent, book 4*; Robert Bellarmine in *On the Word of God, book 2, chapter 2, and on this passage in Volume 5 of his Works, page 118*; Baronius in *Ecclesiastical Annals, year of Christ 34*; François Vatable *on this passage*; John Lorinus *on this passage, page 380*; Gilbert Genebrard *on this passage, page 62*; Johannes Isaac in *book 2 of the Defense of the Hebrew Truth against Lindanus, page 101*; Adam Conzen in *Politics, book 5, chapter 24*; Simeon de Muis *on this passage, page 112*; Johannes d'Espeires in *Treatise 1 on the Hebrew Text, Disputation 2, doubt 2*; Philippus Guadagnolus in *Treatise 1, chapter 6 of the Apology for Christians against Ahmed the Persian*, and others.

Among the **Reformed** <note>Protestant scholars</note>, this view is held by John Calvin *on this passage, page 99*; Johannes Drusius in *Grammar of the Holy Language, page 55, and Questions by Letter, 65*; Joseph Scaliger in *Letters, page 472*; Isaac Casaubon in *Exercises on the Annals of Baronius, exercise 16, number 77, page 444*; Franciscus Junius *on this passage and in his Observations on Bellarmine*; Thomas Fuller in *Miscellany, book 3, chapter 12, page 347*; William Whitaker *against Bellarmine*; Amandus Polanus in *Syntagma of Theology, book 1, chapter 37, page 78*; Johannes Mercerus in *Notes on Pagninus's Lexicon, folio 166*; Andreas Rivetus in *The Prophetic Psalms, pages 159, 160*; Daniel Chamier in *Panstratia Catholica, in the cited place*; Sixtinus Amama in *The Grammatical Crown, page 115, and Antibarbarus Biblicus, page 459*; Johannes Buxtorf the Elder in *Manuscript Diatribes on this passage, found in Gisbertus Voetius's Select Disputations, Part 1, page 35*; Johann Heinrich Hottinger in *Thesaurus Philologicus, in the cited place, page 193*; and Edward Pococke in *Notes on the Gate of Moses by Rabbi Moses Maimonides, page 58*.

This demonstrates a rare moment of cross-denominational consensus in early modern biblical criticism.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of scholars who argue that the Hebrew text of Psalm 22:17 has been altered. The previous page established the two camps of thought: those who defend the common reading "like a lion" (ka'ari) and those who argue for "they pierced" (ka'aru). This page lists Protestant and Catholic authorities supporting the latter and begins to categorize their explanations for how the change occurred—either through malice or accidental scribal error.</meta>

N 3
<meta>catchword: Diff.</meta>

John Viccars, *Decapla, book 1, page 60.* Brian Walton, *Prolegomena 7 in the Polyglot Bible, number 7, page 40.* Samuel Bochart, *Hierozoicon, part 1, book 3, chapter 6, column 779.* Finally, among our own <note>The author refers to fellow Protestant or Lutheran scholars.</note>, Matthias Flacius, *Key to the Holy Scriptures, part 1, column 1143.* Egidius Hunnius, *in the place cited.* Forster, *Hebrew Dictionary, page 394.* Erhard Schnepf, *on this passage, page 121.* Heinrich Moller, *on this passage, page 207.* Schindler, *Pentaglot Lexicon, column 829.* Wolfgang Fabricius Capito, *Institutes of Hebrew, book 1, under letter H. 2.* Johannes Hackspan, *Notes, part 2, page 104.* Master Johannes Frischmuth, *Dissertation on this passage, chapter 2.* Friedlibius, *Theological Examination, page 522.* Heinrich Kipping, *Exercise 52 on the Holy Scriptures, page 514.* and others.

§. 8. All these and many others agree <note>original Greek: ὁμοθυμαδὸν (homothymadon), meaning "with one mind" or "unanimously"</note> that the true reading is **כארו** <note>original Hebrew: *ka'aru*, meaning "they pierced"</note>, but they differ as to what fate or occasion caused that reading to be wiped out. For some believe that the Jews, immediately after the passion of the Lord, when they realized they were being urged and hemmed in by such an evident prophecy of the crucifixion of Christ, maliciously erased the word **כארו** (*they pierced*)—which was originally in the text—and substituted **כארי** (*like a lion*) by deliberate plan. This latter reading makes no sense, or is certainly absurd in this context, yet it was very convenient for their own evasions. This is the view of Socinus in his *Response against Bellarmine and Wiek*. Nor do the most prominent of the **Catholics** <note>original: "Pontificiorum"</note> disagree. Among our own scholars, Flacius, Forster, H. Moller, Hunnius, and Erhard Schnepf also lean toward this opinion in the places cited.

§. 9. Others deny that the Jews corrupted this passage by intentional labor and <note>original Greek: ἐκ προαιρέσεως (ek proaireseōs), meaning "by choice" or "on purpose"</note> by design. They prefer the explanation that a fault crept in <note>original Greek: αὐτομάτως (automatōs), meaning "spontaneously" or "by itself"</note>, by chance and accident, due to the similarity of the letters **ו** (Vav) and **י** (Yod), especially in handwritten manuscripts. They argue this was the primary source of the diversity between copies; and since semi-learned scribes did not fully understand that word **כארו** (*ka'aru*), they multiplied copies that showed the Yod instead. Nevertheless, with the Masoretes <note>Jewish scholars who standardized the Hebrew text between the 6th and 10th centuries</note> acting as monitors, for several centuries **כארו** was written in the text as the <term>Kethiv (what is written)</term>, while **כארי** was placed in the margin as the <term>Qere (what is read)</term>. From this, a new confusion arose: the **כארו** (*Kethiv*) was moved to the margin, while conversely **כארי** (the *Qere*) was placed in the main text for some time, until in the successions of time the <note>original: *Kethiv*</note> written form (which was especially unpleasant to the Jews) perished entirely from their books. Gilbert Genebrard, Hottinger, Sixtinus Amama, and Frischmuth report the matter as having happened in this way.

<summary>This page discusses two theories regarding the variant reading of Psalm 22:17: one suggesting a malicious Jewish conspiracy to hide a prophecy of Christ's crucifixion, and another suggesting the change from "pierced" to "like a lion" was an accidental scribal error due to similar-looking Hebrew letters.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Psalm 22:17, crucifixion prophecy, Jewish-Christian polemics, Masoretes, Kethiv, Qere, Hebrew philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of textual variants in Psalm 22. It focuses on the specific philological explanation provided by the scholar Samuel Bochart regarding how the Hebrew word for "they pierced" (*ka'aru*) might have been corrupted into "like a lion" (*ka'ari*). It also addresses the history of printing these variants, highlighting the conflict between Christian scholars and Jewish printers in the 16th century.</meta>

103

*Differences, chapter 1, 2, § 16.* Samuel Bochart *column 783,* whose words we add here (since the book is handled by few hands): It is clear by what stages error crept into this psalm. First, instead of **כרו** <note>original: *karu*, meaning "they dug" or "pierced"</note> it was written **כארו** <note>original: *ka'aru*</note> with an epenthetic <note>An added letter that does not change the basic meaning but can affect pronunciation or spelling style.</note> Aleph. Because this was less common, and many semi-learned scribes did not understand it, they turned **כארו** into **כארי** <note>original: *ka'ari*, meaning "like a lion"</note>, deceived by the similarity of the letters Jod and Vau <note>The Hebrew letters Yod (י) and Vav (ו) look very similar in many handwritten manuscripts.</note>, which is often so great in hand-written books that they are not easily distinguished even by an attentive reader. From this was born the diversity of copies; to indicate this, the Masoretic Doctors <note>The Masoretes were Jewish scholars between the 6th and 10th centuries who worked to preserve the exact text of the Hebrew Bible.</note> advised that **כארו** should be written in the sacred text and **כארי** in the margin. While scribes observed this for several centuries, finally, in these recent centuries, the Jews chose from the two readings the one that is less favorable to the truth of the Christian religion, with the other being completely suppressed and removed from their books. Yet, they can easily be convicted of the crime they committed by the testimony of their own Doctors. Thus far Bochart.

§. 10. Furthermore, both sides demand that the reading they consider false, **כארי** (*like a lion*), be stripped of its vowel points and the ancient **כארו** (*they pierced*) be restored to the Text. This was done in the *Complutensian Bible* <note>The first printed polyglot (multi-language) Bible, a landmark of Renaissance scholarship.</note>, which *Francisco Ximenius de Cisneros, Cardinal and Archbishop of Toledo,* oversaw in the year 1520. However, Sixtinus Amama seems to have most aptly explained to many why other Christians have not imitated this until now. His words on this matter in *Antibarbarus Biblicus, page 461,* are as follows: *Since the last century, when Christians began learning Hebrew letters—to the great displeasure of the Jews—competition grew between Jews and Christians... A great rivalry arose between the printers and the correctors, both Jewish and Christian; and in this contest, the Jews prevailed. For the fear of financial loss broke the spirits of the Christians. Indeed, a certain Jew, the corrector of a certain Venetian edition, dared to warn Bomberg <note>Daniel Bomberg, a famous Christian printer of Hebrew books in Venice.</note>—when he wished to restore this passage according to the authority of the most correct manuscripts noted by Rabbi Jacob ben Chajim—that he would see to it among his people that not a single copy of that edition would be purchased.* I remember hearing this often from the celebrated Johannes Drusius.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the corruption of the Hebrew text of the Psalms. How few copies of the Hebrew Bibles are still sold among Christians, the booksellers will best testify. And so, I would attribute the origin of whatever defect of this kind we recognize in today's manuscripts to the carelessness of the scribes; its preservation and duration, however, I would attribute partly to the stubbornness and ambition of the Jews, and partly to the faint-heartedness of our printers and the laziness of Christians. Thus says Amama <note>Sixtinus Amama (1593–1629) was a Dutch Reformed theologian and Hebrew scholar who urged Christians to study the original biblical languages.</note>, concerning whom Gijsbert Voët writes in *Selected Disputations, Part 1, Disputation on the Unsolvable Problems of Holy Scripture, page 54.* We approve of his zeal for the Christian faith, but we do not see knowledge or sufficient proof in his argument.

§. 11. But indeed, that the sacred writings were corrupted by the Jews by design is admitted neither by the <term>ἀκοίμητος</term> <note>original: *akoimētos* — "unsleeping" or ever-vigilant</note> providence of God—which would by no means have committed His <term>τὰ λόgia</term> <note>original: *ta logia* — "oracles" or divine utterances</note> to be guarded by the claws of such Harpies <note>In mythology, Harpies were snatched-themed monsters; here used as a derogatory metaphor for those the author views as enemies of the text.</note>—nor by the astounding diligence of the Jews in transcribing, and their most stubborn nature in protecting the sacred codex even down to its smallest particles, as well as their proven faithfulness in other passages that no less effectively strike down Jewish lack of faith from its very foundation. Nor should the illustrious Bochart warn that *one swallow does not make a summer*, and that a rule cannot rest on this example, which might be unique of its kind. For if the Jews can be convicted of a single lie or the crime of forgery by solid arguments, their reliability in general will not unjustly be held suspect. And once proven liars, the Catholics or others would not believe them thereafter, even if they speak the truth. Whence it concerns us greatly that the circumcised <note>original: *verpæ*, a derogatory Latin term for circumcised men, used here polemically to refer to Jewish people.</note> do not blaspheme by corrupting Holy Scripture in their synagogues—contrary to what Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Huguenot theologian.</note> judged in the cited place, section 26, where among other things he says: *"Why,"* he says, *"do you object to me regarding the reading of the Jews? Or what fault is it of Holy Scripture if the circumcised blaspheme in their synagogues?"* Although Chamier himself excuses the Jews to some extent.

<meta>This page continues the technical defense of the Hebrew text of Psalm 22, specifically focusing on the controversial word in verse 16. ...from where, then, did the word <term>ka’aru</term> <note>Hebrew: כארו — "they pierced," the reading favored by Christian interpreters as a prophecy of the crucifixion.</note> enter into certain manuscripts? From where was it rejected into the margin by the <term>Masoretes</term> <note>The Jewish scholars (approx. 6th–10th centuries) who standardized the Hebrew Bible text and added vowel signs and marginal notes.</note>? From where was it pulled back by others from the margin into the main text? From where, finally, was *ka’aru* stripped of authority by the Jews? To be sure, all these things could have happened just as they have been imagined. But from "possibility" to "actuality," the consequence is not valid. Conjectures are not arguments. There are 848 instances of <term>Kethiv and Qere</term> <note>Hebrew: כתיב & קרין — traditional variants where a word is "written" one way in the Hebrew text but "read" aloud as another.</note> counted in the tradition, among which *ka’aru* and <term>ka’ari</term> <note>Hebrew: כארי — "like a lion," the standard Masoretic reading.</note> were never included by the Jews. 

It is without merit that the faithfulness of more recent Jews is brought into suspicion; even today, they keep all those passages of Holy Scripture intact and safe, by which they are no less evidently pressured by Christians. Forced into a corner, they resort to a thousand hiding places to elude the most manifest truth—just as, even if the reading *ka’aru* were accepted, they could seek pretexts for their stubbornness (though vain ones) in the multiple meanings of the word (which might signify *to dig, to buy, to prepare, etc.*). This was shown long ago by the <term>Great Man</term> <note>Latin: *Megalander* — a respectful title often given to Martin Luther.</note> Luther in his *Works on Psalm 22, Volume 2 of the Jena Latin edition, folio 252, near the beginning of the second side.* Furthermore, how could recent Jews have suppressed all copies that showed *ka’aru* either in the text or the margin, such that today hardly one or two survive of which it is certain they express *ka’aru*? Finally, the Jews are protected by the **Masora**, which we shall produce below.

§. 13. Let us test, however, by what reasons others defend the reading *ka’aru*. Here, at the start, we cannot marvel enough at the <term>blindness</term> <note>original Greek: ἀβλεψίαν (*ablepsian*)</note> of Gijsbert Voet <note>Gijsbert Voetius (1589–1676) was a prominent Dutch Reformed theologian.</note> in the cited place, page 59, where he says: *"I see two further arguments opposed to this opinion (concerning the integrity of ka’aru): One is common to almost all theologians, because in the New Testament, Matthew 27, the word <term>they dug</term> <note>original Greek: ὤρυξαν (*ōryxan*), corresponding to the Hebrew "pierced."</note> is cited, which in Hebrew is 'karu'."* And after a few words: *"To the first, it is responded that citations of passages from the Old Testament in the New Testament do nothing to prejudice the Hebrew text, nor do they make a Greek translation that disagrees with the Hebrew source match the Hebrew <term>according to the literal word</term> <note>original Greek: κατὰ τὸ ῥητὸν (*kata to rhēton*)</note>."* 

Clearly, Voet thought this prophecy concerning the piercing of the hands and feet was cited in Matthew 27—perhaps because verse 19 of this Psalm, cited in Matthew chapter 27, verse 35, was present in his memory. Otherwise, a person would have to be more sharp-sighted than a lynx to see the word <term>they dug</term> <note>original Greek: ὤρυξαν</note> there, which is neither in that place...

<meta>This page (104) continues the debate over whether the Hebrew text of the Bible was intentionally corrupted by Jewish scribes. While the previous page likely introduced the specific controversy of Psalm 22, this page shifts to a broader defense of the Jewish transmission of the text against critics like Sixtinus Amama. "How few copies of the Hebrew Bible are even now distributed among Christians, the booksellers will best testify. And so, the origin of this defect, whatever it may be, which we recognize in modern manuscripts, I would ascribe to the negligence of the copyist-scribes; its preservation and duration, however, I would attribute partly to the stubbornness and ambition of the Jews, and partly to the cowardice of our own printers and the laziness of Christians." So says **Amama** <note>Sixtinus Amama (1593–1629), a Dutch Reformed theologian and Hebraist who urged for a return to original languages.</note>, regarding whom **Gisbertus Voetius** <note>A prominent Dutch Calvinist theologian (1589–1676).</note> writes in his *Select Disputations, Part 1, Disputation on the insoluble problems of Holy Scripture, page 54.* We approve of his zeal for the Christian faith, but we do not see in his work either true science or sufficient proof.

§. 11. But truly, the **ever-watchful** <term>akoimētos</term> <note>Greek: ἀκοίμητος — sleepless or unceasing.</note> providence of God—which would by no means have committed His **oracles** <term>ta logia</term> <note>Greek: τὰ λόγια — the words or utterances of God.</note> to be guarded by the claws of such Harpies—did not permit the sacred writings to be corrupted by the Jews by design. Nor did the stupendous diligence of the Jews in copying, and their most stubborn nature in protecting the sacred manuscript even down to its smallest particles, allow for such corruption. Furthermore, their proven good faith in other passages, which no less effectively uproot Jewish "treachery" from its foundation, speaks against this. There is no reason for the Famous **Bochart** <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French Protestant biblical scholar.</note> to warn that *"one swallow does not make a spring,"* nor that a rule can be built on this example, which might be unique in its kind. For if the Jews could be convicted of a single lie or the crime of forgery by solid arguments, their faithfulness in general would not unjustly be held suspect. And once they were found to be liars, the Catholics or others would not believe them thereafter, even if they spoke the truth. Hence, it is of the greatest importance to us that the **circumcised** <note>original Latin: *verpæ* — a derogatory term for circumcised men/Jews used in polemics of this era.</note> do not blaspheme by depraving the Holy Scriptures in their synagogues, contrary to what **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Huguenot theologian.</note> thought in the place cited, § 26, where among other things he says: *"Why do you object to me the reading of the Jews? ...nor is it found anywhere in the entire New Testament, as will be plain to the eye for anyone consulting *Schmid’s Concordance* <note>original: *Schmidii tameion*; a reference to Erasmus Schmid's 1638 Greek New Testament concordance</note> under the word **to dig** <note>Greek: ὀρύττω (*oryttō*), the root word for piercing or digging</note>.

§. 14. In other respects, they place much defense in the manuscripts exhibiting that reading **ka’aru** <note>Hebrew: כארו, "they pierced"</note>. Thus Wolfgang Fabricius Capito <note>A German Reformer and Hebraist, 1478–1541</note> writes in his *Institutes of Hebrew, Book 1, Chapter 13, letter H. 2*: *“And so we once saw [the reading **ka’aru**] written in the margin in a vellum and very ancient book among the **Vangiones** <note>A Latin name for the people of the region around Worms, Germany</note>, pointed out by Matthew Adrianus, who, at my request and with great effort, investigated this place especially and found it easily.”* Furthermore, these are the words of John Isaac—a Jew of the Levite lineage, but later a Christian and Professor of the Hebrew Language at Cologne—in the book titled *Defense of the Hebrew Truth against Lindanus, etc., Book 2, page 112*: *“I, John Isaac, can testify to this same thing [namely, what Rabbi Jacob ben Chayim stated] with the very truth and a good conscience: that I saw such a psalter at my grandfather’s house, where **ka’aru** was written in the text, and I do not doubt that all copies had it so.”* Genebrardus <note>Gilbert Génébrard (1535–1597), a French Benedictine scholar</note> reports on page 62 of this passage that Gerard Veltwyck testified to the same by his own example in the appendix of the book *Paths of Error* <note>original: *sebile tobu*; likely *Shevilei Tohu*</note>. §. 15. But whatever the case may be, one or two copies cannot prejudice the tens of thousands of other written and printed copies. Our hands have eyes; they believe what they see. Let fair judges listen and decide: they say that tens of thousands of copies, which are exposed to the eyes of all, lie in error; we, on the contrary, [say] that one or two [assigned] to this person or that one...

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the Hebrew text of Psalm 22:16, specifically evaluating the physical evidence from manuscripts and the Masoretic tradition. The author is skeptical of reports that ancient codices read "ka'aru" (they pierced) instead of "ka'ari" (like a lion).</meta>

Perhaps they saw it, perhaps they did not. Indeed, since **those of the opposing side** <note>Greek: *hoi ex enantias*</note> themselves say that the similarity of the letters **Vav** and **Yod** <note>Hebrew: ו and י; these two letters are very similar in size and shape in Hebrew script</note> in hand-written copies is often so great that they are not easily distinguished even by an attentive reader, who can say how well those witnesses actually saw? To descend to the specifics of the cited codices, the marginal note **ka’aru** <note>Hebrew: כארו, "they pierced"</note> belonging to **Capito** <note>Wolfgang Fabricius Capito, a Reformer mentioned on the previous page</note> could have been from the hand of someone suffering from a preconceived bias. As for **Isaac** <note>John Isaac, the Jewish convert mentioned previously</note>, it appears he was merely a youth when he viewed the codex at his grandfather's house, and it seems he hardly had enough judgment for this matter—especially since, now as a man, he draws a conclusion for all codices based on just one. The reliability of **Velvicus** has not yet been sufficiently proven. Regarding the Jena copy, it is only fair to place trust in the witnesses; however, what sort of copy it was is not sufficiently clear. Today it seems to no longer exist. For the illustrious **Johannes Frischmuth** <note>A 17th-century German Orientalist and theologian</note>, who treated this argument most diligently, commends in **Chapter 2, Section 1** a manuscript codex of the Salana Library. But he wishes that **the other part, which comprises the Law and the Hagiographa** <note>The "Law" (Torah) and "Hagiographa" (Ketuvim/Writings) are two major divisions of the Hebrew Bible</note>, **were not missing.**

§. 16. A greater weight seems to reside in the argument they seek from the **Masora** <note>The Masora is the traditional body of notes and rules intended to preserve the exact text of the Hebrew Bible</note>. They first cite the Textual Masora **at Numbers 24, verse 9**: **Ka'ari occurs twice, and the signs are: "He crouched, he lay down like a lion [ka'ari]" (Num 24:9) and "Ka'ari my hands and my feet" (Psalm 22:17); and it is written ka'aru.** <note>original Hebrew: כארי ב' וסי' כרע שכב כארי כארי ורגלי כארו : כתיב</note>. However, we oppose the **Spurious Masora** with the **Genuine Masora**. We call "Spurious" those faulty additions by more recent writers which ought to be corrected from the genuine Masora, of which **Buxtorf’s** <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Elder, a famous Hebraist</note> many corrections added to the *Tiberias* <note>Buxtorf's work on the Masora</note> show not a few examples. Compare also **Buxtorf the Son** in *Against Criticism* <note>Latin: *Anticritica*</note>, **pages 478, 479, 481, 501, 503, 507, 812 to 842**, and *On the Antiquity of Points*, **page 115**; and **Cartwright** in the *Preface to Selections in Genesis*, **page 5, column a**. The matter itself speaks to the fact that the note upon which these illustrious men build their case is of this [spurious] kind. It says that **ka'ari** occurs twice, while the **Small Masora** <note>The *Masora Parva*, found in the side margins of Hebrew Bibles</note> between the columns correctly notes: **This word occurs four times: twice with the vowel Kametz and twice with the vowel Patach.** <note>Hebrew: ד' ב' קמצין וב' פתחין</note>. Furthermore, that spurious Masora binds together...

<meta>Continuing the evaluation of historical evidence for the variant reading of Psalm 22:17. The previous page discussed specific manuscript witnesses (like Capito and the Jena copy) and questioned their reliability due to the visual similarity of Hebrew letters.</meta>

...gives two parallel examples from the passages **Numbers 24:9** and **Psalm 22:17**, even though there is a *Qamets* <term>a Hebrew vowel mark indicating a long 'a' sound</term> under the letter *Kaph* in the first instance, while there is a *Patach* <term>a Hebrew vowel mark indicating a short 'a' sound</term> in the second. When these details are carefully weighed, they easily bring that Masoretic note into suspicion.

§. 17. They also retreat to the *Final Great Masorah* <term>a traditional collection of notes on the correct transmission of the Hebrew Bible</term>, where in the section for the letter *Aleph* <note>original: *me'arkat Aleph*</note>, under the arrangement of the word *et*, folio 10, column 8, near the middle, it is written: *“In certain accurate copies I found it written as ka’aru and read as ka’ari.”* <note>Hebrew: כארו (they pierced) and כארי (like a lion)</note> However, these words do not belong to the Great Masorah itself (which approves the other reading, "like a lion," as we shall see) but are the words of **Rabbi Jacob ben Hayyim**, whose services **Daniel Bomberg** <note>A famous 16th-century Christian printer of Hebrew books in Venice</note> employed. Nor does he explain which or how many copies those were, or whether they were considered "accurate" by his own people or by Christians. Meanwhile, regarding the reading *ka’aru*, he adds by way of judgment: *“However, I have searched through all the places written with a Vav at the end and read with a Jod, and I did not find this passage (Psalm 22:17) in their number; nor did I find it recorded among the variant readings between the Easterners and the Westerners.”* <note>The "Easterners" (Babylonian tradition) and "Westerners" (Palestinian/Tiberian tradition) often had minor differences in their biblical manuscripts</note> By these words, as much as he is able, he renders the reliability of those copies quite doubtful—even if, as is likely, he called them "accurate" to please certain Christians on whose whims he depended. Therefore, the illustrious **Siméon de Muis** <note>A 17th-century French Hebraist</note> concludes in vain on page 112 that *ka’aru* was once the universal reading and *ka’ari* never existed; for it only follows that there was no conflicting reading in ancient times, otherwise it would have been recorded among such variants.

§. 18. Next, they seek support from the ancient versions <term>early translations of the Bible into other languages</term>, which, as if by a shared conspiracy, translate this passage: **THEY PIERCED** *my hands and my feet*. From this, they conclude that the translators read *ka’aru* or *karu* <note>Hebrew variants meaning "they dug" or "they pierced"</note>; for "like a lion" would have had to be translated as *sicut leo*, not "They pierced." Thus, in the third century before the birth of Christ, the **Septuagint** <term>the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament</term> used the word *oryxan* <note>Greek: ὤρυξαν, "they dug out/pierced"</note>, and following them, the Greek Fathers, such as **Justin Martyr** in his *Dialogue with Trypho*, and **Athanasius** in his book on...

<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the Masoretic variants of Psalm 22:17 (specifically the word ka’ari/ka’aru). The author is now surveying how early Church Fathers and various ancient translations interpreted this specific passage.</meta>

*On the Incarnation* and *Dialogue 1 on the Trinity* <note>Works attributed to Athanasius of Alexandria, a 4th-century theologian</note>; the author of *Questions to Antiochus, question 136*; and Apollinaris in his *Paraphrasis* (who writes: "they dug through both my hands and feet at once" <note>original Greek: ἡμετέρας ὤρυξαν ὁμῦ χεῖράς τε πόδας τε</note>). Furthermore, Cyprian has: *they dug out* <note>Latin: effoderunt</note>, as if from the Greek *exōryxan* in Book 2 of his *Testimonies against the Jews*. Lactantius says the same in Book 4, Chapter 18. And from *exōryxan* (they dug), the word *exōrisan* (they banished or exterminated) seems to have been corrupted; this latter word is frequent in Tertullian, such as in his book *Against the Jews*, chapters 1, 10, and 13, although he also recognizes the reading: *they dug* <note>Latin: foderunt</note>. They also urge the version of **Aquila** (who was himself a Jew), who used *ēschynan*, meaning *they defiled* or *they foully bloodied*. **Louis Cappel** <note>A 17th-century French Protestant scholar and pioneer of textual criticism</note> in *Critica Sacra*, Book 5, Chapter 5, conjectures that Aquila thought this because the Hebrew word *chaphar* means both *to dig* and *shame*, and he believed the word *karar* <note>likely a reference to the root of the word in question</note> similarly signifies both. However, he could have spared himself this conjecture, since both *ka’ar* and *ka’ar* in Chaldean <term>Aramaic</term> or Syriac mean *to defile* or *to pollute*. Compare Fuller in the cited work, page 350, and Bochart, column 780. To this is added the translation of **Jerome** according to the "Hebrew Truth" <note>Jerome’s effort to translate the Old Testament directly from Hebrew rather than the Greek Septuagint</note>: *They fixed* <note>Latin: Fixerunt</note>, although Trevetus reads it as *they bound*. Finally, a great number of other versions are cited, both Oriental (Syriac <unclear>they dug</unclear>, Arabic <unclear>they pierced</unclear>, Ethiopic: *Kanawuni*, meaning *they perforated me*, etc.) and European, which there is no need to review here.

§. 19. But although we could offer several observations regarding the version circulated under the name of "the Seventy" <term>the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</term>, from which the Arabic and Ethiopic versions were derived, we do not wish to evade the argument in that way; rather, we deny the logical conclusion drawn from it. It is true that esteemed interpreters translated it as *They dug*, but it is false to claim that they therefore could not have been reading the word **ka’ari** <note>Hebrew: כארי, "like a lion"</note>. For we intend to demonstrate below, using invincible arguments, that the word **ka’ari** itself must be explained through the concept of *digging* or *piercing*. Therefore, there is no doubt about the meaning and the translation of the word, but rather about its integrity and the true reading. It is a prejudice of others to think that **ka’ari** cannot support any meaning other than "like a lion." If that prejudice is removed, and it is shown that it conveniently admits the meaning of "those who dig," what more could they desire? In that case, **Bochart** <note>Samuel Bochart, a 17th-century biblical scholar</note> would fear in vain (along with others) that Christian truth might lose something unless the reading **ka’aru** <note>Hebrew: כארו, "they pierced"</note> is adopted. For he says in column 783: *"If," he says, "ka’ari is changed into..."*

<meta>Continuing the discussion from page 109 regarding the variant readings of Psalm 22:17. This page focuses on the theological and linguistic implications of reading "like a lion" (ka’ari) versus "they dug/pierced" (ka’aru), citing both Jewish and Christian authorities. The catchword "tus" at the bottom of the page likely completes the word "cordatus" (wise/judicious).</meta>

"Like a lion" <note>original Hebrew: *ka’ari* (כארי)</note>, from that famous prophecy [Psalm 22:17]—which clearly proves not only the truth of Christ but also the divinity of the Holy Scriptures—would become a meaningless phrase, and one from which, whichever way you turn, you will never extract a sensible meaning. For what would this half-verse mean: "Like a lion my hands [and my feet]"? This zeal for the "lion" reading seems to us—and we say this without intending any insult to that most illustrious man—to be "not according to knowledge" <note>original Greek: *ho kat' epignōsin*; a reference to Romans 10:2, describing zeal that lacks true understanding</note>.

§. 20. Finally, they point to the consensus of both Jewish and Christian scholars. Among the former, we have heard of **Rabbi Chajim** <note>Jacob ben Hayyim ibn Adonijah, a famous 16th-century Masoretic scholar</note>, who nevertheless, through his own experience <gloss>original Greek: *empeira*</gloss>, clearly rejects the reading *ka’ari* [like a lion]. **Galatinus** <note>Pietro Colonna Galatino, a 16th-century Hebraist</note> (in Book 1, Chapter 8 and Book 8, Chapter 17) appeals to **Rabbi Moshe Haddarschan** <note>An 11th-century rabbi from Narbonne known for his allegorical commentaries</note>, who reportedly testified that the masters [rabbis] removed the word *karu* ("they dug" or "they pierced") <note>original Hebrew: *karu* (כרו)</note> and substituted *ka’ari* ("like a lion") <note>original Hebrew: *ka’ari* (כארי)</note>. However, since the credibility of such allegations from that *Haddarschan* has worn thin among most scholars, it does not move us.

It is of greater importance what the illustrious **Frischmuth** <note>Johannes Frischmuth, a 17th-century German Orientalist</note> observes (in the cited work, section 10) regarding the *Midrash Tehillim* <note>An ancient Jewish commentary on the Psalms</note> (folio 21, column 2) and the *Yalkut* <note>The *Yalkut Shimoni*, a medieval compilation of Jewish lore</note> (Part 2, folio 97, column 4): that the word *ka’ari* [like a lion] is explained there using third-person plural verbs. That most learned man [Frischmuth] believes this could not have happened unless the commentators had approved the reading *ka’aru* [they dug]. But it is well known how the Jews, in their explanation of the Holy Scriptures, are accustomed to play with the slightest allusions and "mix square things with round" <note>original Latin: *quadrata rotundis miscere*; an idiom for causing confusion or forcing together things that do not fit</note>, etc. 

Therefore, it is no wonder if that **Rabbi Judah** wished to change *ka’ari* [like a lion] into the verb *ka’aru* [they dug] with a slight adjustment, even though he literally thought it should be explained as: "Like a lion." But if he saw that *ka’ari* was not a singular noun with a prefix [meaning "as a lion"], but a plural participle, why shouldn't he explain it with a plural verb? Regardless, in both the *Midrash* and the *Yalkut*, the reading *ka’ari* is preserved in the cited passages, unless that great man [Frischmuth] believes that through the "injury of time" [textual corruption], it crept into those books in place of *ka’aru*. 

Regarding the Christians, the conclusion of **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier, a French Reformed theologian</note> is too generous (in the cited work, section 29): "To me, this is an invincible argument. All Christians read *Karu* [they dug] in this place. Therefore, this passage is not corrupted." Certainly, the passage is not corrupted, but it is false to say that all Christians read *Karu*, as will be shown from what follows. And I have chosen to present these points about the reading *ka’ari* [like a lion] solely out of a love for the truth and without attacking anyone, wise Reader...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Psalm 22:17 (numbered as Psalm 21 in the Latin tradition). The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing the credibility of historical claims regarding the alteration of the Hebrew text. The catchword "tus" from the previous page completes the word "cordatus" (wise/judicious) at the start of this page.</meta>

...wise and learned man may judge. In the meantime, **if anyone**—as we add here following the famous **Alexander More** in his *Genevan Exercises*, page 93—**out of a desire to confirm our faith by all means, wishes to retain *ka’aru*** <note>original Hebrew: כארו, meaning "they pierced"</note>, **we will not disagree with them too strongly, provided they do not insist that this reading pass into the formal text.** For the authority of Rabbi Chajim, or Johannes Isaac, or Gerhard Veltuyck is not so great that we must yield our assent to them.

### §. 21.

Therefore, since the reading *ka’aru* <note>they pierced</note> rests on no solid reasons, it remains for us to decide in favor of the other: *ka’ari* <note>original Hebrew: כארי, meaning "like a lion"</note>. Since, besides these two, no other reading has been observed by anyone in any manuscript so far, it is clear that once one is rejected, the other must remain—unless they wish the true reading to be fundamentally erased from human memory.

The *Masora* <term>The Masora is the traditional body of notes and rules used to preserve the exact text of the Hebrew Bible.</term> further confirms the reading *ka’ari* <note>like a lion</note> unassailably. First, the **Small Masora** <note>notes placed in the margins between columns of text</note> at **Numbers 24:9**, which was already mentioned above, notes that the word *ka’ari* occurs four times in the sacred text: twice with the *Kamez* vowel and twice with the *Patach* vowel <note>These are Hebrew vowel marks; the *Kamez* usually indicates a long "a" sound, and the *Patach* a short "a".</note>. The specific passages are cited in the **Great Final Masora** <note>the larger index of notes at the end of the Bible</note> at the same place, from:
*   Numbers 24:9
*   Isaiah 38:13
*   Ezekiel 22:25
*   Psalm 21:17 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 22:16 or 17.</note>

Of these, the two in Numbers 24:9 and Ezekiel 22:25 have the word *ka’ari* written with a *Patach* [כַּאֲרִי]; the other two have it with a *Kamez* [כָּאֲרִי]. Now, if the true reading in our Psalm were *ka’aru* <note>they pierced</note>, how could it possibly be included in the list of places where *ka’ari* <note>like a lion</note> occurs?

Add to this the **Small Masora** between the columns for this specific passage, which offers this observation: *It occurs twice with Kamez in two different meanings...* <note>original Hebrew/Aramaic: ב' קמצין בתרי לישני שוי (bet kematzin bitrei lishne shavei). This suggests the same word appears twice with the same vowel but signifies two different things.</note> that is, this word *ka’ari* occurs twice written with the *Kamez* vowel but with a different meaning in each case. The other location is in **Isaiah 38:13**. Now, if the true reading in the Psalm were *ka’aru*, what would that passage in Isaiah have in common with ours?

Furthermore, at **Isaiah 38:13**, the word *ka’ari* is marked with a small circle, and in the margin is written the letter *Bet* [ב]—meaning it occurs twice with the *Kamez* vowel. This is explained more fully in the manuscript codex of the Prophets held in the **Jena Library**, which the famous **Frischmuth** mentioned in the work cited. This is an ancient copy written on large folio parchment, where the Hebrew text is followed by the **Chaldean Paraphrase** <term>Also known as the Targum, an ancient Aramaic translation and interpretation of the Hebrew Bible.</term>. The famous **Buxtorf**, in a letter to the famous **Hottinger**, testifies that he saw a similar copy in Strasbourg. In that manuscript, according to the testimony of the same famous Frischmuth, this note appears:

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the famous prophecy of Psalm 22:16 (Hebrew numbering), focusing on the debate between the readings "ka’ari" (like a lion) and "ka’aru" (they pierced). **"Like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (ka'ari)</note>, according to this illustrious prophecy—which clearly proves not only the truth of Christ but also the divinity of Holy Scripture—would become a worthless sentence, and one from which, whichever way you turn, you could never elicit a suitable meaning. §. 20. Finally, they add the consensus of Jews and Christians. However, since the credibility of such allegations from that *Haddarschan* has worn thin among most scholars, it does not move us.

Of greater importance is what the Illustrious **Frischmuth** observes in the *Midrash Tehillim* <term>A traditional Jewish commentary on the Psalms.</term> and the *Yalkut* <term>The Yalkut Shimoni, a major collection of midrashic interpretations.</term>, where the word *ka’ari* is explained using third-person plural verbs. That most learned man thinks this could not have happened unless they had approved the reading *ka’aru* <note>meaning "they pierced," a verb form</note>.

Whatever the case, in both the *Midrash* and the *Yalkut*, the reading *ka’ari* <note>like a lion</note> is preserved in the cited places, unless the Eminent Man thinks that due to the ravages of time, it crept into those books in place of *ka’aru*.

Therefore, this passage is not corrupted."* Certainly the passage is not corrupted, yet it is false that all Christians read *Caru*, as will be clear from what follows. <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Psalm 22:17 (Hebrew numbering), specifically the dispute over the reading of the word "ka’ari" (like a lion) versus "ka’aru" (they pierced). The previous page established that while Christian tradition favors "they pierced" for its Christological significance, the Masoretic evidence and various scholars suggest "like a lion" is the technically correct reading in the received Hebrew text. This page evaluates the authority of several scholars and examines the Masoretic vowel pointing to resolve the debate.</meta>

...let the learned and educated judge. Meanwhile, if someone—as we add following the **famous Alexander More** in his *Geneva Exercises*, page 93—out of a zeal to confirm our faith by every means, wishes to retain **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note>, we will not disagree too much, provided he does not demand that this reading be admitted into the main text. For the authority of **Rabbi Chajim**, **Johannes Isaacus**, or **Gerhard Veltuyck** is not so great that we must immediately grant them our assent.

§. 21. Since, therefore, the reading **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note> rests on no solid reasons, it remains for us to decide in favor of the other: **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note>. For since no other reading besides these two has been observed by anyone in any manuscript thus far, it is clear that once one is rejected, the other remains—unless they wish for the true reading to be entirely erased from human memory. The *Masora* <note>The traditional body of notes and rules for the exact transcription of the Hebrew Bible</note> further confirms **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> in an invincible manner. First, the *Small Masora* <note>Brief notes found in the side margins of the Hebrew Bible, between columns</note> at *Numbers 24:9*, already cited above, notes that the word **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> occurs four times in the sacred text: twice with a *Kamez* <note>a Hebrew vowel mark indicating a long 'a' sound</note> and twice with a *Patach* <note>a Hebrew vowel mark indicating a short 'a' sound</note>. The specific locations are cited in the *Great Final Masora* <note>A more extensive collection of Masoretic notes found at the end of some Bibles</note> in the same place: from *Numbers 24:9*, *Isaiah 38:13*, *Ezekiel 22:25*, and *Psalm 22:17*. Of these, two—*Numbers 24:9* and *Ezekiel 22:25*—have **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> with a *Patach*; the other two [Isaiah 38:13 and Psalm 22:17] have **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> with a *Kamez*. Now, if the true reading were **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note>, how could it be included in the list of places where **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> occurs? 

Add to this the *Small Masora* between the columns at this very passage [Psalm 22:17], which makes this observation: **"Twice [this word occurs] with a Kamez, in two different senses"** <note>original Hebrew: ב' קמצין בתרי לישני (*Bi qamtzin bitrei lishne*). This refers to the word *ka'ari* appearing with the same vowels but meaning two different things in two places.</note>. The other location is *Isaiah 38:13*. Now, if the true reading is **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note>, what would that passage from Isaiah have in common with our own? Furthermore, at *Isaiah 38:13*, the word **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> is marked with a small circle, and in the margin is written **2** <note>The Hebrew letter *Bet* used as a numeral</note>—that is, it occurs twice with a *Kamez*—a fact that is more fully explained in the manuscript codex of the Prophets in the Jena Library, which the **famous Frischmuth** mentions in the cited work. It is an ancient copy written on large parchment folios, in which the Aramaic Paraphrase <note>The Targum, a translation and interpretation of the Hebrew Bible into Aramaic</note> is placed beneath the Hebrew text; the **famous Buxtorf** testifies in a letter to the **famous Hottinger** that he saw a similar copy in *Strasbourg*. In that manuscript, as the same **famous Mr. Frischmuth** testifies, this note...
