<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Hebrew text of Psalm 22:17. The previous page established that while the author respects the Christian tradition of reading "they pierced" (ka'aru), the physical evidence of the Hebrew manuscripts and the Masoretic notes consistently point to "like a lion" (ka'ari). Here, the author examines specific Masoretic notes and the reliability of Jewish scribal preservation.</meta>

The Masoretic text <note>The authoritative Hebrew text of the Tanakh</note> reads: **"The word *ka'ari* [like a lion] occurs twice with the Kamets vowel; the signs for these are: 'I reckoned until morning; as a lion, so will he break all my bones' [Isaiah 38:13] and 'For dogs have compassed me; the assembly of the wicked have inclosed me' [Psalm 22:17]."** <note>original Hebrew: כארי ב׳ קמ׳ וסימניהון שויתי עד בקר : כארי כן ישבר כל עצמותי כי סבבוני כלבים עדת מרעים הקיפוני</note> This means that the word *Caari* <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*), "like a lion"</note> appears twice with the *Kamets* vowel <note>A long 'a' sound in Hebrew grammar</note> in these specific locations: Isaiah 38:13 and Psalm 22:17. We will present another piece of evidence from the *Masora Magna* <note>The "Great Masora," the more extensive set of traditional notes on the biblical text</note> below. This fivefold testimony of the Masora, which cannot be doubted on any rational basis, easily persuades us toward the reading **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*). The author here suggests that the Masoretic note itself—by linking these two disparate verses—might point toward the hidden presence of the alternative reading "they pierced."</note>.

§. 22. To this fivefold Masoretic testimony, we add the consensus of almost all manuscript codices, except perhaps for one or two, the dispute over which still **"rests on the knees of five judges"** <note>original Greek: ἐν πέντε κריטῶν γόνασι κεῖται—a classical idiom meaning the matter is still being decided by the highest authorities</note>. The *Complutensian* edition <note>The first multi-lingual printed Bible, sponsored by Cardinal Ximenes</note> (also called the Ximenian edition) does not snatch the reading **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note> away from us any more than does the Interlinear Bible codex printed by Plantin in 1572, or the **"authentic"** <note>original Greek: γνήσιον (*gnēsion*)</note> reading **"he"** <note>original Hebrew: הוא (*hu*)</note> in Genesis 3:15 <note>A reference to a famous debate: Does the text say "he" (the offspring) or "she" (Mary) shall bruise the serpent's head?</note>.

§. 23. Finally, with unanimous agreement, all the Jews under the sun approve of our opinion. Indeed, they hold to **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note> so stubbornly that (as the scholar Sixtinus Amama <note>A 17th-century Dutch specialist in Hebrew</note> himself admits) they would consider the entire book to be profaned by the alternative reading **"they pierced"** <note>original Hebrew: כארו (*ka'aru*)</note>, to the point that the manuscript would not even be worth buying. Those who have correctly considered what was said earlier—and especially the Jewish character regarding the preservation of the **literal word** <note>original Greek: ῥητῷ (*rhētō*)</note> and letter of the Holy Scriptures—will better judge the weight of this argument. They consider the crime of forgery, even in the smallest mark, to be a sin to be atoned for by a thousand deaths, believing the world itself might collapse because of it. Since it is in our interest that they not be slandered as forgers of the Holy Scriptures, does it not seem more prudent, if it can be done, to uphold their faithfulness in this one place? For there is no other verse in existence where the corruption of a single tiny letter could be so justly charged against them. Otherwise, we do not excuse their blasphemous interpretations; rather, we hate them with a perfect hatred and curse them with all our hearts.

§. 24. But the matter requires that we investigate the grammatical form and meaning of **"like a lion"** <note>original Hebrew: כארי (*ka'ari*)</note>. The Jews believe it consists of the word **"lion"** <note>original Hebrew: ארי (*ari*)</note> and a comparative particle <note>the 'ka-' prefix meaning 'as' or 'like'</note>, from which they explain this half-verse: *Like a lion [at] my hands and my feet.* They believe, however, that the verb **"they surrounded me"** <note>original Hebrew: הקיפוני (*hikifuni*)</note> must be understood **in common** <note>original Greek: ἐκ κοινοῦ (*ek koinou*)—a grammatical term meaning a word from a previous phrase applies to the current one</note> from the preceding clause. And so that you may better understand how a lion "surrounds" hands and feet, they refer it...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Hebrew text of Psalm 22:17 (verse 16 in English Bibles). Here, the author explores Rabbinic interpretations of "like a lion" and technical grammatical objections raised by Christian scholars like Calvin and Voetius.</meta>

to make a circle, from which the animal cannot exit out of fear. Rabbi David Kimchi graphically describes the whole matter **on this passage**: <note>original Hebrew: כי הקיפוני כמו האריה שמקיף בזנבו ביער וכל חיה שתראה אותה העגולה לא תצא משם ממורא האריה ומפחדו ויראתו ויאספו ידיהם ורגליהם וימצאה האריה טרפו בתוך עגולתו כן אנחנו בתוך העגולה לא נוכל לצאת ממנה שלא נהיה ביד הטורפים וגו</note> <note>Translation of Kimchi: "For they surrounded me like the lion who makes a circle with his tail in the forest; any beast that sees that circle will not dare to exit because of the dread and fear of the lion, but they draw in their front and hind legs, and the lion finds his prey in the middle of his circle. So we, held in captivity, are as if in the middle of a circle from which we cannot exit, lest we fall into the hands of the predators, etc."</note> As he did with most of his commentaries, Rabbi Solomon ben Melech described these things based on Kimchi **on this passage, folio 168, column 2, near the end.** Abraham ibn Ezra turned his eye to the same point, not to mention the *Midrash Tehillim* <note>An ancient Jewish homiletic commentary on the Psalms</note>.

§. 25. Scholars now strive to tear down this Jewish interpretation in more than one way. First, they point out that if the letter *Caph* <note>The Hebrew letter 'כ'</note> in the word *ka'ari* were "servile" <note>A functional prefix meaning "like"</note> as the Jews claim, it would require the vowel point *Patach* <note>A short 'a' sound</note> in this manner: **כַּאֲרִי** (*ka’ari*). So argues Johann Isaac in the cited work, and Heinrich Moller and John Calvin do not strongly disagree. However, it is most manifest from **Isaiah 38, verse 13** that a *Kamez* <note>A long 'a' sound</note> under the *Caph* does not, all things being equal, prevent it from being a servile letter. Similarly, Johann Isaac’s contention that the *Athnach* accent <note>A major musical and grammatical pause in Hebrew</note> on the word *hikifuni* [they surrounded me] prevents it from being applied in common <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ κοινοῦ (*apo koinou*)—a grammatical term for a word that governs two different clauses</note> and constructed with *ka'ari* is rightly refuted by Gisbertus Voetius. Nevertheless, regarding the difference in number <note>Grammatical number, i.e., singular vs. plural</note> between *hikifuni* [surrounded me—plural] and *ka'ari* [like a lion—singular], his observation is not without merit if one considers the Jewish exposition. Yet, the Jews can be most effectively refuted by the *Masora* <note>The traditional body of notes on the Hebrew text preserved by scribes</note>. It observes that *ka'ari* pointed with these vowels occurs only twice in the entire Holy Scripture, but under two different meanings. Thus, the *Masora Parva* <note>The "Small Masora," brief notes found in the side margins of Hebrew Bibles</note> states explicitly on this passage: ***Kaari* is found twice with *Kamets*, in two different senses.** <note>original: "Caari bis reperitur Kamezatum, בתרי לישני [betrei lishnei] sub duabus significationibus."</note> In the final *Masora Magna* <note>The "Great Masora," more extensive notes at the top and bottom of pages</note>, a full catalog is kept of word pairs that each

<meta>This page continues the philological debate regarding Psalm 22:17 (verse 16 in English Bibles). The previous page discussed the Jewish interpretation of the word "ka’ari" (כארי) as "like a lion," specifically addressing Rabbi David Kimchi’s theory that a lion traps prey by drawing a circle with its tail. This page argues that the Masoretic evidence and physical reality make the "like a lion" translation impossible in this specific context.</meta>

These [word forms] are found only twice, but always with a different sense; in the Masoretic notes **on folio 2, column 2, near the middle**, the word *ka’ari* <note>כארי</note> also occurs, and it is the sixtieth among ninety-eight listed examples. Now, in the other passage, **Isaiah 38:13**, which both versions of the Masora <note>The Great and Small Masora are traditional systems of notes on the Hebrew text</note> cite alongside our passage, *ka’ari* signifies *like a lion*, which everyone admits. Therefore, in **Psalm 22, verse 17**, it cannot mean *like a lion*. The most famous Master Frischmuth, following the example of the illustrious Hakspan, shows in **chapter 2, section 2**, how the Jews are strongly constrained by this argument.

Furthermore, if *ka’ari* is explained here as *like a lion*, it makes no sense. Let them explain, I pray, how a lion might "surround" the hands and feet of a person—and why specifically the hands and feet, rather than the head, the chest, or the belly? Our colleague Theander rightly asks in the **cited place, folio 252a**: *"What does it mean,"* he says, *"to say 'like a lion my hands and my feet'? For even if they say the verb 'besieged' should be repeated from the previous clause, so that it reads: 'The council of the wicked has besieged me, like a lion has besieged my hands and my feet,' they will not escape the problem; rather, they run into a greater absurdity. How does a lion besiege hands and feet, when the Holy Scripture usually attributes to him an open mouth, roaring, and predatory nature, so that he devours the whole? Or what trifle of the Holy Spirit would it be, that after being entirely surrounded by a council of evildoers, it should add that the hands and feet are also being 'besieged' by a lion? As if he who besieges the entire body does not also besiege the hands and feet."*

Regarding the fictional "lion’s circle," let us hear from the most sagacious Bochart in **chapter 783**: *"What could be more foolish than this invention?"* he says. *"Who would believe that a lion, upon seeing prey, would not charge straight at it, but instead draw a line around the place? Or that there is some magical power in the circle he draws with his tail, by which wild beasts remain rooted and immobile? Or that the beasts do not flee while the circle is being made, but wait patiently until they are hemmed in?"* Certainly, while Kimchi wanted to make the lion into something of a mathematician, he proved himself a poor physicist <note>In this context, "physicist" refers to a student of natural history or the behavior of animals</note>. Elsewhere, that immortal glory of our Academy, Dr. Abraham Calovius—our venerable patron—vigorously refutes the Jewish interpretation in his *Antigrotian Biblical Dissertations* <note>Dissertations written against the views of Hugo Grotius</note>, works worthy of being preserved in cedar <note>An idiom meaning the works are worthy of immortality</note>, specifically in his **Disputation on the Psalms 2, letter Y. 3**, which the eager reader may consult.

§. 26. Furthermore, there is no lack of Christians who also explain *ka’ari* as:

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the controversial Hebrew word *ka’ari* in Psalm 22:16. The previous page established that the Masoretic notes distinguish this specific instance from other uses of the word meaning "like a lion." Here, the author examines and critiques alternative interpretations offered by both Jewish and Christian scholars who attempt to reconcile the "lion" imagery with the act of piercing or surrounding.</meta>

...[they] maintain the reading **"like a lion,"** and they repeat the word *ka’ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי</note>, which they interpret not as **"surrounding,"** but as **"digging"** or **"piercing."** They derive this not from the root *kwr* <note>Hebrew: כור</note> but from *krh* <note>Hebrew: כרה, meaning to dig or bore</note>. They argue that this is the intended meaning based on passages such as **Isaiah 10:30, Isaiah 24:3, and Job 19:26**, as well as the related word *kerah* <note>Hebrew: כרה</note>. Finally, they cite the testimony of **Philippe d’Aquin**, a Jewish convert to Christianity, who says that *krh* is a **"term for cutting and breaking"** <note>original: לשון כריתה ושבירה (leshon keritah u-shevirah)</note>. Thus, the resulting sense would be: **"Like a lion they have pierced my hands and my feet."** This position is held by **Gisbertus Voetius** in the work cited above, **Alexander Morus** in his *Genevan Exercises* (p. 91), and **Johannes Leusden** in his *Jonah Illustrated* (p. 48). Even **Edward Pococke** does not reject it (pp. 62–63), and **Salomon Glassius** recalls it as a formerly accepted view in his *Philologia Sacra* (p. 92). To speak the truth, this opinion is not absurd; it is tolerable. However, it still troubles us for several reasons: first, because the **Masora** <note>The traditional system of notes on the Hebrew biblical text</note> expressly warns that *ka’ari* is to be understood differently here than in **Isaiah 38:13**; second, because the Jews, not without reason, prefer the more common meaning of the *Hiphil* verb *hiqiph* <note>Hebrew: הקיף</note>, which means **"to surround"** or **"to encircle"**; third, because the meaning of "surrounding" fits the **"council of the wicked"** <note>mentioned earlier in the Psalm</note> more gracefully than "piercing"; and finally, because one should attribute to a lion the tearing of the whole body and the crushing of all limbs or bones, rather than specifically the piercing of the hands and feet.

### §. 27.
Others, rather than repeating the preceding verb, are satisfied with a sensible supplement of some other word. Thus, the **Chaldean** <note>The Aramaic translation or Targum</note> supplied the word **"biting"** <note>Aramaic: נכתין (nakthin)</note>. **Rashi** <note>The preeminent medieval Jewish commentator</note> understands a word for **"crushing,"** while Christians understand **"piercing."** This was the view of **Agostino Giustiniani**, Bishop of Nebbio, in his *Octaplar Psalter* <note>A polyglot edition of the Psalms in eight languages published in 1516</note>, and it is not rejected by **Amandus Polanus** in his *Syntagma* (Book 1, ch. 37, p. 78), **Johannes Quistorp** in his *Biblical Annotations* (p. 413), or the Transylvanian **Benedek Szentkirályi** in his defense of the Old Testament against **Enjedinus** (p. 121), among others. Indeed, we do not entirely deny that such ellipses <note>omitted words understood by context</note> occur in the Sacred Code; however, one should not easily take refuge in them unless necessity itself demands it. Furthermore, the aforementioned Masoretic note also stands in the way, as it excludes the notion of a "lion" in this context, to say nothing of other objections.

<summary>The author critiques the theory that "like a lion" in Psalm 22 implies "piercing," arguing that Masoretic evidence and the context of the surrounding "council of the wicked" favor a different root. He also dismisses the use of implied "biting" or "crushing" verbs as unnecessary linguistic shortcuts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Hebrew word *ka’ari* in Psalm 22:16. The author is evaluating the theory that the word might be a "hybrid" form combining the meanings of "digging/piercing" and "lion."</meta>

§ 28. Others accept both readings and maintain that the word *ka’ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי</note> is composed of *karu* <note>Hebrew: כרו, meaning "they dug" or "they pierced"</note> and *ari* <note>Hebrew: ארי, meaning "lion"</note>, or *ka’ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי, "as a lion"</note>, so that the sense is: **"they pierced like a lion."** In this way, the rage of those piercing and their lion-like madness is signified. This is the view of **Arias Montanus** <note>A 16th-century Spanish Orientalist and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible</note> on this passage, whose words **Rivetus** reports on page 161: *“The witnesses and indicators of the most ancient reading, by whose labor the Masoretic book is circulated, annotate two readings in this place, and assert they should be joined together: 'Charu,' that is, 'they dug'; and 'Chari,' that is, 'as a lion.' Moreover, we understand that lions are the most powerful of all beasts in strength and impulse, and they prevail with claws that are both very hard and very sharp, most strong for wounding, transfixing, and retaining prey. Therefore, the rigid and tenacious force of the nails with which Christ was fastened to the cross, as well as their most sharp penetration, is declared to be very similar to wounds inflicted by a lion; it likewise marks the rage, cruelty, and ferocity of the soul of those who did it.”* 

If Montanus—as **Sixtinus Amama** (p. 459) concludes not without reason—thought that this followed from the words of the **Lesser Masora**, *“in two senses”* <note>Hebrew/Aramaic: בתרי לישני (bitrei lishnei)</note>, he was mistaken. For that phrase does not imply the merging of two words into one, but rather a different meaning for the same word recurring twice. For this is what the heading of that catalog means, which we mentioned previously (Book 1, Chapter 8, near the middle): *“An alphabet containing word pairs, each of which has a different meaning.”* <note>Hebrew: א'ב מן כב ותרויהן בתרי לישני (Aleph-Bet min kav ve-tarveihan bi-trei lishnei)</note> The enumerated examples easily prove the same. 

**Avenarius**, in his *Lexicon* (p. 358) and *Grammar* (p. 3), confirms the composition of the words *karu* and *ari* into *ka’ari* from the **Chaldean translation** <note>The Targum, an Aramaic paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note>, which has: **“biting like a lion.”** <note>Aramaic: נכתין היך כאריא (nakhthin heikh ka'arya)</note> **Dr. Christian Helvicus** calls this same opinion the clearest and simplest, illustrating it with examples (*Vindiciae*, p. 18). **Nicolaus Petræus** (*Hebrew Lexicon*, p. 576) and the most distinguished **Dr. Matthias Wasmuth**, our most honored friend (*Inaugural Disputation*, Controversy 6, p. 28), approve of the same. We shall not pursue that view with hatred; however, we confess that we have not yet been able to bring our mind to accept the **synaeresis** <note>A grammatical term for the contraction of two vowels or syllables into one</note> of the wo...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Hebrew word *ka’ari* in Psalm 22:16. The author is evaluating whether it is a hybrid word or a grammatical variation. On the previous page, the author began refuting the idea that the word combines the meanings of "digging" and "lion."</meta>

...even if we believed that **karu** <note>Hebrew: כרו, "they dug"</note> and **ka’ari** <note>Hebrew: כארי, "as a lion"</note> were merged into a single word **ka’aru** <note>Hebrew: כארו</note>. In our judgment, such a thing seems to disadvantage the simplicity of the literal sense. What will others attempt if it is once granted that the same literal letter in the same word can involve a reference to the most diverse meanings? Nor does the linguistic analogy <term>Analogy: The systematic rules and patterns of a language</term> admit this conflation of a noun and a verb into one word. Furthermore, the Jews laugh at such inventions <note>Greek original: εὑρήματα (heuremata)</note>; they are far from being moved by them. The examples brought forward by Helvicus are either foreign to the matter <note>Greek original: ἀλλότρια (allotria)</note> or wrongly understood; and the most celebrated Wasmuthus—let this be said with the permission of such a great man—brings in the concepts of the **written** <note>Hebrew: כתבן (Kethivan)</note> and the **read** <note>Hebrew: קריון (Keriyan)</note> forms to no avail here. 

But the invention of Lorinus is a too-crude commentary. Having posited that contraction <note>Latin: *crasi*</note> (which he nevertheless does not approve), he unskillfully judges that **ka’ari** can be explained in the genitive case on page 381 in this manner: *“Just as they dug my hands and my feet as of a lion, because (he adds) formerly lions—as just now among us wolves and bears—were fixed to boards placed before the doors of the houses of leading men, or nocturnal birds that had penetrated the hearth, as Junius Columella, Palladius, and Apuleius mention.”* But we do not linger over trifles of this kind.

§. 29. Others, having entirely neglected the notion of a "lion," proceed differently in the explanation of the word **ka’ari**. The blessed Gesner (Commentary on this place, page 225) thinks that with a single small point added, **ka’ari** <note>כארי</note> can become **ka’arei** <note>Hebrew: כָּאֲרֵי</note>, so that it is the plural number in the construct state <term>Construct state: A grammatical form in Hebrew used to link two nouns together, like "of..."</term> instead of **ko’arim** <note>כֹּאֲרִים</note>, meaning "those who pierce." Capito (Institutes, Book 1, Chapter 13) says that he learned this reading from a certain Caspar Amon. 

This same opinion has a great author as well: our own **Luther** (in the place cited, folio 252. a. near the end). Nor does it conflict with the laws of punctuation <note>Referring to the Masoretic vowel points added to the Hebrew text</note>, as the celebrated Mr. Glassius persuades himself in his *Added Grammar*, page 146, and his *Sacred Philology*, page 91. For from the root **kur** <note>Hebrew: כור, "to dig"</note> it would regularly be **karei** <note>כָּרֵי</note> (with the **Qamets** <note>A Hebrew vowel sign indicating a long "a" sound</note> not being changed at all on account of "impurity") and with a mobile, inserted letter *Aleph* <note>The Hebrew letter א (Aleph) is sometimes inserted as a phonetic filler</note> (as in the word *ra’amei*). What prevents us, however, from going fully into this opinion is that it would then have to be granted either that the [vowel] points are not of the same age as the letters (or do not have the same authority as them), or that the Jews—if not in the letters...

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Psalm 22:16, specifically the controversy over the Hebrew word "ka’ari" (as a lion) versus "karu" (they dug). The author is currently refuting various scholarly attempts to reconcile these two readings or to find hidden meanings in the unusual spelling found in the Hebrew text.</meta>

...at least that violent hands were laid upon the vowels; it is certain that neither of these conjectures should be admitted.

§. 30. The renowned Louis **de Dieu**, in his *Observations on this passage*, devised another conjecture: namely, he derives the word *ka’ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי</note> from *ka’ar* <note>Hebrew: כאר</note>, which means "to disfigure" or "to defile." He bases this on a Syriac usage, which is also the source of the translation by Aquila <note>Aquila of Sinope was a 2nd-century translator known for his extremely literal Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> as "they shamed" <note>Greek original: ᾔσχυναν (eskhynan)</note>. From this, the infinitive in the Piel form <term>Piel: A Hebrew verb structure usually indicating intensive or intentional action</term> would be *ka’ar* and, with the added suffix, *ka’ari*, so that the sense would be: *The congregation of evildoers has surrounded me, disfiguring me in my hands and my feet, or disfiguring my hands and feet for me.* But that speculation <note>Greek original: στοχασμός (stokhasmos)</note> does not please me. For besides the fact that it would more likely be written as *ke’eru* <note>Hebrew: כֵּאֲרוּ</note> to carry that meaning, the grammatical construction is quite awkward; furthermore, the verb *ka’ar* with the meaning of "disfiguring" is unknown to the Hebrew language, nor can a suitable reason be provided for using a Syriac form in this instance.

§. 31. Others explain the word without changing the letters: "They dug," but they say the form *ka’ari* is irregular <term>Heteroclite: A word that deviates from standard grammatical patterns, often interpreted by older scholars as containing hidden meaning</term> and indeed believe that by its strange formation it enfolds great mysteries. Thus, the most learned former Professor of Hebrew at Leipzig, Dr. Bernhard Ziegler, said that through the writing of the word with a *Yod* <note>The Hebrew letter י</note> or a diminished *Vav* <note>The Hebrew letter ו</note>, the number of years that passed from the time this psalm was written until the Crucifixion of Christ was mystically determined (namely, through Gematria <term>Gematria: A Kabbalistic method of interpreting Hebrew words by calculating the numerical value of their letters</term>) to be 1,230 years, as reported by Johann Forster in his *Hebrew Dictionary, page 394*. Dr. Nicolaus Selnecker approved of Ziegler’s "pious Kabbalah" in his *German Commentary on this passage, page 131*, with these words: 

**"Therefore, Christ here also uses a particularly strange word, upon which all Jews even to this day stumble and are led astray, and yet they do not know what it is or what it means. For where it says here: 'They have dug through,' the Hebrew language has *Caari*, whereas it should properly be called *Caru*. But as has been said, it was without doubt set down thus by the Holy Spirit with good deliberation and counsel, like the word *Lemarbe* <note>Hebrew: לםרבה. A reference to Isaiah 9:7, where a Hebrew letter is written in an unusual "closed" form, often cited as a mystical prophecy</note> in Isaiah. And the scholars know that in the word *Caari*, which here means as much as 'they have dug through,' the number of the**

...the year is understood / from the time of the prophecy / until the Passion of Christ / namely 1230 years. But that the time does not match (since the psalm was written so many years before the birth of David) is observed by the Blessed Dr. Walther in *Offices p. 99* and *Harmony p. 515*, and with him the Blessed Weller in *Syriac-Hebrew Gleanings 3. qu. 1. §. 2.* and the Magnificent Dr. Martin Geyer, our greatest patron and most learned, in *Lectures on the Psalms p. 370.* And it is otherwise known how such <term>Kabbalistic: Referring to Jewish mystical traditions that seek hidden meanings in the letters and numbers of the Bible</term> observations are to be regarded. They may piously delight, but they convince and bind no one. Such is likewise the observation found in Felix regarding the Aleph <note>The Hebrew letter א</note> and Vav <note>The Hebrew letter ו</note> of this word, who reports—according to Lorinus—that the former expresses the <term>Tetragrammaton: The four-letter sacred name of God in Hebrew, YHWH</term> according to its number, and the latter by its power. The Blessed Weller also mentions this in the place cited above, §. 23, in these words: *What if someone wishes to suggest that the Holy Spirit alludes to the essential name of God* <note>Hebrew original: יהוה (YHWH/Yahweh)</note> *because they were to crucify the Lord of Glory (as Paul testifies); he would certainly put forward nothing contrary to the analogy of faith.* Such also is the interpretation of Paul Elchanon, a converted Jew, in his *Kabbalistic Mystery*, who from the words "as a lion my hands and my feet" <note>Hebrew original: כאari ידי ורגלי (ka’ari yadai v’raglai)</note> through <term>Gematria: A system of assigning numerical value to a word or phrase</term> (the sum being 504) elicits this: *This is for Jesus, son of David* <note>Hebrew original: זה לישוע בן דויד (zeh l'Yeshua ben David)</note>. Pious, but not solid.

### §. 32. 
Another mystery is revealed by Marcus Marinus of Brescia in the *Ark of Noah* under the root *ka’ari* <note>Hebrew: כארי</note>, page 332: namely, that through the extraordinary writing <insert>כארי</insert> (which he assumes should be internally punctuated) instead of a Vav <note>The letter ו</note>, the scars of the nails of Christ are hinted at. His words are: *However, that passage in Psalm 22:17, "they dug my hands and my feet," has an Aleph* <note>א</note> *by <term>Epenthesis: The addition of a letter or sound into the middle of a word</term> and a diminished Vav* <note>A "Vav" written smaller than usual in a manuscript</note>, *so that it clearly hints at the scars of the nails of Christ.* The Blessed Weller calls this reason given by Marinus "neat and quite suitable." However, even though Hakspan (in his *Additions to Lipmann, p. 300*) responds to Marinus that a diminished Vav occurs elsewhere quite often without any connotation of nails or indication of a similar mystery, nevertheless, in this place, we would not deny the mystery, provided the internal point of the letter Jod <note>The Hebrew letter י</note> were sufficiently proven, about which we will soon say more.

<meta>This page continues the technical philological analysis of Psalm 22:17 (verse 16 in most English Bibles), specifically focusing on the controversial Hebrew word "ka’ari." The author is evaluating the arguments of scholars who attempt to reconcile the reading "as a lion" with the Christian interpretation "they pierced," which is central to the crucifixion narrative.</meta>

§. 33. Furthermore, men **worth many others** <note>original: "πολλῶν ἀντάξιοι ἄλλων" — a Greek idiom often used to denote highly esteemed scholars</note>, whose footsteps we humbly follow, believe that the most convenient way of resolving this doubt is if the Hebrew word *ka’ari* <note>כארי</note> is simply explained as **"They pierced,"** and it is said **by way of letter substitution** <note>original: "κατ’ ἀντιστοιχίαν"</note> that the letter *Yod* (י) stands in place of the *Vav* (ו). Thus, the word would be read as *ka’aru* <note>כארו</note>. In this view, the *Yod* is regarded as what they call a "diminished *Vav*"—specifically a *Jod-Shureq* instead of a *Vav-Shureq* <term>Shureq: A Hebrew vowel mark indicating a long "u" sound; here, the debate is whether a letter usually sounding like "y" or "i" is acting as a "u"</term>—as is seen in many other places (for example, *nesha'i* for *nesha'u* in **Ezra 10:44** and four times in a single verse in **Jeremiah 50:10**; compare Avenarius in his *Grammar*). This is why the *Royal Bible* and the *Hutterite Codex* <note>16th-century polyglot Bibles used by scholars</note> present *ka'aru* instead of *ka'ari*. This substitution is pleasing to the Blessed Gerhard, Glassius in his *Grammatical Supplement* and *Sacred Philology*, Weller, Walther in his *Biblical Harmony*, and others.

§. 34. However, no one should find fault with us if, **from the depths of our soul** <note>original: "ἀπὸ βαθείας ψυχῆς"</note>—and while preserving the eternal reputation of these great men and the reverence due to their names—we speak out about what doubt still resides in our mind. If this doubt were removed, we would follow their opinion wholeheartedly. They think it should either be written as *ka'ari* with a point under the letter *Resh* before the *Chireq* <note>Another Hebrew vowel marker</note>, so that it is simply a substitution of the long "i" sound for the long "u" sound, and *ka'ari* is said instead of *ka'aru*. However, such a substitution would be **without any other example**. It could be said to be a "unique instance" <note>original: "exemplum monadicum"</note> of its kind, but that will hardly be proved in a way that puts the minds of doubters at rest. They might also appeal to the Syriac feminine plural ending of the past tense here, but this is hindered not only by the difference in language but also by the grammatical gender. Alternatively, they establish that it should be written *ka'ari* so that there is a so-called *Jod-Shureq* at the end. While a significant number of similar examples exist for this, we are first blocked by the many testimonies of the **Masoretes** <term>Masoretes: Jewish scholars between the 6th and 10th centuries who compiled the system of pronunciation and grammatical guides for the Hebrew Bible</term>. These scholars prove that the spelling of our word *ka'ari* here is identical in every way to the one found in **Isaiah 38:13** <note>In Isaiah, the word clearly means "as a lion"</note>. Since the word in Isaiah does not have the "u" sound (the *Shureq*), who would believe that the Masoretes would so often note ours as a parallel to it if ours had the *Shureq*? Furthermore, the Jews cannot be cleared of all suspicion of falsifying the text by this reasoning. At the very least, this suspicion will always remain with them...

While acknowledging the support of famous scholars for this view, the author expresses doubt because the ancient Masoretic notes link the word to a passage in Isaiah where it definitely means "lion."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Psalm 22:16. The author is concluding an argument that certain scribal changes—moving a vowel point from one letter to another—were made "not without fraud" to alter the meaning of the text. This leads into the author's own conclusion on how to resolve the grammatical tension between "as a lion" and "they pierced."</meta>

...it will be considered a fault that they removed the dot that had been on the letter *Yod* and repositioned it before the *Yod* under the letter *Resh*, not without trickery. If they can be proven guilty of this here, their credibility will be endangered in other matters as well.

§. 35. Now time advises that we finally explain what our own view is regarding the present doubt. Since those Cretans whom Plato says **practiced much thought rather than much talking** <note>original Greek: πολὺνοιαν μᾶλλον, ἢ πολυλογίαν ἀσκῆσαι</note> have always seemed upright to us, we will keep our words brief. Accordingly, we think it is sufficiently established from what has been said that the genuine reading is *ka’ari* <note>כארי</note>, which there is no point in repeating tediously. The meaning of "digging" or "piercing" is easily persuasive, partly because of the solid rejection of the other interpretation "as a lion," and partly because of the **unanimity** <note>original Greek: ὁμοψηφία — literally "a consensus of votes"</note> of all the ancient interpreters. Furthermore, other reasons are drawn from the circumstances and the **continuity** <note>original Greek: συνεχείᾳ</note> of the text, in the light of its fulfillment in the New Testament parallel passage **Zechariah 12:10**, etc. For these, see our great Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a prominent Lutheran theologian and defender of orthodoxy</note> in his *Anti-Grotian writings on this passage at verse 2*, and the Venerable Dr. Johann Müller in *Judaism, Part I, chapter 21, pages 755 and following*. Therefore, a doubt remains only regarding the grammatical form. As we prepare ourselves to resolve this, we have but one wish: that we might find a fair reader, **sincere and pure from head to toe.** <note>original Latin: "Candidus & talos à vertice purus ad imos" — a quote from the Roman poet Horace, meaning a reader without hidden bias or "dirt" on their character.</note>

§. 36. To begin, we propose the root *kur* <note>כור</note>, which we define as **to dig** or **to pierce through.** Although this specific root is not found in the Sacred Code <note>The Hebrew Bible</note>, it is not to be doubted that it once existed in the Hebrew language. This is sufficiently proven by the "kindred" roots ready at hand—namely *karah* <note>כרה</note> and *kur* <note>כור</note>—which have exactly the same meaning. From these, the essential letters and the form of the root *kur* are certain. Regarding the former, it is too well known to need mention that "quiescent" verbs <term>Quiescent verbs: Hebrew verbs containing "weak" letters (like Vav or He) that often remain silent or swap with one another during conjugation</term> of the types *Ayin-Vav* and *Lamed-He* easily exchange their forms while the meaning remains the same.

For example:
- *dur* and *darah* <note>to dwell or circle</note>
- *dum* and *damah* <note>to be silent</note>
- *duch* and *dakah* <note>to crush or pound</note>
- *hum* and *hamar* <note>to make noise or disturb</note>
- *buz* and *bazah* <note>to despise</note>
- *shur* and *sharah* <note>to watch or contend</note>
- *suf* and *safah* <note>to end or consume</note>
- *shot* and *shatah* <note>to swerve or scourge</note>

In the same way, we have *karah* and *kur*. Regarding the second point, it likewise cannot be denied that letters produced by the same vocal organ and sound are **easily changeable.** <note>original Greek: εὐμεταβλήτες</note> For example: *alas* <note>עלץ</note>, *alam* <note>עלם</note>, and *alaz* <note>עלו</note> for **"he exulted"**; *tsachaq* <note>צחק</note> and *shachaq* <note>שחק</note> for **"he laughed."** In the same way, we have *kur* <note>כור</note> and *qur* <note>קור</note> for **"to dig."** Add to this that in the Arabic language, from which the Hebrew often...

<meta>The previous page ended with the author expressing the hope that he might "find a [way]" to resolve the grammatical difficulties regarding the word *ka’ari* in Psalm 22. This page provides the linguistic evidence to justify reading the word as "piercing" rather than "like a lion," which supports the traditional Christian interpretation of the prophecy of the crucifixion. The catchword "derunt" from the previous page likely completed a verb such as "reddiderunt" (they restored), leading into the phrase "granted to it before."</meta>

...[restored] to it what was granted before and is now lost by the injury of time; even today both the roots <term>KWR (כור)</term> and <term>K’R (כאר)</term>—from which, in the second conjugation according to Golius <note>Jacobus Golius, a 17th-century Orientalist and author of a famous Arabic-Latin lexicon</note>, <term>K’R</term> means "he thrust down by digging with a spear," and in Al-Firuzabadi <note>A 14th-century Persian scholar who wrote a major Arabic dictionary</note>, <term>al-KWR</term> means "digging of the earth"—are in common use. And who would doubt the root, when among the Hebrews the noun <term>KWR (כור)</term> exists and denotes a *basin or metallic furnace*, which can quite suitably be named from "digging" <note>original Latin: effodiendo</note>.

§. 37. Furthermore, from <term>KWR (כור)</term>, by analogy, the <term>Benoni Participle</term> <note>The active participle in Hebrew grammar, representing ongoing action</note> becomes *Kar* (כָּר), plural *Karim* (כָּרִים), just as from *QUM* (קוּם) comes *Qam* (קָם), plural *Qamim* (קָמִים). In our example, the inserted <term>Aleph (א)</term> <note>The first letter of the Hebrew alphabet, here used as a vowel-like marker</note> should disturb no one, unless it escapes their notice that in this class of verbs <note>Referencing "hollow verbs" where the middle letter is weak</note> the letter *Aleph* is often used in place of *Vau* [ו], such as *qamim* (קָאמִים) in Hosea 14:10 instead of the standard *qamim* (קָמִים). A whole collection of such examples is provided by Avenarius <note>Johannes Avenarius, a 16th-century Hebrew lexicographer</note> on page 523 and following. Indeed, Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A famous medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator, often called Radak</note> in the *Michlol* (folio 35) establishes that in verbs called "quiescent in the second radical," the middle letter is actually an *Aleph* hidden within the *Kamez* vowel. The illustrious Frischmuth proves this as well from Rabbi Tanchum’s commentary on that passage in Hosea. <margin>Compare the *Journey* [original: ὁδοιπορία] of Rabbi Moses Kimchi from the edition of L’Empereur, pages 156–157.</margin> Therefore, there is no need to either refer the *Aleph* to the "mothers of reading" <note>original: matres lectionis; letters used as vowels before modern punctuation existed</note>—which some falsely imagine supplied the place of vowels in the ancient sacred text—as Lorinus and Hackspan do; nor is there a need to worry about an "Arabism" <note>A grammatical structure borrowed from Arabic</note> as Hottinger and others do in his *Oriental Smegma*, since a form common to both Hebrew and Arabic should not immediately be labeled an Arabism. Thus, instead of *karim* (כָּרִים) we have *ka’arim* (כָּאֲרִים), just as *da’mot* appears for *damot* in Proverbs 24:7, and *sha’tim* for *shatim* in Ezekiel 28:24. Likewise, the *Aleph* is moved in *ka’aru* [they pierced], just as in the past tense *ra’amu* appears for *ramu* in Zechariah 14:10, and in the Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note> participle *qa’amaya* in Daniel 7:18. Finally, from *ka’arim* (כָּאֲרִים) comes *ka’ari* (כָּאֲרִי) through <term>apocope</term> <note>The dropping of a sound or letter at the end of a word</note>, just as in 2 Kings 11:4 and verse 19, where *la-kari* (לַכָּרִי) is said for *la-karim* (לַכָּרִים). Similarly, we see *Ammi* in Joshua 15:63 and 2 Samuel 22:44; *Chori* in Genesis 40:16; and *Chezi* for *Chezim* in 2 Samuel 20:36. Therefore, <term>ka’ari (כָּאֲרִי)</term> is a *plural Benoni participle* in place of *karim*, occurring through the <term>epenthesis</term> <note>The insertion of a letter into the middle of a word</note> of *Aleph* and the *apocope* of the letter *Mem* [ם]. It should be translated as: **"They digging"** (or piercing), so that the sense is: "The assembly (a collective noun) of the malignant have surrounded [me]..."

§. 38. Let us see, however, what might be objected against us. Perhaps some will say the opinion is new and hardly noticed by more than one or two minor scholars. But that moves us little. Whatever is oldest is truest. *Those who moved these matters before us, we say with Seneca, are not our masters, but our guides. <meta>The previous page's translation ended with a discussion of the word "pierced" (foderunt). Although the catchword provided for this page was "qui-", the current OCR begins with "scit", likely part of "sicut" (just as) or a continuation of the previous argument regarding the linguistic roots granted to the word. This page continues the intense grammatical defense of reading "pierced" or "digging" in Psalm 22 instead of "like a lion."</meta>

...as was granted to it before and is now lost through the injury of time, even today both roots <note>original Arabic: كأر & كير (ka’ara and kayara)</note>—from which comes "He struck down, digging with a spear" in *Golius’s second conjugation* <note>Jacobus Golius (1596–1667) was a Dutch orientalist; his "Lexicon Arabico-Latinum" was a standard reference</note> and "A digging of the earth" in *al-Firuzabadi* <note>Abu Tahir al-Firuzabadi (1329–1414), author of the famous Arabic dictionary "Al-Qamus"</note>—are still in use. And who could doubt the root, when the noun <term>kur (כור)</term> <note>original: כור</note> exists among the Hebrews, signifying a *crucible or a metallic furnace*, which can quite fittingly be named from the act of "digging out."

§. 37. Furthermore, from the root <term>kur (כור)</term>, the plural <term>Active Participle</term> <note>The author uses the Hebrew term "Benoni" (intermediate) for the active participle</note> is formed by analogy as <term>korim (כורים)</term>, just as <term>qum (קום)</term> becomes <term>qam (קם)</term>, plural <term>qamim (קמים)</term>. In our specific example, the inserted letter <term>aleph (א)</term> should trouble no one, except those who are unaware that in this class of verbs, <term>aleph</term> is often used in place of <term>vau (ו)</term>. For instance, <term>q'am (קאם)</term> appears in *Hosea 14:10* instead of <term>qam (קם)</term>. A whole collection of such examples can be found in *Avenarius, page 523* <note>Johann Habermann (1516–1590), also known as Avenarius, a German Lutheran theologian and Hebraist</note>. Indeed, *Rabbi David Kimchi* <note>A famous medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator (c. 1160–1235), often called the Radak</note> in the *Michlol, folio 35*, establishes that in verbs called "quiescent <term>Ayin-Vau</term>" <note>Verbs where the second letter of the root is a 'w' or 'y' that often disappears or changes</note>, the middle radical is actually an <term>aleph</term> and lies hidden in the <term>Qamets</term> vowel. The illustrious *Frischmuth* <note>Johannes Frischmuth (1619–1687), a German Hebraist</note> proves this in the cited passage, section 16, following *Rabbi Tanchum* <note>Tanchum of Jerusalem, a 13th-century philologist</note> on that same passage of *Hosea*. *Compare also the "Journey" of Rabbi Moses Kimchi, from the edition by L'Empereur, pages 156 and 157.* 

Therefore, there is no need to follow *Lorin* (page 380) or *Hackspan* (Part 2, page 306) <note>Theodoricus Hackspan (1607–1659), a Lutheran theologian and orientalist</note> in assigning this <term>aleph</term> to the <term>matres lectionis</term> <note>Literally "mothers of reading"—consonants used to indicate vowel sounds in ancient Hebrew</note>, which are imagined to have supplied the place of vowels in the sacred text of old. Nor is it necessary, as *Hottinger* does in his *Harmonic Grammar* (page 155) and *Oriental Smegma* (page 115) <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist; "Smegma Orientale" is a work on Eastern languages</note>, to insist on this being an "Arabism," since a form common to both Hebrew and Arabic should not immediately be called an Arabism. Thus, <term>ka’arim (כארים)</term> stands for <term>korim (כורים)</term>, just as <term>da’amot (דאמות)</term> appears for <term>damot (דמות)</term> in *Proverbs 24:7*, and <term>sha’atim (שאטים)</term> for <term>shatim (שטים)</term> in *Ezekiel 28:24*, and so on. 

Finally, by moving the <term>aleph</term> to <term>ka’ari (כָּאֲרִי)</term>, as occurs in the <term>Perfect tense</term> where <term>ra’amu (רָאֲמוּ)</term> appears for <term>ramu (רָמוּ)</term> in *Zechariah 14:10*, and in the <term>Chaldean</term> <note>A term formerly used for the Aramaic language</note> participle <term>qa’amaya (קָאֲמַיָּא)</term> in *Daniel 7:18*. At last, from <term>ka’arim (כארים)</term> comes <term>ka’ari (כארי)</term> through <term>apocope</term> <note>The dropping of a sound or letter at the end of a word</note>, just as <term>le-khari (לְכָרִי)</term> is said instead of <term>le-kharim (לְכָרִים)</term> in *2 Kings 11:4*, and <term>ha-kari (הַכָּרִי)</term> for <term>ha-karim (הַכָּרִים)</term> in verse 19. Similarly, <term>ammi (עַמִּי)</term> in *Joshua 15:63*, <term>chori (חוֹרִי)</term> in *Genesis 40:16*, and <term>chatsi (חֲצִי)</term> for <term>chatsim (חֲצִים)</term> in *2 Samuel 20*. 

And so, <term>ka’ari (כארי)</term> is a <term>plural active participle</term> standing for <term>korim</term>, formed by the <term>epenthesis</term> <note>The insertion of an extra sound into a word</note> of <term>aleph</term> and the <term>apocope</term> of the final <term>m</term>. It should be translated as: **"Digging,"** so that the meaning is: **"The assembly (a collective noun) of the wicked have surrounded [me], they are digging [my hands and feet]."**

With that opinion now accepted, first the integrity of the word <term>ka’ari</term> is asserted, and the Jews cannot be accused of the crime of forgery. Second, a fitting sense of this passage emerges—one that is highly consistent with the Masorah <note>The Masorah is the traditional Jewish system of textual notation and preservation</note> (which suggests the same spelling but a different meaning here and in *Isaiah 38:13*), with the <term>continuity</term> <note>original Greek: συνεχείᾳ (synecheia)</note> of the text, with the analogy of faith, with grammatical formation (as we have seen), and with syntax (since one need not assume a lack of conjunctions if a participle is admitted). Finally, the authority of so many translators who used the notion of "digging" in their translations remains firm. However, no conjecture has been brought forward previously that does not lack something from these requirements, as is evident from what has been said.

§. 38. Let us see, however, what might be objected against us. The opinion, some will perhaps say, is new and has scarcely been observed by one or two of the <term>lesser gods</term> <note>original: minorum gentium DEO. This is a scholarly idiom for minor authorities or less famous researchers.</note>. But that moves us little. We admit the opinion has been brought back to light recently, but we deny that it is therefore new. *Whatever is oldest is truest. Regarding those who moved these matters before us, we say with Seneca: "They are not our masters, but our guides." Truth lies open to all; it has not yet been fully occupied, much of it is left even for those to come.* It is a sign of a meager intellect to always use what has already been found. Nor should the authority of the speakers be considered here. *Often even a vegetable-seller has spoken timely words.* But if in the meantime this opinion is torn down by suitable reasons and a more convenient one is offered, we will not be stubborn, but will seal up this writing with the words of Persius: *What I had given above, I take back and pull back the rope.* <note>Persius was a Roman satirist; this quote is a metaphor for retracting an argument or "reeling it in."</note>

§. 39. <meta>This page continues the philological defense of reading the word *ka’ari* in Psalm 22:16 as a verb. The previous page discussed the insertion of the letter *aleph* and cited the grammarian Avenarius. This page addresses the potential objection that such a reading creates grammatical "anomalies" or irregularities.</meta>

...indeed remarkable. Nor would it be necessary for us to say that the *aleph* [א] is <term>epenthetic</term> <note>a letter or sound inserted into the middle of a word</note>, since Hebrew grammarians call it natural in those words and thus highly analogous. Moreover, not every <term>apocope</term> <note>the dropping of the end of a word</note> immediately produces as many anomalies as there are examples, but the introduction of many examples here also gives rise to a special rule, the reasoning and foundation of which is <term>euphony</term> <note>ease of pronunciation or "sweetness" of sound</note>. Strictly speaking, an *anomaly* <note>original Greek: Ἀνώμαλον (anomalon)</note> is that which cannot be forced under a common rule with similar cases. The fact that only a few examples are found today of the apocope of the plural ending *-im* [ים] is not surprising, since we possess so few literary monuments of pure, ancient Hebrew. When the language was flourishing, that apocope was undoubtedly more common and better known. Whatever the case may be, let us posit here a double, or even a triple anomaly; let the doubt be resolved in this way. There are many words occurring in the Holy Scripture that, without the granting of a double or triple anomaly—even sometimes one more unusual than in the word *ka’ari* [כּארי]—would have to be resolved on the <term>Greek Calends</term> <note>A Latin idiom meaning "never," because the Greeks did not use "Calends" in their calendar</note>. 

The most illustrious man [Jacob Alting] himself, in his *Grammar of Restored Hebraism, page 70*, concedes that *chatsi* [חֲצִי] stands for *chatsei* [חֲצֵי]. If that is so, the irregularity of this word is no less than that of our *ka’ari*. In our case, there is said to be an epenthesis and an apocope. But in that case, there is the same apocope combined with an extraordinary resolution of the <term>dagesh</term> <note>a grammatical dot within a Hebrew letter</note> into a long vowel. Other examples will be measured out in a full granary by the Index of Buxtorf’s *Thesaurus*, the *Coronis* of Sixtinus Amama, the third part of Avenarius’s *Grammar*, the sixth book of Schindler, the *Auctarium* of Glassius’s *Institutes*, and others. Let a trial be made in these: *vayetâ* [וַיֵּתָא], *hetâyu* [הֵתָיוּ], *etâyu* [אֵתָיוּ], *yekhasyumu* [יְכַסְיֻמוּ], *hu* [הוּא], *vatechatsev* [וַתֵּחָצֶב], *hashammah* [הַשַּׁמָּה], *bachashmal* [בַּחַשַּׁמַּל], *vatavoti* [וַתָּבֹאתִי], *tavonah* [תָּבֹאנָה], etc. 

What absurdity is there, then, if a passage—on whose grammatical explanation so many geniuses have toiled that the scholar Voetius does not hesitate to count it among the "insolubles" of Holy Scripture—is said to suffer from a double anomaly? Even those who prefer the reading *ka’ari* <note>meaning "like a lion"</note> do not escape this, since they are forced to admit both an <term>antistoechon</term> <note>the substitution of one letter for another</note> and an <term>epenthesis</term> simultaneously. Concerning the composition of the word *ka’ari* [כּארי] from *ari* [ארי, lion] and *kelo* [כְּלוֹ] before the emergence of variant readings and versions, no mortal would have thought of it; it is far from flowing easily from the word itself. Finally, the anomaly...

<meta>This page continues the philological defense of the reading of Psalm 22:16, specifically addressing why the Holy Spirit might have permitted "anomalous" or irregular spellings in the Hebrew text to signal a deeper mystery. ...the form which we propose was not used by the Holy Spirit <note>original: Sp. S. (*Spiritu Sancto*)</note> in vain, but seems <term>mysterious</term> <note>original Greek: μυστηριώδης (*mysteriōdēs*)</note>, a certain sign to pay attention. <term>Note well</term> <note>original: *Nota bene*</note>: the Holy Spirit, for this purpose, is judged to occasionally employ irregular writing in other "classic" sayings, such as the irregular <term>Mem</term> <note>The Hebrew letter M (ם); in Isaiah 9:7, a "final" mem appears in the middle of the word *le-marbeh*, which is grammatically incorrect but traditionally seen as a hidden sign</note> in Isaiah 9:7, as well as anomalous formations like those found in Psalm 2:7 and Psalm 8:2, etc. Regarding the present passage, let us hear the Blessed Luther: *Who knows,* he asks, *whether the Prophet, using his liberty, put Aleph [א] in place of Vav [ו] because of the remarkable outcome of this new event? §. 40. §. 41. It might also trouble someone that we establish the word *ka’ari* <note>meaning "like a lion" or "they pierced"</note> as a <term>participle</term>, since all other interpreters have translated it as a third-person past tense verb. However, the sense does not change whether you use a participle in the translation: *They surrounded, [they] piercing*; or the past tense: *They surrounded (and) they pierced*. In the latter case, one must assume an <term>asyndeton</term> <note>the omission of a conjunction like "and" between parts of a sentence</note>, which is not necessary if the participle is admitted. It pleased the "Seventy" <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> to render the Hebrew participle—as they have done more than once elsewhere—through the <term>aorist</term> <note>a Greek past tense</note>, and the rest followed them in a standard formation...

### Addition I.
### *On the reasoning for the forbidden camel meat.*

§. 1. Camel meat is kept away from the tables of the Jews in *Leviticus 11:4*, and the reason is added: because the camel indeed ruminates <note>chews the cud</note> *but does not divide the hoof* <note>original Hebrew: ופרסה איננו מפריס (*u-parsah einennu mafris*)</note>.

§. 2. However, zoologists <note>scientists who study animals</note> testify to the contrary. Let us hear, among others, the Blessed Mr. Johannes Sperling <note>A 17th-century German physician and zoologist</note> in his *Zoology, book 3, chapter 5, on the camel, question 2, page 183*. Regarding the camel, he says: *It has divided feet, which is why it is referred to as a cloven-hoofed animal. Yet in Holy Scripture we read (he seems to be referring to the versions/translations) that the camel is unclean because it indeed ruminates, but does not have a split hoof.*

§. 3. Indeed, we have in the past heard atheists—*such as the black earth nourishes, many widely-dispersed men* <note>original Greek: οἷά τε πολλοὺς βόσκει γαῖα μέλαινα πολυςπερέας ἀνθρώπους; a poetic trope describing the vast variety of people in the world</note>—bringing the authority of Holy Scripture into suspicion from this point, as if Moses did not correctly describe nature. God will punish their unbridled mouths <note>original Greek: ἄθυρα στόματα (*athyra stomata*), literally "mouths without doors"</note> with eternal flames. Surely Moses could be as certain about the feet of camels as any of us are about the feet of horses.

§. 4. In the meantime, we wonder why most interpreters [regard] this doubt... <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "bium" suggests the next word is "dubium" (doubt).</note>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced "Addition I," an investigation into the dietary laws of Leviticus 11. It specifically addressed the apparent scientific contradiction where the Bible claims the camel "does not divide the hoof," while zoologists observe that it does. This page continues that argument by defining the specific Hebrew terminology used for "splitting the hoof."</meta>

...doubt <note>original: *bium*, the conclusion of the word *dubium*</note> was passed over "with a dry foot" <note>original Latin: *sicco pede transiisse*, an idiom meaning to pass over a difficulty easily without getting stuck</note>. Rashi <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki, the preeminent medieval Jewish commentator</note>, whom we consulted, unties the knot while explaining the true emphasis of the word *shasa* <note>original Hebrew: שסע; meaning to split or cleave</note>. Regarding the words in verse 3, "completely splitting the cleft" <note>original Hebrew: *shosa’at shesa*</note>, he notes:

<gloss>that it is separated from above and from below into two claws, as the Targum <note>the Aramaic translation of the Bible</note> renders it: 'dividing the hooves,' meaning it has hooves that are split from above but are not completely cleft and separated because they are connected at the bottom</gloss>

That is to say, a beast is so called **whose both hooves are divided at the bottom and the top**; hence Onkelos <note>the author of the standard Aramaic translation of the Torah</note> translates it with the word *peras* <note>original Hebrew: פרס</note>, meaning to split completely. For there are certain beasts whose hooves are cut at the top, but not entirely split and divided, being instead fused or solid at the bottom. 

Compare also Rabbi David Kimchi <note>Known as Radak, a medieval grammarian and commentator</note> in his *Book of Roots* and Solomon ben Melech <note>a 16th-century commentator</note> on this passage. Therefore, Moses does not simply deny that the camel divides its hoof, but that it "completely splits" it—namely, into two distinct hoofed parts. This is clearly manifest from a comparison of verses 3, 7, and 26, where "dividing the hoof" <note>original Hebrew: *mafris parsah*</note> is explained by "completely splitting the cleft of the hoof." Now, this "splitting of the cleft" does not apply to the camel, which indeed has a hoof cut at the top, but remains continuous or solid at the base. 

Later we discovered that Fagius <note>Paul Fagius, a Renaissance-era scholar of Hebrew</note> on this passage and the illustrious Samuel Bochart <note>A 17th-century scholar famous for his work on biblical animals</note> share our opinion <note>original Greek: *ὁμοψήφους* (homopsēphous), literally "of the same vote"</note>. Therefore, it was shortsighted of "a certain person" <note>original Greek: *ὁ δεῖνα* (ho deina), a polite way to refer to an unnamed opponent</note> in his *Hebrew Lexicon* on page 832 to explain *shasa* as "being split in two, yet in such a way that the posterior part remains connected." Verse 7 alone proves that the opposite is true.

§. 5. Furthermore, the doubt raised by the late Mr. Sperling in his *Zoology*, page 271, regarding the rabbit <note>The text uses the Latin *cuniculus*, which refers to the animal mentioned in the dietary laws, often identified today as the hyrax</note> is easily resolved. The rabbit does not have **divided hooves** because it does not even have a single hoof. As the saying goes, "a non-existent thing has no qualities" <note>A logical principle: one cannot debate the traits of something that does not exist</note>. In the Holy Scriptures, a **hoof** <note>original Hebrew: *parsah*</note> refers to a continuous bony base of the feet. Who would deny that such a thing is correctly said to be missing from the hare and the rabbit in *Leviticus 11, verses 5 and 6*? Cornelius à Lapide <note>A famous 17th-century Jesuit commentator</note> also observed this regarding this passage.

<summary>This page defends the biological accuracy of the Bible's dietary laws by arguing that the Hebrew term for "splitting the hoof" implies a total separation from top to bottom, which the camel lacks. It also clarifies that rabbits and hares are described as "not having hooves" because they lack the specific bony structure defined as a hoof in Hebrew law.</summary>
<keywords>Leviticus, camel, hoof, dietary laws, Rashi, Onkelos, Samuel Bochart, zoology, Hebrew philology, rabbit, hare</keywords>

# Addition II.
# ON THE PUBLIC INTRODUCTION
# OF DIVINE WORSHIP.

§. 1. The word <term>huchal</term> <note>original Hebrew: הוחל; traditionally translated as "begun"</note> in *Genesis 4, verse 26* is derived by some from <term>chalal</term> <note>original Hebrew: חלל; to profane</note>, meaning *to profane*, so that the sense would be: *Then the name of the Lord was profaned by invocation*. These scholars believe this passage describes the invention of idolatry (See **Pseudo-Jonathan** <note>an Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Torah</note>, **Rashi** on this passage, **Kimchi** in his *Book of Roots* <note>original: שרש (shoresh); a famous medieval Hebrew dictionary</note>, **Maimonides** in *On Foreign Worship* <note>original: Avoda Sara</note> chapter 1, **Gedaliah** in *The Chain of Tradition* <note>original: Schalsch. hakk.</note> page 92, **Selden** in *On the Syrian Gods* <note>original: De Dis Syris</note> Prolegomena page 28, and **J. H. Ursinus** in *Miscellany* page 258). Others derive it from <term>yachal</term> <note>original Hebrew: יחל; to hope</note>, from which comes the phrase <note>original Greek: τὸ τῶν ὁ ἐξήλπισε</note> *that which he hoped for*. Still others derive it from the causative form of <term>chalal</term>, <term>hechal</term> <note>original: החל</note>, meaning *to begin*. Some of these explain the text as referring to the first naming of people after the name of <term>Yahweh</term> <note>original: יהוה</note>; they argue that the descendants of Seth then first wished to separate themselves from the descendants of Cain by a distinctive name and be called *the sons of GOD* as mentioned in *Genesis 6:2* (See **Theodoret** on this passage, **Chamier** in *The Universal Panoply* <note>original: Panstrat.</note> Volume 1, Book 14, Chapter 2, Section 10, and Volume 3, Book 20, Chapter 22, Section 8, **Bertram** in *Frankenthaler Lucubrations* pages 2 and 3, **Piscator**, **Grotius**, and **Hackspan** on this passage, and **L'Empereur** in his edition of *Kimchi* page 215). And indeed, by grammatical analogy, it could be derived from any of these roots: *chalal*, *chul*, *yachal*, or *hechal*. However, the circumstances of the text and the emphasis of the phrase <term>to call upon the name of the Lord</term> <note>original Hebrew: לקרא בשם יהוה</note> do not admit those other interpretations.

§. 2. This passage deals with the solemn introduction of the public practice of calling upon GOD and of the entirety of divine worship. For the word <term>hechal</term> <note>original: החל; "he began"</note>, when constructed with the letter <term>L</term> <note>the Hebrew preposition "to" or "for"</note> of the following verb, very commonly carries the meaning of beginning something, as is evident from thirty passages of Holy Scripture and more. Furthermore, the phrase <term>to call</term> <note>original: קרא (qara)</note> joined with <term>upon the name of the Lord</term> <note>original: בשם יהוה (be-shem Yahweh)</note> expresses the whole of divine worship, as seen in *Genesis 12, verse 8; chapter 13, verse 4; Psalm 80, verse 19; Joel 2, verse 32;* and elsewhere. This worship was to be maintained and spread within the family of Seth, the solemn inauguration of which is described here. Compare **Blessed Luther**, **Gesner**, **Gerhard**, **Mercer**, **Junius**, and **Drusius** on this passage, **Dr. Calovius** in *Against Grotius* and his *Commentary* on this passage, **Gwalper** in his *Comparison of Versions*, and **Dr. St. Klozius** in *On the Worship of Angels* <note>original: de Angelolatria</note> page 103. This is enough for now.

<summary>This page analyzes the Hebrew text of Genesis 4:26 to determine whether it refers to the beginning of idolatry (profaning God's name) or the formal establishment of public worship among the descendants of Seth. The author concludes it signifies the solemn inauguration of true divine worship.</summary>
<keywords>Genesis 4:26, Seth, Enosh, divine worship, idolatry, Hebrew etymology, calling upon the name of the Lord, biblical commentary</keywords>

->In the Name of Jesus Christ the Crucified<-

# A SEVENFOLD COLLECTION OF ILLUSTRIUM
### BIBLICO-PHILOLOGICARUM QUESTIONS.
->Without prologue or passion.<-

### THE FIRST QUESTION.
### *Was Anah the discoverer of mules?*

<margin>Responding: Sebastian Bacmeister, in Atterndorn, with Hadelerus.</margin>
->§. 1.<-

Anah (or Hena) in *Genesis 36:24* is said to have found the <term>hayemim</term> <note>original Hebrew: הַיֵּמִים</note>, a word about which philologists <note>scholars of historical languages and texts</note> disagree sharply. The Vulgate <note>the standard 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> translated this as "hot springs" <note>original Latin: aquas calidas</note>, though it is uncertain by what reasoning. Jerome <note>the primary translator of the Vulgate</note> cites the proximity of the Punic language <note>the language of ancient Carthage, related to Hebrew</note>. He says: *"Some think that hot waters are indicated by this word because of the closeness of the Punic language, which is neighboring to Hebrew."* When the famous Samuel Bochart <note>a renowned 17th-century biblical scholar and geographer</note> recounted these things in his *Sacred Geography*, Part II, *Canaan*, Book 2, chapter 16, page 847, he adds: *"Since the vowel points <note>the marks added to Hebrew later to indicate vowel sounds</note> were not yet written, the Hebrew word <term>hayemim</term> could have been read as <term>chamim</term> <note>original Hebrew: חמים</note>; furthermore, the Arabic <term>hamim</term> sounds the same—except for a thicker aspiration at the start of the word—and means 'hot waters'."* Certainly, Giggeius and Golius <note>prominent 17th-century scholars of Arabic</note> note that <term>chamim</term> refers to baths or hot springs. And to the Talmudic writers <note>ancient Jewish legal and theological scholars</note>, <term>hayemim</term> likewise means "hot springs," hence the well-known "Hot Springs of Tiberias" <note>original Hebrew: חמי טבריא (Chamei Tverya)</note>, about which John Lightfoot—a man most skilled of all in Talmudic matters—can be consulted in his *Hebrew Hours, Chorographical Century*, chapter 84. But it is most unfair to demand <term>hayemim</term> <note>with different vowels</note> or <term>chamim</term> instead of the actual word <term>hayemim</term>. For in this way, not only are the vowels treated as worthless—even though they possess divine origin and authority—but the letters themselves are disregarded. One must regard in the same way what Louis Cappel <note>a French Protestant scholar known for his radical views on the Hebrew text</note> conjectures in his *Critica*, Book 5, chapter 9: that the Vulgate read <term>hayemim</term> <note>spelled with an initial 'H' instead of 'Y'</note> in its own manuscript. This is soundly refuted by the famous Johann Buxtorf <note>a leading defender of the traditional Hebrew text</note> in his *Anticritica*, Part 2, chapter 9, page 758. Namely, that whatever the Vulgate translated incorrectly, Cappel believes was simply read differently in its original manuscript. Sol neglected the order,

neglexit,

...neglected it, because the clock went astray <note>an idiom meaning the reasoning was off-track</note>. And so we would have *water*, but from where does the "hot" come? Louis Cappel says the Vulgate *added that from its own accord*. Martin Delrio says the same. Certainly, it added that as it did many other things. But by what right? What if it also invented the "waters" themselves? Furthermore, Cornelius à Lapide suggests the text should be read as *hayemim* <note>original Hebrew: הימים</note> as if derived from *cham* <note>hot</note> and *yamim* <note>waters</note>, that is, *hot waters*; he adds that *this has been noticed by no one else, as far as he knows*. Indeed, how could anyone? Since it is a pure fiction which—if it pleases the gods of Hesiod <note>a classical expression of disbelief or irony</note>—was reserved for the "stony" <note>a pun on the name "Lapide," meaning stone</note> genius of Cornelius. For such a "warm lie," we now owe him a hecatomb <note>a massive ancient Greek sacrifice of 100 oxen</note>, because he so festively observes that instead of *hayemim* <note>original: הימם</note> one should read *hayammim* <note>original: הימים</note>, claiming this monstrous word was miraculously forged from *cham* and *yamim* (or *mayim* <note>water</note>). Who would have gone looking for this gudgeon <note>a small fish, implying a trivial or slippery point</note> in that whirlpool? Finally, those who, following Jerome, understand the word as *pools* or *seas* (for the discovery of which no telescopes were needed), or those like Oleaster who see it as a *vein of salt water*, are likewise forced to change the vowel points of the word or transpose the letters. Therefore, let their *pools* and *salt veins* flow away along with the hot waters of the others.

->§. 2.<-

Among the Hebrews, Onkelos (favored by Rabbi Nachmanides and Rabbi Jacob Abendana) translates the word as *gibbarayya* <note>original Aramaic: גבריא</note>, which his defenders translate as *giants*. They say that Anah rushed upon these giants, who were perhaps intending violence against his donkeys, and he emerged superior in the fight. They believe the word *emim* <note>original Hebrew: אימים, referring to a race of giants</note> is used in this sense in *Psalm 21:9* and *2 Samuel 3:8*. The illustrious Samuel Bochart recently undertook the defense of this opinion in the first part of his *Hierozoicon* <note>a famous 17th-century work on the animals of the Bible</note>, book 2, chapter 21. He particularly urges the similarity of the words *hayemim* and *emim*, and cites the support of the Samaritan version, which has *amka’i* <note>original Samaritan: עמכאי</note>. However, *hayemim* and *emim* are different, even if that translator of questionable reliability, the Samaritan, confused them. Furthermore, Johann Henrich Ursinus clipped Bochart’s wings <note>refuted him</note> in his *Miscellanies*, page 104.

->§. 3.<-

Most scholars explain <term>hayemim</term> as *Mules*. They argue that Anah was the first to produce them by the deliberate and purposeful crossbreeding of horses and donkeys, or at least that he was the first to observe them being born naturally from such unions. Indeed, some even suggest that Anah himself was born of incest, or was a spurious...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "hayemim" in Genesis 36:24, moving from the "hot springs" interpretation to the "mules" interpretation. ...or as the Talmudists record in the *Talmud, tractate Pesachim, chapter 4, folio 54.* Furthermore, the Talmudic scholars translate *hayemim* <note>original Hebrew: הימם</note> as *mules* <note>original Latin: mulos</note> in the same place, as well as in the *Jerusalem Talmud, tractate Berachot, folio 12, column 2*; the *Midrash Bereshit Rabba, section 82, folio 92, column 2*; and in the *Shalshelet Ha-Kabbalah, page 9.* Others who agree include Isidore of Seville in his *Etymologies, book 12, chapter 1*; the Targum of Jonathan, Chizkuni, Rashi, Ibn Ezra, Solomon ben Melech, and the *Ze’enah U-Re’enah* <note>a popular Yiddish homiletic commentary</note> on this passage. Also David Kimchi in his *Book of Roots*, Mordechai Nathan in his *Concordance*, David de Pomis, and Aquinas in his *Lexicon*. It is likewise translated this way in the Persian and Arabic versions, and by scholars such as Pagninus, Arias Montanus, Fagius, Münster, Martin Luther, Vatablus, Mercerus, Drusius, Castalio, Junius, Piscator, Diodati, and the Geneva and Zurich Bibles in their commentaries on this passage. We also find this in Buxtorf, Amama, and others. Compare also Fuller’s *Miscellanea, book 3, chapter 8*; Gualperius’s *Collection of Versions, page 752*; and Dannhauer’s *Psychology, page 51.* In the works of Jerome, some have substituted *wild asses* <note>original Latin: onagros</note>, a view to which Rivetus is not opposed in his *Exercise 144.*

§. 4. This opinion regarding *mules* is favored by the added circumstance of the *pasturing of the donkeys*. From this, it is considered not without reason that Anah found something significant, something either miraculous or worthy of record, which concerned the donkeys and occurred while he was with them. Now, whatever you might suggest other than mules, you would not be speaking sufficiently to the context. Ludovicus de Dieu correctly refers here to what Homer testifies in the *Iliad, Book 2*, that "the race of wild mules" <note>original Greek: ἡμιόνων γένος ἀγροτεράων</note> traces its origin from the Heneti <note>an ancient people of Paphlagonia</note>.

§. 5. However, let us hear what the celebrated Bochart <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a famous French Protestant biblical scholar and zoologist</note> objects against this opinion. First, he urges that a *mule* is called *pered* <note>original Hebrew: פרד</note> by the Hebrews, *kudnya* by the Chaldeans or Syrians, *baghl* <note>original Arabic: بغל</note> by the Arabs, *bakl* by the Ethiopians, *astur* by the Persians, and *pemsham* <note>original Egyptian/Coptic: πεμψαμ</note> by the Egyptians—but it is never called *yamim*. Secondly, he says that the word *matza* <note>original Hebrew: מצא, meaning "to find"</note>, although it appears in the Holy Scriptures more than four hundred times, is never used for the *invention of something that does not exist*, but always for the *discovery of something already existing*. Furthermore, he notes that no mention is made in the text of horses or wild asses, but only of donkeys, which are by no means sufficient on their own to produce mules. Finally, he argues that the Jews are not read to have used mules before the time of David, so it is not probable they were already invented by the Edomites <note>the descendants of Esau/Idumeans mentioned in the text</note>. So he argues.

§. 6. But all these points can be dissolved with little effort. The name *yamim* is a *hapax legomenon* <note>a term that appears only once in the entire Bible</note> and is perhaps an archaic word, which...
