<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "hayemim" in Genesis 36:24. The author is refuting the idea that the word refers to "hot springs" (as argued by scholars like Bochart) and is defending the traditional interpretation that it refers to "mules," while acknowledging the difficulty of the word's etymology by suggesting it may be of Horite rather than Hebrew origin.</meta>

...it would be from a *Horite* <note>original Latin: Horæo</note> origin. Whatever the case may be, negative arguments should not be sought from other languages. For who would tolerate someone inferring the following: The word for *man* <note>original Latin: Vir</note> is elsewhere called *Ish* <note>original Hebrew: איש</note> by the Hebrews, *Gabra* <note>original Aramaic/Syriac: גברא</note> by the Syrians or Chaldeans, *Rajul* <note>original Arabic: رجل</note> by the Arabs, *Bybsi* by the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopic</note>, *Mard* and *Sho* <note>original Persian: مرد and شو</note> by the Persians, *Mard* by the Armenians, *Promi* <note>original Coptic: πρωμι</note> by the Copts, *Er* <note>original Turkish: ار</note> by the Turks, and *Nân* by the Chinese—yet it is called *Ish* by none of these. Therefore, is it not a word for the Hebrews? Furthermore, we do not deny that the origin of the word *hayemim* <note>original Hebrew: הימים</note> is obscure. *Rashi* <note>a preeminent medieval French rabbi</note> says that mules are called *yemim* because <term>their terror is cast upon creatures or men</term> <note>original Hebrew: אימתן מוטלת על הבריות</note>, a point which is also emphasized in the *Ze’enah U-Re’enah* <note>a popular Yiddish commentary</note> at folio 46, column 1. Or, it is said to come from *emim* <note>original Hebrew: אימים</note> because they are robust, as *Fagius* <note>Paul Fagius, a Renaissance-era Hebraist</note> says; from this, others observe that the *Gvarya* <note>original Aramaic: גבריא</note> of *Onkelos* <note>author of the standard Aramaic translation of the Torah</note> refers not to giants, but to these very mules. However, *Rabbi Jehuda bar Rabbi Simon* in the *Jerusalem Talmud, tractate Berachot* and the *Midrash Bereshit Rabba* (in the places cited) contends that *yemim* is related to the Greek *hemi* <note>original Greek: ἡμι</note> or *hemisu* <note>original Greek: ἡμισυ</note>, meaning *half*, because they are *hemionoi* <note>original Greek: ἡμίονοι; literally "half-donkeys" or mules</note>. *Forster* in his *Dictionary*, page 330, thinks it can be derived from *hamam* <note>original Hebrew: חמם; meaning "to be warm"</note>, which we do not understand. There is no need for such a convoluted explanation. The name *yemim* is *Horite*, not *Hebrew*; the Hebrews later used their own word, *peredim* <note>original Hebrew: פרדים</note>, in its place.

§. 7. Next, even if *matsa* <note>original Hebrew: מצא</note> surely signifies "finding" a thing that already exists, it would at least show that Anah was the first who encountered mules born from a heterogeneous mating. Meanwhile, let the fair reader consider whether *matsa* does not sometimes mean *to investigate things not yet discovered by others*. Compare *Ecclesiastes 7:25–28, Job 32:13, etc.*

§. 8. Furthermore, although donkeys alone are not sufficient to produce mules <note>as a horse is also required</note>, they are nonetheless involved. At the very least, it supports the common opinion regarding mules that Anah stood out as the inventor of this thing—which is wonderful and noteworthy—and for which the involvement of the donkey is necessary. If the entire process of the *generation* <note>original Greek: γενέσεως</note> of mules had been described as precisely as that most learned man <note>likely referring to Samuel Bochart</note> demands, what need would there be for words?

§. 9. Finally, [regarding the objection that] the Jews are not recorded as having used mules before the time of David. Therefore, they never used them before. Therefore: the Jews did not use mules previously. Therefore, the Edomites <note>original Latin: Idumæi</note> did not find them, or...

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion from page 132 regarding the word "hayemim" in Genesis 36:24, concluding the argument against Samuel Bochart’s interpretation before moving to a new inquiry into the origins of the "Jubilee."</meta>

...of the Horite language <note>original Latin: Horæi</note>. Let the judgment remain with the prudent. These same readers will judge whether the opinion of the most distinguished Bochart <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a famous French Protestant biblical scholar</note> regarding the "giants" is not extremely forced, since the Hebrew word *matsa* <note>original Hebrew: מצא, meaning "to find" or "to encounter"</note> never carries that meaning without the association of the words *yad* <note>original Hebrew: יד, meaning "hand"</note> or *oyev* <note>original Hebrew: איב, meaning "enemy"</note>, etc. Nor does the word *yemim* <note>original Hebrew: ימים</note> have anything in common with *chamorim* <note>original Hebrew: חמורים, meaning "donkeys"</note> besides a deceptive similarity in sound; nor is it likely that a single groom, left to himself, would have put a giant army to flight. Let everyone decide as they wish. Certainly, we believe the common opinion concerning the mules has not yet been solidly refuted by Bochart.

### SECOND QUESTION.
->*From where does the Jubilee year take its name?*<-

§. 1. Various conjectures present themselves as to why it is called the *Annus Jubilæus* <note>the Jubilee Year</note> or *Jobelæus* <note>from the Hebrew "Yobel"</note> in Leviticus 25:13 and elsewhere. Certain Scholastic writers mentioned by Malleolus <note>Felix Hemmerlin, also known as Malleolus (1388–1458), a Swiss theologian</note> derive it from *jubilando* <note>Latin: "shouting for joy"</note>, as if the word were Latin in origin, although perhaps the Latin *jubilare* might better be understood through the Hebrew *yobel* <note>original Hebrew: יובל</note>. In the *Catena MSS. Patrum* <note>a "chain" or collection of manuscript commentaries from the Church Fathers</note>, it is recorded—quite irrelevantly—that the word *iobel* <note>Greek: ἰωβὴλ</note> was fashioned from the divine names *Ioao* and *Elaa*, that is, *Yahweh* and *Elohim* <note>original Hebrew: יהוה and אלהים</note>. 

Masius <note>Andreas Masius (1514–1573), a Flemish humanist and Hebraist</note> in his commentary on Joshua (page 104) and Bochart in his *Hierozoicon* derive the name from *Jubal*, the "father of all who play the harp and flute," but this etymology has little logic behind it beyond the similar sound. Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist</note> thinks the word was chosen through <term>onomatopoeia</term> <note>a word that imitates a sound</note> from a particular sound, an opinion followed by Leusden <note>Jan Leusden (1624–1699), a Dutch theologian</note>. Avenarius seeks the reason for the name in the "production of a flow of sound" (taking the opportunity from the phrase *yovlei mayim* <note>original Hebrew: יובלי מים, "streams of water"</note>), a view preferred by Fuller <note>Nicholas Fuller (1557–1622), an English philologist</note> due to the "drawing out" of the sound. 

However, we find it difficult to grasp either that onomatopoeia for *yobel* or the "flow of sound." Martin Martinius <note>author of a famous philosophical lexicon</note> most simply believes the word *yobel* is derived from *yevul* <note>original Hebrew: יבול, meaning "the produce of the earth"</note>. This also pleases Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a prominent Jesuit Cardinal</note> in his first book *On Indulgences* (chapter 1), and it seems an especially fitting root for the Papal Jubilee <note>referring to the Catholic tradition of Jubilee years involving indulgences</note>, as noted by Hospinian <note>Rudolf Hospinian (1547–1626), a Swiss Reformed theologian</note>. On the other hand, there is no reason—at least no probable one—for what Zwinger <note>Theodor Zwinger (1533–1588), a Swiss scholar</note> says in his *Theatrum* (vol. 27): that the year was called *Jobel* because among the Hebrews, *Jobel* signifies the number 50.

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the word "Jubilee" (Yobel). The previous page ended by mentioning the "Catena of the Fathers" and began exploring various etymologies for the term.</meta>

...signify. Even if one examines Cabalistic minutiae <note>Cabalistic minutiae: A reference to Gematria, a Jewish mystical tradition where the numerical values of letters are used to find hidden meanings in words</note>, it cannot be proven in that way. For the word *Heikal* <note>original Hebrew: היכל; meaning "temple"</note> contains the numerical value of 42, *Yikbar* <note>original Hebrew: יכבר</note> 47, and *Yobel* <note>original Hebrew: יובל</note> 48.

§. 2. The Hebrews, to a man, all think that *Yobel* denotes a *ram* and, by synecdoche <note>a figure of speech where a part represents the whole</note>, a *horn or ram’s-horn trumpet*, by which the solemnity of this year was announced. In the Talmudic tractate *Rosh Hashanah* (chapter 3, folio 26, page 1), Rabbi Akiba <note>a leading 1st-century Jewish sage</note> says: *“When I was traveling in Arabia, they called a ram ‘Jobela,’ from which the trumpet itself is called ‘Jobel,’ since it is made from a ram.”* Among Christians, this opinion is not rejected by Pagninus and Buxtorf <note>renowned 16th/17th-century Hebraists</note> in their Lexicons, nor by Sigonius (*The Hebrew Republic*, book 13, chapter 15), Fagius (*On the Chaldean Paraphrase of Leviticus 25*), Ravanellus (*The Excellence of the Holy Bible*), and Doctor Balthasar Scheidius—a man beyond all praise in these letters and our most honored patron—in his *Dissertation on the Jubilee Year* (page 18, near the end). Nor is this view hindered by the objection raised by Bochart, along with Masius and Fuller, that ram horns, being solid, are hardly suitable for these uses; as if they could not be hollowed out and prepared. Regarding the practice of the Jews, compare the aforementioned work by Fagius.

§. 3. To us, however, without prejudice or injury to anyone, it seems that reaching the "Jubilee year" via that ram is far too convoluted. For *Jobel*, as they claim, is a *ram*; therefore, the *Year of Jobel* would be the "Year of the Ram," whose solemnity is announced by a trumpet made from a ram's horn. Furthermore, that testimony of Rabbi Akiba seems unreliable. For not even the root <term>بيل</term> <note>Arabic root: B-Y-L</note> is found among the Arabs today, as far as we know. Nor is any word derived from the related root <term>وبل</term> <note>Arabic root: W-B-L</note> that denotes a *ram*. As for the fact that <term>وبلة</term> <note>Arabic: wablat</note> and its tenth conjugation are used to refer to the *lust of a sheep*, that seems to have very little to do with the matter. It is otherwise a common practice for Jews, when faced with obscure and desperate terms, to flee to "Arabism" <note>the use of Arabic language parallels</note> as an "asylum of ignorance" <note>a phrase used by scholars to describe an easy but unproven explanation for a difficult problem</note>; Hottinger provides examples and specimens of this practice (*Analecta*, page 142, at the end).

§. 4. We rest in the opinion of those who derive *Yobel* from the root *Yabal* <note>original Hebrew: יבל</note> in the *Hiphil* <note>a Hebrew verb form indicating causative action</note> *Hobil*, meaning *to lead back or restore*. Because it brought back—

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the Hebrew word "Yobel" (Jubilee), transitioning from the discussion of the ram's horn to the concept of "restoration" or "bringing back."</meta>

...restored rest to the fields; **freedom to the servants; to debtors, along with the return of their pledge, the beneficial ownership and possession of the mortgaged estate**; it was the יובל <note>original Hebrew: *Yobel*</note> or *Jubilee* in Israel, *a general year of restoration*, as the venerable Augustus Varenius <note>a 17th-century Lutheran theologian and professor at Rostock</note>, our respected patron, puts it. Nor does Josephus <note>a 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian</note> seem wrong in explaining the word *iobēlō* <note>original Greek: ἰωβήλῳ</note> as *freedom* <note>original Greek: ἐλευθερίαν</note>. Furthermore, the etymology we have provided is required both by the analogy of the language (for from הובל <note>original Hebrew: *hōbal*</note> <term>to lead or bring back</term> <note>The author is arguing that the root of "Jubilee" is the Hebrew verb for "bringing back" or "restoring"</note>, יובל <note>*Yobel*</note> is very conveniently derived) and by a careful comparison of the texts in *Leviticus 25, verses 10 to 25*. Compare also Fuller in the place cited and Morin, *Biblical Exercises, page 219*.

## §. 5.
Nor is our opinion hindered by the fact that the word יובל <note>*Yobel*</note> was in use before that solemn emancipation of the fiftieth year was instituted—namely, in *Exodus 19:13*. Anyone who claims a contradiction here will find themselves in some difficulty. The whole matter should be understood as follows: *Jobel* precisely and formally denotes a *bringing forth* or *leading*. From this, two primary material meanings of the word arise. For it either expresses the *bringing forth* of a gathering by a heavy and majestic sound, as if you were to call it a *bringing* or *summoning sound* in *Exodus 19:13* (unless you prefer to believe the angels were equipped with ram’s-horn trumpets); or it expresses a *leading* or *bringing back* to rest and freedom, and in this sense it is called שנת היובל <note>original Hebrew: *Shenat ha-Yobel*</note> in *Leviticus 25 and 27*, that is, *the year of restoration*, the year of bringing back, of release, of restoration, of freedom, etc. <note>original Greek: ὁ ἔτος τῆς ἀναγωγῆς, ἀφέσεως, ἀποκαταστάσεως, ἐλευθερίας</note>. Hence יובל <note>*Yobel*</note> is used absolutely for the Jubilee year itself, which is the year of restoration. And from this, קרן היובל <note>original Hebrew: *qeren ha-yobel*</note> in *Joshua 6:5* is the *horn of the Jubilee*, that is, the one used in the proclamation of the Jubilee. Likewise, שופרות היובלים <note>original Hebrew: *shōphrōt ha-yobelīm*</note> in *Joshua 6:4, 6, 8, 13* are the *trumpets of the Jubilees*, that is, those accustomed to be used in the Jubilees.

## §. 6.
Furthermore, for more on the *year* of the *Jubilee*, especially that of the Hebrews, and the controversies argued back and forth concerning this subject, we refer the reader at this time to the authors who have either written on *Leviticus* or have otherwise addressed this argument. Among the most prominent are the Talmudists in the tractate *Erachin, chapters 6, 7, and 8*; Maimonides <note>the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and jurist</note> in *The Laws of Sabbatical and Jubilee Years*; Serarius on *Joshua*; and Justus Ryckvius.

<summary>The author argues that the word "Jubilee" (Yobel) is derived from a Hebrew root meaning "to lead or bring back," signifying the restoration of land and freedom. He reconciles this with earlier biblical uses of the word, suggesting it refers generally to a "summoning sound" or a "year of restoration."</summary>

### THIRD QUESTION.

-> *Was Zipporah, the wife of Moses, an Ethiopian?* <-

§. 1. Many wonder why the wife of Moses is called a *Cushite* <note>original Hebrew: כושית (*Kushit*)</note>, which is, as commonly interpreted, an *Ethiopian woman* in *Numbers 12, verse 1*. There are some who believe this woman was distinct from *Zipporah*. Certainly, Josephus <note>a 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian</note> in *Antiquities of the Jews, book 2, chapter 10*, and following him Eusebius, Isidore, and others, recount that while Moses was still living in Pharaoh's court, he waged a certain war on behalf of the Egyptians against the Ethiopians. They claim he conquered them through the help and betrayal of *Tarbis*, the daughter of the Ethiopian King, whom he subsequently married. Anyone who delights in fables may also read the invention concerning King *Kurkuno* of Ethiopia, Balaam, and Moses, which Gedaliah ibn Yahya records in *The Chain of Tradition, pages 11 and 12*, and from there Hottinger in his *Oriental Treasures, page 408, final part*.

§. 2. Others admit that Zipporah is called an *Ethiopian*, but they offer very different reasons for this name. Some believe she was called this because she was of a dark complexion, like an Ethiopian woman; just as today we are accustomed to calling a very tanned person a *Tartar* or a *Turk*, or a black person an *Ethiopian*, etc. They provide this reason for the biblical dispute: that Miriam and Aaron said Moses abstained from his wife not *for the sake of heaven* <note>original Hebrew: לשם שמים (*le-shem shamayim*), meaning for a holy or divine purpose</note>, but because she was less than delicate and not easy to look upon. Thus, the trifling author of the *Tze’enah U-Re’enah* <note>a popular 17th-century Yiddish-language commentary on the Torah, often called the "Women's Bible"</note> on folio 140, 4, reports this in these words, citing Bechai <note>Bahya ben Asher, a medieval Spanish rabbi</note> and Haskuni <note>Hezekiah ben Manoah, a 13th-century French rabbi</note>: **"and Bechai and Haskuni write that Zipporah was just like a Gypsy and was all black like a Moorish woman. Therefore Miriam and Aaron say that Moses should separate from Zipporah, because she..."**

<summary>This page lists academic references on the Jubilee and begins a discussion on whether Moses's wife, Zipporah, was the "Ethiopian woman" mentioned in the Book of Numbers, exploring both historical legends and Rabbinic interpretations of her appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Zipporah, Moses, Ethiopian woman, Cushite, Josephus, Tarbis, Rabbinic commentary, Tze’enah U-Re’enah, Numbers 12:1</keywords>

...she was not beautiful, etc. Let the person who has the leisure read the rest. But also Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a 12th-century Spanish rabbi and scholar</note>, although he had initially given a more certain reason for the word Cushite <note>original Hebrew: כושית (*Kushit*)</note>, he eventually falls back on that opinion.

§. 3. Others, on the contrary, think Zipporah is called an *Ethiopian woman* by way of *antiphrasis* <note>a rhetorical device where a word is used to mean its opposite, often for emphasis or irony</note>, because she was not at all such a person, but rather the most beautiful of all. For this reason, Onkelos <note>the reputed author of a famous 2nd-century Aramaic translation of the Torah</note> translated it as: *a beautiful wife* <note>original Aramaic: אתתא שפירתא (*ittata shapirata*)</note>. However, the Jerusalem Targum <note>a collection of Aramaic translations and commentaries on the Hebrew Bible</note> explains it more extensively: *And Miriam and Aaron spoke with Moses concerning the Ethiopian wife whom he had married. But Zipporah, the wife of Moses, was not an Ethiopian; rather, just as an Ethiopian differs in his flesh from all other men, so Zipporah, the wife of Moses, was of fitting form and beautiful in appearance, and distinguished by good works above all her contemporaries.* Rashi <note>Shlomo Yitzchaki, the preeminent medieval French commentator</note> and Rabbi Jacob Abendana <note>a 17th-century Sephardic scholar</note> agree *in this place*. They provide more than one reason for the name. Rashi pursues this analogy: just as everyone is forced to admit that an Ethiopian is black, so no one could have called Zipporah's beauty into doubt. Soon he calls Kabbalah <note>Jewish mystical tradition</note> to his aid, and points out that through <term>Gematria</term> <note>a system of interpretation where words are assigned numerical values based on their letters</note> the word *Cushite* <note>כושית</note> totals the same as *beautiful of appearance* <note>original Hebrew: יפת מראה (*yifat mareh*)</note>, with both resulting in the number 736. Baal Turim <note>Jacob ben Asher, a 14th-century legal authority</note> also reveals this mystery. Rashi also adds that she is called this as a charm against fascination <note>a protective euphemism meant to ward off unwanted attention or bad luck</note>, just as someone might call his beautiful son an *Ethiopian*, *lest the evil eye dominate him* <note>original Hebrew: שלא תשלוט בו עין רעה (*she-lo tishlot bo ayin ra-ah*)</note>, *so that he might not be jinxed* <note>original German: *damit er nicht beschriyen werde*</note>, as superstitious women are wont to say. And from this hypothesis, they likewise hand down a different cause for the dispute. Onkelos and Rashi attribute this as a fault to Moses...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the identity and ethnicity of Zipporah, Moses' wife, specifically addressing the theological and geographical reasons why she is called a "Cushite" in the Bible.</meta>

They think Moses was blamed because he had divorced such a beautiful wife so that, as Abendana <note>Jacob Abendana, a 17th-century Jewish scholar</note> says, he might be considered more ready for divine revelations, even though marital relations were by no means an obstacle to other equally holy and prophetic men. Based on the same hypothesis, Rabbi Moses Albilda in *Olat Tamid, page 193, column 3*, gives the cause of the dispute as follows: that they misinterpreted the situation, thinking Moses had preferred a foreign woman to an Israelite woman because he was captivated by her beauty. But Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra, a 12th-century scholar</note> rejects that invention regarding the "antiphrasis" <note>the rhetorical device of using a word to mean its opposite, suggesting she was called "dark" because she was actually "fair"</note> and does not think it likely that what is otherwise cited as praise should be swapped for something that usually signifies disgrace.

§. 4. Otherwise, the most prominent interpreters agree that Zipporah is called a *Cushite* <note>original Hebrew: כושית (*Kushit*)</note> from her homeland, which was *Midian*. Compare Aben Esra, Solomon ben Melech, the author of *On the Miracles of Holy Scripture* (who is believed to be Augustine), *chapter 28*; Theodoret *question 22 on Numbers*; Rupertus, Lyranus, Tostatus, Vatablus, Oleaster, Cornelius à Lapide, Fagius *at this place*; Drusius *chapter 52 on the difficult words of Numbers*; B. D. Walther *Harmony of the Bible, page 255*; and others. In the meantime, most judge that she is called an *Ethiopian woman* for the reason that Eastern Ethiopia was neighboring the land of the Midianites, and therefore because of the proximity of the regions, Miriam and Aaron preferred to call her an *Ethiopian woman* rather than a *Midianite* for the sake of an insult.

§. 5. But truly, the distinguished Samuel Bochart—a man of vast learning—has so clearly demonstrated in his *Sacred Geography, Part 1: Phaleg, book 4, chapter 2*, that *Cush* <note>original Hebrew: כוש</note> is not *Ethiopia*, but *Arabia*, and that the *Cushites* are the same as the *Scenitae* <note>a term for nomadic "tent-dwellers"</note> of the ancients and the *Saracens* of the moderns, that one would have to be a Carneades <note>a reference to the Greek philosopher known for extreme skepticism</note> to contradict him further. Indeed, it is established from Josephus *Antiquities, book 2, chapter 5*, Jerome *On Hebrew Places*, Ptolemy *book 6, chapter 7*, and others that the city of *Midian* was in Arabia on the shore of the Red Sea. Therefore, a *Cushite* <note>original Hebrew: כושית</note> is not an *Ethiopian woman*, but an *Arabian* one—unless someone should imagine another Ethiopia for themselves in Arabia, as the distinguished Bochart says. From this it happens that Chusan and Midian

*Midian* [and Cushan] are used as synonyms <note>original Greek: ἰσοδυναμοῦντα (*isodynamounta*), meaning "equivalent in power or meaning"</note> in *Habakkuk 3:7*.

§. 6. However, we would not admit what that most learned man says (in the place cited, page 243): that the wife of Moses was called a *Cushite* rather than a *Midianite* as a form of disparagement <note>original Greek: κατ' ἐξοθενισμὸν (*kat' exothenismon*), meaning "by way of contempt or treating as an outsider"</note>. For what kind of disparagement would this be? It would certainly be as if someone were to call a person from *Wittenberg* a *Saxon*, or call a *Saxon* a *German*—both of which can be done without any insult. Therefore, Moses is only blamed by Miriam and Aaron for this: that he had taken a foreign wife—specifically an *Arabian*—rather than an *Israelite* woman into his bed. The *Silluk* <note>a Hebrew cantillation mark that indicates the end of a verse and a logical pause</note> proves this point. Let the scholars judge.

### FOURTH QUESTION.

->*Whether "the anointed of the God of Jacob" in 2 Samuel 23:1 is a description of the Messiah, or of David?*<-

§. I. The beginning of the "Last Words of David" in *2 Samuel 23* is memorable: *The oracle of David the son of Jesse, and the oracle of the man raised up concerning the anointed of the God of Jacob.* From that small word *al* <note>original Hebrew: על (*'al*), usually meaning "on," "above," or "concerning"</note> a doubt arises: should the words *the anointed of the God of Jacob* be understood *subjectively* or *objectively*? That is, does this title refer to David himself, or to the Messiah promised to the Fathers? If the latter is true, we have a clear testimony of David’s full assurance <note>original Greek: πληροφορίας (*plerophorias*)</note> and faith in the Messiah. This is what the divinely inspired <note>original Greek: θεόλατος (*theolatos*), likely meaning "seized by God"</note> man Luther had in mind when he translated: **"The man spoke, who is assured of the Messiah of the God of Jacob, etc."** In this same sense, the Vulgate <note>the standard Latin translation of the Bible</note> had translated: *The man said, to whom it was ordained concerning the Christ of the God of Jacob.* Osiander <note>Lucas Osiander, a 16th-century theologian</note> corrected this to: *who was confirmed or certified, etc.* For these translators, the small word *al* is a preposition meaning *upon* or *concerning*. But Hackspan <note>Theodoricus Hackspan, a 17th-century scholar of oriental languages</note> passed this judgment on the Vulgate version in his *Biblical Notes, Part 1, page 694*: "Poorly done. For, first, it ignores the *Zaqef Katon* accent <note>a Hebrew musical and grammatical mark that indicates a minor pause and connects words</note> placed on the word *al*, which shows that the word *al* refers to what precedes it, not to what follows..."

...to [beho]ve. 2. He treats *al* (על) as a syncategorema <note>a grammatical term for a word that has no independent meaning, like a preposition or conjunction, and must be joined with another word</note> and reads it with a *Patach* vowel, whereas it is actually a categorema <note>a word with independent meaning, like a noun</note> and has a *Qamets* vowel underneath it. The most excellent Dr. Wasmuth is of the same opinion <note>original Greek: ὁμόψηφος (*homopsēphos*)</note> in his *Inaugural Disputation, Controversy 3, page 9*, where he proves from the foundation of the [Hebrew] accents that this meaning was by no means intended by the Holy Spirit in this passage: "The accent *Zaqef*," he says, "on the word *al* (על), being the greatest punctuation mark of all those words, prevents it from being taken in construction with the following word *Messiah* (משיח) and thus being rendered as 'over' or 'concerning'; likewise, the conjunctive accent on the word *huqam* (הוקם - 'raised up') prevents its distinction from the following word." In this view, they were preceded by Arias Montanus, Vatablus, and Junius on this passage, as well as Avenarius, Schindler, and Buxtorf in his *Lexicon*. So also the Dutch [Bible] recently: *The man who is highly raised up / the anointed of the God of Jacob.* <note>original Dutch: "De man de hoge is opgericht / de gesalfde des Gotts Jacobs"</note> Nor is the opinion of the Hebrew commentators different; they explain the word *al* (על) either as a noun—as Kimchi does: *established as the highest and king* (הוקם עליון ומלך), or Solomon ben Melech: *an adjective, as if to say "exalted"* (תואר כלומר עליון)—or adverbially, as Rashi, Rabbi Isaiah, and Rabbi Levi ben Gerson do: *established on high* (הוקם למעלה). Furthermore, Sebastian Münster took *al* (על) as a preposition, yet still applied the following words to David in this way: *Who was established concerning the Christ <note>the anointed one</note> of the God of Jacob.*

Secondly, we observe that the small word *al* (על) never functions as a noun. Two places indeed [are cited] in this...

<meta>This page continues the technical debate from the previous page concerning the Hebrew word "al" (על) in the "last words of David" (2 Samuel 23:1). The author is arguing against scholars who interpret "al" as a noun meaning "The Exalted One" (referring to God or a high status for David), suggesting instead that it functions as a preposition or adverb meaning "above" or "upward."</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<sig>S 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: it is clear</meta>

These matters are usually brought forward from the Book of Hosea, namely chapter 7, verse 16, and chapter 11, verse 7. But in neither place, with all due respect to the judgment of others, does *al* <note>Hebrew: על</note> seem to us to be a noun. The first passage is conveniently explained as: "they turn back *not to the one above*" <note>Hebrew: *lo al*; original Latin: *non supra*</note>, that is, *not upward* <note>Hebrew: *lo le-ma'alah*; original Latin: *non sursum*</note> (for they soon slip back to their former ways) and they are like a deceitful bow. The Blessed Luther correctly states: *They convert themselves, but not rightly; rather, they are like a false bow.* <note>original German: "Sie bekehren sich/ aber nicht recht; sondern sind wie ein falscher Boge."</note> The other passage is more difficult. A note by Luther hints at this: *No one shall understand this verse, not even in the Hebrew.* <note>original German: "Diesem Vers soll niemand verstehen/ auch im Ebreischen nicht."</note> There are those who explain *al* in that place as *the Most High* <note>Hebrew: *Elyon*</note> and apply it to <term>God</term> <note>original Latin: *Deo*. The author is debating whether the word refers to the Divine name.</note>. However, the main difficulty will vanish if you translate it as: *And to the height* <note>Hebrew: *el-al*; original Latin: *ad supra*</note>, that is, *upward* <note>Hebrew: *le-ma'alah*; compare the Greek translation of Job 2:7, "to the top" <note>original Greek: εως ανω (*heos ano*). Note: This reference likely intends Job 5:7, where sparks fly "upward."</note></note> *they call him*; where the following word *he exalts* <note>Hebrew: *yeromem*</note> will respond well: *there is no one who lifts himself up*, or as Luther puts it, *no one desires to raise himself up* <note>original German: "es begehret sich keiner aufzurichten."</note> Nor should it seem strange whether we take *al* as an adverb, or whether we allow it to be joined with the preposition *el* <note>Hebrew: אל</note>. 

For since the particle *from below* <note>Hebrew: *mittachat*</note> acts as an adverb in Genesis 49:25, and its opposite, *al*, follows the same logic; indeed, *from above* <note>Hebrew: *me-al*</note> is also used adverbially in that same verse. Furthermore, since we find the construction *to below* <note>Hebrew: *el-tachat*</note> in Leviticus 14:42 and Judges 6:19, why do we hesitate concerning the construction *to above* <note>Hebrew: *el-al*</note>? Thus other associations of inseparable particles are found, such as *upon-from-above* <note>Hebrew: *al-me-al*</note> in Ezekiel 41:20 and verse 17, and *upon-between* <note>Hebrew: *al-bein*</note> in Ezekiel 19:11, etc. Hence, if *al* in this place cannot be anything but a preposition—which we think is clear from what has been said—the meaning expressed by Blessed Luther flows naturally from it. 

Added to this is the fact that the verb *to establish* <note>Hebrew: *heqim*</note>, and therefore also its passive form *was established* <note>Hebrew: *huqam*</note>, when joined with *al*, can conveniently mean—depending on the context—"to be confirmed and made certain concerning some thing." Compare Jeremiah 29:10 with chapter 35:14, and "to be solidly founded as if upon a rock" in Psalm 40:3. This is why in matters concerning covenants, nothing is more frequent than this verb *to establish*. But if the phrase *established over* <note>Hebrew: *huqam al*</note> had the meaning "ordained or appointed as the exalted one," the nature of the language would require the word *established* to be followed by the letter *Lamed* <note>the Hebrew letter ל, often used to mean "as" or "to"</note>, as in...

it is clear from *Deuteronomy 28, verse 9.* Finally, anyone who has weighed the intention of David and the subject matter of his words—especially in verse 5—in the fear of the Lord, and who has examined the opening of the passage, will love and embrace Luther’s version more and more; it is to me <note>The author uses a classical metaphor from the poets Virgil and Ovid, referring to the famously sweet honey from Mount Hybla in Sicily.</note> *sweeter than Hyblaean thyme.*

§. 3. Nor do the arguments of Hackspan <note>Theodor Hackspan (1607–1659), a German Lutheran theologian and Orientalist.</note> and the celebrated Wasmuth <note>Matthias Wasmuth (1625–1688), a scholar known for his work on Hebrew accents.</note> seem so constructed as to move us. It troubles these most learned men that the word *al* <note>Hebrew: על, meaning "on high" or "above"</note> possesses the *Zakef Katon* <note>A "minor" Hebrew cantillation mark that usually indicates a pause and signifies the word is a noun.</note> accent. But he will cease to wonder who sees that this same particle *al*, or similar ones, especially before the continuation of a grammatical construction (as in this place), can have the *Zakef Gadol* <note>A "major" accent.</note> accent, which is equivalent to the *Zakef Katon*, as in *Exodus 35, verse 17:* "and the screen of the gate" <note>original Hebrew: ואת מסך שער</note>. And likewise in *Ecclesiastes 8, verse 1:* "and because of the oath of God" <note>original Hebrew: ועל־דברת שבועת אלהים</note>. Also, outside of that case, the word *al* appears in *Jeremiah 28, verse 10* and *Esther 3, verse 1*; and the word *ke-al* in *Isaiah 63, verse 7.* The celebrated Daniel Fabricius <note>A Hungarian pastor and scholar from Kunuszvágás.</note>, a minister in Cunoschovensis in Hungary, has diligently collected such examples, even if not all of them apply to this matter; the theologian-philologist <note>The author uses a Greek phrase here: πολλῶν ἀντάξιος ἄλλων, meaning "worth many others," a compliment borrowed from Homer’s Iliad.</note> D. D. Johannes Acoluthus—the venerable leader of the people of Wroclaw and our esteemed patron—shared Fabricius's learned manuscript discourse on this subject with us.

Furthermore, the curious and diligent reader will discover that the Author of the Holy Scriptures willed—by the very placement of the accents—to <note>The author uses the Greek phrase πρὸ ὀμμάτων ποιεῖν, meaning "to place before the eyes" or "to make visible."</note> *set before the eyes* the most weighty words of David as he hurried toward death. They are, as it were, divided into thirds and delivered with certain intervals and breaths, containing emphatic silences and omissions that must be meditated upon more deeply. *As if to say: The man spoke, having been confirmed* (or firmly persuaded) *concerning! Concerning the Messiah,* I say, *of the GOD of Jacob.* Compare this further with verses 4, 5, and 6, and you will not deny what we have just said. In the meantime, the word *hukam* <note>Hebrew: הוקם, "raised up."</note> fits perfectly with the following particle *al*, since it governs it, and through it, the following noun. As for Hackspan’s other objection concerning the *Kamez* vowel on the word *al* <note>The *Kamez* is a long "a" sound; its presence can sometimes indicate a specific grammatical state.</note>, the celebrated Wasmuth has responded on our behalf: namely, that this can happen because of the accent. <note>The author concludes with the Greek phrase ὅπερ ἔδει δεῖξαι, which is the equivalent of the Latin *Quod Erat Demonstrandum* (Q.E.D.)—"which was to be demonstrated."</note>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on Hebrew particles and transitions into a new "Question" concerning the botanical identity of a plant mentioned in the Book of Jonah. It follows the author's defense of Martin Luther’s translation choices.</meta>

page-num 143
<meta>catchword: Kimchi,</meta>

### FIFTH QUESTION.
### What kind of plant was Jonah’s Kikajon?

§. 1. The question does not seem to be of great importance. Nevertheless, the famous "gourd war" <note>original Latin: *bellum cucurbitarium*. This refers to a heated 4th-century intellectual dispute between Saint Jerome and Saint Augustine.</note> between Jerome and Augustine is well-known; the latter defended the traditional opinion of the **gourd**, while the former attacked it and substituted **ivy**. Jerome did not do this because he necessarily thought the *Kikajon* of Jonah (Chapter 4, verse 6) was literally ivy, but rather something analogous to it. Because of this, they exchanged letters of protest; indeed, Jerome was even accused of heresy and sacrilege in Rome by a certain "gourd-lover" <note>original Latin: *cucurbitario*</note>, as he calls him, and his followers were treated so indignantly that the matter nearly came to blows. Francisco Ribera <note>A Spanish Jesuit biblical scholar (1537–1591).</note> provides more details on this conflict in his *Commentaries*.

§. 2. Otherwise, the Septuagint, the Syriac, the Arabic, the Ethiopic, Pagninus, Martin Luther, and Forster all translate *Kikajon* <note>Hebrew: קיקיון</note> as **gourd**. Aquila, Symmachus, Theodotion, the Vulgate, Cornelius à Lapide, Oecolampadius, and others translate it as **ivy**. Others prefer **cucumber**, some the **plane tree**, others the **white vine**, some the **fig tree**, and others the **Mauz** <note>An old term for the banana plant.</note>. Ribera and Sanchez believe it is a general name for shrubs that grow quickly and produce leaves for making shade. Thus, Muhammad in the Quran, Sura 36 <note>The author likely means Sura 37:146.</note>, explains the tree as *Yaqtin* <note>Arabic: يقطين</note>, which is to say "expanded" or "spread out," as Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist.</note> argues in his *Oriental History*, pages 78 and 79, although Giggejus and Golius <note>17th-century lexicographers of Arabic.</note> believe it refers to a gourd. Others prefer the term *epiche* <note>original Greek: ἐπιχὴ</note>. Thus the Geneva Bible: *And the Eternal God prepared a kikaion, etc.*

§. 3. To us, having properly weighed all arguments, Jonah’s *kikajon* seems to be the *ricinus vulgaris* <note>Commonly known today as the Castor Oil plant.</note> described by Bauhin. It is called by others *Alcberva* <note>from Arabic *al-khirwa*</note>, *kiki* <note>a Greek transcription of the Egyptian name</note>, *tseluliba* <note>Aramaic: צלוליבא</note>, *kroton* <note>Greek: κρότων</note>, *scillocypria*, *trixis*, *Pentadactylos* <note>meaning "five-fingered"</note>, *Palma Christi* <note>"Palm of Christ"</note>, and *Cataputia major*. In German it is called the **Creutzbaum** <note>Cross-tree</note> or **Wunderbaum** <note>Wonder-tree</note>. Agreeing with us are Kimchi,

<summary>This page explores the historical and linguistic debate over the identity of the "Kikajon" plant that provided shade for Jonah, famously known as the "Gourd War" between Jerome and Augustine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of scholars and the linguistic analysis concerning the identity of the plant "Kikajon" mentioned in the Book of Jonah. It follows page 143 where the author began listing authorities who reject the "gourd" or "ivy" translations in favor of the castor oil plant.</meta>

Kimchi, Solomon ben Melech along with the Talmudic scholars, Arias Montano, Jean Mercier, Francis Junius, Johann Tarnovius, Johannes Piscator, Johannes Cocceius, Hugo Grotius *on this passage*, Johannes Drusius *here and in "Questions and Answers," question 44, book 2*, Sixtinus Amama in his *Biblical Conferences on this passage*, Johann Buxtorf in his *Talmudic Lexicon, page 2029*, Johann Heinrich Hottinger in the *History of the Orient* in the place cited, Valentin Schindler in the *Pentaglott Lexicon* under the root <note>Hebrew: קיק</note>, Matthias Martinius under the words *Cici* and *Ricinus*, Johann Heinrich Ursinus in his *Sacred Herbarium, pages 111, 112*, and others.

§. 4. First, the *etymology* is not inconsistent with this view. Although the famous Forster in his *Dictionary* (page 753), <note>original Greek: *tē hypothesei douleuōn* — "being a slave to his own theory"</note>, thinks it is named for the fruit, which he considers to be "hollowed out," and claims it has a kinship of meaning with the words *nakah* and *nakak* <note>Hebrew: נקה and נקק</note>, which signify emptiness or hollowness, you might more conveniently derive it from *qi* <note>Hebrew: קיא — to vomit</note>. From this comes *kikajon* meaning "vomiting," and with the letters doubled for emphasis <note>Hebrew: קיקיון</note>, you might call it a "vomitorium." For medical writers testify that swallowing the grains of the **ricinus** <note>The castor oil plant (*Ricinus communis*).</note> induces vomiting. 

Secondly, there is the Egyptian name *kiki*. In the Coptic-Arabic Lexicon of *Ibn al-Shaykh* published by Athanasius Kircher (which he calls the *Great Scale*), <note>original Latin: *scalam magnam*</note> in chapter 17, page 185, it is written *kiki* <note>Greek: κικι</note> and is explained in Arabic as *Hab al-khirwa'* <note>Arabic: حب الخروع — "seed of the castor plant"</note>. That this is the **ricinus**, which the Greeks call *krotōn* <note>Greek: κρότων — literally "tick," because the seeds resemble the insect</note>, is testified by Herodotus in his book *Euterpe*, Theophrastus in book 1, chapter 38, Galen in his work *On Simples*, Pliny in the *Natural History*, book 15, chapter 7, Matthiolus, and others. What is more likely, all things being equal, than that *kiki* is the same as *kikajon*? 

Furthermore, the Arabic word *al-khirwa'* as written by *Gjeuharius*, or *al-khirwa'* as written by *Meidanius*, denotes the **ricinus**, as cited by Jacobus Golius in column 699. Jerome also testifies that the plant of Jonah is called *Elkeroa* <note>Hebrew: אלכרוע — transliterated Arabic</note> in Syria and Palestine. Moreover, the oil expressed from the *kiki* seeds is called *kikinon elaion* <note>Greek: κίκινον ἔλαιον — castor oil</note>. Thus Pliny writes in the place cited: *The next oil is made from the Cici tree, which is abundant in Egypt, etc. Our people call it the ricinus, etc.* And Dioscorides in book 4, chapter 164, writes that an oil named "cicinum" is expressed from the seed of the *Cici*, which is foul for food, but useful for lamps and medicinal plasters. This is also mentioned by the Talmudic scholars—

<summary>This page argues that the plant "Kikajon" in Jonah is the castor oil plant (Ricinus communis) based on its linguistic roots related to vomiting and its identification with the Egyptian "kiki" and Arabic "al-khirwa'."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical and linguistic investigation into the identity of the "Kikajon" plant from the Book of Jonah. The author is providing evidence from Jewish tradition (the Talmud and medieval commentators) and classical naturalists to support the identification of the plant as the *Ricinus communis* (castor oil plant) rather than a gourd or ivy.</meta>

page 145.

T

It is called <term>Shemen Kik</term> <note>Hebrew: *Shemen Kik* — "Kik oil," traditionally identified as castor oil.</note>. Thus **we read in the tractate *Shabbat*, chapter 2, at the beginning:** **"With what [oil] may they light the Sabbath lamps?"** <note>original Hebrew: ולא בשמן קיק</note> **"and not with *kik* oil."** <note>original Latin: *neque oleo kikino*</note> **"What is *kik* oil?" Resh Lakish answered: "** <note>original Hebrew: קיקיון דיונה</note> **"the *kikajon* of Jonah,"** etc. Furthermore, Rabbi Samuel ben Hophni says that in Arabic it is called <note>original Arabic: אלכרוע — *al-khirwa‘*, the castor oil plant</note>. Rabbah bar bar Channa argues with Zeluliba, etc. Compare Kimchi and Ben Melech **on this passage**. Finally, the circumstances of the text very well admit the **ricinus** <note>The castor oil plant.</note>. The *kikajon* plant grew quickly; once grown, it provided shade with its broad leaves and kept away the heat of the sun, for which reason it was most pleasing to Jonah (the idea that Jonah was then seized by a fever is a "feverish" invention of Isaac **Abravanel**). 

Now truly, as Jerome describes it, the **Elkeroa** (that is, the *ricinus*) is a small tree with broad leaves like those of a vine, providing a very thick shade, supporting itself on its own trunk. It grows very frequently in Palestine, especially in sandy places, and once sown it rises up very quickly, so that within a few days you look up at a small tree where you had seen only a plant. Or, as Matthiolus notes from Dioscorides: "The name *Cici* was taken from its resemblance to the *ricinus* animal <note>The Latin *ricinus* refers to a tick; the seeds of the plant were thought to look like ticks.</note>. The tree grows to the height of a small fig tree, with leaves like a plane tree but larger, smoother, and darker; its branches and stems are hollow like reeds; its seed is in rough clusters, which, when removed from the husks, resembles the tick insect." 

Furthermore, the words <note>original Hebrew: וימן יהוה אלהים — "And the LORD God prepared..."</note> seem to suggest quite clearly that the **growth** <note>original Greek: ἐκφυΐαν — *ekphuian*</note> of the *kikajon* was not purely natural. Hence we will not insist upon the point that—in contrast to the <note>original Greek: τὸν σίκυα — *ton sikua*, "the gourd"</note>—**the gourd and the ivy** creep along the ground and do not seek higher places without supports, **which would not fit this passage**, since it rose up in one night while the Prophet was unaware and provided a dwelling. Moreover, as for Cornelius à Lapide’s objection that the *Kiki* (the *ricinus*) is a poisonous plant and therefore its shade is harmful—this has not yet been proven; and even if it were proven, it does not harm our case.

It emphasizes the plant's rapid growth and broad leaves as fitting the biblical narrative better than a gourd or ivy.</summary>

<header>SIXTH QUESTION.</header>

### Does *Bar* in Psalm 2:12 signify "the Son" in Hebrew?

§. I. The words <term>nashku-bar</term> <note>Hebrew: נשקו בר — traditionally translated as "Kiss the Son."</note>, although clear enough, have nevertheless been troubled by various explanations from interpreters. From this, it by no means follows that there are as many literal meanings as there are opinions about the sense, as was the view of Salmeron <note>Alfonso Salmeron (1515–1585), a Spanish Jesuit theologian.</note> in his *Prolegomena*, volume 1, issue 8—a point noted by the Blessed Walther in his *Biblical Harmony*, page 501. It is as if whatever men dream up concerning Holy Scripture should be attributed to the Scripture itself! Furthermore, the Septuagint <note>The Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> translated it as: "lay hold of instruction" <note>Greek: δράξασθε παιδείας — *draxasthe paideias*</note>. Likewise the Chaldean <note>The Aramaic Targum.</note>: "receive instruction" <note>Aramaic/Chaldean: קבילו אולפנא — *kabbilu ulpana*</note>. The Roman Arabic: "cling to discipline" <note>Arabic: الزموا الادب — *al-zimū al-adab*</note>. The Ethiopic: "listen to wisdom" <note>Ethiopic: *Aznywa latbab*</note>. The Vulgate <note>The standard Latin Bible.</note>: "Apprehend discipline" <note>Latin: *Apprehendite disciplinam*</note>. It is uncertain by what reasoning the Chaldean and the Septuagint acted. For the Arabic, Ethiopic, and Latin translators followed them as if with their eyes closed. Indeed, Mayer <note>John Mayer (1583–1664), English commentator.</note> estimates in his *Sacred Philology*, Part 1, page 351, that the Chaldean had the version of the Seventy before his hands, or at least a Greek version made from it; therefore, he thinks it is not the Seventy who followed the Chaldean, which is a view we do not believe we should follow. Two people can feel and say the same thing even without sharing their plans. Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler, Hebraist.</note> conjectures that the Seventy read <term>nashgu</term> <note>Hebrew: נשגו — "stray" or "wander"</note> instead of <term>nashku</term> <note>Hebrew: נשקו — "kiss"</note> and took <term>bar</term> to mean "purity," namely, of doctrine. But John Viccars in his *Decapla on the Psalms*, page 7, prefers to think they derived it from <term>ba’ar</term> <note>Hebrew: באר — to explain or declare</note>. It is also a "guess" of Cappellus <note>Louis Cappel (1585–1658), French Protestant scholar.</note> that they read <term>nashgu</term>, and he adds that they first wrote "the child" <note>Greek: παιδα — *paida*</note>, from which was later made "instruction" <note>Greek: παιδείας — *paideias*</note> (*Critica Sacra*, page 243). Likewise, Hackspan <note>Theodor Hackspan (1607–1659), Lutheran theologian.</note> in his *Biblical Notes*, Part 2, page 13, posits that they initially translated it as "of the son" <note>Greek: παιδὸς — *paidos*</note>, and later "of instruction" was substituted by others. However, <term>nashag</term> <note>Hebrew: נשג</note> is not used in the <term>Piel</term> <note>An intensive grammatical "stem" or pattern in Hebrew verbs.</note> form, nor should we think that however much the interpreters may have raved, they read something different in the manuscript code. They could have read <term>nashku</term> and yet looked toward <term>nashgu</term> or <term>hashigu</term>, thinking that letters of the same vocal organ here would not change the meaning. The famous Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664), Hebraist.</note> in his *Anticritica*, page 673, judges that the Seventy and the Chaldean, in their allegorical opinion, wanted to render the words in the text through their proper equivalents; they took <term>bar</term> in the sense of "grain" <note>Latin: *frumenti*</note>, which they thought should be compared to the doctrine of the law. He also observes that the Talmudists <note>Jewish scholars of the Talmud.</note> explain the word <term>bar</term> in this passage as referring to the law—

...placed it in the *Sanhedrin* <note>A tractate of the Talmud concerning legal and judicial procedures.</note>, last chapter, folio 92, page 1. Moreover, he says that for them "to kiss *doctrine*" is the same thing as to embrace and lay hold of it, because kissing is joined with the embrace of its subject, according to Proverbs 7:13. But we will not answer for the faults of the Seventy <note>The *Septuagint* (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament produced by seventy scholars.</note>, nor do we see what that blind-eyed *Jose* <note>Likely Rabbi Jose ben Halafta, a prominent 2nd-century scholar.</note> saw or did not see. Concerning the common version [the Vulgate], it is pleasing to add the judgment of Galatinus <note>Pietro Galatino (1460–1540), an Italian Franciscan who wrote on Hebrew truth and Catholic faith.</note> in *On the Truths of the Catholic Faith*, book 3, chapter 7. He says: *“The author of our edition [the Vulgate], deceived by the ambiguity of words, translated many things far differently than the Hebrew truth contains.”* We concede the whole argument: let the Papists forgive him since he has confessed. Meanwhile, that translation can be refuted without much effort. For:

1. <term>Nashak</term> <note>Hebrew: נשק</note> is nowhere "to apprehend," but is commonly "to kiss."
2. <term>Bar</term> <note>Hebrew: בר</note> nowhere signifies "doctrine."
3. Nor is "a kiss" appropriate for doctrine; at least in the Holy Scriptures, it is never used in that way.
4. And how will what follows fit: *Lest he be angry*? I ask, please, by what reasoning would doctrine become angry? Furthermore, the word "Lord" is added without basis. Let the Papists learn from this that the Vulgate departs from the authentic Text even in places concerning the faith; on which matter, compare Sixtus Amama <note>A Dutch Hebraist and critic of the Vulgate (1593–1629).</note> in his *Antibabylonica*, pages 19 and 445.

§. 2. Others translate the word <term>bar</term> <note>Hebrew: בר</note> with the notion of "purity" and "choice," and this either *nominally* (as a noun) or *concretely*, such as Kimchi <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), a medieval rabbi and grammarian.</note>: *Kiss him who is pure*, a sense which Abendana <note>Jacob Abendana (1630–1695), a Sephardic rabbi and scholar.</note> declares. Kimchi again: *Kiss the chosen one*, from 1 Samuel 7:8; so too Rabbi Solomon ben Melech <note>A 17th-century Jewish grammarian and commentator.</note> in this place. Or they take it *abstractly*, as Rashi <note>The preeminent medieval Jewish commentator Shlomo Yitzhaki (1040–1105).</note> does: <term>Armate l. accingite vos puritate cordis</term> <note>Hebrew original: זרוז עצמיכם בבור לבב — "Arm or gird yourselves with purity of heart."</note>. From this, the German *Le kach tof* <note>Likely referring to a specific Jewish-German translation or commentary.</note> has: **Be armed with a pure heart** etc. Or they take it *adverbially*: Thus Aquila <note>A 2nd-century convert to Judaism who produced a literal Greek translation of the Bible.</note>: "Kiss selectively" <note>original Greek: καταφιλήσατε ἐκλεκτῶς</note>. Symmachus <note>A late 2nd-century translator of the Old Testament into Greek.</note>: "Worship purely" <note>original Greek: προσκυνήσατε καθαρῶς</note>. Jerome <note>The church father and translator of the Latin Vulgate.</note>, in his *Psalter according to the Hebrews*: *Worship purely*, lest he should offend the Jews, as he indicates in his *Apology against Rufinus*, Volume II, page 202. But <term>bar</term> is nowhere used *adverbially*. It might be permitted to translate it nominally as "the pure one." But there is no mention or occasion for it in the preceding text, nor is there any such determination of the subject, so we shall pass over the rest.

§. 3. Others have taken <term>bar</term> <note>Hebrew: בר</note> to mean *grain* or *wheat*, and they translate <term>nashku bar</term> as *Kiss* or *receive the wheat*. In the *Midrash Tehillim* <note>An ancient Jewish commentary on the Psalms.</note>, this is explained by *Rabbi Hanina* as referring to the people of Israel being gathered like wheat into granaries; others use it to refer to the Law. Catholic commentators distort this to refer to the Eucharist. For instance, a certain *Tyroboscus* <note>Possibly a reference to a lesser-known commentator cited by Lorinus.</note> cited in the works of *Lorinus* <note>Jean Lorin (1559–1634), a Jesuit commentator.</note> says: *Kiss or adore the covenant* (reading it as <term>berit</term> <note>Hebrew: ברית</note>) *or the grain, that is, the Gospel law and Christ in the Eucharist.* And *Salmeron* <note>Alfonso Salmeron (1515–1585), a founding Jesuit and biblical scholar.</note> declares that sense of "wheat" in this way: *Adore or kiss—that is, with a kiss of reverence—the Son of God under the species of bread and wine, etc.* <note>original: "Adorate vel osculamini, id est osculo reverentiæ sub speciebus panis & vini filium DEI, &c."</note> But *Mayer* <note>Johann Friedrich Mayer (1650–1712), a Lutheran theologian.</note>, in the work cited on page 353, judged—not without reason—that the architects of this interpretation should be fed not on wheat, but on hay. And it is ridiculous that *Salmeron* also makes mention of wine, as if that were also made from wheat. Compare the work of *Dr. Walther* <note>Michael Walther (1593–1662), author of the *Harmonia Biblica*.</note> in his *Harmony of the Bible* at the same passage.

§. 4. But the word <term>bar</term> in this place is most correctly explained as *Son*, and <term>nashku bar</term> as *Kiss the Son*. Among the Hebrew scholars, *Aben Ezra* <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a major medieval Jewish philosopher and commentator.</note> and *Kafvenaki* <note>Likely Jacob ben Isaac Luzzatto, known for his work *Kaftor va-Ferach*, or another Jewish commentator.</note> explain it this way, and a fitting allegory for this matter is brought forward in the *Midrash Tehillim* as cited by *Galatinus*. A great number of Christian interpreters agree, nor do the Catholics strongly deny that the Hebrew reads this way. Compare *Genebrardus*, *Bellarmine*, *Jansenius*, *Sa*, and others <note>A list of prominent 16th-century Catholic scholars and cardinals.</note>. Certainly, the usage of the language in Proverbs 31, verses 2 and 5, allows <term>bar</term> to denote *son*, and the internal consistency of the text requires it, since a clear mention of the "son" has already preceded in verse 7. Furthermore, the emphasis and the reasoning for this "kiss" were explained by the Blessed *Luther* in his *Commentary on the Psalms* at this location; by our great *Dr. Calovius* <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a champion of Lutheran Orthodoxy.</note> in his *Antigraphe on Psalm 2*; and by *Dr. Dorsecheus* <note>Johann Georg Dorsche (1597–1659), a Lutheran theologian.</note> in his *Pentadecad*, dissertation 2, paragraph 53, to whom we refer the reader.

§. 5. But truly, let wise men judge whether he himself does not "wander from the door" who, without necessity, imagines Chaldaisms in a pure Hebrew text. We...

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion from page 148 regarding the Hebrew word "bar" (son). The author argues that rather than being a loanword from Aramaic (Chaldean), it is an indigenous Hebrew term derived from the root meaning "to choose."</meta>

We believe that the Chaldeans received both <term>bar</term> <note>Hebrew: בר</note> and <term>bara</term> <note>Hebrew: ברא</note> from the Hebrews. Its etymology is well known to the common people. However, without prejudice to anyone, we derive <term>bar</term> not from <term>barar</term> <note>Hebrew: ברר, meaning to purify or clarify.</note>—since the form <term>beri</term> occurs—but from <term>barah</term> <note>Hebrew: ברה</note>, meaning *he chose* (just as *chai* comes from *chayah*, *kav* from *kavah*, *dal* from *dalah*, etc.); thus, you might call it *chosen offspring*. Nor is there such a need for the Hebrews to seek from Chaldea what is their own and what they have at home.

### SEVENTH QUESTION.
#### Who are the *Chasmannim* in Psalm 68:32?

§. 1. Mayer <note>Johann Friedrich Mayer (1650–1712), a Lutheran theologian.</note> and Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch jurist and theologian.</note> derive the word <term>Chasmannim</term> <note>Hebrew: חשמנים, traditionally translated as "ambassadors" or "nobles."</note> in Psalm 68, verse 32, from Egypt. The words of the former in *Sacred Philology, Part I, page 147*, are as follows: *The word <term>Chasmannim</term> is foreign. Its meaning cannot be extracted from the Hebrew language. But because these <term>Chasmannim</term> are said here to come from Egypt, we can easily conjecture that it is a word of the Egyptian language, which was later adopted into the Hebrew language like many others.* Truly, no traces of this word exist in the modern Coptic language <note>The latest stage of the Egyptian language, used in the liturgy of the Egyptian church.</note>; where then did Mayer discover the word was Egyptian? Because the <term>Chasmannim</term> are said to come from Egypt, he concludes that the word <term>Chasmannim</term> is Egyptian—as if those coming from Egypt could not be called by a Hebrew name in a Hebrew speech. Moreover, the illustrious man suggests that *perhaps* the <term>Chasmannim</term> were those who in Genesis 41, verse 34, are called <term>paqidim</term> <note>Hebrew: פקדים, meaning overseers or officers.</note> and are called <term>toparchai</term> <note>Greek: τοπάρχαι, meaning local governors.</note> by the Seventy <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note>, since Egypt was once divided into Prefectures or <term>Nomes</term> <note>From the Greek *nomos*, an administrative district in ancient Egypt.</note>, and those were further divided into Toparchies. On this subject, one should see Strabo’s *Geography*, book 17, and Pliny’s *Natural History*, book 5, chapter 9. We will not stubbornly deny that the <term>Chasmannim</term> might have been Egyptian *Toparchs* or even *Nomarchs* <note>Governors of a "nome" or district.</note>; meanwhile, the question remains regarding the name: is it Hebrew or Egyptian, and what does it formally imply?

§. 2. The Vulgate, Arabic, Ethiopic, and other versions translate it as *envoys* or *ambassadors*. Thus they accepted the word <term>presbeis</term> <note>Greek: πρέσβεις, meaning ambassadors or elders.</note> from the Seventy, which nonetheless can have another meaning; for you might derive it just as easily from the singular <term>presbys</term> <note>meaning an elder</note> as from <term>presbis</term> <note>meaning an embassy</note>. However, [he] excuses...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Chasmannim" from Psalm 68:32. The previous page introduced the theories of Mayer and Grotius, who argued the word was of Egyptian origin because the passage mentions ambassadors coming from Egypt.</meta>

...interpretation of the Vulgate, Nicholas Fuller <note>original: "terpretamentum Vulgati Fullerus." Fuller (1557–1626) was an English philologist.</note> in *Miscellany, Book 2, chapter 13, page 258*, says that the translator did not translate it unlearnedly, *if you take the word as a compound from* <term>chush</term> <note>Hebrew: חוש</note> *which means "to hasten," and* <term>manah</term> <note>Hebrew: מנה</note> *meaning "to appoint" or "to place in charge." For nothing more becomes an ambassador than speed.* Gregory Francus <note>Gregorius Francus, author of the "Lexicon Sanctum" (1634).</note> agrees with this in *Sacred Lexicon, number 145, page 184*. Truly, it is quite apparent how monstrous this "learned" blending of words actually is. Nor do we see why he lists speed among the chief praises of an Ambassador. If he means couriers, we admit the point. Otherwise, nothing is more unbefitting of Ambassadors than haste, which is usually the mother of error.

§. 3. It is worth hearing the opinions of others. Jerome <note>The 4th-century translator of the Latin Vulgate.</note> in his *Version according to the Hebrew* explains <term>Chasmannim</term> <note>Hebrew: חשמנים</note> as if from the root <term>chush</term> <note>meaning "quickly."</note>. Rashi <note>The preeminent medieval Jewish commentator (1040–1105).</note> interprets it as *gifts* <note>Hebrew: דורונות (doronot).</note>, though it is not clear why. Rabbi Menachem <note>Menachem ben Saruq, a 10th-century Spanish-Jewish philologist.</note> thinks it is a name for a people, as if to say *Inhabitants of Hashmonah* <note>Hebrew: יושבי חשמונה</note>. John Viccars, on page 146, conjectures via <term>antistoichia</term> <note>Greek: ἀντιστοιχία, a substitution of letters.</note> that the letter <term>Chet</term> (<term>ח</term>) stands for <term>He</term> (<term>ה</term>), so that <term>Chasmannim</term> stands for <term>Hashmannim</term> <note>Hebrew: השמנים, meaning "the fat ones," often used to denote the wealthy or powerful.</note>. Forster, in his *Dictionary, page 289*, derives it from <term>choshen</term> <note>Hebrew: חשן, the high priest's "Breastplate"</note> with the letter <term>Mem</term> (<term>מ</term>) inserted, and explains it as *Orators* or *Ambassadors*. Constantijn L'Empereur <note>A 17th-century Dutch Hebraist.</note> in his notes to *Middot, page 27*, composes it from <term>chashan</term>, which in Arabic signifies *reverence* (as in Psalm 67:6: *let them revere him* <note>Arabic: פלתחשאה (fal-tahshahu)</note>) and <term>manah</term> (*to set over*), from which come <term>memunim</term> (*overseers*), as if to say *revered leaders and overseers*. Others suggest other meanings.

§. 4. We hold <term>Chasmannim</term> to be a Hebrew word from the root <term>Chasham</term> <note>Hebrew: חשם</note>, which does not occur in the sacred text itself, but can be retrieved from Arabic usage. In Arabic, as Golius <note>Jacobus Golius (1596–1667), a Dutch Orientalist.</note> reports from Al-Qamus <note>A famous 14th-century Arabic dictionary.</note>, <term>hashama</term> among other things means *to have a great household, or many servants and followers*. From this, <term>hashamon</term> is a *retinue of servants*, and to al-Jawhari <note>An influential 10th-century lexicographer.</note> a <term>hashim</term> is a *man of a great and noble retinue*, and the verbal noun <term>hasham</term> means *to be the master of a great retinue*. The illustrious Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss scholar of Oriental languages.</note> notes in his *Oriental Smegma, page 158*, that this is all the more easily accepted because the pomp of a great retinue is highly appropriate for Egypt and Ethiopia (or rather Arabia), which are the subjects under discussion. The meaning, therefore, is: *Great nobles with an ample retinue and company shall come out of Egypt, etc.*

<summary>This page evaluates several competing etymologies for the obscure Hebrew word "Chasmannim" in Psalm 68:32, ultimately arguing that it refers to noblemen with large retinues, based on Arabic linguistic evidence.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 68:32, Chasmannim, etymology, Arabic roots, retinue, ambassadors, biblical philology, Nicholas Fuller, Jerome, Rashi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the word "Chasmannim" from Psalm 68:32. The previous page discussed the views of scholars like Nicholas Fuller and Rabbi Menachem regarding the origins and speed of ambassadors.</meta>

Page 151.

§. 5. With these <term>Chasmannim</term> <note>Hebrew: חשמנים, the mysterious word from Psalm 68</note>, the **Hasmoneans** <note>The ruling dynasty of Judea, also known as the Maccabees</note> are fruitlessly confused by some. According to the testimony of Josephus in *Antiquities of the Jews, Book 12, chapter 8*, the name "Hasmonean" is a family name <note>derived from an ancestor named Hasmon</note>. Therefore, we do not approve of the view of L'Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur (1591–1648), a Dutch scholar of Hebrew</note> in the place cited, who says Mattathias and his descendants were so named because they were the *prefects* of the Jews, and that the Jews revered them as "fathers of the fatherland." 

The famous Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist</note> seems to be "chasing the wind" in his *Thesaurus Philologicus, Book 2, chapter 2, page 530*, where he derives the **Hasmoneans** from the Arabic word <term>chashama</term>. He first explains it as meaning *angry ones*, because they were kindled with a just anger and zeal for God; next, assuming the meaning of *blushing*, he thinks they are noted as those in whose presence we should blush; then he observes that in Arabic the root expresses *relatives* and *kinsmen*, and thus the descendants of Mattathias are called this *by antonomasia* <note>original: "κατ' ἀνטוνομασίαν" — a rhetorical term for using a title or epithet instead of a proper name</note>; finally, he adds the meaning of *beating*, because the **Hasmoneans** were the "scourges" of the enemies of the Church, by whose wounds they fell. Furthermore, Aben Ezra, Kimchi, and Ben Melech <note>Influential medieval Jewish grammarians and commentators</note> do not sufficiently distinguish these <term>Chasmannim</term> from the **Hasmoneans**.

§. 6. Furthermore, the conjecture of the famous Bochart <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French Protestant biblical scholar</note> does not please us. In *Sacred Geography, Part 2, on Canaan, Book 1, chapter 29, page 604*, he derives the city of **Casmena** <note>An ancient city in Sicily</note> from the <term>Chasmannim</term>, because the neighboring Carthaginians <note>referred to as "Pœni"</note> used to call their *nobles* <term>Chasmannim</term>. But the famous man himself adds: *“But I bring these things forward with hesitation, because it is not established when Casmena was first named; nor does it seem sufficiently convenient to derive the Greek name of a city from a Punic source.”*

§. 7. Finally, it is not clear why the Italian Jews called **Cardinals** <term>Chasmannim</term>. *Thus says Elias in Tisbi:* <note>Elijah Levita (1469–1549), a Hebrew grammarian. His work "Tisbi" is a dictionary of Rabbinic Hebrew.</note> *The Italian Jews call the Cardinals Chasmannim, and they interpret this passage accordingly: "Chasmannim shall come out of Egypt." But I do not know where they learned to say this. Certainly, in the time of David there were no Cardinals, much less in Egypt.* <note>original: "היהודים הלועזים קורין להקרדינאליס חשמנים... והלא לא היו קרדינאלי בימי דוד אף כי במצרים"</note> I do not know, says Mayer <note>Johann Friedrich Mayer (1650–1712), a Lutheran theologian</note> on page 148, whether they wish to mock the Cardinals in this way. For if you explain this word according to their *Targum* <note>The Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Bible</note> (which uses the phrase "the assembly of Hasmoneans" <note>Aramaic: כניחם חוסמניא</note>), the **Cardinals**, whom they label with this name, would be "sons of the degenerate and impious Ham." <note>A play on the similarity between the name "Chasman" and "Cham" (Ham), the son of Noah associated with Egypt and, in some traditions, a curse.</note> Whatever the case may be, if the name displeases the Cardinals, let them answer the Jews for themselves; we shall stop here.

<meta>The catchword "ADDI-" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with "Additamenta" (Additions) or a similar word expanding on this philological study.</meta>

<summary>This page evaluates several linguistic theories regarding the word "Chasmannim," rejecting its identification with the Hasmonean dynasty and discussing a curious linguistic connection made by Italian Jews between this word and Catholic Cardinals.</summary>

# SUPPLEMENTS

I. *What is the meaning of the word <term>margemah</term> in Proverbs 26:8?* <note>Hebrew: מרגמה</note> The Ancient Versions translated it as a **heap of Mercury**, from which they offer various interpretations regarding the throwing of stones at it <note>original: "acervum Mercurii"; this refers to the ancient practice of travelers throwing stones at a statue or heap dedicated to Mercury/Hermes for good luck</note>. See the *Midrash*, *Kasvenaki*, and *Rashi* on this passage. See also Maimonides in the *Commentary on the Mishnah*, chapter 3; Drusius in *Hebrew Additions, Decade 8*, and *Sacred Proverbs, class 1, book 2, proverb 34*; Scaliger in *On the Emendation of Time, book 5, page 486*; Selden in *On the Syrian Gods, Synthesis 2, chapter 15*; and Buxtorf in the *Talmudic Lexicon, column 1262*. Aben Ezra says <term>margemah</term> is a **heap of stones**, and <term>eben</term> is a **precious stone**. Megalander <note>A term often used by scholars of this era to refer to Martin Luther</note> translates <term>margemah</term> as **Raven-stone** <note>German: *Rabenstein*, a place of execution or a gallows</note>. To Kimchi, it is **Purple** <note>Hebrew: *argaman*</note>. Others say different things. However, <term>margemah</term> is best translated as a **Sling** for the throwing of stones. Certainly, the root *ragam* appears often in the <term>Targum</term> <note>The ancient Aramaic translations and paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible</note> meaning "to throw, to hurl, or to aim with a sling" (See 2 Samuel 16:13, Lamentations 1:18, 2:14, Esther 5:14, etc.). And in Arabic, the word *ragjama* in the third conjugation means "he contended by throwing stones." Therefore, the sense is: *As tying a stone in a sling, so is he who shows honor to a fool.* A stone tied to a sling does not reach its mark. It is a futile effort, even if you try a thousand times. Thus, he who honors a fool is frustrated in his aim, and the honor progresses no further or penetrates the fool's mind no more than if one were to venerate a log or a tree trunk.

II. *What is understood by **Behemoth** and **Leviathan** in Job 40 and 41?* <note>Hebrew: בהמות and לויתן</note> For many, Behemoth is the devil, and his colleague is Leviathan (as Mersenne discusses in his commentary on Genesis). The Jewish fables concerning both are well known. Commonly, they literally understand **Behemoth** to be the **Elephant** and **Leviathan** to be a **whale or sea monster**. However, Beza and Diodati had said that Leviathan is the **Nile Crocodile**, and Samuel Bochart <note>A famous 17th-century French Protestant scholar and author of the *Hierozoicon*, a study of animals in the Bible</note> tries to prove this at length in his *Hierozoicon, Part 2, book 5, chapters 16, 17, and 18*. His arguments come down to these points: 1. He shows the etymology does not fit a whale. 2. He shows nothing is said in the text that cannot be conveniently applied to the crocodile. 3. Many things do not fit the whale. For instance, what is said about the rows of teeth and the scales in chapter 41, verses 6, 7, and 8, whereas whales have smooth skin. Likewise regarding the neck in verse 13, which a whale lacks; and the hardness of the skin in verses 17, 18, 19, and 20, whereas the skin of a whale can be penetrated even with a small knife, etc. Certainly, while <term>Leviathan</term> is a whale in some places, it hardly seems so in the Book of Job. Let the learned judge. But regarding what Bochart says in the same book, chapter 15—that Behemoth is the **hippopotamus**—we do not accept this. It is clear from the name that a purely land animal is intended, as well as by its association with the beasts of the field in chapter 40, verse 15. Nor is there anything in the text that fits the hippopotamus better than the Elephant.

->GLORY TO GOD ALONE.<- <note>original: "SOLI DEO GLORIA"</note>

<keywords>Proverbs 26:8, Margemah, Sling, Behemoth, Leviathan, Job, Samuel Bochart, Elephant, Crocodile, Hebrew philology</keywords>
