## FIRST DOUBT.

### *How do the final words of Isaiah 8, verse 20 (אשר אין לו שחר) connect with those preceding them?* <note>The Hebrew phrase translates to "for whom there is no dawn" or "no light."</note>

->§. I.<-

We shall not overstep the boundaries of the Philologist <term>Philologist</term><note>A scholar who studies the history of language and the meaning of ancient texts.</note>, nor shall we put our sickle into the harvest of the Theologians. That harvest was most bountifully displayed by that theologian who is "worth many others" <note>Greek original: πολλῶν ἀντάξιος ἄλλων</note>, the Blessed Doctor Johannes Georgius Dorscheus, in his *Disputation on this passage concerning the infallible testimony of divine truth*, which is found in the first *Pentadecas* <note>A collection of fifteen scholarly works.</note>. For now, it is merely our pleasure to examine: what is the true grammatical connection and sense of these final words with the preceding text?

§. 2. Here, it must be known above all else that some relate the words *lo asher* <note>Hebrew: לו אשר ("to him/it which")</note> to a **thing** or **doctrine**, while others relate them to a **person**. Among the former, the first who should be named are the *Seventy Elders* <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> whose interpretation is this: *For he gave the law for help, so that they might say not according to this word, concerning which it is not possible to give gifts.* <note>Greek original: νόμον γὰρ εἰς βοήθειαν ἔδωκεν, ἵνα εἴπωσιν οὐχ ὡς τὸ ῥῆμα τοῦτο, περὶ οὗ οὐκ ἔστι δῶρα δοῦναι περὶ αὐτοῦ</note>. Saint Jerome, in his *Commentary on Isaiah*, judged the meaning of this somewhat obscure version to be: *God gave the law to the Jews as a help, so that the nations fleeing to their own oracles might not speak like this word, which was given to the Jews as a help; and since this word was given freely, it must be received freely, and it is not permitted to offer gifts for it.* Origen (in *Homily 7 on Isaiah, p. 108*), Cyril of Alexandria (on this passage, *p. 106*), and Basil the Great (in *Volume 4 of his Works, Commentary on Isaiah, p. 445*) all built their own commentaries upon this same version. Meanwhile, it is not our place to pronounce what the Seventy intended or did not intend, nor what moved them toward that translation; we only know that they wandered away from the original source <note>The author suggests the Greek translation deviates from the original Hebrew meaning.</note>.

<summary>This page begins a philological analysis of Isaiah 8:20, specifically examining how the phrase "there is no light/dawn in them" connects grammatically to the law and testimony. The author reviews early Greek translations and Patristic interpretations that suggest the passage refers to the freeness of God's word compared to pagan oracles.</summary>
<keywords>Isaiah 8:20, philology, Septuagint, Jerome, Origen, Cyril of Alexandria, Basil the Great, Dorscheus, Hebrew grammar, biblical commentary</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Isaiah 8:20, specifically focusing on the confusion between the Hebrew words "shachar" (dawn) and "shochad" (bribe/gift) in ancient translations like the Septuagint, Syriac, and Arabic. It then transitions to Jewish rabbinical interpretations regarding the use of "law and testimony" as a form of an oath.</meta>

154.

But if they took *shachar* <note>Hebrew: שַׁחַר, meaning "dawn"</note> to mean *shochad* <note>Hebrew: שֹׁחַד, meaning "a bribe" or "a gift"</note>, they must have been purblind in their translating. Nor is this "blindness" <note>Greek original: ἀβλεψία (ablepsia)</note> of theirs infrequent, as Hottinger shows in his *Philological Treasury, book 1, chapter 3*. However, the Syriac and Arabic translators have placed their incautious feet in the footsteps of these predecessors. *The Syriac version translates it:* "To the law and to the testimony, lest they speak according to this word, for which it is not possible to offer a bribe" <note>Syriac: לְנָמוֹסָא וּלְסָהֲדוּתָא דְלָא בְאמְרוּן אֵיךְ מִלְתָא: הָדָא דְלֵית לְמֵתַל עֲלֵיהּ שׁוּחְדָא</note>—that is, *to the law and the testimony, lest they speak according to this word, for which there is no offering of hands.* See there, *shochda* <note>Syriac: שׁוּחְדָא</note> used for *shachar*! *The Arabic translation in the English Work <note>Referring to the London Polyglot Bible (1657).</note> is rendered thus: "For he gave them the law as a help, so they would not say: Nothing is equal to this word, for which no equal gift can be offered."* Anyone who understands both Greek and Arabic will see that the Arabic translator followed "in the footsteps" <note>Greek original: κατὰ πόδας (kata podas)</note> of the Seventy <note>The Septuagint.</note>.

### §. 3.
Furthermore, almost all other Jewish commentators refer the phrase "to which it" <note>Hebrew: אֲשֶׁר לוֹ (asher lo)</note> to the *divinatory doctrine* <term>Pythonic doctrine</term><note>The term "Pythonic" refers to the spirit of divination, named after the Oracle of Delphi (the Python), but used here to describe the forbidden practices of mediums and spiritists mentioned in Isaiah.</note>. And thus, not a few believe that there is the *force of an oath* in the following words, "to the law and the testimony." *Thus Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A significant medieval Jewish grammarian and commentator (1160–1235).</note> on this passage:* "An oath / to the law and to the testimony / like a man who says: 'By the law it shall be thus and so'; and likewise the words 'if not' imply an oath, as in Psalm 131:2: 'If I have not composed and quieted [my soul]' and similar places. They will say to those I have indicated; and when they say to you, you shall know in truth that they will speak to you according to this word which I have said, namely a word for which there is no dawn—meaning light—it is entirely without any sense, a dark and gloomy thing." <note>The original Hebrew text quoted from Kimchi explains that the lack of "dawn" means the words of the diviners are devoid of truth and clarity.</note> Rabbi Solomon ben Melech does not disagree on this passage, nor does Don Isaac Abravanel reject this gloss <note>An explanatory note or interpretation.</note>; his *Commentary on this passage (folio 22, column 3, near the middle)* contains these words: "And it is possible to interpret 'to the law and the testimony' as a form of an oath that the prophets swore, that the mediums and the spiritists he mentioned would not say anything to them except a thing that has no dawn and no light, because the diviners will not say a thing..."

<summary>This page critiques ancient translations (Syriac and Arabic) for confusing "dawn" with "bribe" in Isaiah 8:20 and presents Rabbinic interpretations that view the phrase "to the law and testimony" as a solemn oath against the dark, nonsensical words of diviners.</summary>
<keywords>Isaiah 8:20, shachar vs shochad, Septuagint, David Kimchi, Isaac Abravanel, philology, biblical oaths, divination, Syriac translation, Arabic translation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation of Isaiah 8:20. The previous page discussed how ancient translators (Septuagint, Syriac, Arabic) potentially misread "dawn" (shachar) as "bribe" (shochad). This page shifts to exploring whether the phrase "To the law and to the testimony" functions as a formal oath.</meta>

U 2

->It is a clear matter: it is nothing but the shadow of death, lacking order, and consisting of doubtful sayings. The phrase "if not" <note>Hebrew: אִם לֹא (*im lo*)</note> pertains to the matter of an oath—just as in the phrase, "If I have not stilled and quieted my soul" <note>Psalm 131:2</note>. That is, *it can also be understood as an oath, as if the Prophet were swearing that those Soothsayers and Diviners* <note>original Latin: *Pythones & Arioli*</note> *whom he mentioned would say nothing to them unless it were something devoid of dawn or light; for those who practice divination do not speak clear words, but mere darkness, disorders* <note>Greek: ἀταξίας (*ataxias*)</note>, *and ambiguous speeches. Furthermore, it is evident from Psalm 131:2 and Isaiah 14:24 that the expressions "if not" are used for oaths. François Vatablus* <note>A 16th-century French humanist and Hebrew scholar</note> *drew this same conclusion from the Hebrew commentators in his notes on this passage. Or, it may be read as: "By the law and the testimony! If they do not speak such things in which there is no common sense." That is, it is as certain that some of the people will consult diviners as the law itself is true. May I perish (away with such formulas! "Stay far, far away, you profane ones"* <note>Greek: ἑκὰς ἑκὰς ἔστε βέבηλοι (*hekas hekas este bebeloi*), a traditional call to exclude the uninitiated from sacred rites</note>*) unless there will be some among the people of God so foolish that they will urge others to consult diviners—a thing that lacks all common sense. Thus Pagninus* <note>Sante Pagnino (1470–1541), a Dominican monk and leading Hebraist</note> *renders it: "By the law itself and for a testimony, I call God to witness, if they will not speak according to this word concerning the seeking of the dead, in which there is not even a glimmer of light." To these views may be added those of Marc. Marinus in his "Noah's Ark"* <note>A significant Hebrew dictionary and grammatical work of the 16th century</note>.*<-

### §. 4.
But Rabbi Abraham Aben Ezra <note>A celebrated 12th-century Spanish biblical commentator and grammarian</note> rejects that interpretation and explains the phrase "to the law" <note>Hebrew: לַתּוֹרָה (*la-Torah*)</note> as meaning *on account of*. His words are: "Regarding 'to the law and to the testimony,' the letter Lamed <note>The Hebrew prefix ל (*l-*), meaning 'to' or 'for'</note> is used here as it is in Genesis 20:13, 'Say *of* me <note>Hebrew: לִי (*li*)</note>, he is my brother.' It means: On account of the teaching of prophecy, if they continue to speak according to this word, we will not listen, for it is like a night that has no dawn—that is, it does not bring hidden things to light. Some say that 'to the law' has the sense of an oath, but we have not seen such a formula for swearing in the entire Holy Scripture." *This is most correctly stated regarding the latter point. For indeed, no one has produced a passage where the letter Lamed functions as a particle for an oath.*

<summary>This page analyzes the grammatical structure of Isaiah 8:20, debating whether the text should be read as a prophetic oath or as a statement about the darkness of divination. It highlights the disagreement between scholars like Pagninus, who see an oath, and Aben Ezra, who argues the grammar does not support it.</summary>

<keywords>Isaiah 8:20, Abraham Aben Ezra, François Vatablus, Sante Pagnino, Hebrew grammar, Lamed prefix, oaths, divination, philology, biblical commentary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation of Isaiah 8:20, moving from the possibility of the text being an oath to examining different scholarly interpretations of the Hebrew phrasing, specifically how the particles connect to the surrounding words.</meta>

156

Compare Hakspanius <note>Theodor Hackspan (1607–1659), a Lutheran theologian and Orientalist</note> in his *Notes, Part II, page 563.* However, the cited passage from Aben Ezra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Jewish biblical commentator</note> is not similar to our case. Others translate it as: *Concerning me.* If you explain it as: *For my sake*—or as the German phrase goes, *tell me as a favor* <note>original German: "sage mir zu Gefallen"</note>—the phrasing fits a person, not an inanimate thing.

§. 5. There are also those who refer the word "to it" <note>Hebrew: לוֹ (*lo*)</note> to the "doctrine" <note>referring to the Law</note>, and they separate the phrase "if not" <note>Hebrew: אִם לֹא (*im lo*)</note> from the following words, translating it as *otherwise* or *if not.* They treat it as a concession made by someone who is indignant or despairing, in this sense: *“Let them consult the Law and the Testimony; if they refuse, let them speak according to this word, for which there is no dawn.”* That is to say: let them believe it to their own ruin, since that is what they want. Johannes Piscator <note>A 16th-century German Reformed theologian</note> explains it in this way in his notes on this passage, as do B. D. Glassius <note>Salomon Glassius (1593–1656), author of *Philologia sacra*</note> in his *Sacred Philology, page 1217*, and Sixtus Amama <note>A Dutch professor of Hebrew</note> in his *Antibabylon, book 4, page 635.* Castalio <note>Sebastian Castellio (1515–1563), a French preacher and theologian</note> explains "if not" as *otherwise* in this manner: *“Otherwise they will speak those things to him in which there will be no dawn.”* But these views are refuted by the Hebrew accents alone. For since the phrase "if not" <note>Hebrew: אִם־לֹא</note> carries the "Serving" accent *Mapach* (and not a "Universal" <note>Greek: καθολικὸν (*katholikon*)</note> *Jetif* accent, as "so-and-so" <note>Greek: τὸν δεῖνα (*ton deina*)</note> might suggest), it cannot be separated in sense from the words that follow it.

§. 6. Others take the phrase "if not" <note>Hebrew: אִם־לֹא</note> as a question, standing for "Is it not?" <note>Hebrew: הַאִם (*ha-im*)</note>. This is how Abravanel <note>Isaac Abravanel (1437–1508), a Portuguese Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> explains it: *Do not seek mediums and wizards, but seek the Law and the Testimony, for nothing is lacking in them. And if you listen to those who chirp and mutter, and those impostors and seducers, will they not speak to you according to the tenor of the words I mentioned, which have no dawn or light? For such speech is usually dark and obscure.* <note>Translated from the Hebrew block provided in the source</note> He hints at the purpose for which they say: *“Seek the mediums and wizards,”* namely, with the intent of seducing. Abendana <note>Jacob Abendana (1630–1685), a Sephardic Rabbi</note> says it this way: *And if those men who chirp and mutter say to you, "Seek the mediums and wizards," etc., you must seek the Law and the Testimony, in which nothing is lacking. Will they not speak to you according to this word that I mentioned, which has no dawn?* <note>Translated from the Hebrew block</note> *And if those chirping and muttering men should say to you, "Seek the mediums and wizards," etc., you should seek the Law*

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation of Isaiah 8:20, specifically debating how the Hebrew particles function and how different commentators identify the subject of the phrase "no dawn."</meta>

*“Seek the law and the testimony, in which nothing is lacking; will they not speak to you according to the mentioned tenor of the words, for whom there is no dawn?”* They would have done a worthwhile service if they had pointed out places where the phrase **if not** <note>Hebrew: *im lo* (**אם לא**)</note> begins a question.

§. 7. Furthermore, those who refer the words **which has no dawn** <note>Hebrew: *asher ein lo shachar* (**אשר אין לו שחר**)</note> to a *person* do not even agree among themselves. Some believe it refers to a false god <note>original: *Deastrum*, a diminutive Latin term for a minor or false deity</note>. Thus, the *Midrash* <note>A genre of ancient rabbinic biblical exegesis</note>, as cited by Rashi <note>Shlomo Yitzchaki (1040–1105), a preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note> and Kimchi <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), a medieval rabbi and grammarian</note> on this passage: **There is no dawn for him: He does not illuminate himself with the dawn, for he has eyes and does not see—how then could he shine for others?** <note>original Hebrew: *Ein lo shachar al atzmo ein mizrach et ha-shachar she-harei einayim lo ve-lo yireh ve-eikh yizrach al acherim?*</note> To this they link the version by Jonathan <note>The Targum of Jonathan, an ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Prophets</note>: **Thus you shall say to them: We have heard the Law, which was given to us as a testimony. Unless you pass over among the peoples and they say to you according to this word: then there will no longer be anyone for him who rises early in the morning to seek it.** <note>original Aramaic: *Kahin timrun le-hon ve-la-ora itah d’atyeheivat lana...*</note> The English scholars, following the lead of Arias <note>Benito Arias Montanus (1527–1598), a Spanish orientalist who worked on the Polyglot Bible</note>, render it this way. We have heard the Sibylline leaves <note>A classical idiom referring to something cryptic or difficult to interpret, like the prophecies of the Sibyl</note>. This seems to be the sense Jonathan intended: *“Thus you will say to them: We pay attention to the law given to us and to the testimony. But you will be carried away among the peoples, and they will speak to you according to this word; then it is not right that anyone should expect or seek it.”* Furthermore, Rashi refers this to a *false god* in this way: **To the law and to the testimony.** **This will indeed be your response: that you keep the law, as was said above in verse 16: “Bind the testimony, seal the law.” Do not believe their words if they do not speak according to this word to which there is no dawn. That is: after you have returned this answer, you will see and hear whether they will not be about to approve** <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, continued by the catchword "baturi," likely completing the word "approbaturi"</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Isaiah 8:20, transitioning from the interpretation of "dawn" as a person or idol to a discussion of how various theologians and translators understand the syntax of the entire verse. It maintains the catchword "DOUBT" (DUBIUM) for the following section.</meta>

...[that] they might be more prudent, your words and those who are about to speak in this sense: it is true that there is for him (namely, for the God of the medium and the soothsayer) no **dawn** <note>original Hebrew: *shachar* (**שחר**)</note>—or, *it is not right to seek His face*, according to the Targumist <note>The author of the ancient Aramaic paraphrase of the Bible</note>. But how forced these interpretations are is apparent.

§. 8. You will more correctly refer these words to those who were being sent back to the mediums <note>original Latin: *Pythones*, referring to spirit-mediums or necromancers</note>, along with Saint Jerome <note>The 4th-century translator of the Vulgate</note> *on this passage*, Blessed Luther *on this passage, Tome 3, Jena Latin edition, folio 315*, Francisco Forerius *on this passage, p. 143*, Hector Pinto of Portugal *p. 62*, Gaspar Sanchez *p. 106 & 115*, Heinrich Moller *folio 89*, the Great Doctor Calovius <note>Abraham Calovius (1612–1686), a leading Lutheran theologian</note> *Against Grotius on this passage, Collection 5, Discussion 2*, Doctor Wasmuth *Vindication of the Hebrew, part 2, p. 242*, and others. Therefore, the connection of the words seems to be this: **To the law and to the testimony!** <note>original Hebrew: *le-torah ve-li-te’udah* (**לתורה ולתעודה**)</note> (Let all those who send others or are themselves sent to mediums approach here, return here, and be recalled to this point!) **If they do not** <note>original Hebrew: *im lo* (**אם לא**)</note>—unless they have spoken (those whose action was "they shall say" in verse 19) according to this word (namely, that which is contained in the law and the testimony). Here the accent *Zakef Katon* <note>A Hebrew cantillation mark that acts as a comma or minor pause</note> creates a greater **rest** <note>original Greek: *anapauma* (**ἀνάπαυμα**)</note> in the sense; therefore, for a more convenient explanation of what follows, an ellipsis <note>A grammatical omission where words are understood but not stated</note> must be granted, just as occurs in the preceding verse: *If they say to you: "Seek out the mediums, etc.," say, or answer: "Should not a people seek their God? etc."*

So here, in the same way, we might prefer to supply the words: *Say or answer, because of which, or this happens because, this is from there*, along with Zacharias Ursinus <note>A 16th-century German Reformed theologian</note> and the English and Spanish versions; or *Know ye*, with Diodati <note>Giovanni Diodati, whose 1607 Italian translation is quoted here</note> (whose version is: *To the law and to the testimony: if anyone will not speak according to this word (know ye) that there is no dawn for him*); or *As the Lord lives*, with someone cited by Doctor Dorsche <note>Johann Georg Dorsche (1597–1659), a Lutheran orthodox theologian</note> *page 41*; or even to say that the word **which** <note>original Hebrew: *asher* (**אשר**)</note> is redundant or assertive, as Moller suggests. **That** <note>original Hebrew: *asher* (**אשר**)</note> **there will not be for him** <note>original Hebrew: *lo* (**לו**)</note> (that is, for none of them, as most feel, or more simply **for the people** <note>original Hebrew: *la-am* (**לעם**)</note> from verse 19—that people who ought to have consulted God but instead consult puny idols <term>Deastros</term> <note>A disparaging term for false gods or idols</note>, which is why the singular is continued in the following verses) a **dawn**, namely, that illumination about which theologians treat more extensively from *2 Peter 1:19*.

->DOUBT<-

The author synthesizes various scholarly views—from Luther to Diodati—on the grammatical structure and the symbolic meaning of the "dawn" that is denied to the unfaithful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the Book of Isaiah, moving from the analysis of "dawn" in chapter 8 to a new inquiry regarding the specific vocabulary of Isaiah 16:1. It addresses the conflicting interpretations of the Hebrew word 'kar' among ancient versions and medieval Jewish scholars.</meta>

## SECOND QUESTION.
### How is the word *kar* <note>Hebrew: **כר**</note> in Isaiah 16:1 to be explained?

->§. 1.<-

One can see as many opinions here as there are heads. Some seem not to have used their eyes quite correctly. Otherwise, the Syriac translator <note>The *Syrus*, or author of the Peshitta</note> certainly would not have read the letter *Beth* (**ב**) instead of *Kaph* (**כ**). For he translates it thus: *and over the remainder of the land I will send the son of the ruler of the land* <note>original Syriac: **ועל שרכא דארעא אשדר לבר שליטא דארעא**; the translator read *bar* ("son") instead of *kar* ("lamb")</note>. Furthermore, the <term>Septuagint</term> <note>original Greek: **της ό**, shorthand for the seventy translators of the Greek Old Testament</note> must have been blind if theirs is the first translation, which the Sixtine Codices, Basilius, and Cyril display: *I will send them like reptiles over the land* <note>original Greek: **ὡς ἑρπετὰ**</note>. From this the Arabic version also follows: *And he sent a creeping thing upon the earth*. Caspar Sanchez and Cornelius à Lapide *on this passage* guess that the Septuagint read *karmes* <note>creeping thing</note> for the land instead of *kar*, the ruler of the land. However, all Hebrew manuscripts testify that they read it incorrectly.

§. 2. Among those who find it a religious duty not to deviate from the written text <note>original Greek: **τὴν γραμμὴν κινεῖν**, an idiom meaning to change the established line or letter of the text</note>, they are nevertheless split into various parties. Rabbi Jonah—as cited by Rabbi David Kimchi and Rabbi Jacob Abendana *on this passage*—*interpreted it as a young camel, which is called the ruler of the land because of its excellent running, its speed, and its ability to reach the places it aims for.* <note>original Hebrew: **פירש שלחו כר מושל ארץ בן הגמל ונקרא מושל ארץ מרוב מרוצתו וקלותו והגעתו אל המקומות אשר יכוין אליהם**</note> Ibn Ezra agrees that the *kar* is a camel, but denies that "ruler of the land" is its description: *The "kar" is a species of camel powerful in running, based on 2 Kings 11:19. Some say that "ruler of the land" describes the "kar" because its stride on the earth is greater than any other step; but it is more correct that "kar" is in the construct state here, as it is hardly found except among the powerful.* <note>original Hebrew: **שלחוכר/ מין ממיני הגמל שירוץ מגזרת הכרי והרצים ויש אומרים כי מושל ארץ יתואר הכר בעבור שפסיעתו באר/ גדולה מכל פסע והנכון להיות כר סמוך כי לא ימצא רק אצל המושלים**</note> However, it has not yet been proven that the *kari* mentioned in 2 Kings 11 are camels; most interpreters explain them as leaders and as the vanguard of the flock. As for the claim by others that the word is a shortened form of *Cherethites* <note>original: **כרתי**, referring to the royal bodyguards of King David</note>, that seems a thin argument.

<summary>This page analyzes the translation of the Hebrew word 'kar' in Isaiah 16:1, debating whether it refers to a lamb, a young camel, or a ruler, while critiquing errors in the Syriac and Septuagint versions.</summary>
<keywords>Isaiah 16:1, philology, Hebrew linguistics, Septuagint, Syriac Peshitta, Ibn Ezra, David Kimchi, biblical commentary, camel vs lamb</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into Isaiah 16:1, specifically focusing on the term "kar" (lamb/ram). It moves from discussing the basic identification of the word to exploring its metaphorical, Christological, and sarcastic interpretations among various commentators.</meta>

§. 3. Since *karim* <note>Hebrew: **כרים**</note> otherwise means "rams," some understand *kar* here as a certain leader or noble. Thus Rabbi Solomon ben Melech says: "The leader and the noble are called this by way of metaphor, just as they are also called *ayilim* <note>Hebrew: **אילים**, meaning 'rams'</note> and *atudim* <note>Hebrew: **עתודים**, meaning 'he-goats'</note>, and in this place it is an absolute noun" <note>original Hebrew: **נקרא כן הנגיד והאציר דרך ההשאלה כמו שנקראו גם אילים ועתודים והוא מוכרח**</note>. Others (as observed by Caspar Sanchez and Cornelius à Lapide), with whom Foreirius does not disagree, understand "ram" metaphorically as war machines <note>battering rams</note> named for their likeness to rams, as if the Prophet is ironically provoking the Moabites to battle.

§. 4. But commonly, they interpret *kar* properly as "lamb" or "ram," a sense in which the plural *karim* occurs more than once. However, they do not agree on its meaning and relationship <note>original Greek: **σχέσει**</note> to the phrase "ruler of the land" <note>Hebrew: **משל ארץ**</note>. For some take "ruler of the land" as an appositive in the Accusative case. Thus the <term>Septuagint</term> <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, according to the Complutensian codices and others, translated it: "I will send a lamb ruling over the land" <note>original Greek: **Ἀποστελῶ ἄμνον κυριεύοντα ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν**</note>. The <term>Vulgate</term> <note>The late-4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> translates: "Send forth the lamb, O Lord, the Ruler of the earth" <note>original Latin: **Emitte agnum, Domine, Dominatorem terræ**</note>. But that word "Lord" does not exist in the text, nor is the Hebrew word *shalach* <note>Hebrew: **שלח**</note> "I will send" or "send forth" (in the singular), but rather "send ye" (in the plural). Furthermore, many interpreted that lamb, the "ruler of the earth," as Christ—to add this in passing <note>original Latin: **אגב אורחא**, a Hebrew/Aramaic idiom for 'by the way'</note>—and they traced the following words "from the Rock of the desert" etc., to Ruth the Moabite, from whom Christ was to be born. Compare, following Jerome: Lyranus, Carthusianus, Hector Pintus, Caspar Sanchez, Cornelius à Lapide, Tirinus, Menochius, and others on this passage. It is as if Ruth, who had long since passed away, is here finally demanded to be sent; but we leave these matters to be discussed by the theologians.

§. 5. Samuel Bohlius explained it in another sense through apposition. As the Great Doctor Abraham Calovius reports in his *Anti-Grotius*, Bohlius thought the King himself was called a "ram" by way of a sneer <note>original Greek: **μυκτηρισμὸν**, a rhetorical term for a mocking or sarcastic gesture</note> in this manner: "In the past, the Moabites used to send a certain number of rams to the king of Judah as a recognition of his superiority; now they are advised by this sneer to send the King himself, the Ruler of the Land, as if he were the ram."

<summary>This page analyzes Isaiah 16:1, debating whether the word 'kar' refers to a literal lamb, a metaphorical leader, or a sarcastic reference to the King of Moab. It highlights the Christological interpretation linking the 'lamb' to Christ’s descent from Ruth the Moabite.</summary>
<keywords>Isaiah 16:1, kar, ram, lamb, Christology, Ruth the Moabite, Solomon ben Melech, Abraham Calovius, biblical philology, metaphor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Isaiah 16:1, specifically investigating the grammatical relationship between the word "lamb" (kar) and "ruler of the land" (moshel eretz). It weighs the opinions of various Reformation-era scholars and Jewish commentators regarding whether the "lamb" refers to a literal tribute or a metaphorical figure.</meta>

...and test whether they might be able to find favor in the eyes of their superior. This might perhaps seem to some to be a far-fetched interpretation. **Martin Luther** and **Arias Montanus** <note>Benedictus Arias Montanus, a Spanish Orientalist and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible</note> explain the word **ruler** <note>Hebrew: **מושל** (*moshel*)</note> in the Vocative case <note>as a direct address: "O ruler..."</note>, which is not unsuitable, except that the difference in number between the command "Send ye" <note>Hebrew: **שלחו** (*shilchu*), which is plural</note> and the word "ruler" <note>which is singular</note> stands in the way, although even this can be excused in a certain way. Indeed, the analogy of the language more easily allows one to explain "ruler" in the Vocative than in the Dative case <note>meaning "to the ruler"</note> as **Isidore Clarius**, **Hugo Grotius**, and others do; for to avoid confusion, a sign <note>a preposition like "to"</note> would be needed, and in this manner the word **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> would require a "Lord" accent <note>A disjunctive mark in Hebrew cantillation that separates words</note>, whereas it actually has a "Servant" accent <note>A conjunctive mark that links words together grammatically</note>.

### §. 7 
To briefly set forth our own opinion, it seems that **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> is best translated collectively as "lambs," and the following phrase **ruler of the land** as a term very closely joined in the genitive case <note>meaning "the lamb *of* the ruler"</note>, following the translations of **Pagninus**, **Junius**, **Glassius**, and others. Thus the sense is: "Send (O Moabites) the lamb" or lambs (for such names can be taken for both the species and the individual) "of the ruler of the land," that is, those lambs which belong to the ruler of the land—preeminently <note>original Greek: **κατ' ἐξοχὴν**</note> the land of Israel—or are rightly owed to him. 

Specifically, the Moabites had formerly been made tributaries to **David** for the sake of peace and protection (2 Samuel 8:2), and a clear account exists concerning a certain sum of lambs—**one hundred thousand lambs** <note>original Hebrew: **מאה אלף כרים** (*meah elef karim*), from the singular *kar*</note>—and rams paid by the Moabites to the Kings of Israel until the time of King **Ahab** (2 Kings 3:4). Therefore, the Moabites would have been rightfully tributary to **Hezekiah**, even though he was not the King of Israel <note>the Northern Kingdom</note>, both as the heir of David and because the King of Judah had once suppressed the rebellious Moabites alongside the King of Israel, standing as if they were a single person (2 Kings 3:7). Hence, these lambs are demanded now not so much as sacrificial animals (as **Oleaster** and others would have it) but as tributary ones, and this is said not without a certain irony, which **Henry Moller** seems to have explained well in his commentary on this passage.

### §. 8. 
Most of the **Jewish scholars** agree on the main point of this case, and it is pleasing now to add their votes. **Rashi** <note>The preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note> says:

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the first verse of Isaiah chapter 16, specifically examining different interpretations of the word "lamb" (kar) and its symbolic or literal meaning in the context of the Moabite tribute.</meta>

§. 3. Since **lambs** <note>Hebrew: **כרים** (*karim*)</note> are elsewhere understood as *rams*, some here understand **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> to mean a certain leader or perhaps a nobleman. So says Rabbi Solomon ben Melech: <note>original: **נקרא כן הנגיד והאציל דרך ההשאלה כמו שנקראו גם אילים ועתודים והוא מוכרח**</note> "The ruler and the noble are called so by way of metaphor, just as they are also called 'rams' and 'he-goats,' and it is necessarily an absolute noun in this place." Others (as noted by Caspar Sánchez and Cornelius à Lapide), with whom Francisco Forerio is not in disagreement, understand the "ram" metaphorically as military engines <note>battering rams</note> named for their resemblance to rams, as if the Prophet were ironically provoking the Moabites to battle.

§. 4. But commonly, **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> is interpreted properly as a *lamb* or *ram*, a meaning in which the plural form **lambs** <note>Hebrew: **כרים** (*karim*)</note> appears more than once. However, scholars do not agree on its meaning and its relationship to the phrase **ruler of the land** <note>Hebrew: **משל ארץ** (*moshel eretz*)</note>. For some take **ruler of the land** as an apposition in the Accusative case. Thus the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, according to the Complutensian Codices and others, translated it: <note>Greek: *Ἀποστελῶ ἄμνον κυριεύοντα ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν*</note> "I will send a lamb ruling over the land." The Vulgate <note>The 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> has: "Send forth the lamb, O Lord, the Ruler of the earth." But that word "Lord" does not appear in the original text, nor is the Hebrew **send ye** <note>Hebrew: **שלחו** (*shilchu*)</note> translated as "I will send" or "send forth [singular]," but rather as "send ye [plural]." Furthermore, regarding that "lamb, the ruler of the earth," many—to add this <note>Latin: *agab orcham*, a Hebrew-derived idiom meaning "by the way" or "incidentally"</note>—have understood it to mean Christ. They have interpreted the following words about *the Rock of the desert, etc.* as referring to Ruth the Moabitess, from whom Christ was to be born. Compare, following Jerome, the works of Nicholas of Lyra, Dionysius the Carthusian, Hector Pintus, Caspar Sánchez, Cornelius à Lapide, Jacobus Tirinus, Giovanni Menochio, and others on this passage. It is as if Ruth, who had long since passed away, were here finally being demanded to be sent; but we leave these matters to be discussed by the theologians.

§. 5. Samuel Bohlius explained it in another sense through apposition, while—as reported by the Great Doctor Abraham Calovius in his *Anti-Grotius*—he thought the King himself was called a "ram" by way of mockery <note>Greek: *μυκτηρισμὸν* (*myktērismon*), a rhetorical term for a sneer or sarcasm</note> in this manner: *Formerly the Moabites used to send a certain number of rams to the king of Judah as a recognition of his superiority; now they are warned by this mockery that they should send the King himself, the Ruler of the Land, as if he were the ram.*

<summary>This page analyzes diverse interpretations of the word "lamb" in Isaiah 16:1, ranging from metaphorical "noble leaders" or "battering rams" to the Christological interpretation linking the passage to Ruth the Moabitess.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Isaiah 16:1, specifically debating the grammatical case of the word "ruler" and the historical context of the Moabite tribute of lambs to the Kings of Israel and Judah.</meta>

...and try to see if they can find grace in the eyes of their superior. This might seem like a bit of a stretch to some. Blessed Luther and Arias <note>Arias Montanus, a 16th-century Spanish orientalist</note> interpret **ruler** <note>Hebrew: **מושל** (*moshel*)</note> in the Vocative case <note>A grammatical case used for direct address: "O Ruler!"</note>, which is not inconvenient, except that the difference in number between **ruler** (singular) and **send ye** <note>Hebrew: **שלחו** (*shilkhu*), which is plural</note> stands in the way, although even this can be excused in a certain way. Indeed, the analogy of the language more easily allows you to interpret **ruler** in the Vocative than in the Dative <note>The "to" or "for" case</note>, as Clarius, Grotius, and others do; for to avoid confusion, a specific marker would be needed, and in that case, the word **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> would require a "Lord" accent <note>A disjunctive accent in Hebrew cantillation that marks a pause</note>, whereas it actually has a "Servant" accent <note>A conjunctive accent indicating the words should be read closely together</note>.

### §. 7
To briefly explain our own opinion, **lamb** <note>Hebrew: **כר** (*kar*)</note> is best translated as a collective noun for "lambs," while the following phrase **ruler of the land** <note>Hebrew: **מושל ארץ** (*moshel eretz*)</note> seems most closely joined to the **lamb** in the genitive case <note>The possessive case: "lambs *of* the ruler"</note>, following the translation of Pagninus, Junius, Glassius, and others. Thus the sense is: *Send (O Moabites) the lamb or lambs (for such names can be used for both the species and the individual) of the ruler of the land*—that is, the lambs that belong to or are rightfully owed to the ruler of the land, pre-eminently <note>original: *κατ' ἐξοχὴν*</note> the land of Israel. 

Clearly, the Moabites had formerly been made subjects to David for the sake of peace and protection, as seen in *2 Samuel 8:2*. A clear account exists in *2 Kings 3:4 and following* concerning a specific amount of lambs <note>original: **מאה אלף כרים** (*me'ah elef karim*), meaning "one hundred thousand lambs"</note> and rams paid by the Moabites to the Kings of Israel until the time of Ahab. Therefore, the Moabites would have been rightfully tributary to Hezekiah, even though he was not the King of Israel, both as the heir of David and because the King of Judah had formerly joined with the King of Israel to subdue the rebellious Moabites, standing together as if they were one person, as noted in *2 Kings 3:7*. Hence, these are not so much sacrificial lambs (as Oleastrius and others argue) as they are "tribute lambs" now being demanded—and this is said with a certain irony, which Henry Mollerus seems to have explained well in his commentary on this passage.

### §. 8.
Most Jewish commentators agree on the main point of this matter, and I will now add their votes. Rashi says:

<meta>This page continues the philological and historical analysis of Isaiah 16:1. It focuses on the historical tribute of lambs once paid by the Moabite kings to Israel, arguing that since the Northern Kingdom of Israel has fallen, Moab should now send this tribute to King Hezekiah in Jerusalem (Zion) to seek protection.</meta>

Do not pride yourselves so much in your arrogance. Did you not know that the ruler of your land—Mesha, King of Moab—was a sheep-breeder <note>Hebrew: **נוקד** (*noked*), a specialist in a specific breed of sheep</note> among kings, and he paid to the King of Israel one hundred thousand lambs? Send that lamb of your ruler from Sela <note>The "Rock," often identified with the city of Petra</note> of the desert to the mountain of the daughter of Zion. *Do not exalt yourselves so greatly; do you not know that the ruler of your land—though this title should be referred to Hezekiah—Mesha, King of Moab, is called a sheep-breeder in the Book of Kings <note>Reference to 2 Kings 3:4</note> and paid the King of Israel a hundred thousand lambs? Your ruler sent that number of lambs from the rock of the desert to the mountain of the daughter of Zion.* Rabbi David Kimchi <note>A prominent medieval Jewish biblical commentator and grammarian, known as the Radak</note> explains this more clearly:

"Send the lamb"—meaning those lambs and rams that you used to send to the King of Israel as tribute <note>Hebrew: **למס** (*le-mas*)</note>, but then you rebelled against him. You should have sent that tribute to him, and then this evil would not have come upon you if you had done so. And he says *from Sela of the desert*, which is the capital city of the kingdom of Moab. And he says *to the mountain of the daughter of Zion*, which is the capital of the kingdom of Israel <note>Kimchi refers to Zion as the capital of the Hebrew people generally</note>. Since there was no longer a king in Samaria <note>The Northern Kingdom of Israel had been conquered by Assyria at this point in history</note>, you ought to send the tribute to Zion, to the King of Judah, etc.

*Send the lamb, that is, those lambs and rams which you were accustomed to send to the King of Israel for tribute and then rebelled against him; now you ought to send it in place of tribute, which if you do, you will escape the imminent evil. Indeed, he says: "from the Rock of the desert," that is, the capital of Moab, and adds: "to the mountain of the daughter of Zion," that is, the capital of the Israelite kingdom; however, since there is no longer a king of Samaria, you must send to Zion to the King of Judah, etc. Let the most learned Abravanel <note>Isaac Abravanel, a 15th-century Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> approach this passage (folio 32, column 3, near the middle):*

And it is more correct to explain that he said to Moab: "Why do you Moabites pride yourselves? Do you not know that the ruler of your land was a sheep-breeder and paid the King of Israel a hundred thousand lambs every year? Because you grew strong and were proud of yourselves, you rebelled against the King of Israel and did not give him tribute. Now that humiliation has come upon you, return to your servitude; even though there is no longer a King of Israel, send that lamb to the ruler of the land of Judah, who is Hezekiah, from Sela of the desert, which is the capital of the kingdom of Edom <note>original: **אדם** (*Edom*); the mountainous region neighboring Moab where Sela/Petra is located</note>, to Mount Zion which is the head...

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Isaiah 16:1, specifically regarding the tribute Moab owed to the Southern Kingdom of Judah (under King Hezekiah) following the fall of the Northern Kingdom of Israel. It then transitions to a new discussion on the etymology and meaning of the name 'Ariel' in Isaiah 29:1.</meta>

...the head of his kingdom / and be humbled and be submissive to him. <note>original: **ראש מלכו / וחורדו ותהיו נכנעים אליו**</note> *It is better to explain it this way:* that the Prophet says to the Moabites: "Why do you lift yourselves up in pride, O Moabites? Do you not know that the ruler of your land is rich in livestock and was accustomed to pay the King of Israel a hundred thousand lambs every year? But because you grew powerful and exalted yourselves, it happened that you rebelled against him of your own accord and refused to pay him tribute. But now, while you are being humbled again, you should return to your obedience; and although the King of Israel no longer exists <note>The Northern Kingdom of Israel fell to Assyria in 722 BCE</note>, send those lambs to the ruler of the land of Judah—Hezekiah—from Sela <note>Petra</note>, the desert capital of Moab, to Mount Zion, the capital of Judah, and submit yourselves." One should also consult Abendana, who in this place follows the footsteps of Rabbi Joseph Kimchi. Finally, the path is open to any of the Christian interpreters who share this same view, such as Brentius, Osiander, Clarius, Moller, Junius, Calvin, Piscator, Grotius, and others. See also Glassius, *Sacred Philology*, page 1165, and Sixtinus Amama, *Antibarbarus Biblicus*, book 4, page 666, at the end.

### THIRD UNCERTAINTY.
### *Why is* Ariel <note>Hebrew: **אריאל**</note> *so called in Isaiah XXIX. 1?*

§. I. The word *Ariel* is composed of *Ari* (lion) and *El* (God). It is used twice, namely in 2 Samuel 23:20 and 1 Chronicles 11:22, in reference to lions. For it says that Benaiah the son of Jehoiada struck down *the two Ariels [heroes] of Moab* <note>Hebrew: **את שני אריאל מואב**</note>, and shortly thereafter another lion in a cistern during a time of snow—a time when lions are otherwise famished and more ferocious, as Peter Martyr <note>Pietro Martire Vermigli, a 16th-century Italian Reformer</note> reminds us. Jonathan <note>The Targum Jonathan, an Aramaic translation</note> explains this as *the two heroes of Moab* <note>Aramaic: **תרין רברבי מואב**</note>, that is, men endowed with a lion-like spirit. Rashi (on 1 Chronicles 11), Kimchi (on both passages), and Solomon ben Melech all agree. Solomon ben Melech (on 2 Samuel 23) says: "He names the great and mighty men Ariel; for the lion is great, brave, and strong among the beasts; likewise, the word *Ari* [lion] is a term for strength and power, just as in the phrase 'it is in the power <note>original: **אל** (*El*)</note> of my hand' [Genesis 31:29]. Therefore, the word is composed of *Ari* and *El*." <gloss>He names the great and strong men Ariel; for the lion is great, strong, and robust.</gloss>

<summary>This page argues that Moab should redirect its historical tribute of lambs to King Hezekiah in Jerusalem and begins an etymological study of 'Ariel,' defining it as 'Lion of God' or a term for great heroes.</summary>
<keywords>Moab, tribute, Hezekiah, Zion, Sela, Ariel, etymology, Benaiah son of Jehoiada, lion, Isaiah 16:1, Isaiah 29:1</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name 'Ariel,' specifically analyzing its components 'Ari' (lion) and 'El' (God or strength). It examines various interpretations of the term as found in 2 Samuel 23:20 and transitions to its use in Ezekiel 43 regarding the altar of the Temple.</meta>

...among living beings, and the word *El* <note>Hebrew: **אל**</note> likewise denotes strength and power, as in Genesis 31:29. Therefore, the word is conflated from *Ari* <note>Hebrew: **ארי**, meaning 'lion'</note> and *El* <note>Hebrew: **אל**, meaning 'God' or 'strength'</note>. Others consider *Ariel Sheni* <note>Hebrew: **אריאל שני**, 'two Ariels'</note> to imply two very fortified citadels. This is the view of Rabbi Levi ben Gersom <note>Also known as Gersonides (1288–1344), a medieval Jewish philosopher and commentator</note> on 2 Samuel 23:

-> "A fortified tower is called Ariel, and for this reason the Holy Temple is called Ariel. The meaning here is that he [Benaiah] struck the men of those fortified towers that were in Moab. The word is composed of *Ari* and *El*, and its meaning is 'Strong Lion,' because the lion is of great strength. It is also possible that they had the form of a lion depicted on them to indicate power and strength." <note>original: **הנה המגדל החזק יקרא אריאל ולזה נקרא בית המקדש אריאל והרצון בו שהוא הכה אנשי המגדלי / החזקי / ההם שהיו במואב והמלה מורכבת מארי ומאל וענינו ארי תקיף כי הארי הוא רב הגבורה ואפשר גם כן שהיה בהם צורת אריה להורות על התוקף ועל החוזק**</note> <-

A fortified citadel is called Ariel, from which the temple is also called Ariel. It is hinted here that Benaiah killed those who held those fortified Moabite citadels. The word is composed of *Ari* and *El* and is to be explained as: "Strong Lion," for the lion excels in strength. It is also possible that a figure of a lion was depicted on those citadels as a symbol of strength. However, the "two Ariels" <note>original: **אריאל שני**</note> mentioned here are two immense lions, and there is no reason for us to depart from the literal meaning, especially since a narrative about another lion being killed follows immediately. Since *El* in such a compound is usually not the adjective "strong" but the name of GOD, you might not absurdly translate it as "Lions of GOD"—that is, the strongest of all and witnesses to the power of the Creator.

§. 2. Elsewhere, *Ariel* (from which Gregorius Francus in his *Sacred Lexicon*, page 352, too readily believes the word *aram* <note>possibly referring to a high place or altar hearth</note> is derived) is certainly used in Ezekiel 43:15–16 regarding Ezekiel’s altar, or at least its uppermost part. Here, interpreters are concerned as to why the altar is called *Ariel*. As for what Saint Jerome <note>The 4th-century translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> noted—that *Ariel* (as if it were *Uriel* <note>Hebrew: **אוריאל**, 'Flame of God'</note>) could signify the "illumination of GOD"—the rules of the language do not allow it. Furthermore, it is a weak argument which Cornelius à Lapide <note>A prominent 17th-century Jesuit biblical exegete</note> (following Arias Montanus) explains on page 260 of his commentary on Isaiah and page 1226 on Ezekiel, suggesting among other things "Ram of Malediction," because rams were offered on the altar for the removal of a curse, etc. According to the literal sense <note>original Greek: **Κατὰ ῥηטὸν**</note>, *Ariel* sounds like "Lion of GOD." The reason given by the ancients for this...

...as [they] render [it], such as Rashi <note>Solomon n Isaac (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note> on *Isaiah 29:1*, Kimchi <note>David Kimchi (1160–1235), a medieval rabbi and grammarian</note> on *Ezekiel 43*, Rabbi Solomon ben Melech <note>A 16th-century grammarian and author of *Mikhlol Yofi*</note> on both passages, and Abravanel <note>Isaac Abravanel (1437–1508), a Portuguese Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> on *Ezekiel*, folio 213, column 4: "Because the sacred fire descending from heaven rested upon it like a lion." <note>original: **לכי שהאשהקרו שה היורדת מן השמים היתה רבוצה בו כארי** / *quod ignis facer cœlitùs delapfus in illo recubuerit instar Leonis*</note> Furthermore, concerning the sacred fire, the Talmudic scholars record these things in the tractate *Yoma*, chapter 1, at the end of folio 21, column 2: 

<margin>Five.</margin> "Five things are said regarding the fire of the woodpile [on the altar]: it rested like a lion, it was as bright as the sun, it possessed substance <note>literally "real substance" or body</note>, it consumed wet things as easily as dry ones, and finally, it emitted no smoke." <note>original: **חמשה דברים נאמרו באש של מערכה רבוצה כארי וברה כחמה וישבה ממש ואוכלת לחין כובשין ואינה מעלה עשן**</note> Shortly after: "How can it be said to have rested like a lion? Is there not a tradition that Rabbi Hanina, the deputy high priest, said: 'I saw it resting like a dog'?" <note>original: **רבוצה כארי והתניא אמר ר' חנינא סגן הכהנים אני ראיתיה ורבוצה ככלב**</note> But this is easily resolved: the former [the lion] refers to the First Temple <note>Solomon's Temple</note>, and the latter [the dog] to the Second Temple <note>The temple rebuilt after the Babylonian exile</note>. <note>original: **ולא קשיא כאן במקדש ראשון כאן במקדש שני**</note> 

In the book *Kaftor Va-Ferach* <note>A 14th-century work on the laws and geography of Israel by Ishtori Haparchi</note>, folio 70, column 2, citing the book *Torat Ha-Olah* <note>A philosophical work on the Temple by Moses Isserles, the Rema</note>, part 3, chapter 2, this analogy of the sacred fire to a lion and a dog is judged not by its shape, but by its nature. It is said that in the First Temple, where many men of excellent piety lived, the fire "rested like a lion that tears and devours, having its food in abundance," <note>original: **היתה רובצת כארי הדורס ואוכל ומזונו מצוייה**</note> but in the Second Temple—an age less fertile in pious men—it rested "like a dog, that is, thin and lacking in food." <note>original: **ככלב רל דלה וענייה ממזונותיה**</note> 

Compare the renowned Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664), a Christian Hebraist</note> in his *Dissertation on the History of the Sacred Fire*, chapter 2, page 245. Villalpandus <note>Juan Bautista Villalpando (1552–1608), a Spanish Jesuit architect known for his reconstruction of the Temple</note>, among other things, seeks the reason for this name in the carvings of the Cherubim, and thus partly (according to his own hypothesis) in the lion figures around the altar, and the fact that it [the altar] had lion-like legs for feet. <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name 'Ariel,' transitioning from the symbolic fire mentioned on the previous page to the broader interpretations of the term in biblical and rabbinic literature. It explores the lion metaphor in relation to the altar, the architectural structure of the Temple, and the city of Jerusalem itself.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>

God on the altar, as if a lion, keeps watch for the protection of His city and nation. Oecolampadius <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), a leading German reformer and Hebraist</note> *on Isaiah 29* offers this analogy between the lion and the altar: just as a lion is given a lamb or a calf in the morning and evening, so too a lamb was offered on the altar every morning and evening. To us, it seems that the altar of burnt offerings can be fittingly compared to a lion because, like a massive lion devouring its prey, it consumed countless sacrificial victims and was drenched in their blood. Since this was done by divine decree, it was for this reason called *Ariel* <note>original: ארי אל</note> or *Lion of God*. Those who argue that the addition of the name *El* <note>The Hebrew word for God</note> indicates excellence or greatness are to be tolerated, provided they understand it not as the primary meaning, but by way of an epithet. Compare Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra (c. 1270–1349), an influential Franciscan commentator</note>, Brentius, Vatablus, Sanctius, Osiander, Junius, Piscator, the Dutch translators, and others *on Isaiah 29*. See also Glassius <note>Salomon Glassius (1593–1656), a Lutheran theologian known for his work on biblical philology</note>, *Sacred Philology, page 1277*.

§. 3. We come now to the passage in *Isaiah 29:1*, where some take the word *Ariel* to mean the *altar*, others the *temple*, and others the *city*. Regarding the *altar*, Jonathan <note>The Targum Jonathan, an ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Prophets</note> translates it as: "Woe to the altar, the altar built in the city in which David dwelt, etc." <note>original Aramaic: **וי מדבחא מדבחא דבני בקרתא דישרא בה דוד** / *Væ altari altari ædificato in urbe, in qua habitavit David &c.*</note> Regarding the *temple*, the Talmudic scholars (whose opinion Rashi and Kimchi do not reject in this place) provide this reasoning in the tractate *Middot, chapter 4, Mishnah*: "The Temple was narrower at the back and broader at the front, resembling a lion; as it is said: Woe to Ariel, Ariel, the city where David camped. Just as a lion is narrower at the back and broader at the front, so the Temple was narrower at the back and broader at the front." <note>original Hebrew: **והיכל צר מאחוריו ורוחב מלפניו ודומה לארי שנאמר הוי אריאל קרית חנה דוד מה הארי צר מאחריו ורחב מלפניו אף ההיכל צר מאחוריו ורחב מלפניו** / *Templum erat Angustius à posteriori, sed latius ab anteriori referebatq; leonis figuram...*</note> Compare Villalpandus <note>Juan Bautista Villalpando (1552–1608), a Spanish Jesuit known for his architectural reconstructions of the Temple</note> *Volume 2, page 298*, though there are some who deny this appearance of the Temple, as L'Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur (1591–1648), a Dutch Hebraist</note> notes *on Middot, page 168*. Otherwise, most scholars take it to mean the *city*, yet they do not agree on why it is called *Ariel*. Some, as Glassius reports on *page 980*, believe it is so called because it belonged in part to the tribe of Judah, whose emblem is considered to be the lion according to *Genesis 49:9*.

Aben Esra <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish rabbi, philosopher, and biblical commentator</note> says <note>original Hebrew: יש אומרים שנקראת כן בעבור שמזלה אריה</note> *that some wish for the city to be called thus because its zodiac sign was Leo*, but he adds <note>original Hebrew: וזה רחוק</note> *that this is far-fetched or alien to the truth*. Musculus <note>Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563), a German Reformed theologian</note> *on this passage says that Jerusalem is so named because it was fierce against God and His prophets, whom it cruelly killed, just as a lion finds a lamb in the desert; as if to say: you are not* Ariel <note>original: עריאל</note> *the city of GOD, as you wish to seem, but* Ariel <note>original: אריאל</note> *the lion of GOD, etc.* Blessed Luther <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the primary figure of the Protestant Reformation</note> *in Tom. 3. Jen. Lat. f. 356. elegantly notes on this passage that Jerusalem was previously* Ariel, *the Lion of GOD in an active sense—that is, conquering through God—but is now called the lion of God in a passive and objective sense—that is, to be attacked and conquered by GOD.*

§. 4. Nevertheless, without prejudice to others, we believe the city received its name directly from the altar, from which it held its chief excellence. Abravanel <note>Isaac Abravanel (1437–1508), a Portuguese Jewish statesman and philosopher</note> *on this passage, folio 47, column 1, near the middle, puts it well:* <note>original Hebrew: וקרא את ישראל אריאל שהוא שם המזבח וכן קראהו יחזקאל ומהאריאל ומעלה לפי שהיה ירושלם בקדושתו במזבח השם והיה בתקפו וגבורתו ארי בהיותו עם אלהיו ולכן אמר כנגדה קרית חנה דוד כי הוא עבד אתה / בכל לבבו ובכל נפשו</note> <margin>See de Vaux, *Exercises*, page 244 <note>Likely referring to a scholarly work on biblical antiquities</note></margin> <insert>he reads *as the altar* <note>original Hebrew: כמזבח</note> and disturbs the sense</insert> *He calls the Israelites Ariel, which is the name of the altar, as Ezekiel called it in chapter 43. For Jerusalem flourished in its holiness through the altar of the Lord, and it was a lion in its strength and power while it clung to God. And in this respect it is added: The city where David encamped; for here he worshipped God with all his heart. Hence, what is said in verse 2,* And it shall be to me as Ariel, *that is, "And Ariel shall be to me like Ariel," must be understood thus: The city, which bears its name from the altar, shall indeed be made like it, as it will be no less drenched with the blood of men than the altar is with the blood of victims. Compare verse 6 and following.* Jonathan <margin>See the Targum <note>The ancient Aramaic paraphrase of the Hebrew Bible</note> on this passage</margin> correctly translates: *And I will bring distress to the city of the altar [Jerusalem]... and it shall be empty and desolate, and it shall be drenched before me with the blood of the slain, even as the altar is drenched with the blood of the holy sacrifices on a feast day.*

*“And I will distress the city in which the altar is, and it shall be wasted and empty, and it shall be surrounded before me with the blood of the slain, just as the altar is surrounded on all sides by the blood of sacrifices on a feast day.”* Nor does Rabbi Solomon ben Melech <note>A 16th-century Jewish scholar known for his work *Mikhlal Yophi*, a commentary on the Bible</note> explain the sense poorly in the cited place: <note>original Hebrew: אני אעשה את העיר כמו המזבח שזורקין עליו הדם כן תהיה כל העיר מלאה דם שיהרגו אותם בשוקים וברחובות וזהו שאמר והיתה לי כאריאל</note> *“I will make this city like the altar, upon which they sprinkle blood; so also shall the whole city be full of blood, for they shall kill them in the streets and squares: this is what he said: ‘And it shall be to me as Ariel.’”* That is all.

# SUPPLEMENTARY ADDITIONS.

I. **Totaphot** <note>original Hebrew: טוטפות</note> *Exodus 13:16; Deuteronomy 6:8; & 11:18.* The Talmudists in *Sanhedrin fol. 42* and *Menachot fol. 34*, as well as Raschi <note>Rashi (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval French rabbi and commentator</note> and others, compose the word from *tat* <note>original: טט</note> and *phot* <note>original: פת</note>, claiming that the former means "two" in "Europe" <note>likely referring to Phrygian or Coptic</note> and the latter means "two" in "Africa" <note>likely referring to a Libyan dialect</note>. From this, the Jews construct the four small houses or cells of their **tephillin of the head** <note>original Hebrew: תפלין שלראש — ritual leather boxes containing Torah verses, worn during prayer</note>. Rabbi Menachem, cited in Rashi, derives it from *nataph* <note>original Hebrew: נטף</note>, meaning *to drip* or *to prophesy*, so that it denotes "speech." Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler (d. 1604), a German philologist and Hebraist</note> derives it from *taphaph* <note>original Hebrew: טפף</note>, *to beat* or *to clap*. Munster <note>Sebastian Munster (1488–1552), a German cartographer and Hebraist</note> in his notes on *Exodus 13*, and Mayer in *Philologia Sacra, book 1, page 129*, say the word is Egyptian and should be translated as **mirrors** or **spectacles** <note>original Hebrew: מראות</note>. However, the etymology is more correctly sought from an Arabism, where *tafa* <note>original Arabic: טאף</note> means *to surround*, from which also comes the Chaldean word *totaphta* <note>original: טוטפתא</note>, meaning **tiara** or **bracelet**. Thus the sense would be: *“And it shall be (namely the rite of unleavened bread, indeed the whole law, Deuteronomy 6:8) like a sign or seal on your hand, for a perpetual memory, as in Isaiah 49 verse 13; and like a tiara, or ornaments of the head which you wear on the forehead, let it always be before your eyes.”*

II. **Partemim** <note>original Hebrew: פרתמים — translated as 'nobles' or 'chiefs'</note> *Daniel 1:3; Esther 1:3.* Some consider this a corrupted form of the Greek *entimoi* <note>original Greek: ἐντίμοι — meaning 'honored'</note>, that is *homotimoi* <note>original Greek: ὁμόטיμοι — 'equal in rank'</note>, who in the works of Curtius <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus, a Roman historian</note> are the "King’s kinsmen," and in Xenophon <note>Greek historian and philosopher</note> are the *“relatives of the king”* <note>original Greek: συγγενεῖς τοῦ βασיλέως</note>. Junius <note>Franciszek Junius (1545–1602), a Huguenot theologian</note> derives it from the Greek *protoi* <note>original Greek: πρῶτοι — 'first' or 'chiefs'</note>, a view supported by Mayer in *Philologia Sacra, Part II, page 188*. Others derive it from **Perath** <note>original Hebrew: פרת — the Euphrates river</note>, suggesting they were captains from the regions around the Euphrates.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the symbolic meaning of 'Ariel' as an altar of blood and begins a philological supplement investigating the origins of the words 'Totaphot' (phylacteries) and 'Partemim' (nobles).</summary>
<keywords>Ariel, Isaiah 29, Solomon ben Melech, Totaphot, Tephillin, Rashi, Philology, Partemim, Biblical etymology</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of Persian-derived Hebrew terms from the previous page, specifically focusing on the word 'Partemim' (nobles), before moving into the third and fourth sections of the 'Supplementary Additions' regarding the Magi and the Libertines.</meta>

as Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler (d. 1604), a German philologist and author of a famous polyglot dictionary</note> says. The conjecture of Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian</note> in *Oriental Smegma* <note>The title *Smegma Orientale* literally means 'Oriental Soap,' implying a cleansing or refinement of Eastern languages</note> *p. 77* is not bad, deriving *partemim* <note>original Hebrew: פרתמים; a term for nobles or high officials used in the Book of Esther</note> from the Persian *par* <note>original: באר</note> meaning *high* or *great*, and *tem* <note>original: תם</note> meaning *spirit*, as if you were to call them "magnanimous heroes."

III. **Magi** <note>The 'Wise Men' of the Nativity story</note>, *Matthew 2:1.* They are not so named from the Greek *megas* <note>original: μέγας; meaning 'great'</note>, as if they called themselves *great [men]*; nor from *magis* <note>original: μαγὶς</note>, a tripod (according to certain authors in Ludovicus de Dieu <note>A Dutch Protestant theologian and leading 17th-century orientalist</note>, *on this passage, p. 3*); nor (as Saumaise <note>Claude Saumaise (Salmasius), a renowned French classical scholar</note> conjectured) from *Mog*, a surname of Zoroaster, from which they say a certain famous wise man named *Mochus* <note>original Greek: Μῶχον</note> was known; nor from *Magog*, according to Cedrenus <note>An 11th-century Byzantine historian</note>; nor from the Hebrew *ma'oz* <note>original: מעז</note> meaning *strength*, nor from *hagah* <note>original: הגה; to meditate</note> as if they were *mehagim* <note>original: מהגים; meditators</note> (see L'Empereur *on Middot p. 176*); nor from *mash* <note>original: מש</note> which hints at "touching," according to David de Pomis in de Dieu’s *on Acts p. 123*; nor from *much kush* <note>original: מוח כוש</note> or the "short ears" of the first author of the sect, as Al-Firuzabadi <note>A 14th-century compiler of a major Arabic dictionary</note> testifies; but rather from the Arabic root *gjafasa* <note>original: גפש</note> or *gjassa*, meaning *to explore* or *to investigate*, because they devoted themselves to the scrutiny of divine and natural matters. Compare de Dieu in the place cited and *Appendix p. 506*; Pococke <note>Edward Pococke (1604–1691), the first Professor of Arabic at Oxford</note> *on Abulfaraj p. 146*; and Hottinger, *History of Creation p. 132*.

IV. The name **Libertines** in *Acts 6:9.* Some judge it to be Egyptian, from the name *Lubra*, which would denote a synagogue situated in a high place, from which the *Lubratenu* would be those belonging to that Egyptian synagogue. Thus says Junius <note>Francis Junius (1545–1602), a Huguenot theologian</note> *on Genesis 8 and Acts 6*, whose foundations regarding the *place of disembarkation* <note>original Greek: ἀποβατηρίῳ</note> of Lubar were examined by Fuller in *Miscellanies book 2, chapter 1*. De Dieu thinks the matter can be easily settled if the following <unclear>He</unclear> <note>Referring to the Hebrew letter 'He' (ה) in the name</note> is not considered, which he notes the Syriac translator did not do. In that case, any Jewish "Libertines" <note>In Roman law, a 'libertinus' was a manumitted or freed slave</note> would be meant, followed by their specific national names. However, that most learned man correctly confesses that the <unclear>He</unclear> cannot be removed. Therefore, you should more correctly understand by *Libertines* those who had been manumitted and expelled by the Romans and, returning to their fatherland, held their own assembly [collegium], according to other scholars. They are recorded, however, as a nation, because Roman citizenship is implied by the title of "Libertines." Compare John Selden <note>A famous 17th-century English jurist and scholar of ancient law</note> in *On the Law of Nature and Nations, book 2, chapter 5*.

->GLORY TO GOD ALONE.<-

<summary>This page concludes a philological investigation into the origins of three biblical terms: the "nobles" (Partemim) from Esther, the "Magi" from the Gospel of Matthew, and the "Libertines" from the Book of Acts. The author argues that these terms are best understood through their original Persian, Arabic, and Roman legal contexts rather than through Greek or Hebrew phonetic coincidences.</summary>
<keywords>philology, etymology, Partemim, Magi, Libertines, Biblical scholarship, Hottinger, Ludovicus de Dieu, John Selden, Roman freedmen, Zoroaster</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of specific biblical terms, concluding the discussion on "Ariel" from the previous page before transitioning into a series of "Supplements" or "Additions" regarding difficult Hebrew words with foreign origins.</meta>

*“And I will distress the city in which the altar is, and it shall be devastated and empty, and it shall be surrounded before me with the blood of the slain, just as an altar is surrounded on every side by the blood of sacrifices on a feast day.”* <note>original Latin: *Et coanguſtabo civitatem...* This is a paraphrastic explanation of Isaiah 29:2 regarding Jerusalem (Ariel).</note> Nor does Rabbi Solomon ben Melech <note>A 16th-century Jewish grammarian known for his work *Mikhlal Yofei*</note> poorly explain the sense in this place: <margin>“I will make the city like the altar upon which blood is sprinkled; so shall the whole city be full of blood, for they shall be killed in the streets and the squares; and this is what it means: ‘And it shall be to me as Ariel.’”</note> *“I will make this city like the altar, which they sprinkle with blood; so also the whole city will be full of blood, for they will kill them in the streets and the squares: this is what it means: ‘And it shall be to me as Ariel.’”* That is all.

# SUPPLEMENTS.

I. **Totafot** <note>original Hebrew: טוטפות; usually translated as "frontlets," referring to the phylacteries (tefillin) worn by Jewish men during prayer</note> *Exodus 13:16, Deuteronomy 6:8, and 11:18.* The Talmudic writers (*Sanhedrin, folio 42; Menachot, folio 34*), Rashi <note>The preeminent 11th-century French rabbi and commentator</note>, and others compose the word from *Tat* and *Pat*, of which the former signifies *two* in Europe <note>specifically "Coptic" or "African" in some traditions, though the text here attributes it to "Europe"</note> and the latter signifies *two* in Africa (from which the Jews construct the four little houses or cells of their *tefillin* worn on the head). Rabbi Menachem, cited in Rashi, derives it from *nataf* <note>original: נטף</note> meaning *to drip* or *to prophesy*, so that it denotes a *word* <note>meaning a spoken revelation</note>. Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler, a 16th-century philologist</note> derives it from *taphaph* meaning *to clap*. Munster <note>Sebastian Munster (1488–1552), a German cartographer and Hebraist</note> on *Exodus 13* and Mayer (*Philologia Sacra, book 1, page 129*) say the word is Egyptian and should be translated as *spectacles* <note>original Hebrew: מראות; meaning something looked through or seen</note>. However, the etymology is more correctly sought from an Arabism, where *taf* <note>original: טאף</note> means *to surround*, from which comes the Chaldean *totaftha* meaning *tiara* or *bracelet*. Thus, the sense would be: *And it shall be—namely the rite of the unleavened bread, or indeed the whole law (Deut. 6:8)—like a sign or a seal upon your hand, for a perpetual memorial (as in Isaiah 49:13); and like a tiara, or ornaments of the head, which you wear on the forehead, let it always be before your eyes.*

II. **Partemim** <note>original Hebrew: פרתמים; a term for "nobles" or "magnates" of Persian origin</note> *Daniel 1:3, Esther 1:3.* Some think it is a corrupted form of the Greek *homotimoi* <note>original Greek: ὁμότιμοι; meaning "those of equal honor"</note>, who in Curtius <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus, a Roman historian</note> are called "Kinsmen of the King," and in Xenophon <note>A Greek historian</note> are called "the king’s relatives" <note>original Greek: συγγενεῖς τοῦ βασιλέως</note>. Junius <note>Francis Junius (1545–1602), a Reformed theologian</note> derives it from the Greek *protoi* <note>original Greek: πρῶτοι; meaning "the first ones" or "chiefs"</note>, which Mayer supports (*Philologia Sacra, Part II, page 188*). Others derive it from *Perath* <note>the Euphrates River</note>, as if they were the captains of the region around the Euphrates.

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of "Ariel" using Jewish commentary and begins a supplement on difficult Hebrew terms, exploring the etymological origins of "Totafot" (phylacteries) and "Partemim" (nobles).</summary>
<keywords>Ariel, Solomon ben Melech, Totafot, phylacteries, Rashi, Partemim, etymology, Persian loanwords, biblical philology</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page concludes the etymological discussion on the Hebrew word "Partumim" before moving into the third and fourth sections of the "Supplements," which examine the origins of the terms "Magi" and "Libertines" as found in the New Testament.</meta>

...as Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler (d. 1604), a German Hebraist and author of a famous pentaglott dictionary</note> says. The conjecture of Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger (1620–1667), a Swiss philologist and theologian</note> (*Oriental Smegma, p. 77*) is not a bad one, deriving *Partumim* <note>original Hebrew: פרתמים; a term used in the books of Esther and Daniel to refer to nobles or princes of royal blood</note> from the Persian *par*, meaning "high" or "great," and *remi*, meaning "spirit," as if you were to call them magnanimous heroes.

III. **The Magi** (*Matthew 2:1*) are not named from the Greek *megas* [great], because they would then call themselves <margin>great ones</margin> "megalo-"; nor from *magos*, meaning a tripod (according to some in Ludovicus de Dieu <note>A Dutch orientalist and scholar of the Syriac language</note>, *on this passage, p. 3*); nor (as Salmasius <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a French classical scholar</note> conjectured) from *Mog*, a nickname of Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism</note>, from which they say a certain famous sage named *Mochus* is derived; nor from *Magog*, according to Cedrenus <note>Georgius Cedrenus, an 11th-century Byzantine historian</note>; nor from the Hebrew *ma’oz*, meaning "strength," nor from *hagah* as if *mehagi* (see L'Empereur <note>Constantijn L'Empereur, a Dutch scholar of Hebrew and rabbinic literature</note> *on the Middot, p. 176*); nor does it hint at *ma'as* [to reject], according to David de Pomis in L. de Dieu (*Acts, p. 123*); nor from *mukh kush* or "short ears" <note>original Latin: *curtis auribus*</note> belonging to the first founder of the sect, according to Al-Firuzabadi <note>A 14th-century Persian lexicographer</note>. Rather, it comes from the Arabic root *gjafasa* <note>original Hebrew script: גפש</note> or *gjassa*, meaning "to explore" or "to investigate," because they devoted themselves to the study of divine and natural things. Compare L. de Dieu (in the cited place and *Appendix, p. 506*), Pococke <note>Edward Pococke (1604–1691), a pioneering English Orientalist</note> (*on Abulfaraj, p. 146*), and Hottinger (*History of Creation, p. 132*).

IV. The name of the **Libertines** (*Acts 6:9*) is thought by some to be Egyptian, derived from the name *Lubra*, which might denote a synagogue situated in a high place; from this, the *Lubratenu* would be those belonging to the Egyptian synagogue. So says Junius <note>Francis Junius the Elder (1545–1602), a Huguenot theologian</note> (*on Genesis 8 and Acts 6*), whose arguments regarding the *Lubar* <note>a term associated with the landing place of Noah’s Ark, later linked to the Libertine synagogue by some scholars</note> were examined by Fuller <note>Nicholas Fuller (1557–1626), an English Hebraist and philologist</note> (*Miscellanies, book 2, chapter 1*). De Dieu thinks the matter can be easily settled if the following "and" is ignored, noting that the Syriac translator did not include it. In that case, it would imply any Jewish "Libertines" [freedmen], followed by their specific names. However, a very learned man rightly admits that the "and" cannot be removed. Therefore, you may more correctly understand the *Libertines* as those who had been manumitted <note>original Latin: *manumissi*; the legal process of freeing a slave in Rome</note> and expelled by the Romans, and returning to their fatherland, they maintained their own College [synagogue], according to others. They are listed as a nation because Roman citizenship is implied by the title of "Libertines." Compare John Selden <note>An English jurist and scholar of ancient laws</note> (*On the Law of Nature and Nations, book 2, chapter 5*).

->GLORY TO GOD ALONE.<-

<meta>This page continues the philological "Supplements" (Additamenta) from the previous page.
