...as Schindler <note>Valentin Schindler, a 16th-century Hebraist</note> says. Not bad is the conjecture of Hottinger <note>Johann Heinrich Hottinger, a Swiss philologist; see his *Oriental Smegma*, p. 77</note>, who derives **Partumim** <note>original Hebrew: פרתמים; referring to nobles or princes in the Book of Esther</note> from the Persian *par* <note>original Persian/Hebrew script: באר</note> meaning "high" or "great," and *tam* <note>original Hebrew script: תם</note> meaning "Spirit," as if you were to call them "magnanimous heroes."

III. **Magi** <note>The "Wise Men" or astrologers mentioned in the Nativity story</note> (*Matthew 2:1*) are not so called from the Greek *megas* <note>original Greek: μέγας</note> because they called themselves "great ones" <note>original Greek: μεγάλους</note>; nor from *magis* <note>original Greek: μαγὶς</note>, meaning a tripod (according to certain authors in Louis de Dieu’s commentary *on this place*, p. 3); nor (as Salmasius <note>Claude Saumaise, a great 17th-century classical scholar</note> conjectured) from *Mog*, the surname of Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet</note>, from which they say a certain famous sage named *Mochus* <note>original Greek: Μῶχον</note> originated; nor from *Magog*, according to Cedrenus <note>An 11th-century Byzantine historian</note>; nor from the Hebrew *ma'oz* <note>original Hebrew: מעוז</note> meaning "strength"; nor from *haga* <note>original Hebrew: הגה</note> as if they were *mehagim* <note>original Hebrew: מהגים, meaning "those who meditate or mutter"</note> (see L’Empereur, *on the Middot*, p. 176); nor does it hint at *massa* <note>original Hebrew: משא, meaning "a burden" or "prophecy"</note>, according to David de Pomis <note>A Jewish physician and lexicographer</note> in Louis de Dieu’s *on Acts*, p. 123; nor from *kuach muach* <note>original Hebrew: כוח מוח</note> or the "short ears" of the first founder of the sect, as witnessed by Al-Firuzabadi <note>A 14th-century compiler of a famous Arabic dictionary</note>; but rather from the Arabic root *gjafasa* <note>original Hebrew script: גפש</note>, *gjassa*, meaning "to explore" or "to investigate," because they devoted themselves to the scrutiny of divine and natural things. Compare Louis de Dieu in the place cited and in the *Appendix*, p. 506; Pococke <note>Edward Pococke, a renowned English Orientalist</note> *on Abulfaraj*, p. 146; and Hottinger, *History of Creation*, p. 132.

IV. The name of the **Libertines** (*Acts 6:9*) is thought by some to be Egyptian, derived from the name *Lubra*, which denotes a Synagogue situated in a high place, from which the *Lubratenu* would be those belonging to that Egyptian Synagogue. So says Junius <note>Francis Junius, a Reformed theologian</note> *on Genesis 8* and *Acts 6*, whose arguments regarding the "place of landing" <note>original Greek: ἀποβατηρίῳ</note> called *Lubar* were examined by Fuller in *Miscellanies*, Book 1, Chapter 2. Dieu thinks the matter can be easily settled if the following letter *He* <unclear>ה</unclear> is ignored, noting that the Syrian translator did not include it. In that case, any "Freedmen" <note>original Latin: Libertinos; individuals released from slavery</note> of Jewish descent would be meant, whose specific national names then follow. However, as a very learned man correctly admits, the letter *He* <unclear>ה</unclear> cannot be removed. Therefore, you should more correctly understand the *Libertines* as those who had been manumitted [freed] and expelled by the Romans and, returning to their homeland, maintained their own Association <note>original Latin: Collegium</note> alongside others. They are listed as a nation because Roman citizenship is implied by the title of "Freedmen." Compare John Selden <note>A famous 17th-century English jurist and scholar of Jewish law</note>, *On the Law of Nature and Nations*, Book 2, Chapter 5.

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page follows the completion of the alphabetical index of authors (Index III). It is the title page for a philological dissertation on the Masora, indicating a new section or a specific academic defense within the larger volume. The previous page ended with a list of authors from Vatable to Zanchius.</meta>

->The Tradition of Tradition<- <note>original Hebrew: "Masoret Ha-Masoret"; a title likely referencing Elijah Levita's famous 16th-century work on the Hebrew text</note>
->OR<-
### A PHILOLOGICAL DISSERTATION
->*Concerning*<-
# THE MASORA,
->OR THE<-
## SACRED CRITICISM
### OF THE HEBREWS,
->*Which*<-
->With the help of God,<- <note>original Greek: "Σὺν Θεῷ παλάμαι" (Syn Theō palamai)</note>
->**In the nourishing Leucorea,**<- <note>original Latin: "In Almâ Leucoreâ"; "Leucorea" is the traditional academic name for the University of Wittenberg, a Greek translation of the town's name meaning "White Mountain"</note>
->UNDER THE PRESIDENCY OF<-
## MASTER AUGUST PFEIFFER,
->Adjunct of the Philosophical Faculty and currently Professor Extraordinary,<- <note>August Pfeiffer (1640–1698) was a celebrated German Lutheran theologian and orientalist known for his expertise in Hebrew and biblical hermeneutics</note>
->*For the public examination of fellow scholars*<- <note>original Greek: "συμφιλολογούντων ἐξετάσει" (symphilologountōn exetasei)</note>
->*is presented by*<-
## MICHAEL MEYER,
->of Wilster, Holstein,<-
->*In the year 1665.*<-
->*On the [blank] day of August, in the Great Auditorium,*<-
->*During the morning hours.*<-
->REVISED IN THE YEAR 1670.<-

---
->WITTEBERG,<-
->At the expense of **Daniel Schmak**, University Typographer.<-

<summary>This is the formal title page for a 17th-century academic dissertation on the Masora (the tradition of Hebrew biblical preservation), defended by Michael Meyer under the supervision of the noted scholar August Pfeiffer at the University of Wittenberg.</summary>
<keywords>Masora, Philology, Wittenberg, August Pfeiffer, Michael Meyer, Sacred Criticism, Hebrew Bible, 17th-century academia</keywords>
